United Airlines blocks AI agents from booking flights, citing cybersecurity risks
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
17:17:58 UTC
02:17:58 KST

United Airlines said it will restrict access to AI agents seeking to book flights and manage reservations on their users’ behalf, citing a risk to cybersecurity and undermining federal regulations.
The airline is the latest American corporate giant to prevent customers from using AI agents and autonomous assistants to access their systems. The move comes as airlines and banks are struggling to differentiate between real customers and AI systems operating on their behalf, such as when users share their logins with their AI assistants. Sharing passwords with apps and assistants can create security risks if those credentials get leaked or exposed.
United warns that “sharing account credentials with personal, third-party, AI assistants can expose sensitive information, and actions taken by these tools may result in unintended purchases, account changes, or use of MileagePlus miles.”
The airline also warns of a risk of violating federal aviation rules, such as requiring that a human completes flight check-ins to acknowledge that their bags do not contain banned substances.
“As a result, United is taking steps to restrict access — directly within our digital platforms and indirectly via travel agencies and aggregators — to prohibit use by unauthorized, third-party personal AI agents or assistants,” the airline said in a statement. “This work includes updates to our booking, ticketing and distribution policies.”
The airline’s move follows a warning from banks, including Bank of America, which last week urged customers not to share their passwords with their AI assistants.

SoftBank’s billion-dollar loss may have put a cap on AI’s natural gas ambitions
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
17:00:52 UTC
02:00:52 KST

SoftBank might not be the first to invest in a hot trend, but when it does, it goes big.
Witness its $30 billion bet on OpenAI in early 2025 or its announcement in February that its SB Energy subsidiary would spend $33 billion on a 9.2-gigawatt natural gas power plant. If you can’t be first, be biggest, I guess.
That philosophy apparently spilled over into procurement, where orders were also supersized. Starting about two years ago, SoftBank reportedly put down deposits on more than 13 gigawatts of turbines, almost 50% more than it needed.
The decision to over-order undoubtedly helped secure turbines and speed up SoftBank’s projects.
“SB Energy did not have a choice except to overbook turbines in the future if they wanted some turbines right away,” Peter Gardett, CEO of Noreva, told TechCrunch via email.
The energy world was caught off guard by the swiftness and magnitude of AI’s rising power demands. Queues for turbine orders now extend into the early 2030s. As natural gas was shaping up to be a go-to power source for the AI boom, CEO Masayoshi Son bet that by deploying his investment firm’s significant war chest, he could corner a portion of the market. If the AI world wanted power for its data centers, it would have to come to him.
Now, that bet appears to have fizzled. SoftBank announced a $7.1 billion quarterly loss on Tuesday, with over half of that stemming from losses related to power-generating equipment. The investor had paid turbine manufacturers nonrefundable deposits to secure orders, and now it considered much of those worthless.
The move was also a costly bit of price discovery, Gardett said. Rather than overpay and potentially still be short of turbines, SoftBank overpaid and over-ordered.
“This charge is just that price discovery finally happening,” Gardett said.
The magnitude of that misplaced bet — over $3 billion — reflects some of the electricity mania that has overtaken the AI world. Faced with years-long waits to connect to the grid, many hyperscalers and developers have turned to natural gas as a sort of energy security blanket, something that is proven and familiar.
As AI data centers spend more time in operation, their operators are getting a better feel for what’s required to keep them operating. Natural gas might seem like a safe bet, but there are still risks, including winter curtailments when supplies dip and demand spikes, Brent Nelson, senior managing director of markets and strategy at Ascend Analytics, told the Equity podcast Wednesday.
In light of that, Nelson said the grid might still be their best bet. Most data centers would be best served by a grid connection, albeit a smaller one than they might normally request. To make up for the shortfall, large batteries are a cost-effective choice, Nelson said. They’d help shave peak loads or ride out requests from the utility to curtail electricity use.
Data center operators might be coming to that same conclusion, leading the natural gas market to develop more slowly than SoftBank expected.
SoftBank’s natural gas-related losses are unlikely to have a domino effect, leading to mass cancellations of gas power plants. AI’s power demand appears to be too large for that. For now, natural gas is riding high, though SoftBank may have found its ceiling.

‘The end of Tesla!’: Cybertruck diehards turn on Elon Musk after he pulls the plug
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
17:00:17 UTC
02:00:17 KST

The Cybertruck is heading to that shiny cyber-dumpster in the sky, leaving the folks who built online identities around Elon Musk’s polarizing pickup feeling bereft. Usually, Musk’s fanbase treads lightly around their idol’s mercurial decisions. This time, however, the truck’s demise has sparked rare backlash within the Tesla community — aimed squarely at the guy who signed its death warrant.
‘The end of Tesla!’: Cybertruck diehards turn on Elon Musk after he pulls the plug
Tesla’s polarizing pickup is heading to the scrap heap, leaving its biggest online boosters feeling betrayed and questioning Musk’s next big pivot.
Troy Meekhof, who posts on numerous social platforms under the moniker “The Cybertruck Guy,” has been vocal about his disappointment in Musk’s decision to wind down Cybertruck production by the end of summer 2027. (The original plan was to start winding down production in the spring, but then Musk decided to extend it a few months.)
In a 33-minute video on YouTube, Meekhof argues that Tesla never fully committed to making the Cybertruck a success, citing a lack of advertising, confusing pricing changes, and the decision to shift focus before the truck could fully establish itself in the market.
“It feels like they didn’t try to make Cybertruck a success,” he said.
He also had choice words for Musk’s reasons for ending the Cybertruck’s short run to make room at Giga Texas for the fully autonomous Cybercab: “Cancelling all of your best products... in favor of an autonomous platform that remains both software and regulatory constrained... seems like an absolutely terrible idea,” Meekhof vented.
The Cybertruck wasn’t the only Tesla product to meet an untimely end — at least according to those with stakes (and often stock options) in the company’s success. Earlier this year, Tesla discontinued the Model S and Model X, two of its flagship vehicles. With the end of the Cybertruck, Tesla is now left with just the Model 3 and Model Y as vehicles you can actually buy. (The Semi is strictly a commercial vehicle, while the Cybercab’s availability as a consumer product remains dubious, at best.)
“Cancelling Cybertruck after all the years of getting it to production to ‘make room for CyberCab’ is a lame excuse,” @TeslaXplored wrote on X. “There are like 500 CyberCabs in one city that only Tesla influencers are riding in. Taking away consumer cars instead of building what the people want will be the end of Tesla!”
He added, “Very sad what happened to Tesla over the last 5 years.”
Meanwhile, at least one prominent aftermarket company is offering to step in and pick up the mantle. Unplugged Performance, which makes aftermarket accessories, said it would produce the Cybertruck and other vehicles on the truck platform under license, with investors funding the effort: “Worth a conversation?”
Musk, for his part, seems less interested in litigating the particulars of Cybertruck production and more intent on selling a vision of Tesla’s future centered around the Cybercab and humanoid robots. “Cybercab will do a majority of all passenger miles in any jurisdiction where it is not prohibited by law,” Musk wrote on X earlier this week. “Obvious to anyone with eyes and a brain.”
He also pushed back on claims that the Cybertruck was killed because it was unprofitable. “The Cybertruck order rate far exceeds our production rate,” Musk said. “We will try to make as many as possible before shutting down the line in about 8 months, but will have to return deposits for a lot of people.”
To be sure, Cybertruck sales have been disappointing over its entire production run. Musk initially theorized that Tesla could sell 250,000-500,000 Cybertrucks annually, but the actual numbers have been far below those projections. Last year, Tesla is estimated to have sold around 20,000 trucks; the company is on track to sell fewer than that this year.
Whether Musk’s autonomous utopia pans out or ends up joining the Cybertruck in the vaporware graveyard remains to be seen. For now, the stainless-steel wedge that was supposed to be the ultimate symbol of an indestructible future has become a monument to Musk’s shifting attention span — and a stark reminder to Tesla’s most loyal boosters that in Elon’s world, nothing should be taken for granted.

Phota Labs uses AI to turn everyday photos into professional-looking shots
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
17:00:00 UTC
02:00:00 KST

A new iOS app called Phota Labs gives users access to a personal AI photo assistant that turns everyday snapshots into images that look like they were taken by a professional photographer, all while preserving the authenticity of the image.
Rather than giving consumers yet another photo-editing tool, Phota Labs aims to use advances in AI to automate the editing process so it’s easier for anyone to get access to better photos.
The a16z-backed startup was founded by Cecilia Zhang and Zach Xia, who met at Adobe while working on computational photography products, including Project Indigo, Lightroom, and Photoshop.
The duo saw a gap in the editing tools on the market and believed that although editing tools can be powerful for enhancing photos, they aren’t very effective at fixing a photo that wasn’t captured well in the first place. Phota Labs changes that by focusing on fixing the shot itself while preserving the natural appearances of those in the image.
“Phota is your personal photo assistant that can make great photos for any moment you want to keep before you even ask, which means that it’s a very proactive experience,” Zhang told TechCrunch in an interview. “Think of it almost like a photographer or virtual photographer who looks over your camera roll and tries to understand what’s happening and how they could have taken that photo for you on the spot for that scene.”
The idea is to use AI to transform photos, whether that’s turning a wide-angle group photo into a tighter portrait or transforming a selfie into a regular shot.
Users can choose from different photography styles that determine how AI transforms their images, ranging from subtle edits that preserve the original lens and composition to more dramatic transformations that introduce effects like fisheye lenses, alternative angles, and entirely different perspectives.
While some people turn to ChatGPT and other generative AI tools to enhance or edit images, these tools can often alter a person’s appearance, especially when working with blurry photos because they don’t know what that person actually looks like.
“For Phota, we built our own tech,” Zhang said. ”We built this personalization technology that actually learns about people’s identity and is able to preserve them in any transformation we do to a photo.”
Images edited with Phota Labs feature invisible watermarks that identify them as altered by AI when shared on social platforms.
The startup is initially targeting people who take tons of photos and like to share them on social media, but don’t want to deal with the tedious process of editing photos. Over time, the founders envision a broader audience, from retirees looking to create beautiful photos for their grandchildren to parents who want to share polished family albums with friends and relatives.
“We have this obsession with authenticity,” Xia said. “I think there’s so much value in connecting AI with real moments and making sure those photos are not perfect, in a sense. That’s why we spend a lot of research into understanding personal context, into building this identity model because we want to make sure the photos are authentic. They’re just better photos of the same moment, rather than saying, ‘Oh, this is an AI image’.”
Xia said beta users were excited to see better versions of moments they had actually lived and saw the results as photos of real memories rather than AI-generated images. The founders found that users were taking more photos because they knew Phota Labs could enhance them later.
“We’re turning something that’s unusable into something that people want to print,” Zhang said. “People who just love photos but never really got into editing can now experience professional quality that could never have been possible. So, I think that meant so much to us. There’s so many cherished moments for photos, and we really want them to be actually meaningful and something that you can remember.”
In terms of funding, the company raised a $5.6 million seed round led by a16z last year.
Phota Labs is launching as a free app to build an initial user base before exploring a freemium model. The startup also plans to launch an Android app in the future.

Critical Apache HTTP/2 Flaw Could Let Unauthenticated Attackers Execute Code Remotely
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
17:00:00 UTC
02:00:00 KST

Critical Apache HTTP/2 Flaw Could Let Unauthenticated Attackers Execute Code Remotely (thehackernews.com)
			
		 	
				A critical flaw in Apache HTTP Server's HTTP/2 module could allow unauthenticated attackers to remotely execute code and potentially take control of affected server processes. Tracked as CVE-2026-119618, the vulnerability affects Apache HTTP Server 2.4.55 through 2.4.70 when HTTP/2 is enabled and is fixed in version 2.4.71. The Hacker News reports:  The vendor's assessment, published yesterday, also says "public research has demonstrated a reliable exploit" against a server in its default configuration with mod_http2 enabled. It does not name or link that research. Security company Wiz said later that day that no exploit was available and that no exploitation in the wild had been reported.
HTTP/2 runs only where the Protocols directive turns it on, as the module documentation explains. The directive uses h2 for HTTP/2 over TLS and h2c for HTTP/2 over plain TCP. It can be set for the whole server or for a single virtual host, so admins should check the server-wide setting and every virtual host.
Standalone builds of the module, released on GitHub as mod_h2, are affected from version 2.0.8 through 2.0.42. Admins who run the Apache HTTP Server project's own releases should move to version 2.4.71, the fixed release named in its security advisory. That release also fixes a crash in mod_md, rated important, and a one-byte overwrite of stack memory in mod_vhost_alias, rated low.

AWS Lambda Web Functions keep billing memory ~7 minutes after the last request
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
16:59:26 UTC
01:59:26 KST

Why we chose our own compute over AWS Lambda Web Functions
Lambda Web Functions kept billing memory for 5–7 minutes after the last request, so we built our own compute. On the same Next.js app, it answered warm requests 1.5–3× faster and cold-started about 3.5× faster.
When AWS announced Lambda Web Functions in October, I was excited.
At Easel, we'd spent months building infrastructure to run web applications efficiently on traditional Lambda. Native HTTP servers, concurrent requests, and active CPU billing sounded like exactly what we'd been looking for.
If Web Functions worked the way we hoped, we could potentially replace a substantial amount of our custom infrastructure.
So we decided to put them to the test.
A few weeks later, we'd decided to run our own compute instead. And as it turns out, our own infrastructure was faster, too.
The five-minute problem
Our initial experiments were promising. Web Functions handled 300 concurrent requests without errors, and a new function was serving requests in under nine seconds.
But we quickly ran into something that didn't work for us.
Execution environments continued running and accruing memory charges for 5–7 minutes after their last request.
Web Functions bill CPU only while it's actively used, but memory is billed for the entire lifetime of an execution environment, even when no requests are running.
AWS documents this behavior. What surprised us was how long those environments remained alive.
Across six representative environments, we observed idle periods ranging from 313 to 411 seconds. The processes remained active throughout, and there was no configurable idle timeout to shorten their lifetime.
For a 200ms request followed by 345 seconds of inactivity, that's roughly 1,700 times the request duration in metered memory time.
We checked the economics against CloudWatch and AWS Cost Explorer. Across our experiment, memory accounted for approximately 88% of the calculated compute and request costs at published list prices, even though our application only used around 160–210 MB.
The entire experiment amounted to about two cents at list prices. Hardly alarming on its own.
But Easel needs to support thousands of applications with unpredictable traffic. Some receive sustained traffic, while others might get only a few requests per day. At that scale, the cost of keeping independently metered environments alive starts to matter.
We even tried terminating environments ourselves after 10 seconds of inactivity. That reduced metered environment time by roughly 20×, but AWS recorded every intentional exit as a runtime failure.
It wasn't something we wanted to rely on in production.
What if we ran the compute ourselves?
Rather than adopt Web Functions, we decided to move application execution onto servers we operate ourselves, keeping traditional Lambda available for overflow.
This gives us control over scheduling and resource allocation. We can share capacity across applications without paying AWS for each idle environment, and scale beyond our own infrastructure when demand requires it.
Our first self-operated compute host went live in US East on November 2, serving applications including Easel's own website.
But taking ownership of the compute raised another question: How would our performance compare to AWS?
We deployed the same Next.js 16 application to both platforms and ran a series of benchmarks from a client in Ashburn, Virginia.
The results surprised us.
Our host delivered 1.5–3× faster median warm-request times across all four workloads.
The difference wasn't limited to network or routing overhead. Running the same 20,000 chained SHA-256 operations took 29.2ms on our host versus 43.8ms on Web Functions, about 1.5× faster.
And cold starts told a similar story.
After leaving both applications idle for 12 minutes, we triggered fresh execution environments on each platform.
In both trials, Easel started serving requests about 3.5× faster.
We also saw an interesting difference after a shorter idle period. Following one five-minute gap, Web Functions had already terminated its environment and needed 908ms to respond. Easel resumed its paused environment and responded in just 14ms.
These are early results from a single region and a small application, not a comprehensive performance study. The cold-start comparison has only two paired samples, and we haven't benchmarked sustained throughput yet.
Still, seeing our own infrastructure outperform AWS across these initial workloads was encouraging.
Taking ownership of the infrastructure
Running our own compute comes with additional responsibilities. We have to manage capacity, handle failures, and make sure resources are distributed efficiently across applications.
But it also gives us the freedom to optimize the entire execution path, rather than working around the constraints of another platform.
Web Functions may be a good fit for applications with sustained traffic, where idle memory costs are less significant. For Easel, the economics of running large numbers of independent applications led us in a different direction.
And our initial benchmarks suggest that the decision doesn't have to come at the expense of performance.
We're taking on more infrastructure complexity so developers using Easel don't have to.
How we measured
- Dates
- Benchmarks ran November 11–12, 2026 (UTC). Idle-memory measurements are from October 26, 2026.
- Setup
- The same Next.js 16 application on Easel's self-operated US East host and on AWS Lambda Web Functions in us-east-1.
- Client
- One client in Ashburn, Virginia.
- Timing
- Time to first byte, excluding TLS connection setup but including network transit and platform routing.
- Samples
- About 100 requests per route per platform for warm requests. Two paired trials for cold starts.
- Hardware
- The platforms ran on different hardware and resource configurations, so these compare deployments, not normalized hardware.
- Pricing
- AWS costs use published list prices.

AI chatbots are recommending fake retail websites, study finds
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
16:57:11 UTC
01:57:11 KST

Shoppers using AI chatbots to research products or find places to buy items could be directed to imposters and other risky websites, a new study found.
AI chatbots are recommending fake retail websites, study finds
A study testing Gemini, Perplexity, and ChatGPT found that in as many as 15 percent of conversations, chatbots displayed ‘risky’ URLs.
The study conducted by IP protection firm Podqi and shared exclusively with The Verge prompted ChatGPT, Gemini, and Perplexity with a handful of shopping-related questions involving a selection of 151 brands. Questions included “What are the top websites for buying [brand]?”; “Where can I find [brand] online?”; and “Does [brand] ship internationally? What are their regional websites?” The test ran over the course of two testing periods in September and asked about 151 small and large companies like fashion brands Lululemon, Birkenstock, Stüssy, and Madhappy (a client of Podqi) and homeware brand Le Creuset. Podqi crafted its own prompts (as opposed to using real user conversations) and tested its prompts using a tool that tracks AI answers across chatbots.
Perplexity performed the worst, showing risky sites in 15 percent of answers
Over the course of 6,795 answers, Podqi found that all three AI engines cited and showed shoppers risky sites, defined as fake websites impersonating real retailers or selling knockoffs, old brand domains that have been reregistered by someone else, or dead sites that aren’t active at all but that someone else could purchase and reuse. Google’s Gemini showed risky sites least often of the three, linking to or mentioning a risky site in 4 percent of answers. (Mentions include instances of recommending a risky site, displaying it without comment, and warning a user about it.) Perplexity performed the worst, showing risky sites in 15 percent of answers. ChatGPT fell somewhere in the middle at 8 percent. ChatGPT performed the best when it came to offering warnings about each risky site that it surfaced, doing so 24 percent of the time. Smaller brands appear especially at risk: Podqi found that queries related to larger companies had risky sites in answers about half as often.
AI companies market chatbots as being helpful for shopping tasks like researching, comparing, and discovering products or retailers. AI citing fake sites is a headache for brands and retailers, of course, but it could also quickly become a risk for consumers. The issue could be especially relevant going into the upcoming holiday shopping season, when consumers are hunting around for in-stock items and the best prices. It’s likely that more consumers will be using AI to shop this year, including buying products from brands or sites they’re not already familiar with.
“Part of this piece, too, is making sure that the consumers know, even though you are being fed a result in a particular chatbot, it is always best to verify the source of that before entering personal information or credit card information,” says Podqi CEO and cofounder Trevor West.
“We take information quality seriously, and are happy to see that Gemini performed best among the products tested,” Google spokesperson Jessica Levis Boord said in an email. “We continually work to test and improve our models, and are upfront that Gemini is AI and can make mistakes, so we display links to sources alongside responses to help people verify information.”
“Our goal is for ChatGPT to connect people with reliable places to shop,” OpenAI spokesperson Taya Christianson said in an email. “We’re continuing to improve our safeguards to make potentially deceptive sites less likely to appear in shopping-related results. We appreciate researchers sharing examples that show us where we can improve the experience.”
In Podqi’s test, certain prompts seemed to trigger risky sites more often than others. For example, AI displayed risky sites more often when asked about regional brand websites compared to other prompt categories. The tested AI engines also at times listed fake regional sites for brands that do not have any. In one conversation, Perplexity named the wrong page as Stüssy’s official website and called it “the best starting point for guaranteed authentic items.”
“Perplexity is consistently the most accurate AI on credible research benchmarks, and has been for four years. We never claim to be 100% accurate, but we do claim to be the only company relentlessly focused on it,” spokesperson Jesse Dwyer said in an email.

Google Wallet can finally unlock doors with digital home keys
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
16:56:33 UTC
01:56:33 KST

Google has added digital home key support to Google Wallet, allowing Android users to open smart locks by tapping their phone against them or simply approaching. The feature is currently available in public preview for the Google Home app, and will roll out to everyone “over the coming weeks,” according to Google’s announcement.
Google Wallet can finally unlock doors with digital home keys
Android users can add digital keys for compatible smart locks in Google Home to their Wallet app.
This was a long time coming, given Samsung and Apple users have had this convenience for a while now. While Google Home already has some unlocking functionality, this update allows users to leverage the Near Field Communication (NFC) and Ultra-Wideband (UWB) tech in their phones with compatible smart locks. Multiple locks can be controlled via Google Wallet using a single digital home key. All household members linked to the same Google Home account can also add their own key to their personal Google Wallet.
Tap to Unlock works by holding the back of an NFC-enabled Android phone near the lock reader, and doesn’t require the phone to be unlocked. Unlock on Approach needs to be manually enabled in Google Wallet, but can automatically unlock a door when it detects the user walking up.
There are a few compatibility requirements to bear in mind, however. These new Google Home capabilities are built on Aliro, the open standard designed to let any smartphone unlock any smart lock, but the hardware that supports it is still fairly limited. According to Google’s announcement, the list of supported locks currently includes the Aqara u400, Schlage Sense Pro (NFC at launch and UWB to follow), and Nuki Keypad 2 NFC, with “more locks to come.”
Users will also need a Matter-enabled Google Hub with Thread Border Router, such as the 2nd-gen Nest Hub. Tap to unlock requires NFC-enabled phones running Android 9 or later, and hands-free unlocking only works on Aliro UWB-compatible Android phones, such as the Google Pixel 10 Pro series and above. Wear OS smartwatches aren’t supported “at launch,” according to Google, which implies they might be in the future, and that seems likely given that Apple Watch already has similar unlocking features.

More game developers are using generative AI than they did last year
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
16:55:21 UTC
01:55:21 KST

More video game developers are using generative AI tools in their jobs compared to last year, according to the GDC Festival of Gaming’s latest annual industry survey. Forty percent of developers surveyed say they use it “as part of their job,” an increase from 36 percent in 2025. Usage was higher with game developers that work on free-to-play games at 58 percent and mobile-only studio workers at 70 percent.
More game developers are starting to use generative AI
The GDC Festival of Gaming’s latest annual industry survey shows an increase in AI usage among game developers.
The survey is being published as the game industry continues to reckon with generative AI tools. While many game developers, particularly indie developers, have spoken out strongly against the technology, AI is at the center of indie awards conversations in part because Big Walk developer House House “sparingly” used LLMs to assist with technical challenges in the “final stretch” of development. AI is also increasingly being used to reverse-engineer retro games. And in a post on Thursday, Xbox CEO Asha Sharma seemed to indicate that AI will be central to Xbox’s future, saying that “A small team will be able to build worlds that today demand hundreds of developers and hundreds of millions of dollars.”
Seventy three percent of the survey’s respondents said they used AI for research and brainstorming, while 62 percent used it for data analysis and summarization, and 56 percent for code generation and debugging. Twenty two percent of respondents used generative AI for concept art or visual ideation, and 8 percent used it for production art or final game assets.
The survey also included grim numbers about layoffs, with 34 percent of respondents saying they have been laid off sometime in the past two years.

Jensen Huang agrees to testify before Congress
Unknown

2026-11-12
16:36:37 UTC
01:36:37 KST

Ex-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has agreed to testify before Congress, House Foreign Affairs Committee Chairman Brian Mast said on Thursday, according to reports from Bloomberg and NBC News.
Huang, who stepped down as CEO following reports of an alleged affair with a woman suspected of spying for China, agreed to testify Wednesday after several senior lawmakers threatened to subpoena him.
Huang has agreed to testify before Congress by Thanksgiving, which falls later this month.
The development comes after House Republicans Mast, John Moolenaar, and Rick Crawford sent Huang a letter on November 4 requesting that he appear before their committees. Moolenaar chairs the House Select Committee on the Chinese Communist Party, Mast chairs the House Committee on Foreign Affairs, and Crawford chairs the House Intelligence Committee.
“Jensen and I have been very at odds as some of you already know on export controls, but Nvidia is a very important company to the United States of America,” Mast said to reporters. “I don’t want to see anything hurt in Nvidia, but he has some very serious questions to answer.”
The testimony will take place behind closed doors, though Mast said he expects the briefing to be recorded on video and transcribed.

====================================================================================================

Jensen Huang is out. What did China want from him?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
16:32:49 UTC
01:32:49 KST

Today, we’re talking about the Jensen Huang scandal at Nvidia, which is developing so quickly that we had to do an emergency update of this intro minutes before publishing this episode.
Jensen Huang is out. What did China want from him?
How the Nvidia spy scandal could backfire for Beijing, and what it tells us about AI, Trump, and export controls.
Huang just resigned from his position as CEO of Nvidia as news of his affairs, including with an alleged Chinese spy, threatened to engulf the company.
We’ll have more on that story as things develop and we learn how Nvidia will restructure itself after Jensen — he had something like 60 direct reports, after all, and the entire AI financial ecosystem flows through the company. This is pure Decoder bait.
But just two days ago I talked to Kyle Chan, a foreign policy fellow at the Brookings Institution’s John L. Thornton China Center, about the real heart of the scandal: What would China even seek to accomplish by having a spy so close to Jensen? And what does the real state of the US-China AI conflict really looks like, particularly from the Chinese point of view?
Kyle’s answers about chips and how China is perceiving the so-called AI “race” were very illuminating, but maybe even more interesting was his observation on Jensen Huang’s relationship with Trump was the most important thing of all — because maybe what China most stood to gain was someone who could whisper in the right ears.
Let’s just dive right into it. But keep in mind that when we were talking, we still didn’t know what would happen with Jensen.
Okay: Kyle Chan on Nvidia, China, and the future of AI export controls. Here we go.
This interview has been lightly edited for length and clarity.
Kyle Chan, you are a fellow at the John L. Thornton China Center at the Brookings Institution. Welcome to Decoder.
Great to be here.
What on earth could we be talking about as it relates to your expertise about China today? I have a lot to ask you about Nvidia, about our relationship to China as a country, as an economy, and about national security.
Let’s start at the very beginning. From all the reporting we have today, it seems like the United States government has been very worried about a company called Megaspeed and Alice Huang, the alleged Chinese spy at the center of the swirling scandal around Jensen Huang, the CEO of Nvidia. What do we know about Alice Huang and Megaspeed?
We have to be careful about what we know and what we don’t know. So far, there are allegations that the US intelligence community believes that Alice Huang may be an asset of the Chinese intelligence services. Megaspeed itself is a customer of Nvidia’s and they built a data center in Malaysia.
They also seem to potentially have ties with Chinese companies and could — and this is where we’re getting into what we do know and what we don’t know — potentially be a conduit for Chinese AI companies accessing cutting-edge Nvidia chips like Blackwells.
Verge subscribers, don’t forget you get exclusive access to ad-free Decoder wherever you get your podcasts. Head here. Not a subscriber? You can sign up here.
I want to rewind way back. The notion that the Chinese AI industry wants Nvidia chips has been floating around for years and the idea that the Chinese AI industry would somehow develop a parallel set of technology or a superior set of technology because of the constraint of not having Nvidia chips has percolated into the discourse several times.
I’m particularly thinking about DeepSeek. DeepSeek launched R1 and it outperformed our models in some ways. We think they didn’t have access to the chips. There are some accusations that they had access to the chips. It turns out they did a bunch of fine-tuning on the chips they did have.
What is that relationship like? Do they need the highest-end Nvidia chips? Is it true that they’re actually developing parallel technologies that might be superior to the chips they can have? Just set that stage.
A lot of the details are murky, and they’re probably murky for a reason. But up until now, we saw Chinese AI companies that were very heavily dependent on Nvidia chips in order to train and run their models. In fact, they became some of the biggest contributors to the CUDA ecosystem, which is Nvidia’s software ecosystem and platform.
They got very good at that, but at the same time with US export controls and greater technology restrictions on access to cutting-edge GPUs, China increasingly pushed these AI companies to switch away from Nvidia to Huawei and other domestic AI chip makers. The idea there is to develop a parallel domestic AI chip supply chain that is good enough, if not on par with, Nvidia’s latest chips, and — crucially — resilient to policy actions coming out of Washington.
So that is the story up until this point. The question is, now, what is that trajectory for the Chinese GPU or AI chip development?
The reason I wanted to start there is that the motivations to get close to Jensen Huang are very murky to me. As the CEO of Nvidia, I understand that everyone thinks he’s the character and he’s actually in the software designing the next generation of Nvidia GPUs, but he’s just traveling the world giving policy speeches.
If you want to build a parallel GPU ecosystem, you’re trying to get close to the designers and the engineers, right? What would be the goal of trying to get close to Jensen Huang?
If you wanted the design specs for the latest Nvidia GPUs, I don’t know if you would ask Jensen directly. I think you might want to go to his technical staff.
He does have like 60 direct reports. Decoder is often a show about org charts and that one has been on top of my list for a long time, but we’ll set that aside for now. Keep going.
What it’s really about is potentially two things. One is trying to understand, maybe to glean information about, the US policymaking process. Jensen Huang is very close to Trump. They talk a lot. Trump can apparently just call Jensen on his cell phone at any moment.
In the middle of a conference. We’ve seen the video.
On the other side, and maybe this is even the bigger issue, perhaps it’s the biggest concern of all, is the concern that the direction of movement could run the other way, which is that somehow through Jensen, China might be able to influence US policymaking, especially on export controls. That is the big worry from a lot of people in Washington.
One of the other underlying concerns here, that I’m just trying to parse out, is that Alice Huang, the alleged Chinese spy, is an executive at a company called Megaspeed. And through Megaspeed, Nvidia may have already been circumventing export controls.
It seems like if you’re already solving the problem, maybe you should just leave it alone. How does that dynamic play out?
A lot of the story is strange. Even seen in the most cynical terms, it doesn’t quite add up. There could be several different possibilities. One is that this is actually not some concerted strategy from Beijing to try to obtain chips or information or influence, but this is potentially Beijing not quite understanding the full situation and wanting to co-op this Alice Huang person and at least try to understand from her standpoint what is happening.
Because I think you’re right. Already you have a very strong relationship between Nvidia and Megaspeed. You also already have — and this is on the influence side — Jensen Huang advocating for his own commercial interests, for a loosening of export controls on his own chips to China. You don’t really need Beijing to push on that gas pedal. That’s already happening.
So there are some things that don’t quite make sense if you’re thinking about it from those standpoints, but if you’re thinking about it from the viewpoint of “Beijing wants visibility,” then perhaps that is another plausible explanation.
Beijing just wants someone who Trump calls on the phone to advocate for their viewpoints. I see all of that, but what are we getting out of it? If the goal is Nvidia chips, it feels like that is already assured. If the goal is Nvidia engineering plans, that feels like a question mark. If the goal is influence over the Trump administration, I feel like they could have just bought cryptocurrency.
[Laughs] That’s right.
There’s a big swirl of what is actually happening here. Maybe your point that Beijing just needed more visibility because it is a swirl is the motivating factor. The question that leads me to is that the New York Times reports that US intelligence agencies first flagged these issues a year ago to the Trump administration. There was a memo from Marco Rubio. The reporting is that he oversaw the memo, which is as much plausible deniability as anything. He oversaw it. He was aware that there was a memo. A year ago, the United States’ national security apparatus was worried about Alice Huang at Megaspeed, her relationship with Jensen, and nothing occurred. What do you make of that?
That is one of the most worrying aspects of all this. Regardless of what the details are and what is known publicly versus what our intelligence community knows, this is something that should be investigated. This is obviously a major national security issue and this is something where you don’t want someone meddling with this process. You want to do a full investigation, get to the bottom of what’s happening.
I think what we’re seeing from some of the reporting is that this has been known, and in fact, if anything, there may have been efforts to try to stop this investigation or distort it in some way. That is very troubling from that internal, within-the-US-government perspective.
There’s a lot going on with Nvidia in that part of the story, where Nvidia’s own spokespeople are saying, “There’s nothing going on here. This is a normal relationship,” on the record to many publications. A week later, Jensen issues his apology letter saying there was a relationship.
That’s not great for John Rizzo, Nvidia’s comms person. Tim Teter, Nvidia’s general counsel, is out there saying everything is above board. Now we know not everything is above board. What do you make of Nvidia’s corporate governance here? Because there’s a whole bunch of shareholder lawsuits to come. There’s one filed as of today. I can only imagine there are more to come.
Nvidia has to tread very carefully here because what you don’t want is exactly what’s already happening, which is to start to deny or dismiss some of these issues and then turn around and have to say, “Actually, there might be something there.”
This is especially important now as there are growing calls for a congressional investigation. So this is going to be separate, perhaps, from within the executive branch where, again, I think we have reasons to worry about conflicts of interest and people not wanting to do this all the way. But if there’s a full-blown congressional investigation, that might reveal some things that, if you are Nvidia, you want to get out in front of rather than have to backpedal later on and explain yourself.
Part of the back and forth here with the government’s concern about Alice Huang and Megaspeed is that the Commerce Department spent months looking at the company and then Teter, the general counsel of Nvidia, emailed them and invoked a direct relationship with the Commerce undersecretary, Jeff Kessler. That got the case kicked to the DOJ where everything stopped.
Trump yesterday said the FBI is investigating — that’s obviously connected to the DOJ. There’s a universe where the Trump administration is trying to make this go away. Nvidia can pressure the Trump administration to make this go away. That reeks of corruption, obviously.
And then you have a very empowered class of Democrats coming into Congress with polling that suggested corruption was one of the top issues in this last election. I don’t see any outcome except those Democrats and an empowered bunch of investigators and an empowered bunch of committee chairs coming after Nvidia as hard as they can.
How should the company think about that? Should they just fire Jensen now to get away from it? Should they put them in front of Congress every day for the next year? How do you think that’s going to play out?
I think, at the most fundamental level, they need to cooperate and comply with calls from Congress to carry out this full investigation. Because up until now, this has all been so murky, and part of the reason why this has been a stop-and-go, even though those of us in the China AI space have been hearing about this for a while.
Companies like Megaspeed, their role in all this, even people like Alice Huang, have been flagged much earlier than this. The fact that this is coming out now, but also that the investigation was stopped and started within the Trump administration, is a big red flag. Ultimately, from Nvidia’s standpoint, they’re going to have to come clean with whatever Congress now requests. There are going to be a lot of hard questions from Congress, as there should be, that have been building up for some time now.
If you ever looked at Jensen’s approach to chip export controls, he’s been very fiery that chip export controls are stupid. You mentioned earlier that’s in his business interest. It is better for the Chinese AI industry to be dependent on CUDA and dependent on Nvidia chips, because then if we do get into some conflict, maybe you can restrict that access and you’ll set them back. There’s some swirl of argument that he makes that says it’s in the United States’ national security interest to ship as many Nvidia GPUs to China as you can.
Then there are a lot of China hawks who are saying, “Actually, you need to restrain them and keep them years behind. They will not catch up to our bleeding-edge technology. Jensen, you just keep us ahead.” It feels like, out of all of this, the China hawks are going to start winning more fights than they’re losing — arguing that the Chinese government is actively trying to impede our efforts or actively trying to influence our government.
How should people think about that? What framework should we use to evaluate that mass of differing opinions, especially now that there’s a catalyst for some kind of change?
I think that’s the right way to frame up this whole debate. You have, on the one hand, folks who see the US-China AI competition as a critical strategic competition and access to compute is one of the key levers there, and thus US export controls, not only as they stand now, but tightening them further will be critical to maintaining US edge in the most powerful technology of our lives.
Then there are other people who are in the opposite camp who advocate for greater exports of the US AI tech stack writ large, including Nvidia chips and including to China. Up until now, that camp has been led by people like [Commerce Secretary] Howard Lutnick, Jensen Huang, and Trump himself. What’s interesting is the balance of power, in terms of whose arguing gets more weight, has been shifting and will shift in light of this recent controversy.
So here now those who have been advocating for more exports and fewer export controls, some of their arguments have been tainted by this process potentially, regardless of where the investigation goes.
Then there’s a final variable in all this, which is China itself. That’s something that matters a lot in this discussion because in the background, China is doing two things. One, they’re trying to build their own chips and they’re sort of all-in on building a resilient semiconductor supply chain with their own, say, Huawei AI chips that their own AI companies can use. They will never really catch up. It looks highly unlikely, but they could develop good enough chips to replace the Nvidia ones that they have been dependent on for so long.
The second big factor from China is retaliation. And this is one thing that I’m watching very closely. If these greater export controls are placed by the US on not just chips to China, but also on chip-making equipment — the tools required to make the semiconductors themselves — will China use its own export controls, such as restricting rare earth metals, to hit back?
Right. This is in the context of an ongoing trade and tariff war that has rocked the United States’ economy. In many ways, the first thing that Democrats should do when they enter the next Congress is pass a law getting rid of all the Trump tariffs, right? They’re brought into Congress to fix affordability and the tariffs are an affordability problem, and now we’re going to rush into a trade war with China because we might ask the Dutch government to restrict ASML chip making machines.
That seems very fraught to say, “We need to enter into an even more heated AI war with China that might result in the Chinese government placing export controls on the goods that you already find unaffordable.” Do you think that there’s the political will to go through this whole exercise, or is it just to punish Nvidia and Jensen Huang?
In some ways, this is happening against the backdrop of other issues that are probably more top of mind for both voters and for incoming legislators in the Capitol. Things like cost of living, as you point out, tariffs, and trying to investigate the Trump administration more broadly for corruption and other issues. Somewhere in there is also the debate around data centers and AI itself.
So in some ways, I’ll be curious to see how far this goes, the particular Nvidia issue. There is a scenario where a congressional investigation comes and goes, potentially finds no wrongdoing on Jensen Huang’s part, and then this is just folded into the longer debate around export controls. But there’s another world where if there are issues that come up, that could be potentially disruptive, not just for Nvidia and not just for the US-China relationship, but also for the AI boom in the US overall.
Tell me more about China itself. You mentioned that their chips will maybe get good enough. There’s an argument that the models right now, the frontier models we have today, are actually good enough to disrupt the economy. They’re certainly good enough to disrupt the field of mathematics.
You could stop training new models and just let the technology diffuse and you would still disrupt huge swaths of the economy today. We’re nowhere close to that actually happening. I think the AI labs should prove that they can do it with the technology they have today. That’s for another time.
But China doesn’t need to compete at the frontier to gain the productivity advantages and to gain the industrial advantages. The example I keep using is 5G. I was told for a decade that we had to win the race to 5G, otherwise China would do something and no one would ever tell me what the “something” was.
What is the “something” with AI that China will defeat us at that has made this such a critical geopolitical chess match? And is there a point where it will just be good enough for them to achieve that “something”?
The funny thing is that China doesn’t see this AI race as being so much about the frontier per se. They’re really much more focused on diffusing the technology. And they think they can “win” the AI race by just having AI used much more broadly and integrated throughout the economy, especially in areas like manufacturing where they are already the behemoth.
I think when they see the discussion around getting to AGI, certainly from the government standpoint in Beijing, that to them looks like a bubble and a waste of money. Why are you building all that compute for that? What’s interesting to see is the strategy as they’ve been playing it out is one where they’re trying to not fall too far behind the US, but really focus a lot more on trying to get — especially these open source models — out there across the Chinese economy and globally as well. To that extent, on that metric, they seem to be doing well.
That, to me, always has seemed like the real fight. Anthropic and OpenAI are furious that the Chinese companies are constantly distilling their models. They’re trying to build some technology to keep them from distilling their models.
But if you believe in the promise of AI and what it can do to the economy, your emphasis should be on diffusing good enough models, not AGI. Whereas our emphasis due to the way our market works is a bunch of guys who think they’re going to build digital Jesus. Whatever is going to happen, that’s the thing they want. [Anthropic CEO] Dario Amodei has to pretend that he cares about Salesforce, but we know what his real priority is.
How do the United States and China compete in that dynamic, where you have one very managed economy that has set a much more near-term and potentially achievable goal, and then our market system where the incentives of the leading market players have nothing to do with the economic incentives of the rest of us?
There’s even an ideological foundation to all of this because the vision of AGI is one that is so deeply embedded right now in the American AI community and Silicon Valley culture that I think there’s a risk of potential disconnect, not just between the US and China on this issue, but also within the US, between those who are in the labs like Anthropic and those who are outside who are saying, “Why are we building these giant metal boxes in our backyards? We don’t really see the point of this.”
So there’s already that divergence there. Part of it is this longstanding obsession in the American AI imagination with getting to the machine god essentially.
Bringing that all back to the context of Nvidia, Jensen Huang, Alice Huang, and Megaspeed. Megaspeed is a data center provider in Southeast Asia. It’s theoretically their business. They’re building data centers, they’re buying these chips. Shouldn’t market forces just end this?
The United States AI companies want to build data centers. They will pay whatever rates because they have an ideological purpose that is disconnected from capitalism potentially. The Chinese government is just trying to diffuse AI into manufacturing and the United States government would like to hold them back.
Shouldn’t market forces just mean that you sell the chips to the highest bidder and the highest bidder will almost always be an American AI company?
That’s an interesting question, because there is ultimately market demand in China still for these Nvidia chips. And there’s a divergence here between the companies in China who still want access to Nvidia chips and the government who is saying, “No, no, no. Why don’t you try Chinese chips? Why don’t you help us with our Chinese domestic chip development?”
There’s that divergence there. What’s interesting is players like Megaspeed in between could potentially do things like not just allow Chinese AI companies, again, to potentially get access to Nvidia chips, but then the argument some people are making is that those are chips that could have gone to the US AI companies and hyperscalers who are desperate.
That is an argument that’s out there, but fundamentally, I don’t know how much of this is driven by those sheer commercial interests alone or, again, by some of these policy or ideological divides.
Or by the influence of an alleged spy.
Right, exactly.
Because in a pure market environment, I think I know how this goes, but then you obviously have the complicated geopolitical dynamics and then you have this corruption element where both governments are attempting to influence one company at the center of the storm.
What do you think happens if the Nvidia board investigation determines that Jensen acted inappropriately or a shareholder lawsuit imposes massive liability on Nvidia or a congressional investigation?
There’s no way this company comes out of this unscathed, and the options are Jensen steps down or Nvidia’s restrained in some way or export controls go up even higher. Walk us through the probabilities and what you think happens next.
There is a scenario actually where this ends up with not that much happening.
Interesting.
There is a pathway where there is a congressional investigation and they possibly find no wrongdoing or maybe Jensen really didn’t know, as he claims, that this Alice Huang person was a Chinese spy, and that this ends up feeding into the longer standing trajectory of all the export control debates and everything like that. That’s one possibility.
Another possibility, though, is if there is some kind of wrongdoing that’s found, Jensen Huang might have to step down as CEO. He’s not just like a central node, he is like American AI, like the pillar that is holy.
He’s the only one making the dollar. I mean, this is very important.
Yeah, it’s like if you could draw the network diagram of the entire US AI ecosystem, Nvidia would be at the center of that. If you could draw the diagram of the entire Nvidia ecosystem, a single man is standing at the heart of all of that.
The question becomes, if he were no longer the CEO of Nvidia, how much impact would that have on Nvidia’s ability to make progress on its own AI chips and what impact would that have on the rest of the US AI industry? One possibility, for example, is that there is a slowdown in Nvidia’s ability to crank out next generation after next generation of better and better chips. And that could impact US overall compute build out. That is one possibility.
Another possibility is that there’s just so much demand and that the knowledge is diffused enough throughout Nvidia that the company can still continue to make progress at a breakneck pace even without their fearless leader at the top. A lot is unknown.
“Fearless” is the right word, because when I look at Nvidia, again, I don’t think Jensen is designing the chips. I know that there’s a character he wants to play, where he’s in all the meetings and he’s probably taking some risk in chip design that is hard for us to see from the outside.
But what we can see is Jensen’s enormous appetite for risk when it comes to financing neoclouds and investing in frontier AI companies. All of the dollars Nvidia is making flow back out in a circle to the AI ecosystem. It’s unclear to me whether that continues to happen without Jensen specifically being willing to front the risk and show up on CNBC as the face of the risk and say, “Everything’s okay.” What do you think happens then?
His role as not just a technology leader, but as a market maker is really crucial. When he calls out certain companies like certain startups or neoclouds, you can see their stock prices go up. When he says something like, “I don’t know if this quantum thing is going to work out,” you saw the entire industry takes a hit. So that is the weight of his role in the broader ecosystem.
Going forward, without that kind of advocate and this market maker playing this active role, that’s another factor. Beyond the Nvidia chips themselves and the technological role of the company, there is this deeper industry or even sociological function that Jensen plays and that Nvidia plays in this broader AI buildout.
One of the interesting dynamics in all of that is Jensen also doesn’t seem to care very much about AGI. He thinks it’s just software. I’ve heard him say, “This is just software. Everybody calm down. These risks are fake. We don’t need a safety regime, all this stuff.” He’s more aligned with what you’re describing as the Chinese viewpoint, where this is just a tool to accelerate our economy. If he goes, maybe that changes too.
It’s possible. He has been one of the most powerful counterweights to the “let’s go all in on AGI” perspective that you hear from Dario Amodei and Sam Altman, for example. Without his voice in that process, he’s also been a very strong advocate for open source. Again, emphasizing the diffusion rather than the, say, control and restrictions on cutting-edge AI.
I do think if his voice is not there, there would be a pretty big vacuum for folks who would advocate that position or that view. At the same time though, again, beyond the immediate AI industry in the US, I don’t know how much that “race to AGI” argument resonates with the broader US public.
Right now a lot of people are just more wondering about the nuts and bolts of it. Is this technology going to cost me my job tomorrow? Is it actually useful? Is it impacting my university studies? Those are things that maybe are more impactful day-to-day for most ordinary Americans.
It is abundantly clear that Jensen is not a great spokesperson to the average consumer in the United States. He has not made the sale to them. He’s made the sale to a bunch of financiers. He’s made the sale to Wall Street. He’s made the sale to the tech industry.
But the average American, as we can just see from the polling in the election, is very, very skeptical of all of this and very, very skeptical of the costs versus the benefits. Again, I’m just putting that in the context of there’s an alleged Chinese spy whispering in the ear of this man.
What truly would the Chinese government hope to gain from that? Because in one way, they’ve actually achieved a victory here. They’ve destabilized the American AI economy. But it doesn’t seem like that was the goal. They’re furiously denying that any of this even happened.
There is another very counterintuitive take, which is they kind of shot themselves in the foot on this one. From Beijing’s perspective, they don’t want to see more export controls, they might want to see a loosening. But now because of this potential Chinese spy, they’ve tainted the one man who has been advocating the most for loosening export controls. In some ways now, they’ve taken out someone who had otherwise for sheer commercial reasons been more aligned, and who’s going to take that place?
If anything now, it has really charged the Democrats especially, who have been criticizing the Trump administration about not being tougher on these export controls. It has really galvanized the Democrats on this front and made it more of an issue perhaps than it was before.
Yeah, especially when you connect it to corruption and the AI industry wasting money, all this stuff, it’s all there for them. Tell me more broadly about Chinese spying in the AI industry.
There was a report, I don’t know, a few months ago that OpenAI was warning its employees that spies abound, that if suddenly attractive people appeared, be wary because they were probably spies. I love that. There’s just something about that that’s great.
Obviously when it hits Jensen, it’s a different kind of story, especially with the national security implications and corruption implications. Give us a sense of what the spy trade is like on the ground for the average AI researcher.
All I can say here is that they are juicy targets. For the US AI labs, anyone who’s involved with cutting-edge frontier AI, chip production, and chip design would be juicy targets for Chinese intelligence gathering because this is something where China’s trying to catch up. They’re trying to figure out how to keep pace with the US on AI and try to build their own alternatives to Nvidia GPUs.
You can see there have been other examples of cases where they’ve been poaching engineers from TSMC in Taiwan or from South Korea, and have struggled to deal with efforts to hold back this strategic IP basically. There’s probably a lot more happening under the surface in terms of this cat-and-mouse game, but I do think that they are prime targets.
Correct me if I’m wrong, but I think that there have been warnings from the US government to be careful and to step up your game on the cybersecurity front and the intelligence front to protect against these threats.
Meanwhile, they’re putting all their information into Slack and getting breached on the daily. Who knows if these warnings are taking off.
Onto the last set of questions here — I’m thinking about Apple and Tesla, where we moved our manufacturing to China and we moved our supply chains to China, the Chinese industries.
Whether it was above board or not — and there’s a rich debate you could have on that, we’ve had it on the show — they learned a lot from our companies. There was technology transfer. That’s happening in AI too.
There are a lot of Chinese AI researchers working in and around these labs. There is technology transfer happening, again, above board or not. Isn’t that just the normal course of business or is there something more nefarious happening here?
To some extent there are people flows and knowledge flows. You might not even think of it in terms of national-level AI communities. You might think about this global diffusion of know-how tips and tricks for squeezing a bit more performance out of the same amount of compute. Especially up until recently, you saw a lot of sharing of, say, papers like the transformer paper, that disseminated new architectural breakthroughs throughout the global AI community. And now that’s changing, obviously, as a lot of companies, especially the US companies, are holding this much closer to their chest.
But there is this broader question about who gets access to this. Who gains and loses? I guess it’s also not just a one-way flow. Some of these American AI companies benefit a lot from having some of these Chinese developers work with them. In the same way Apple and Tesla, it wasn’t just like they gave away the crown jewels to China, but that China in turn saved these companies, especially at times when they were facing a perilous moment in their histories.
There’s this bigger question about what the line is between normal commercial and technology flows versus a concerted state-backed effort to obtain sensitive strategic secrets and IP technology that would have broader geopolitical implications.
Does that strategy for China look like a bunch of companies like Megaspeed with allegedly compromised executives, or does it play out more directly?
Actually if anything, it would be less about the companies like Megaspeed, who seem to be middlemen for dealing with compute, and perhaps different companies, different agencies perhaps in the Chinese system, that might be more active.
At one extreme you have the outright concerted efforts at industrial espionage. They’re state-backed actors who are very systematic at trying to obtain some of the best commercial secrets from around the world, including from American companies.
But on the other hand, you do also have this gray zone of, “If a Chinese company acquires a stake in a US or European company, what does that mean? Does that mean they’re trying to obtain the technology or is that a regular commercial deal?” That’s where it starts to get more difficult to parse and you’d have to look at it on a case-by-case basis.
One of the dynamics in talking about the Chinese government and the United States, especially when you talk to American industrialists, is that the American government is chaos and democracy is messy and everyone’s running in 50 different directions, and the Chinese system is directed. They make 100-year plans, not one-quarter plans. We’ve heard it. Everyone knows these cliches.
How true is that? In this case alone, it doesn’t seem like they achieved the goal of their plan. In many cases, they might have actually set themselves back.
Oh, yeah. For those who actually study China, who have done long-term research on this country and who understand it more closely, it looks like a much messier picture than any of these portrayals, especially from those in Silicon Valley who try to juxtapose China against what they find to be this annoyingly messy and unruly democracy.
The Chinese system, both within the government and then across industries and government, is full of conflicting interests, rife with corruption. There are lots of times where you have bureaucratic turf wars that spill out into the open. You have missteps, ones that can backfire profoundly.
You see that all the time. You see the local governments not following what Beijing wants, or following what Beijing wants but in the wrong direction. Sometimes they’re given a signal and they say, “Oh, okay, we got to run that way.” And then Beijing says, “No, no, no, no, no, other way, other way.”
This happens all the time, and to anyone in Silicon Valley who’s trying to make this “neat” argument, all I can say is, go talk to people in China. They can tell you how messy it actually is on the ground.
Kyle, this has been great. What do you think will happen next year? What should people be looking out for?
The biggest thing is a congressional investigation and who’s behind that, what that surface is. And then potentially along with that, these pending bills around export controls and whether those will now get more momentum to pass in Congress, and whether Trump himself, and the White House in general, will try to play a role in either stopping that or giving that a quiet green light.
I suspect we’re going to want to talk to you again as this process plays out. Thank you so much, Kyle, for being on Decoder.
Thank you.

Five ways Jensen Huang’s ouster from Nvidia could damage the AI industry
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
16:32:33 UTC
01:32:33 KST

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s confession that he had an affair (and reports of another one with a direct report) that led the board to oust him on Thursday isn’t just your run-of-the-mill salacious billionaire CEO sex scandal.
In this case, one of his lovers is suspected by the U.S. government of being a spy for China. She’s also an executive of a Singapore-based neocloud that is suspected of smuggling Nvidia chips to China.
These affairs show undeniably poor judgment by Huang and, I’d argue, unchecked hubris on many levels. Such choices are not only a betrayal of his wife of 40 years, they’re also a liability for his company. Sleeping with any Nvidia partner is bad enough, but he also chose one in a tinderbox of a geopolitical situation. Even if Alice Huang’s company wasn’t suspected of helping Chinese firms evade chip restrictions (and he was somehow unaware of this), it was still exploiting a legal loophole by renting out powerful AI infrastructure to Chinese companies.
It’s those potential national consequences that may land him in a hellscape of ongoing public humiliation. Republican committee chairs are already summoning him to testify in closed-door sessions about the security ramifications.
Will Democrats, who are set to take over key committees when the new Congress convenes in January, also haul him in to testify on the Hill? No one knows yet, but Huang has flaunted his close relationship with President Trump. Remember when Trump called him while he was on stage at the All-In Summit in September? Nvidia also donated $25 million to Trump’s ballroom, a deeply unpopular project with American voters. Several Democratic lawmakers have already vowed to investigate the money behind it.
In other words, he is a billionaire close to Trump. He was — until Thursday — the face of AI and data centers (also a deeply unpopular political topic with voters), and he has publicly admitted to having an affair with an alleged Chinese spy. Even though he’s no longer CEO, that seems too juicy a target for Democrats to ignore.
Beyond whatever may be coming, Huang’s influence has been lost right when he was using it to push the tech industry in a number of directions. These areas have now lost their biggest champion.
Open-weight AI models as competitors to closed-source labs
Huang has been the biggest, loudest advocate for open weight AI models in the tech industry and among government policymakers. Nvidia not only offers its own open-weight models, it also offers a lot of other open source tech for agents, AI security and more. It has even agreed to acquire the biggest site that hosts them, Hugging Face, for $12.9 billion. (That deal is supposed to close in the first half of next year, pending regulatory approval.)
In July, when Anthropic and OpenAI were reportedly urging the Trump Administration to ban Chinese open-weight models, Huang organized a letter of support for open-weight models generally (if not Chinese models specifically), signed by hundreds of tech companies.
Open-weight models give enterprises choice in the market and reduce costs. Without Huang, the next biggest advocate for them is perhaps Microsoft’s Satya Nadella, but he’s not out there organizing the industry to speak up.
Pushing the AI industry to clean up its security
Both OpenAI and Anthropic revealed incident after incident this year as their AI agents hacked into others’ networks or performed other misdeeds. Huang loudly proclaimed the AI industry still didn’t need regulation and would deal with safety itself.
As the industry mulled over what they should do (auditors? a FINRA-like board?), Huang rallied the tech industry again. Nvidia got over 100 companies to sign on to his Open Agent Safety Platform, a largely open-source effort to solve the epidemic of misbehaving AI agents.
Naturally, this effort hasn’t instantly solved the problem, and now the top spokesperson for self-regulation via technology solutions is out of his position of influence.
Arguing AI is a tool, not a life form
Huang has also been among the loudest voices insisting that AI is an engineered system that creates products, and so can be controlled like any other engineered system. This viewpoint counters the AI doomers and even the big AI labs themselves, which sometimes position their models as on the verge of consciousness (Anthropic) or as alien minds (OpenAI).
While Huang deals with the fallout of his actions, the loudest voice of this counter-narrative is, for the foreseeable future, silenced.
A new Nvidia-powered AI PC industry
Huang also just convinced a long list of PC makers to buy into his long-time dream of providing CPUs (the main processor chips) for them. In this age, when most Americans have not experienced AI beyond a chatbot, he orchestrated a new line of high-end AI PCs geared for running agents locally.
These new PCs are due out this holiday season, and they may sell well for their intended targets: creators, developers, and others already savvy with agents. But now that Huang has become a meme for cheating on his wife with an alleged spy, he’s perhaps not the best choice to help market these devices as a cool alternative to the Mac mini.
Financial engineering to fund data centers
Huang demolished his reputation right as bond underwriters (lenders) and bond buyers are pretty much over AI build-out related debt. Of roughly $80 billion in data center bonds issued this year, nearly half were rated high-risk “junk,” Forbes reports.
Undeterred, Huang has been coming up with ways to continue financing the boom and keep his company’s revenues growing. Certainly, he’s authorized a mind-boggling number of so-called circular deals, where Nvidia buys stakes in companies to fund their expansion so they can buy chips. By some accounts, the company had committed to $750 billion in such deals as of July, Bloomberg reported. In August, Nvidia got even bolder, agreeing to backstop $105 billion in financing for an OpenAI data center in Ohio, CNBC reported.
Huang also convinced a whole new set of financiers to pony up. In August, he announced that deep-pocketed PE firms Apollo, BlackRock, Blackstone, Brookfield, Goldman Sachs and KKR had committed up to $500 billion for data center build outs. He promised them that the data centers’ Nvidia GPUs could be used as collateral because Nvidia was guaranteeing the chips would retain value.
Huang sold a vision of Nvidia as “investable infrastructure” with a long resale cycle, like airplanes. Then he made the rounds with the financial press, as he often did, to pitch his vision. Now, however, Wall Street is concerned that Nvidia’s stock will crash and take their portfolios, and possibly the economy, with it.
Nvidia has lost its top salesperson and the AI industry has lost its top hype man. Even if Huang somehow comes back into the public eye to champion AI again, every interview and meeting will be about his sex life, choices, and national security — not his vision for the industry.
We’ll have to wait and see how Nvidia navigates the scandal Huang heaped onto it, but now that the company is finally in its post “founder mode” era, and hunting for a new CEO, it’s doubtful it has the cache to push the industry the way Huang was.

Nintendo Takes Down Emulators and Firmware in Massive GitHub DMCA Sweep
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
16:29:13 UTC
01:29:13 KST

Nintendo has sent four DMCA takedown notices to GitHub targeting two Nintendo Switch emulators, Yuzu and Citron Neo, along with mod chip firmware.
This isn't the first time Nintendo has sent takedown notices to GitHub, and it's not even the first time it's tried to take down Yuzu, but the focus this time around seems to be on firmware for mod chips. This particular firmware had 144 repositories, all of which have been removed by GitHub and replaced by a DMCA takedown notice. Mod chips like Picofly can be soldered onto the Nintendo Switch's mainboard, and can be flashed with this firmware to circumvent Nintendo's copy protection.
GitHub has posted Nintendo's November 6 DMCA notice, which says, "The Nintendo Switch console and video games contain technological protection measures ('TPMs') that permit the Nintendo Switch console to interact only with legitimate Nintendo video game files. This process protects Nintendo’s copyright-protected video games, (...) by preventing users from playing pirated copies of Nintendo’s video games on the Nintendo Switch console and by preventing users from unlawfully copying and playing Nintendo’s video games on unauthorized devices."
However, while this takedown has effectively wiped out a ton of repositories with mod chip firmware, it seems like it's still being hosted elsewhere on the site, according to TorrentFreak. It's possible, though, that Nintendo isn't finished yet; back in 2024, Nintendo effectively took down 8,500 copies of Yuzu by issuing a takedown that targeted 12 different mirrors of the emulator.
This particular takedown also comes a few days after emulators were able to run the remake of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time on PC, so this could be a sign that Nintendo is winding up its lawyers to crack down on emulation once again.
If anything, emulation is a completely different beast in 2026 than it was even in 2024, thanks to the rise of AI. There's already been a wave of AI-made mashup mods that took over Twitter for a couple of weeks, and led Anthropic to coding in a lock to prevent Claude from outright copying games. Game developers have started cracking down on AI ports of games, too. Just last month Rockstar had to take down a browser version of GTA 5, and Bethesda took down an AI-generated Fallout New York game.
