25 years later, Xbox is trying to reinvent itself again
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
16:19:02 UTC
01:19:02 KST

Microsoft has spent the past 25 years trying to shake up the game console market. The original Xbox, which launched on November 15th in 2001, took on an industry dominated by Sony and Nintendo. Twenty-five years later, Xbox finds itself in a similar position. It’s not starting from scratch this time around, but to reach another 25-year milestone, it needs to do something radically different.
25 years later, Xbox is trying to reinvent itself again
Xbox has always been about trying something new, so what does Microsoft do next?
The original Xbox was radically different. Born out of a fear of Sony putting a PC in the living room, Microsoft built its first game console around familiar PC technology. A custom Intel Pentium III processor powered the original Xbox, alongside an Nvidia GPU and 64MB of unified RAM. It was different from the fully custom architectures that Sony and Nintendo used at the time, allowing Microsoft to entice developers to its platform with tools that were more familiar to PC game makers.
Microsoft also included two hardware features that were unusual for a console at the time: a built-in hard drive and Ethernet port. The hard drive let game developers cache content without having to rely fully on optical discs, and Xbox players could also use it for game saves without having to purchase a separate memory card. You could even rip CDs to an Xbox and listen to custom soundtracks while playing games.
Xbox wasn’t the first to bring online gaming to consoles, but Microsoft took a different approach. Instead of relying on a built-in modem like Sega’s Dreamcast, Microsoft included an Ethernet port, anticipating a shift toward broadband internet connections. Xbox Live, which launched in November 2002, really nailed the entire online experience. There were friends lists, unique gamertags, voice chat through a bundled headset, and integrated multiplayer services. Microsoft created an entire platform for online gaming, making it easier for developers to support and fundamentally changing how people played games together.
The Xbox 360 took these ideas a step further, with swappable hard drives, digital game downloads, wireless controllers, and backward compatibility. It also turned Microsoft’s game console into an entertainment hub for the living room, complete with streaming video apps. Kinect was perhaps its most radical addition. Launched in 2010, the motion-sensing device used cameras and microphones to let players control games with their bodies and voices. It quickly became the fastest-selling consumer electronics device in early 2011.
But the success of Kinect and Microsoft’s ambition to turn Xbox into a home entertainment device helped lead to its first major strategic misstep with the Xbox One. Microsoft positioned the successor to the Xbox 360 around TV, entertainment, and an always-online future, instead of focusing on the games that had made Xbox successful. Its plans for online DRM and restrictions on used games sparked a huge backlash, while bundling Kinect pushed the console’s price $100 above Sony’s PlayStation 4. Six months after launch, Microsoft was forced to drop Kinect from the Xbox One console bundle to close that price gap.
The Xbox One showed what happens when Microsoft loses sight of what gamers want. The company spent most of that generation playing catch-up to Sony’s impressive PlayStation 4 sales. But its weakened position also forced Microsoft to once again try something radically different with Xbox Game Pass.
Introduced in 2017, Game Pass shook up the very economics of how people play games. The subscription service lets you access hundreds of games instead of buying them individually. Microsoft went on an acquisition spree to power its Game Pass ambitions, buying up smaller studios, Bethesda, and eventually Activision Blizzard.
Game Pass has become one of Xbox’s most compelling features, but it has also raised difficult questions about the economics of putting all of Microsoft’s first-party games into a subscription service on day one. It has pleased Xbox fans, but Microsoft hasn’t demonstrated that it can turn the service into the mass-market success it once envisioned. Microsoft has also stopped regularly disclosing subscriber numbers, making it harder to judge if the service is growing or not.
These days, Microsoft is spinning many Xbox plates. The Xbox Series X and S consoles that launched in 2020 are way behind Sony’s PS5 sales numbers, and Xbox Cloud Gaming hasn’t yet been able to reach the millions more players that Microsoft has always wanted for its ecosystem.
Xbox Cloud Gaming promised to make games accessible on phones and tablets without needing a dedicated console, and combined with Microsoft’s messy project to bring Xbox games to PS5 and Nintendo consoles, the efforts have raised questions about what the Xbox brand actually represents these days. Microsoft hasn’t quite decided whether it wants to be a full third-party publisher, an ecosystem owner, or an unusual combination of both.
Some of Microsoft’s recent radically different Xbox bets haven’t paid off, so it’s now turning its attention to a much smaller Xbox division, with a restructuring that will see overall Xbox headcount reduced by 20 percent by the end of Microsoft’s fiscal year in June 2027. “I believe the future of gaming will look more like its beginning, when a small team with a big idea could change how the world plays,” Xbox CEO Asha Sharma wrote in a message to Xbox employees today.
Microsoft is celebrating 25 years of Xbox today with an event in Seattle for fans, where it’s launching Xbox Playback, a personalized look back at players’ 25-year Xbox history. There will be more fan-focused announcements throughout the day, but I’m not expecting any news on the next Xbox console. The celebration is awkwardly timed, because rumors have suggested that Microsoft is also preparing for more Xbox layoffs in November, during the same month it’s celebrating a milestone.
The reset also comes as Microsoft is preparing for a next-generation Xbox console, codenamed Project Helix, which will play both Xbox games and PC titles. It sounds like an effort to combine Windows and Xbox like never before. The next Xbox could also play every Xbox game ever made, thanks to Microsoft’s impressive bets on backward compatibility over the years.
It’s an interesting full-circle moment. The original Xbox was essentially a PC built for the living room. Project Helix appears to be taking a similar approach, but this time around bringing the flexibility of PC gaming into a console experience. If Microsoft gets it right, it could open Xbox up to a much bigger library of games, and entice people trying to decide between a console or a gaming PC.
The problem for Xbox is getting consumers to care about this next console, especially when its games are available on PC and sometimes on Sony’s and Nintendo’s platforms. “Xbox has the best console ecosystem around, packed with consumer-friendly features like free cloud saves, Game Pass, and my preferred controller,” says Jon Clarke, cofounder and editor-in-chief of DayOne. “None of these things are particularly sexy or flashy. They’re simply features that work, and that can make them difficult to get customers excited about.”
Rebuilding the Xbox brand and launching a new console gives Microsoft an unusual opportunity to make another bold bet on the future, just like it did 25 years ago. But the risks this time around are greater, after more than a decade of trying and failing to catch up to the competition.
“Up against competitors that have firmly established their place in the market, Xbox finds itself in a difficult position,” says Clarke. “Rebuilding the brand will be an expensive endeavor, and the bold ambition of combining the best of console gaming with the openness and flexibility of PC is easier said than done.”
Xbox also needs good games, and after years of studio acquisitions it now has plenty of IP to play with. “Many of those franchises have lost their luster owing to years of mismanagement and potentially a generalized lack of vision,” says Jez Corden, executive editor at Windows Central. “Microsoft from the top will need to be willing to invest here to ensure Xbox’s franchises can not only find new audiences, but simply maintain what they already have.”
Xbox also faces the challenge of RAMageddon’s impact on the hardware industry right now, pushing the prices of components beyond levels that consumers can comfortably afford. “If Xbox is to reclaim any of its former glory, innovating on the costs of the traditional video game ‘console’ will be absolutely paramount, otherwise it’s hard to imagine that it will continue to exist in its current form,” says Corden.
A console projected to cost between $1,200 and $1,800 won’t grow the Xbox ecosystem. It could make more sense to let PC makers build their own Xbox hardware, opening up the platform to a wider range of prices and devices rather than relying on expensive flagship consoles.
Twenty-five years ago, Microsoft built a PC and called it an Xbox. Now it’s preparing to build something that looks increasingly like a PC again, only this time it needs to convince people that it should still be called an Xbox. Microsoft has another opportunity to shake up the console industry, but its biggest challenge might be convincing players that Xbox needs to exist as a console at all.
The pad
- Protesters break into Microsoft data center site in Amsterdam. Protesters broke into the construction site of a new Microsoft data center in Amsterdam earlier this week. Around 100 people made it into the site, with some scaling a crane to place a “fuck AI” banner. Protesters also unfurled a “Microsoft powers genocide” banner on the construction site, and chanted “Microsoft has got to go” and “free Palestine.” There has been intense opposition to new data centers in Amsterdam, after the city banned new projects until 2030. Microsoft will be the sole tenant of this new data center, and the project is proceeding as it was planned as far back as 2016, before a ban was put in place.
- Microsoft is reportedly “winding down” a cloud AI deal with Oracle. Bloomberg reports that Microsoft has been turning off rented capacity from Oracle, after declining to renew a 2023 deal. Oracle has been using older-generation Nvidia chips, reportedly making the renting deal less useful to the software maker. Microsoft is also reportedly reconsidering deals to rent server capacity from so-called “neoclouds” to power its AI efforts.
- GTA VI will be available on Xbox Cloud Gaming for Xbox One consoles. Microsoft and Rockstar Games have officially confirmed that Grand Theft Auto VI will be available on Xbox Cloud Gaming next week, but there’s a pretty big catch. You’ll only be able to access the game using an Xbox Series S / X or Xbox One console. That’s unusual as you can normally stream Xbox Cloud Gaming titles to phones, PCs, TVs, tablets, and even web browsers. Microsoft also effectively has an exclusive on streaming Grand Theft Auto VI from the cloud for launch, albeit only to Xbox consoles.
- Microsoft’s DLSS competitor is out for the Xbox Ally X and works in handheld mode. Microsoft is now rolling out Automatic Super Resolution (Auto SR), an upscaling technology that competes with Nvidia’s DLSS, for the Xbox Ally X, Xbox Ally X20, and select Windows Copilot Plus PCs. The full release of Auto SR now works while you’re playing handheld and running on battery, and it also supports games running at 1080p. Previously, Auto SR was also limited to frame rates of “around” 60 to 75fps, but support for a new Auto SR Performance mode will allow you to hit frame rates of up to 120fps. The new performance mode and compatibility with Auto HDR is rolling out through Windows updates.
- Hackers are already exploiting an Outlook spoofing vulnerability. Microsoft is urging IT admins to install its latest Outlook patch this week, after it discovered a flaw that is being actively exploited. Hackers can send a specially crafted message to Outlook users that would force them to connect to an attacker-controlled network where authentication hashes could be stolen to impersonate users. Microsoft has marked this as moderate, but security researchers say a stolen hash can open the door to many other parts of a network, beyond just a mailbox.
- Anthropic has its own AI-powered office suite now. Microsoft has ruled the productivity software world for decades with its own Office suite, but Anthropic unveiled new tools this week that are targeted at knowledge workers. Claude Meetings, Claude Sheets, and Claude Whiteboards are all what they sound like, equivalents to Microsoft’s Office apps that join Claude Docs, Slides, and others. The tools are some of the company’s first native products for non-technical workers and are the latest AI-shaped warning shot against Microsoft’s Office empire.
- Microsoft is bringing two popular Sysinternals tools to Linux and macOS. Linux users are getting a native version of Autoruns, the popular utility for managing what apps run at startup in Windows. Process Explorer, which is essentially a more powerful Task Manager, is also arriving on macOS. These are the latest Sysinternals tools to arrive on non-Windows platforms, as Neowin notes Sysmon, Procmon, and ProcDump are all available for Linux, and ZoomIt for macOS.
- Microsoft’s Project Zenith devices are now available. At IFA earlier this year Microsoft and AMD both announced Project Zenith, a new “distraction-free Windows experience” for developers. This tweaked version of Windows 11 is now launching first on new AMD Ryzen AI Halo devices. You don’t need a whole new device to take advantage of the config tweaks of Project Zenith, as developers can use Microsoft’s Windows Developer Config from GitHub.

If you’ve heard about any of Microsoft’s secret projects, you can reach me via email at notepad@theverge.com or speak to me confidentially on the Signal messaging app, where I’m tomwarren.01. I’m also tomwarren on Telegram, if you’d prefer to chat there.

Trump floats Apple chairman Tim Cook as Jensen Huang’s successor

16:12:44 UTC
01:12:44 KST

In the wake of the industry-shaking news of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s resignation on Thursday, U.S. President Donald Trump is floating the idea that former Apple CEO Tim Cook should take over the position.
In a post on Truth Social, Trump wrote that “only one man” could take Huang’s place: Tim Cook of Apple.
“A Legend replaces a Legend!,” he wrote.
Cook stepped down as Apple’s CEO on September 1, 2026, after 15 years running the company, but he’s still very much involved with Apple. While John Ternus took over as Apple’s CEO, Cook transitioned into the role of Executive Chairman of Apple’s board of directors, which sees him continuing to engage with global policymakers and manage international relationships, including Apple’s relations with China.
Trump is not the only one to suggest Cook as a replacement for Huang. Jeffrey Sonnenfeld, Senior Associate Dean for Leadership Studies at Yale School of Management, who studies CEO leadership and corporate governance, penned an op-ed in Time on Wednesday, suggesting Cook should be among the potential candidates. He wrote that Cook has “an unparalleled track record of succeeding a bigger-than-life founder,” in reference to Cook taking over from Apple founder Steve Jobs.
Whether or not Trump agrees with this opinion, or is simply blasting off an idea that hopped into his mind, is anyone’s guess.
“Tim Nvidia,” however, doesn’t quite have the same ring as “Tim Apple” does.

Jensen Huang Resigns As Nvidia CEO

Jensen Huang Resigns As Nvidia CEO 46
			
		 	
				Jensen Huang has stepped down as CEO of Nvidia after 33 years leading the company, with CFO Colette Kress taking over as interim CEO while the board searches for a permanent replacement.
"The Board has full confidence in Colette and the leadership team, and we expect the company's operations, customer commitments and product roadmap to continue without interruption," said board chair Harvey Jones in a release announcing the news.
The abrupt departure follows revelations about Huang's affairs, including one with a woman U.S. intelligence reportedly believes was a Chinese spy. CNBC reports:  Huang, 63, apologized on Sunday for being "unfaithful to my wife," though he provided no details about the affair with businesswoman Alice Huang, who has worked for a Chinese company and Nvidia customer called Megaspeed. Late Wednesday, The New York Times reported that the Nvidia CEO also had a separate affair, this one with Kimberly Powell, vice president of health care at the chipmaker. Later that afternoon The Times and other outlets reported that Nvidia's board met to discuss the company's future.
Jensen Huang denied allegations that he had compromised national security or Nvidia secrets after reports of the Alice Huang affair. The news sparked concerns that Nvidia knew companies based in Singapore and Malaysia had been evading U.S. export controls in accessing its technology. The board said on Tuesday that it had hired Wachtell Lipton to investigate the intelligence allegations.

Whoop just launched a social network
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
16:00:00 UTC
01:00:00 KST

Whoop, the maker of screen-less fitness bands, is launching a social network on Thursday that lets its members follow friends and family and share their health data.
The network, called Whoop Humans, will be rolled out in the Whoop app to members at every membership tier at no extra cost. Members can follow anyone on Whoop and choose what to share, from sleep and recovery to workouts and achievements. Followers can react and comment; members can also attach photos and commentary to their data. It’s all opt-in, CEO Will Ahmed tells us.
Whoop already has a social feature, Teams, which a little more than half its customers use, according to the company. But Teams is built around groups and leaderboards, and everyone in a group sees everyone else’s information. Humans is meant to be more intimate. Members decide who follows them and who sees what, so a trainer can track a set of clients without the clients seeing one another’s data, for example.
I found myself questioning how much a spouse wants to know about the other’s sleep score when Ahmed mentioned this particular scenario, so I asked him how else beta members used these newer social features. He said the company learned that people were more engaged when someone reported a low recovery score than a nearly perfect one, suggesting that the new feature may be particularly helpful for understanding when someone is struggling and might need attention.
Indeed, the bigger story here is where Whoop continues to continue headed, which is into medical wellness. Like its most direct rival, Oura, the company is pushing faster and more aggressively into the wellness opportunity that an aging population presents.
Whoop had already added FDA-cleared ECG and atrial fibrillation detection, blood pressure insights, blood testing and medical-record uploads. Asked if older customers are gravitating to Whoop, Ahmed said, without offering specifics, that Whoop has seen “huge growth” among people over 60 over the past 18 months. He also said his was among the first companies accepted into a Medicare program that reimburses qualifying wearables that can show they improve health outcomes, and that it now has Medicare members.
He added that he expects families to be big users of Humans. (You can imagine adult children checking in on aging parents.)
But the company’s efforts to be thought of as the “brand for personal health,” raises questions, naturally, about privacy. Ahmed said members choose which metrics to share, can remove followers, and can switch on “stealth mode” to go dark for a few days without changing their settings. Activities marked sensitive aren’t shared by default, and Ahmed said health alerts and medical information aren’t shared at all. Members can also report any comment or photo that they deem disturbing, and the company said reported content is reviewed.
As for minors, Whoop’s minimum age is 13 for all members (higher in some countries), meaning customers of that age can share their data subject to consents and privacy settings. Asked whether a parent could follow a teenager’s data, he didn’t answer directly and pointed to the privacy controls.
Ahmed also said Whoop doesn’t sell member data. “Our product is not you; it’s the membership,” he said, adding that the company doesn’t license data to third parties.
He was candid about dealmaking. Asked about Oura’s announcement this week that it will pay $625 million for Counsel Health, a service offering on-demand physicians, Ahmed said he expects “more consolidation” among AI-focused, clinician-focused and wearable companies, and that Whoop is always on the prowl. Its deep relationship with members, he said, puts it “in a position to be an acquirer.”
He was more guarded about the company’s own finances, declining to share revenue or say whether Whoop is profitable. He did say that the company now has about 1,200 employees, membership on its software platform has increased more than 70% a year for three years running, and that it expects to have nearly 4 million members by year-end.
As for an IPO (the company raised a very big round earlier this year), he said only that Whoop “will certainly IPO in the future.” He also wouldn’t comment on Oura, which shelved its own IPO of up to $2.2 billion in September citing market uncertainty.
In the meantime, Whoop is widening its reach. The company announced that its devices will be sold through Ulta stores beginning next March (around 500 locations of the beauty retailer to start, and a target to retail in more than a thousand stores eventually). Indeed, Ahmed said Whoop’s female audience has more than doubled from last year.
As for competition, in addition to Google’s new Fitbit Air band, Whoop may soon have to contend with Apple, which is reportedly developing a Whoop-style band. But Ahmed mostly shrugged those concerns off, suggesting that building a product doesn’t guarantee success. Instead, he said, Whoop’s job is to stay true to the “aspiring” people it serves.

Read Nvidia’s internal emails about the ‘extraordinarily difficult period’ amid CEO shakeup
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
15:48:12 UTC
00:48:12 KST

Jensen Huang resigned as CEO of Nvidia this morning, a company he co-founded in 1993. The decision came following the company’s board opening an investigation into multiple extramarital affairs Huang had with a direct report and an alleged Chinese spy. The new, and first, Chair of the Board, Harvey Jones, shared news of Huang’s resignation with the company in an email, which also announced CFO Colette Kress as interim CEO.
Read Nvidia’s internal emails about the ‘extraordinarily difficult period’ amid CEO shakeup
Nvidia’s board and interim CEO Colette Kress told employees they’re ready to ‘move forward together.’
You can read the full text of the board’s internal memo and Kress’s first message to employees as interim CEO below.
Subject: Statement from NVIDIA Board Chair
Dear NVIDIA Employees,
I am writing to share an important update. Jensen has resigned as CEO and Board Member, and the Board has asked our Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress to serve as interim CEO while it conducts a search for a permanent successor looking at both internal and external candidates. I will serve as the first-ever Chair of the Board.
As CFO for over 13 years, Colette has been a key architect of NVIDIA’s growth and capital strategy. She possesses a deep understanding of this business and the markets in which we operate. The Board has full confidence in Colette to deliver on our commitments to shareholders, customers, partners, and to each of you.
Jensen’s contributions to NVIDIA are profound. NVIDIA is one of the most consequential technology companies in the world. We pioneered accelerated computing, defined the modern GPU, and built the platform that powered artificial intelligence.
The Board takes seriously our duty as stewards of this great company.
The strength of this company resides in our employees, leadership team, technical strength and partner ecosystem. I am confident that under Colette’s leadership, with the full support of the Board, we will continue to grow our business, and lead the industry forward. Thank you for your focus, your professionalism, and your commitment to NVIDIA.
Harvey Jones
Colette Kress’ first email to Nvidia employees as interim CEO:
Subject: RE: Statement from NVIDIA Board Chair
Dear NVIDIAns,
I believe deeply in this company and in its future. More than anything, I believe in each of you, in our customers and partners and in the vital role we play every day in the global economy.
The Board, Estaff and I recognize that this has been an extraordinarily difficult period and I want you to know how grateful we are for your resilience, professionalism and continued dedication. Thank you.
This company has achieved so much because of the work that all of you bring to it every single day. You are our greatest strength. You will hear more from leadership in the coming days about our future together, and we will host a company meeting on November 18 at 9am PT.
We play a vital role in building the technology that is powering the AI revolution, and we have important work ahead of us. So let’s move forward together, with the same determination and commitment that have always defined this company.
Thank you for all you do,
Colette
Correction, November 12th: This story’s headline has been adjusted to better describe the context of Kress’ quote; she is not referring to a period “ahead,” as the headline initially stated.

Jensen Huang is out at Nvidia — who is Colette Kress, his interim replacement?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
15:37:29 UTC
00:37:29 KST

The Nvidia Board of Directors announced on Thursday morning that the company’s co-founder and CEO of 33 years, Jensen Huang, has resigned as president, CEO, and member of the board, effective immediately.
This is a shocking twist for one of the most valuable companies in the world, which designs the chips that make the AI boom possible. Few people would have predicted this turn of events even just a week ago, as Huang has been so central to Nvidia throughout the company’s existence that he never even established a succession plan. But Huang has now been ousted after reports that he had affairs with both an alleged Chinese spy and one of his direct reports at Nvidia.
While the company conducts a more thorough search for Huang’s successor, Nvidia CFO and Executive Vice President Colette Kress has taken over as interim CEO. But beyond her name and job title, what do we know about the person who will now lead a company worth about $3.8 trillion?
According to Nvidia’s website, Kress has worked at the company since September 2013. Before that, she spent three years as an SVP and CFO of Cisco’s Business Technology Operations Finance organization. She also worked at Microsoft for 13 years, where she spent four years as CFO of the Server and Tools division. She holds a finance degree from the University of Arizona and an MBA from Southern Methodist University.
The California-based 59-year-old has a very limited social media presence — on Linkedin, she has commented some well wishes on former colleagues’ posts about their retirement. But as CFO, she’s been a regular presence on Nvidia’s earnings calls, making her a familiar figure for analysts covering the company.
According to Forbes’ list of America’s Richest Self-Made Women, Kress’ net worth is estimated to be about $1.2 billion. Her Forbes profile adds that she initially joined Nvidia in part because her two sons endorsed the company for its role in the video game industry, which it was best known for at the time.
“Nvidias strength has always come from its people, and together with our leadership team, I am committed to building on the extraordinary foundation Jensen created over the last 33 years,” Kress said in a statement. “We will continue advancing the work that has made Nvidia a leader in accelerated computing and AI, and execute on the opportunities ahead. Our focus remains on serving our customers, partners, developers and shareholders.”
Nvidia is slated to report its quarterly earnings on Tuesday, when we will likely hear more from Kress about her plans for the company’s immediate future.

Claude Fable 5.2 is a frontier model from Anthropic that can work autonomously on long coding tasks. Given one detailed prompt, it can plan, write, test and refine a complete game over several hours of agentic work, with no human writing code in between. Every game in this arcade was produced that way.
Everything ships inside one .html file: markup, CSS, JavaScript, shaders, procedurally generated textures and synthesized audio. 3D games draw through WebGL (often with an inlined copy of Three.js), sound comes from the Web Audio API, and the levels, models and music are generated in code at runtime rather than loaded as separate assets. Open the file in any modern browser and it runs, offline, without a server or build step.
A one-shot prompt is a single instruction given to the model at the start: the design brief, constraints and acceptance criteria all in one message. The model then builds autonomously. Where the original or a faithful reproduction is available, open the “View Prompt” tab on any game to read and copy it.
Yes. Games we host are served from a sandboxed endpoint with a Content-Security-Policy sandbox and an iframe sandbox that allow scripts and pointer lock only, so game code cannot read this site’s cookies or storage, open pop-ups, or navigate the page. Games marked “Author-hosted” are embedded from the creator’s own website in a sandboxed iframe on their own origin, which keeps them isolated from this site too.
Every game has a “Download .html” button and a source inspector. Because each one is a single file, you can open it in any editor, change it and run it locally straight away. Please respect the original author’s license and give credit when you share a remix.
Use the Submit page: upload your .html file (up to 10 MB), add the prompt and controls, and link to your post. No account is needed. Submissions go live after a quick review.

ExcalidrawZ: Local Native Excalidraw on macOS/iOS
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
15:25:52 UTC
00:25:52 KST

Excalidraw is an excellent drawing tool. ExcalidrawZ keeps the familiar Excalidraw canvas, then adds native file management, iCloud sync, history, export, AI assistance, MCP integration, and Apple-platform interaction details around it.
Non-App Store version
- Download the latest .dmg from Releases.
- Open the .dmg and drag ExcalidrawZ into Applications.
ExcalidrawZ manages drawings as real app documents instead of loose browser downloads.
- Organize drawings with groups and custom file sorting.
- Work with ExcalidrawZ library files, local folders, and temporary external files.
- Open and edit .excalidraw, .excalidraw.png, and .excalidraw.svg files directly.
- Sync app-managed files across macOS, iPadOS, and iOS with iCloud.
- Keep file history checkpoints so important changes can be reviewed and restored.
ExcalidrawZ records checkpoints for app-managed drawings, so you can review earlier versions and restore important work when needed.
The canvas remains compatible with Excalidraw while the surrounding app is built for Apple platforms.
- Customize toolbar tool order; number shortcuts follow your configured order.
- Use Apple Pencil on iPad, including familiar undo and redo gestures.
- Use mouse and trackpad scroll and zoom gestures on iPad and iPhone.
- Import PDFs and convert Mermaid diagrams into editable Excalidraw content.
Use Excalidraw collaboration when you want to draw with others.
ExcalidrawZ includes an AI assistant designed for canvas work.
- Ask AI to read, create, and revise drawings.
- Attach images and let AI use visual context when needed.
- Use proposal previews before applying AI-generated content to the real canvas.
- Control whether each file is visible to AI.
- Use AI from compact iPhone toolbars, iPad floating panels, or the macOS inspector.
ExcalidrawZ can expose the current app as an MCP server for compatible AI clients.
- Basic mode provides the familiar excalidraw-mcp drawing workflow.
- Optimized mode adds deeper ExcalidrawZ integration for current-file editing, file navigation, history, canvas inspection, export, library workflows, and math tools.
- Connect local MCP clients through the app-hosted HTTP endpoint.
ExcalidrawZ includes richer tools for technical drawings and study notes.
- Insert LaTeX-rendered formulas.
- Render function graphs with configurable axes and styles.
- Use reusable math templates and AI-assisted formula generation.
- Edit inserted math images later from the canvas.
For sensitive drawings, ExcalidrawZ adds protection around both storage and AI access.
- Lock files with local authentication.
- Encrypt locked files and their checkpoints.
- Use a Recovery Key for fallback access.
- Keep locked content hidden from AI; AI can work through proposal canvases instead.
- Use encrypted backups as an additional recovery path.
ExcalidrawZ supports common export and sharing workflows.
- Export images, editable Excalidraw files, and PDF.
- Preserve editability when exporting .excalidraw.png and .excalidraw.svg.
- Share through clipboard, files, and the system share sheet.
- Archive app-managed files for backup.
ExcalidrawZ can open, import, edit, or export common Excalidraw-related formats:
- .excalidraw
- .excalidraw.png
- .excalidraw.svg
- PDF import and export
- image export
Welcome to my Discord server to share suggestions or report issues for ExcalidrawZ.
- The Excalidraw core used by ExcalidrawZ is also open-source. You can find it here.
Create Config/Overrides.xcconfig relative to the repository root, alongside Config/Project.xcconfig:
DEVELOPMENT_TEAM = YOUR_TEAM_ID
ICLOUD_CONTAINER = iCloud.com.yourname.ExcalidrawZ
APP_GROUP_IDENTIFIER = group.com.yourname.ExcalidrawZ
Replace these example values with identifiers registered to your Apple Developer team. The shared configuration leaves DEVELOPMENT_TEAM empty; set your team in the local overrides. The app and QuickLook extension must use the same App Group. All targets, including the extension and tests, inherit the team from Config/Project.xcconfig, which includes your local overrides. These overrides apply to all build configurations.
Create Config/Secrets.plist relative to the repository root. The project already references this file and includes it in both app targets' resources. The app's configuration loader stops with Unable to load Secrets.plist if the file is missing or invalid.
For local drawing development, this minimal configuration is sufficient:
<?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?>
<!DOCTYPE plist PUBLIC "-//Apple//DTD PLIST 1.0//EN" "http://www.apple.com/DTDs/PropertyList-1.0.dtd">
<plist version="1.0">
<dict>
    <key>COLLAB_URL</key>
    <string>https://example.com</string>
</dict>
</plist>
COLLAB_URL must be a valid absolute URL. The example URL is a placeholder for local drawing work; to test collaboration, replace it with the URL of your deployed collaboration-enabled Excalidraw web client.
Optional string keys are ONEDRIVE_CLIENT_ID, GOOGLE_DRIVE_MACOS_CLIENT_ID, and GOOGLE_DRIVE_IOS_CLIENT_ID. Configure your own OAuth client IDs when developing the corresponding integrations.
Config/Overrides.xcconfig and Config/Secrets.plist are ignored by Git and should stay local.

I'm making LLMs work on small and old devices
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
15:23:10 UTC
00:23:10 KST

Every device around you is more capable than it looks. TV boxes, old phones, single-board computers and routers all ship with CPUs, RAM and flash that sit idle most of the day. smolmaxxing is about unlocking that capability: finding the biggest language model each device can really run, then pushing it further with custom kernels, smarter memory use and weight streaming. Every claim is measured on the hardware itself.
Device #1 is a Xiaomi TV Box S (3rd Gen): Amlogic S7D, 4× Cortex-A55 @ 2.5 GHz, 1.9 GB of RAM (about 1 GB usable), eMMC flash, Android 14, no root. On it, this repo runs models from 260 K to 16 B parameters:
- Qwen3.5-0.8B: 16.8 tok/s (2.2× the stock 7.5; measured in the evening of 11-05, see §17).
- Qwen3.5-2B: 7.7 tok/s in real chat. The box's speed drifts by ~10 % over a day, so every step is measured back to back: §16 gave +11 %, §17 another +7 %.
- An 8B mixture-of-experts model (Moon-Beam-8B-A1B): 7.2 tok/s in real chat, its experts paged from flash on demand and prefetched one layer ahead (§19).
- A dense 1-bit 8B model (Bonsai-8B): 5.2 tok/s in real chat, up from 1.2: fully resident in a "headless" mode plus a lookup-table kernel (§19).
- A 16B mixture-of-experts model (Ling-mini-2.0): 5.1 tok/s in real chat, coherent over 600-word answers, the same expert pager applied unchanged (§20). Twice Moon-Beam's size, but not better per byte: bigger only helps on this box when the model is also better.
- A dense 9B model: 0.27 tok/s, streaming most of its weights from flash.
Device #2 is a first-generation iPad Air (2013): Apple A7, 2 cores, NEON without dotprod, 1 GB of RAM, iOS 12.5.7, jailbroken, CPU only. A 13-year-old tablet runs a 1.7B-parameter model at 24-26 tok/s (18-22 in long chats), a 4B one at about 10 tok/s, an 8B mixture of experts at about 3.5 tok/s (5 with half its experts, at a quality cost), and a 16B one at 2.6-4.5 tok/s, with their experts paged from flash, from a chat page in its own Safari. See Results on device #2.
Every step is written up experiment by experiment in EXPERIMENT.md (E1-E160).
The techniques aren't tied to one box. The 1-bit lookup-table kernel needs only base NEON, as the iPad shows; any ARM chip with dotprod gets the other kernels; and any Linux or Android device with more model than RAM can use the streaming engine.
"First run" is the first time each model ran on the box:
- 0.8B: the stock Q4_K_M file.
- 2B: the stock file, streamed from flash because it didn't fit in RAM. Its first build that fit ran at 4.8, so from there the gain is ×1.6.
- 4B: the stock file, which thrashed.
- 8B dense: the last 0.1 GB streamed from flash.
- 8B MoE: the first expert-pager run, without routing bias.
- 9B: streamed.
- 16B: started on the Moon-Beam recipe, so its gain is small.
On this box, the 2B is the best speed-quality balance. Ling-mini-2.0 shows that bigger isn't automatically better: it is twice Moon-Beam's size, but scores worse per byte and runs slower.
What we built, grouped by the bottleneck it attacks (details and numbers in What's in here):
Quality is English perplexity on a fixed 22 KB text, converted to bits per byte so models with different tokenizers compare fairly (lower is better). Speed is generation on the TV, measured either through llama-server with real chat requests ("chat") or with llama-bench ("bench").
* measured on the pruned head's positions (1.3 % of tokens fall outside it), so not exactly comparable; the stock full-vocabulary Q4_0 file scores 1.024.
Other small models, stock files (screening, §15 E54):
Granite 4.0 H 1B was the one model close to the 2B's quality. Rebuilt our way (E62), it runs at 5.29 tok/s, below the 2B's 6.55 at slightly lower quality: it carries about twice the 0.8B's weight bytes per token.
Physics of the box: one process can hold ~0.96 GB before Android starts killing things (~1.15 GB in headless mode, with the launchers and Google services force-stopped), and flash reads top out at 278 MB/s, with a lone ~1 MB random read costing ~6 ms. A model that has to stream X MB per token can never beat 278 / X tokens per second. That, not the CPU, is what limits everything above 2B, and why a mixture-of-experts model, which touches ~1 B of its 8 B weights per token, is the way past it.
10 tok/s at 8 B? Measured, not guessed (E98): Moon-Beam reaches 10.4 tok/s with 2 experts per token, but the text turns to gibberish; the best coherent setting is ~7-8 tok/s. Dense Qwen3-8B is Bonsai-8B at 1 bit, and the smallest Qwen MoE (35B-A3B) touches 3× Moon-Beam's weights per token.
A 2013 iPad Air: Apple A7, 2 cores, NEON without dotprod, 1 GB of RAM, iOS 12.5.7, jailbroken with Amethyst. Everything runs on the CPU with 2 threads and a 1,024-token context, cross-compiled from a Mac. The write-up is §21 of EXPERIMENT.md, and build steps are in ipad-air/README.md.
Only the Bonsai and Moon-Beam rows were measured on the latest build. The other rows predate the attention and sampler work below.
"Can't load": iOS 12 lets a process map at most about 1.7 GB, so stock llama.cpp fails on the 2.6 and 5.5 GB files before running a token. "First run" for Moon-Beam is the first expert-pager run (pool 8, no bias).
What we built on the iPad, grouped by the bottleneck it attacks:
How Bonsai-1.7B got from 2.75 to about 25 tok/s (same model file throughout):
In long chats (400-token answers), the three turns went from 15.4 / 7.6 / 8.2 to 21.8 / 17.9 / 18.4 tok/s through the attention work alone. The pruning threshold that holds long chats above 17 costs +0.42 % perplexity at 1,024 tokens of context, near the ±0.3 % noise floor; GGML_FA_EPS=0.0001 trades that back for about 15 tok/s.
An 8B model, through an expert pager. iOS 12 caps a process at about 1.7 GB of mapped address space (1.3 GB for the web server's user), so Moon-Beam-8B's 2.6 GB file can't even be mapped, and keeping fewer experts to make it fit more than doubles its perplexity (§21.7). Dense Bonsai-8B runs at 0.08 tok/s from flash. So the model file is rewritten with its experts last, only the 474 MB before them is mapped, and each layer keeps 14 experts in RAM, reading the others from flash with pread when the router picks them. Results match the unpaged model exactly. A small routing bias toward experts already in RAM (+0.3 % perplexity, inside the noise) brought it to 2.4-2.8 tok/s in about 600 MB (§21.8). Round 2 (§21.9) queues flash reads by when they're needed, prefetches the next layer's likely experts, and runs the experts through a lookup-table kernel for Q2_K: 3.4 tok/s, 40× the dense 8B, at +0.7 % perplexity on the full eval text. The flash is still the limit: each token reads about 53 MB at 266 MB/s. Wiring weights in RAM kernel-panicked the iPad, like a pool of 16 did before. Round 3 (§21.10) evicts by decayed use frequency instead of recency, for 3.5 tok/s at no quality cost. 5 tok/s takes 4 experts per token instead of 8 (+27 % perplexity); choosing the count per token adaptively turned out no better than a fixed count. A 16B model (§21.11): Ling-mini-2.0, 16.3B parameters with 256 small experts per layer, runs through the same pager at 2.6 tok/s with unchanged routing, 3.3 at +2.6 % perplexity and 4.5 with a strong cache bias, in under 580 MB. Its 5.5 GB file was assembled from two published quantizations over HTTP range requests and streamed straight to the iPad, since the Mac had no room for it. It is twice Moon-Beam's size at the same speed, but not a better model (+3 % perplexity).
Limits worth knowing. Bonsai is a 1-bit model and still gets facts wrong (in one test it called Mercury the closest planet to Earth). About 700 MB is the practical memory ceiling: 1-bit weights don't compress, so iOS starts killing processes past it, which is why the 4B needs a 512-token context. The jailbreak doesn't survive a reboot. The fast output head adds 2-3 s to the first prompt after each launch.
Kernels for Cortex-A55 (ARMv8.2 dotprod, no i8mm), as a patch on llama.cpp v0.8.0, each with an exactness test against a scalar reference:
Memory tricks:
- Pruned LM head: keep only the ~49-66 K most useful vocabulary rows, chosen by token frequency on English, code and 7 other languages. The other logits are scattered to −inf. Saves 0.2-0.5 GB and misses ~1 % of English tokens.
- Repack cache on flash (GGML_REPACK_CACHE): repacked weights are written to a file once and mapped afterwards. Models load in seconds.
Headless mode (tools/tv-llm-mode.sh on|off): a small foreground activity, then the launchers, Google services and other heavy apps are force-stopped, and svc power stayon keeps the animated screensaver (17 % of a core) away. Nothing persistent except stay-awake, which off restores. MemAvailable ~1000 → ~1300 MB. A fork-free guard (tools/tv-watch.c) replaced a shell loop that started ~25 processes a second and cost 10 % of the speed.
Expert pager for mixture-of-experts models (ggml-cpu/moe-pager-tv.inc):
- Experts live on flash: each expert tensor is overlaid with empty anonymous memory, and right after a layer's routing the missing experts are read with parallel O_DIRECT straight into their own addresses; a per-layer LRU pool (43 of 128 experts) keeps the rest. Kernels see an ordinary tensor. The GGUF's own expert bytes are hole-punched once the repack cache holds them, so the model costs one copy of its weights on flash.
- Cache-aware routing (GGML_MOE_BIAS): a small bonus for experts already in RAM cuts misses from 13 % to ~2 % for a measured quality cost (arXiv 2412.00099 has the idea on other models). Fewer experts per token (LLAMA_MOE_USED=6) is the other dial.
- Warm start from a list of each layer's most used experts, and pre-gated prefetch: layer L+2's router applied to layer L's hidden state, predicting what the router will actually pick (sigmoid, the model's own selection bias and the cache bonus), loads ~60 % of misses before they are needed.
- Why answers looped, and the fix: 2-bit experts break long-form coherence after ~250-300 words, and routing bias amplifies it. DRY sampling only hides it (the model paraphrases its loop); a repeat penalty of 1.15 over 256 tokens brings degenerate long answers from 12/13 to 1/13 on the TV (E99, E100).
Weight streaming for models bigger than RAM (ggml-cpu/stream-tv.inc):
- What stays in RAM: a chosen part of the weights stays resident. The rest lives in "stream zones".
- Prefetch: a hook on every matmul records the order the weights are used. Reader threads then fetch zone weights ahead of time with parallel 8 MB O_DIRECT reads straight into the weights' own addresses, at the flash's full 278 MB/s.
- Correctness: every compute thread waits until its weight is completely loaded, so a wrong prediction costs time, never correctness.
- Drop-behind: each zone weight is freed right after use.
- Verify mode: GGML_STREAM_VERIFY=1 compares every streamed tensor with the file. It found two silent-corruption bugs, which are fixed and documented in E48 and E50.
- Speculative decoding: with the model's own MTP head, verifying 2 tokens costs about one pass over the weights when the pass is flash-bound, so it pays off (+69 %). On the in-RAM 2B it lost (E31).
Profiling (env switches in the patch): GGML_OPPROF=1 per-op table, GGML_OPPROF=2 a node-by-node, per-thread trace of one graph (tools/optrace.py), LLAMA_UBATCH_PROF / GGML_CPU_PROF phase timings. They showed where a small model's time really goes (E57): ~71 % Q4_K matmuls, and only ~2 ms per token of barriers and scheduling.
Tooling:
- Streaming GGUF rewriter: adds a pruned head, extra tensors and metadata in one sequential pass, so Hugging Face → Mac → TV pipelines never store the model on the Mac.
- MTP fetcher: range-reads only the MTP tensors from safetensors.
- Memory guard: wraps every heavy run on the TV.
- Benchmark and perplexity harnesses, a model screener that streams any Hugging Face GGUF to the TV and scores it (tools/screen-small.sh), and a live token-stream web UI.
You need: the TV box (or another ARMv8.2 Android device) with network ADB, adb, zig (for static aarch64 musl builds), cmake + ninja, python3, and uv (for numpy).
export ADB_SERIAL=<tv-ip>:5555          # every script talks to the box through this
adb connect $ADB_SERIAL
# 1. llama.cpp v0.8.0 + the patch, cross-compiled for the TV
git clone https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp third_party/llama.cpp
cd third_party/llama.cpp && git checkout 8642541 && git apply ../../patches/llama.cpp-v0.8.0-tvbox.patch
cmake -S . -B build-tv -G Ninja -DCMAKE_SYSTEM_NAME=Linux -DCMAKE_SYSTEM_PROCESSOR=aarch64 \
  -DCMAKE_C_COMPILER=$PWD/../../zigcc/cc -DCMAKE_CXX_COMPILER=$PWD/../../zigcc/c++ -DCMAKE_BUILD_TYPE=Release \
  -DCMAKE_EXE_LINKER_FLAGS=-static -DBUILD_SHARED_LIBS=OFF -DGGML_NATIVE=OFF \
  -DGGML_CPU_ARM_ARCH=armv8.2-a+dotprod+fp16 -DGGML_OPENMP=OFF -DLLAMA_OPENSSL=OFF -DLLAMA_BUILD_TESTS=OFF
ninja -C build-tv llama-server llama-cli llama-bench llama-perplexity llama-quantize
cd ../..
adb -s $ADB_SERIAL shell mkdir -p /data/local/tmp/llm/patched25 /data/local/tmp/llm/patched
adb -s $ADB_SERIAL push third_party/llama.cpp/build-tv/bin/llama-{server,cli,bench,perplexity} /data/local/tmp/llm/patched25/
adb -s $ADB_SERIAL push third_party/llama.cpp/build-tv/bin/llama-quantize /data/local/tmp/llm/patched/   # used by tools/tv-quant.sh
zig cc -target aarch64-linux-musl -O2 -static -o gguf_recut tools/gguf-recut/gguf_recut.c && adb -s $ADB_SERIAL push gguf_recut /data/local/tmp/llm/
# 2. models: rebuilt from their Hugging Face sources straight onto the TV (see below)
tools/rebuild-models.sh 2b          # also: 08 4b b8 tb8 q9 mb lm caches
# 3. the live chat page (default: the 2B on patched25 with fp16 state), then open http://<tv-ip>:8090 from any device on the network
tv-llm-ui/start.sh
tv-llm-ui/start.sh stop
Other models:
# Moon-Beam-8B-A1B MoE, ~7.2 tok/s (first: `tools/rebuild-models.sh mb`, needs a native Mac build and ~9 GB on the Mac while it runs).
# start.sh turns on headless mode, the expert pager, routing bias, prefetch and the repeat penalty; POOL / BIAS / EXPERTS override them.
MODEL=mb-q2-h60.gguf tv-llm-ui/start.sh
# Ling-mini-2.0 16B MoE, ~5.1 tok/s (first: `tools/rebuild-models.sh lm`)
MODEL=lm2-q2-h60.gguf tv-llm-ui/start.sh
# Bonsai-8B 1-bit fully resident, ~5.2 tok/s (first: `tools/rebuild-models.sh b8`; the repack cache is written on first start)
MODEL=b8-q1-hml.gguf tv-llm-ui/start.sh
sh /data/local/tmp/llm/tv-llm-mode.sh off   # on the TV, afterwards: launcher and screensaver back
# Qwen3.5-0.8B, ~16 tok/s (first: `tools/rebuild-models.sh 08`, needs a native Mac build of llama-quantize)
MODEL=q08-q4k-h65p8.gguf tv-llm-ui/start.sh
# The 8B / 9B numbers below were measured with the build of that time (patched16); the current build has the same
# code plus §15-17. GGML_CHUNK_GUIDED=0 in SENV restores the old matmul scheduling. No fp16 state with MTP.
# Bonsai-8B 1-bit with streaming and no headless mode, ~1.2 tok/s (first run: `tools/rebuild-models.sh b8 caches`)
MODEL=b8-q1-hml.gguf BIN=patched25 EXTRA="-ctk q8_0 -ctv q8_0 -fa on -cram 0" \
  SENV="GGML_REPACK_CACHE=/data/local/tmp/llm/cache/b8c GGML_REPACK_RESIDENT_MB=800 GGML_STREAM_MODE=cache GGML_STREAM_WINDOW_MB=64" tv-llm-ui/start.sh
# Qwen3.5-9B with MTP, ~0.27 tok/s
MODEL=q9-iq2-h65-mtp.gguf BIN=patched25 EXTRA="-nr -ctxcp 0 -cram 0 -ub 16 -b 64 --spec-type draft-mtp --spec-draft-n-max 1" \
  SENV="LLAMA_STREAM_RESIDENT_MB=600 LLAMA_STREAM_PIN=output.,blk.32. GGML_STREAM_WINDOW_MB=64" tv-llm-ui/start.sh
Everything on the box lives in /data/local/tmp/llm and runs as the adb shell user. The only system setting touched is stay-awake in headless mode (svc power stayon), which tv-llm-mode.sh off turns back off. adb shell rm -rf /data/local/tmp/llm removes it all.
The model files (0.5-4 GB each) are not in this repo. tools/rebuild-models.sh recreates every one from public Hugging Face files, streaming them through tools/gguf_build.py onto the TV. The SHA-256s below are of the files used in the experiments. Rebuilds of the 4B, Bonsai-8B and 9B files were checked byte-identical.
Ready-made downloads. The best file of each size class is on Hugging Face exactly as the TV runs it. They need the patched llama.cpp above; stock llama.cpp can't load the pruned head. Each card has the run command and the measured numbers:
Download with hf download hyperionaidev/<repo> --local-dir models/, then adb push the files to /data/local/tmp/llm/, with moe-warm-*.txt next to them. That replaces the 08 / 2b / 4b / mb / b8 / lm targets. For the 8B and 16B models, the first start writes the repack cache.
iPad Air downloads. Every model run on the iPad, where to get it, and its speed there. The three MoE files are our own builds, laid out for the iPad's expert pager and lookup-table kernel: they need the iOS 12 patch (ipad-air/), and any other llama.cpp loads them but gets scrambled experts. The rest are used exactly as published; the Bonsai models' fast layouts are built in RAM at load time. 53 experiments in all (E108-E160, §21):
The head-id lists in data/ were made by tools/vocab_select.py (Qwen3.5 tokenizer) and tools/head_select_tok.py (any tokenizer, via llama-tokenize), from the corpus tools/fetch_corpus.py downloads (Wikipedia + Project Gutenberg).
- On this box the 2B at 4 bits beats every bigger model at 1-2 bits. Post-training quantization below ~2.5 bits breaks normal models (E22, E23, E35). Models trained for 1-bit or ternary hold up, but at 4-8 B they don't fit without streaming.
- Past RAM, flash bandwidth sets the speed. The kernel's LRU is the worst possible policy for a cyclic scan bigger than the cache: it re-reads everything every token. Keep a fixed resident set and stream the rest yourself.
- Batching is free when you are flash-bound. Prompts and speculative verification cost about one weight pass, whatever their size.
- Measure, then verify. Two streaming bugs gave plausible speeds and wrong numbers; only comparing tensors with the file, and perplexity with a Mac reference, caught them.
- Small models are mostly vocabulary. Qwen3.5-0.8B's 248K-row head is a third of its weights; pruning it to 65K rows plus all-Q4_K took it from 7.5 to 12.1 tok/s (E55). Small models also lose more to 4 bits (+7 % perplexity vs +2.3 % for the 2B).
- Simulate before you write a kernel. Activation sparsity (E58) and a low-rank head (E60) both looked promising on paper; a quality simulation on the Mac killed each in under an hour.
- On an in-order core, count NEON ops as well as bytes. Tighter packing saved 20 % of the bytes and cost 2.5× the ops; undoing it made the 4B 33 % faster in chat (E76, E77). The 4-bit kernel is the opposite case, limited by RAM (E27), so the trick does not help the 2B or 0.8B.
- Check that every big matrix reaches the fast kernel. The 2B's head (65,668 rows, ~10 % of each token's weights) silently ran on the generic kernel for weeks because ggml only repacks row counts divisible by 8; four extra rows gave +5.8 % (E71, E72). The load log's repack buffer size gives it away.
- Most of the RAM was Android's, not the model's. Force-stopping the launchers and Google services for the duration (nothing persistent) let Bonsai-8B sit fully in RAM: 1.2 → 3.6 tok/s before any kernel work (E80). An animated screensaver and a guard that forked 25 times a second each cost another 10-15 % (E82, E88).
- For a mixture-of-experts model the flash is a cache miss, not a stream. A ~6 ms miss per expert means the pool hit rate decides the speed; routing toward cached experts and prefetching what the next router will pick turned 4.4 into 7.2 tok/s.
- Perplexity can't see loops. It scores one token at a time on clean text; the 2-bit model scored fine and still fell into loops in 12 of 13 long answers. Score long free-running generations too (tools/loop_stats.py), and compare models with different tokenizers in bits per byte, never in perplexity.
- Past 9B, count resident bytes, not parameters. The ceiling for a mixture-of-experts model here is the non-expert weights that must stay in RAM plus a useful expert pool. Ling-mini-2.0 (16B) keeps 279 MB resident and runs at 5 tok/s; Qwen3.6-35B-A3B would need ~870 MB before caching a single expert (§20).
- Guard the box. After two early kernel panics, every heavy run goes through a wrapper that kills it below 180 MB of free memory.
MIT. The llama.cpp patch is a derivative of llama.cpp (MIT). Model weights keep their own licenses, and the evaluation texts are Wikipedia excerpts (CC BY-SA 4.0).
Built on llama.cpp and llama2.c. Models from Qwen, unsloth (GGUFs and importance matrices), PrismML (Bonsai), Google (Gemma) and stories260K. The evaluation texts are excerpts from Wikipedia (CC BY-SA 4.0). The 🤏 logo is the pinching-hand emoji from Google's Noto Emoji (Apache 2.0).

Fitting Four 32 GB Resizable Bars Behind a PCIe Switch on Linux
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
15:14:52 UTC
00:14:52 KST

Fitting Four 32 GB Resizable BARs Behind a PCIe Switch on Linux
My PCIe switch is a Broadcom PEX88096 in the board’s one x16 slot, with four AMD Radeon AI PRO R9700s and the two RTX 3090s from my PCIe splitter post behind it, and the build post has the whole box.
The switch gives each card eight lanes and lets the cards copy to each other without going through the CPU. AMD’s ROCm calls that peer-to-peer (P2P), and it only turns it on when each card’s whole 32 GB is mapped. On Linux, that means a 32 GB Resizable BAR on every card. A base address register, or BAR, maps a range of the CPU’s address space onto a device’s memory. Resizable BAR is the BIOS option that lets a card’s BAR be the full 32 GB instead of the default 256 MB.
A few people asked for the module after the build post, so it’s on GitHub as
gpu-rebar-layout. It leans on kernel internals that
could change in any release, so read the README before you load it. A patch series on the mailing
list fixes the sizing bug, but this board will still need the module’s resize and rescan after it lands,
with its padding turned off (pad=0).
Why the BIOS can’t map six 32 GB BARs 🔗
With the 3090s added, the board stopped at the white VGA light on its POST LEDs, because every 32 GB BAR takes up 64 GB of the BIOS’s address space. The address space post has the math.
Linux 7.0’s bridge window sizing bug: can’t assign; no space 🔗
This board’s BIOS has no per-card Resizable BAR setting. Linux can give large BARs to some cards and leave others small, as long as it does it before any GPU driver loads. With all six cards in and Resizable BAR off, I wanted Linux to do the allocation. The plan was to set each R9700’s BAR size register to 32 GB, remove the CPU port the switch hangs off, and rescan, so the kernel lays out the whole switch again from the new sizes.
Each R9700’s 32 GB BAR is followed by a doorbell BAR, for registers the driver writes to tell the card there’s new work. With Resizable BAR on, the BIOS makes it 256 MB. Linux leaves it at 2 MB.
The kernel gave the switch a window of about 128.6 GB. With the doorbell placed after each BAR, the way Linux 7.0 lays them out, the four windows span 224 GB (three 64 GB slots plus the last card’s 32 GB and its doorbell). Two R9700s got their BARs, and the rest of the switch got nothing.
pci 0000:1b:00.0: bridge window [mem size 0x800200000 64bit pref]: can't assign; no space
That was on 7.0.6. The unpatched v7.4-rc1 kernel placed three of the four in the control run that the Linux kernel patch-testing post (coming soon) covers.
0x800200000 is 32 GB plus 2 MB, one card’s window. The kernel had sized the switch’s window as the plain
sum of its children: four of those plus the two 3090s’ 288 MB windows.
The function is pbus_size_mem() in drivers/pci/setup-bus.c. For each child of a bridge it adds the
child’s size, or its alignment if that’s bigger, to the total.
r_size = resource_size(r);
size += max(r_size, align);
That leaves no gap when every child’s size is a multiple of its alignment, which is true for ordinary BARs because they’re powers of two. A GPU’s window here is 32 GB plus 2 MB with a 32 GB alignment, so the sum leaves out the gap of almost 32 GB between one card’s doorbell and the next card’s start.
It’s a known regression. Nikolas Britton reported it on linux-pci in September, and the thread’s title describes my problem almost word for word.
Ilpo Järvinen, who maintains this code, has a fix in a 14-patch series of resource placement fixes, at v5 as of November 6 and not merged yet. I tested it and sent a Tested-by to the linux-pci mailing list. With it, all four R9700s get their 32 GB.
Even with the fix, this board still needs something to set the BAR sizes and rescan before the GPU drivers load, because amdgpu’s resize at load time can’t grow a window that other cards already sit in.
A kernel module that pads the BARs 🔗
The kernel already knows how to raise a BAR’s alignment without changing its size, but naming a device
in the pci=resource_alignment= boot option uses the resizing mode instead. Asking for 32 GB alignment
on an R9700 would also grow its small BARs, including the 512 KB register BAR, which can’t be aligned to
32 GB below 4 GB.
The workaround is an out-of-tree module that runs from the initramfs, the tiny early-boot filesystem the kernel starts from, before any GPU driver loads. For each matching card it sets the BAR size register, then it removes the root port above the switch and rescans.
Between the scan and the address assignment it walks every bus segment under the switch. It raises each smaller 64-bit prefetchable BAR’s alignment to the size of the largest 64-bit prefetchable BAR on that segment, and does the same for the other memory BARs as a separate group. Each R9700 window becomes exactly 64 GB and each 3090 window exactly 512 MB. Once every window is a multiple of its alignment, the kernel’s allocator does the rest.
After a reboot the R9700s sat at 160, 224, 288, and 352 GB, the 3090s just above them, and amdgpu reported the whole card.
amdgpu 0000:14:00.0: [drm] Detected VRAM RAM=32624M, BAR=32768M
With the ACS redirect setting
the box already had (pci=disable_acs_redir= on the R9700 switch ports), P2P between the R9700s ran
at 14.0 GB/s for every pair, which is what an eight-lane gen4 link delivers in practice, and all four
copying in a ring at once moved 54.7 GB/s.
If your GPUs won’t all get their BARs 🔗
- Search the linux-pci mailing list for your can't assign error first. Someone may have already
reported it, or even fixed it.
- Resize the BARs and rescan before the GPU drivers load, which means doing it from the initramfs.
- Don’t remove or rescan a GPU that already has its driver loaded. That locked up my box.
- If one card gets its BARs and the next one doesn’t, look at the small BARs next to the big ones.
- Check the cards again a few minutes after boot, in case they go to sleep and don’t wake back up.
I had help with this one. Claude wrote the module with me, ran the commands and tests on the box over SSH, and helped me draft this post. I did the physical work, read all the words, checked the numbers, and rewrote anything that sounded like a chatbot, so the mistakes are mine.
Sources 🔗
- gpu-rebar-layout, the module’s source and README
- PCI: bridge window undersized when child bridge windows are larger than their alignment, the linux-pci thread with the regression report and Ilpo Järvinen’s reply
- PCI: Resource placement algorithm fixes (v5), Ilpo’s series, including “PCI: Fix composite resource sizing” and “PCI: Place resources to either edge of the window”
- drivers/pci/setup-bus.c, pbus_size_mem()
- pci_request_resource_alignment(), the two alignment modes behind pci=resource_alignment=

Cyberattack on Fireworks AI allowed hackers to access Notion customers’ chat transcripts
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
15:02:28 UTC
00:02:28 KST

A cyberattack at Fireworks AI has exposed some of its customers’ data, the company has confirmed. Fireworks AI, a platform that developers use to access and use open AI models, said an attacker breached its cloud provider to gain access to a Fireworks development system, which allowed access to some of its customers’ data.
The company said in its latest security advisory that the cybercriminals took Fireworks’ customers’ billing information and some datasets. Fireworks touts tens of thousands of customers worldwide using its platform, including legal tech company Harvey and AI company Cursor.
At least one confirmed affected customer is online document editor and notetaker Notion, which said in a disclosure that the breach affected around 1% of its own customers.
Notion said that Fireworks alerted the company to a security incident and later confirmed that the hackers had stolen some Notion customer data. On November 3, Notion said it disconnected its systems from Fireworks, deleted the data stored in its systems, and stopped all connectivity between the two.
Notion confirmed this week that the hackers took chat transcripts of its Notion AI customers who used the “Insights” feature. The company said that the transcripts include “any input to or output from the model that was written to the chat,” but added that much of the data was unattributable and could not be determined from which customer it was obtained.
The breach at Fireworks AI shows how tech companies are reliant on other tech providers for their own offerings, and how cyberattacks can, as a result, spill over into other customers.
Fireworks said that it believes the hackers are financially motivated and that their goals are focused on selling tokens from breaching access to large language models, or LLMs. These schemes rely on selling to unauthorized third parties, who can burn through AI model usage without using their own resources.
This is the second breach affecting Fireworks in as many months, following a security incident in October.

Running web sites makes me not want to
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
14:30:38 UTC
23:30:38 KST

Running web sites makes me not want to
It is so just depressing these days.
Several years ago now I started self-hosting things. Initially the learning process and how to set up everything drove me to eagerly keep learning more and more, and even how to manage keeping everything up and reliable. But then the LLM Scrapers arrived.
I looked at Anubis and decided I didn't want the delay loading for regular viewers, so moved to iocaine which is slightly different in what it does, and that was fine until so many scrapers hit my site that it saturated my upload with generated content feeding the them. So I removed it, watched iftop - for not even very long - over a few evenings and noted a bunch of IP ranges owned by cloud companies and the likes of Meta(Facebook) who's servers have zero reason to hit my sites. Pretty sure no one is sharing links to my blog there. Also Azure, Alibaba... and a bunch of others.
Large cloud provider servers don't need to hit my website.
I do realise that scrapers are also using residential IP ranges via SDK pitched to app developers that add backdoors to utilise the connection for "other things" (I cbf finding a link to put here but you can search for them if you want to get even more depressed about the state of the web), but most of the traffic was from a small number of large cloud allocated /16 IP ranges so I just dropped those in nftables.
I also added nft-blackhole with what seems to be a few not overly zealous IP lists, and it seems to help a bit too.
So anyway, on to my point...
People who check my photography site would have noticed I haven't updated it in a couple of years now. I have talked about it a few years ago, but it is driving me to consider removing my photography website as it currently is, and just have links to sell physical zines per event.
Zero digital image uploads.
This is more work for me, but it really irks me how entitled the corporate scraping of the web has become just so they can sell back to us a poor imitation of creativity.
I do not want my time spent on something that ends up feeding that machine. Yes I know they are now buying books and destroying them to feed it new data because the online pool it slurps up is now so muddied with LLM slop that it corrupts its own training data, but it seems unlikely they would buy some shitty zine from a local photographer in New Zealand - and I can monitor that.
I watched a video recently where a bunch of devs said they miss the old web, when everyone had their own site. I can't see this being likely again with the amount of "legal" DDOS we have to put up with if we have a website hosted on our home server. You just can't be a casual tech enthusiast and have enough limiting/protection in place these days. A certain amount of knowledge and skill is required, unless you... pay another large corporate to be the CDN in front of it. And the amount of traffic I should be getting means I very much dislike that answer.
Maybe I will enjoy the zine/book-making process if I can streamline the process to a certain point. If I get something useful sorted, I will update here of course.
Sorry.
This is yet another post on the depressing reality of the current times and corporate tech over-reach.
But maybe it will force me to make something that only exists in the physical world instead, so there is that for our silver lining.

====================================================================================================

PostgreSQL 18.7, 17.12, 16.16, 15.20, 14.25, and 19 RC 2 Released
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
14:25:23 UTC
23:25:23 KST

The PostgreSQL Global Development Group has released an update to all supported versions of PostgreSQL, including 18.7, 17.12, 16.16, 15.20, and 14.25, as well as the second release candidate of PostgreSQL 19. This release fixes 52 security vulnerabilities and over 140 bugs reported over the last several months.
There are several issues that may require you to take extra steps after updating, which are described in detail below. See the "Updating" section for more information on post-update steps you may need to take.
For the full list of changes, please review the release notes.
This is the final release of PostgreSQL 14. PostgreSQL 14 is now end-of-life and will no longer receive security and bug fixes. If you are running PostgreSQL 14 in a production environment, we suggest that you make plans to upgrade to a newer, supported version of PostgreSQL. Please see our versioning policy for more information.
The following security vulnerabilities are closed in this update release. You can find more details about the vulnerabilities and their affected versions in the links below:
ALTER ... SET DATA TYPE executes arbitrary code (CVSS v3.1: 8.8)dblink and subscriptions can append to arbitrary files (CVSS v3.1: 8.1)INSERT ... ON CONFLICT can read or modify rows despite auto-updatable view WHERE condition (CVSS v3.1: 5.4)pg_basebackup can use backup manifest content as a format string (CVSS v3.1: 3.7)EXECUTE privilege on cast functions (CVSS v3.1: 3.1)RECORD typmod use-after-free executes arbitrary code (CVSS v3.1: 8.8)plperl prepared-query use-after-free executes arbitrary code (CVSS v3.1: 8.8)dict_int reads beyond zero-length tokens (CVSS v3.1: 4.3)COPY error context uses freed field storage (CVSS v3.1: 4.3)DROP OWNED can remove restrictive row security policies from non-owned tables (CVSS v3.1: 5.4)MERGE can place the wrong row in a trigger transition table (CVSS v3.1: 5.4)vacuumdb --missing-stats-only lets a database owner execute code as the vacuumdb user (CVSS v3.1: 7.2)pg_restore executes arbitrary code due to missing non-plain archive validation (CVSS v3.1: 8.8)tsvector_delete() heap buffer overflow executes arbitrary code (CVSS v3.1: 8.8)ts_rank_cd() heap buffer overflow may execute arbitrary code (CVSS v3.1: 8.8)pg_dump not-null constraint name allows SQL injection (CVSS v3.1: 8.8)return_next() use-after-free executes arbitrary code (CVSS v3.1: 8.8)pltcl reentrant result conversion use-after-free executes arbitrary code (CVSS v3.1: 8.8)reindexdb index name quoting allows SQL injection (CVSS v3.1: 8)security_barrier analysis can expose hidden values through indirect expressions (CVSS v3.1: 4.3)REINDEX CONCURRENTLY does not block DROP INDEX during index functions (CVSS v3.1: 8.8)REASSIGN OWNED bypasses role SET and CREATEDB checks (CVSS v3.1: 4.3)plpgsql record-field type confusion executes arbitrary code (CVSS v3.1: 8.8)pg_show_all_settings() use-after-free discloses memory (CVSS v3.1: 4.3)
This update fixes over 140 bugs that were reported in the last several months. The issues listed below affect PostgreSQL 18. Many of these issues also affect other supported versions of PostgreSQL.
pg_trgm GiST indexes
that could cause searches to miss matching rows. You need to reindex after
updating;  see the "Updating" section.missing chunk number errors.
If you've encountered this issue, after updating, you'll need to reindex. See
the "Updating" section.NOT NULL NO INHERIT constraints on partitioned tables in all
cases, including CREATE TABLE ... LIKE. Previously, this could allow a
partition to contain NULL values when it should not. See the "Updating"
section for how to find and repair affected constraints.bpchar without a
length, which could cause index-only scans to return wrong results. If you have
B-tree indexes on pg_control file, and avoiding
truncated or zero-padded copies of files such as postgresql.auto.conf that
are replaced while the backup is running.CLUSTER or
VACUUM FULL) when a row had been updated by a subtransaction that later
aborted.LATERAL references, outer joins, self-join elimination
on tables using inheritance, DISTINCT ON in a subquery with set-returning
functions, and queries using non-deterministic collations.RANGE-partitioned
tables when a query filters only on the leading columns, DEFAULT partitions
on tables using MINVALUE or MAXVALUE bounds, and concurrent updates that
could silently skip rows that should be updated.SERIALIZABLE
isolation. Previously, concurrent updates that should have failed with a
serialization error could succeed.WHEN NOT MATCHED BY SOURCE when the target row is concurrently deleted.
Previously, a WHEN NOT MATCHED BY TARGET action could run instead, giving
incorrect results.JSON_TABLE and JSON_VALUE that could return incorrect
results.psql to accept
multiple COPY ... FROM STDIN
commands in a single command string. The fix for CVE-2026-6464 caused AT TIME ZONE in a FROM clause or a target alias named set, tsvector
and tsquery values that could fail to reload.
This release also updates time zone data files to tzdata release 2026f.
Beginning in November 2026, Canada's Northwest Territories (America/Inuvik)
are on year-round UTC-06 and Manitoba (America/Winnipeg) is on year-round
UTC-05. This release assumes their TZ abbreviations will be CST and EST
respectively (though this could change). It also reflects that Cuba will stay
on daylight saving time this winter, and that the EST5EDT, CST6CDT,
MST7MDT, and PST8PDT zone names now use their indicated UTC offsets for
old timestamps instead of the history of their associated city-named zones.
All PostgreSQL update releases are cumulative. As with other minor releases,
users are not required to dump and reload their database or use pg_upgrade
in order to apply this update release; you may simply stop PostgreSQL and
update its binaries.
If you suspect that a GIN index is affected by one of the corruption issues fixed in this release, you should reindex it, for example:
REINDEX INDEX your_index_name;
If you use pg_trgm, you should reindex any GiST indexes that use the
gist_trgm_ops operator class.
If you see missing chunk number errors when reading a table, you should
reindex the table, which also reindexes its TOAST table, for example:
REINDEX TABLE your_table_name;
If you use incremental backups, we recommend taking a new full backup after updating and using it as the basis for subsequent incremental backups.
If you use partitioned tables, the following query finds NOT NULL NO INHERIT
constraints on partitioned tables:
SELECT conrelid::regclass, conname
FROM pg_constraint
WHERE contype = 'n' AND connoinherit
  AND conrelid IN (SELECT oid FROM pg_class WHERE relkind = 'p');
You can remove unwanted constraints with ALTER TABLE ... DROP CONSTRAINT ....
Constraints you want to keep should be repaired with
ALTER TABLE ... ALTER CONSTRAINT ... INHERIT, which will fail if any of the
table's partitions contain NULL values in that column.
If you have B-tree indexes on bpchar columns (that is, columns declared as
bpchar without a length), you should reindex them after updating. Columns
declared as char(n) are not affected.
Users who have skipped one or more update releases may need to run additional post-update steps; please see the release notes from earlier versions for details.
For more details, please see the release notes.
This release marks the second release candidate of PostgreSQL 19. As a release candidate, PostgreSQL 19 RC 2 will be mostly identical to the initial release of PostgreSQL 19, though some more fixes may be applied prior to the general availability of PostgreSQL 19. PostgreSQL 19 RC 2 also includes the fixes in this update release that apply to PostgreSQL 19.
The planned date for the general availability of PostgreSQL 19 is November 19, 2026. Please see the "Release Schedule" section for more details.
To upgrade to PostgreSQL 19 RC 2 from earlier versions of PostgreSQL, you will
need to use a major version upgrade strategy, e.g. pg_upgrade or
pg_dump / pg_restore. For more information, please visit the documentation
section on upgrading:
https://www.postgresql.org/docs/19/upgrading.html
If you use pg_upgrade and have indexes that use
full text search,
pg_trgm, or
fuzzystrmatch, please
review the
"Migration to Version 19"
section of the release notes, as you may need to reindex them after upgrading.
Several bug fixes were applied to PostgreSQL 19 during the RC 1 period. These include:
JSON_ARRAY() and JSON_OBJECT()
expressions over arrays, composite types, and range types to be used in
expression indexes, generated columns, and partition keys, as these indexes
could become out-of-date. If you created any of these objects while testing
PostgreSQL 19, you need to drop or redefine them before upgrading, as
pg_upgrade will fail to recreate them.GROUPING SETS
queries, where a column not included in the current grouping set could show a
value instead of first_value() and nth_value() when using
IGNORE NULLS with
some EXCLUDE frame options.LISTEN /
NOTIFY queue becoming
stuck if a session is interrupted while reading notifications.work_mem
limit based on the planner's row estimate.
For a detailed list of fixes, please visit the open items page.
This is the second release candidate for PostgreSQL 19. Unless an issue is discovered that requires a delay or an additional release candidate, PostgreSQL 19 should be made generally available on November 19, 2026.
For further information please see the Beta Testing page.
If you have corrections or suggestions for this release announcement, please send them to the pgsql-www@lists.postgresql.org public mailing list.
