Abandoned Quake 4 expansion finished by modders and now playable
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
14:18:49 UTC
23:18:49 KST

openQ4 0.14.1, the Awakening Edition: play the recovered Quake 4 expansion beside the original campaign, with stable shadow mapping, a new interface, Vulkan on Windows, smarter bots and an experimental Android app.
							
The Quake 4 expansion that never shipped is now playable. openQ4 0.14.1, the Awakening Edition, is out now, and it is the biggest update since 0.13.2. It brings a second campaign, stable shadow mapping, a rebuilt interface, Vulkan on Windows, smarter bots and an experimental Android app, then spends the extra weeks polishing all of it toward production quality.
0.14.0 was withdrawn soon after release, so for most of you this is the first look at everything below. 0.14.1 replaces it, with every 0.14 feature refined, fixed and optimized. It is a big step on the road to 1.0.
The Awakening, at last
Raven Software and Ritual Entertainment built an expansion for Quake 4 that was never released. Justin Marshall recovered and published it, and openQ4 now plays it beside the original campaign: pick Single Player > Campaign and choose Quake 4 or The Awakening.
- All 13 maps on openQ4's own code. The expansion's monsters, weapons and vehicles are rebuilt in openQ4's engine and game code. openQ4 never loads the alpha's game DLL.
- Finished where the alpha wasn't. Where the alpha's own code or content stopped something working as designed, openQ4 fixes it: the Valkaryne finale, the beach's death-ray turret, the Pain Lord's weak spot and Retches that could never move are the biggest examples.
- New toys. A chaingun turret with heat and its own HUD, and vehicle HUDs in both the new and classic interfaces.
- Two campaigns, kept apart. Each keeps its own saves and settings, and returning to Quake 4, Arena or multiplayer removes the expansion's overrides.
openQ4 ships none of the expansion's content; you supply the files. The Awakening guide shows how.
Looks better, runs faster
- Shadow mapping you can leave on. The Quality and Ultra presets cast soft, stable shadow maps on OpenGL and Vulkan, with sharp contact shadows that soften with distance. They are cached while lights and casters stay still, so they cost less every frame and no longer shimmer as you walk. The flashlight casts shadows on every preset that draws them.
- Vulkan on Windows is now supported and the automatic choice for new installs. It was tested across every stock single-player map and 20 multiplayer maps, picks your GPU in Settings, recovers from driver resets, and falls back to OpenGL in the same launch if it has to.
- Presets re-tiered by frame rate. Ultra now plays smoothly instead of asking for everything at once; the old everything-on Ultra lives on as Maximum. New Low and Enhanced tiers fill the gaps, and Minimum and Low Power suit weak or battery-powered hardware.
- Smarter Auto-Detect reads your active GPU, memory, resolution and CPU, and fresh installs use it automatically.
- No more restarts. V-Sync, MSAA, texture quality, window and fullscreen changes and most presets apply to the running game.
- Quicker loading. Maps load faster, sounds decode in the background, declarations parse a fifth faster and start-up is over a second quicker.
- Cleaner motion. Temporal AA rebuilds full detail below native resolution and holds still when nothing moves, motion blur looks the same at any frame rate, and cutscenes, cameras, ragdolls and spectating stay smooth on high-refresh screens.
A whole new interface
- The Remastered HUD with Minimal, Competitive and Classic layouts, controller button prompts, and Reduced Motion and Reduced Flashing options.
- Rebuilt menus and tabbed settings, including a Display page that keeps or reverts a change on a countdown, plus New Game and Chapters pages for the campaigns.
- Vector weapon wheels and scopes for Marine and Strogg, with live ammo.
- Animated crosshairs. Each weapon's original crosshair assembles, kicks, charges and reloads with the weapon.
- Built for every screen: controllers, touch, portrait and tall layouts, and text up to 200%.
- Arena regains its ceremony: the match-start camera move and "Fight!", opponent introductions and a real pause menu.
Prefer the original look? The classic screens and HUD are one setting away: seta ui_retained 0.
Multiplayer, bots and demos
- Your inputs reach the server on time again, and prediction and lag compensation no longer correct the same delay twice.
- Bots that take orders. They plan safer routes, hear the fight, time item respawns and follow commands such as everyone follow me. They also stop knocking team-mates off walkways, swim to underwater routes and escape napalm and grenades.
- Talk to your team: quick chat on D-pad Down or V, push-to-talk voice chat with per-player mute, and objective markers for flags, assault points and DeadZone zones.
- Demos you can navigate. Multi-view demos gain kill, capture and round markers, Auto-Track and near-instant seeking, and you can record a whole campaign, level transitions included, as one demo.
- Hosting works as set: Create Server options take effect, the Admin page accepts remote-console passwords, dedicated servers change maps faster, and networking is hardened throughout.
Sound and languages
- Room reverb for every map, as the original game had with EAX hardware: closed doors muffle, hangars boom and radio chatter stays dry.
- Truer audio: sound variety, stereo width, radio levels and distance falloff now match the original game, and unplugging your headphones no longer cuts the music.
- Dialogue subtitles with size, backing and speaker names, plus a separate Dialogue Volume.
- Six new languages: German, Brazilian Portuguese, Czech, Hungarian, Turkish and Ukrainian menus and game text.
New frontiers (experimental)
- Android: a signed ARM64 APK with touch controls, portrait play and a touch layout editor, built on Emile Belanger's port and OpenGL ES renderer.
- VR: head-tracked stereo through OpenXR, with motion-controller aiming, room-scale movement and comfort options, now on Vulkan too.
- Level editor: edit brushes, patches and entities in 2D and 3D views, then build and play the map in one click.
- For modders and mappers: physically based materials with reflection probes, recompiled maps that keep their AI cover positions, and clip brushes that block what they were built to block.
Sturdier under the hood
Three code audits and static analysis fixed crash, memory-safety and save-restore defects throughout the engine and game code. Saves are now written in a portable format and reported only once they are safely on disk, and openQ4's own data ships as separate packs so future patches can be small downloads.
Before you install
- You need your own copy of Quake 4 from Steam or GOG. openQ4 finds it on its own.
- Install into a fresh folder. 0.14.1 repacks openQ4's own data, so don't copy it over an older openQ4.
- Your settings, bindings and saves carry over. Reapply your preset in Settings > System to get the re-tiered values. New saves use a format older builds can't read, so back up your saves if you might go back.
- Multiplayer needs matching openQ4 0.14.1 clients and servers. Retail Quake 4 and mods built for its original game DLLs are not compatible.
- Mappers: recompile maps that use vehicle, fly, shot or item clip brushes.
Experimental means experimental: VR has not yet been qualified on headset hardware, Android has been qualified on one phone so far, and The Awakening is built from unfinished alpha content, with full playthrough qualification still in progress. Your reports help.
Download
Grab it from the openQ4 downloads here on ModDB, or get any package from the GitHub release page:
- Windows x64, as an installer or a zip
- Linux x64, as an AppImage or an archive (also used on Steam Deck)
- macOS on Apple Silicon (preview); signed builds are also offered by Mac Source Ports
- Linux ARM64 (preview), Windows ARM64 and Android ARM64 (experimental)
The full release notes list everything new since 0.13.2, with upgrade notes and known limitations.
The road to 1.0
0.14.1 is a deliberate push toward 1.0: fewer new experiments and more polish on what already exists. The same engine work now underpins the rebased openPREY and openD3 projects, which share fixes with openQ4 in both directions.
Thank you to Emile Belanger for the Android port, Justin Marshall for recovering and publishing The Awakening, Schnapple of Mac Source Ports for the signed macOS builds, akacross for tireless playtesting and pull requests, and everyone who reported issues after 0.14.0. Come and tell us how it plays in #openq4 on the DarkMatter Discord.

====================================================================================================

0.14.0 was withdrawn soon after release, so for most of you this is the first look at everything below. 0.14.1 replaces it, with every 0.14 feature refined, fixed and optimized. It is a big step on the road to 1.0.
The Awakening, at last
Raven Software and Ritual Entertainment built an expansion for Quake 4 that was never released. Justin Marshall recovered and published it, and openQ4 now plays it beside the original campaign: pick Single Player > Campaign and choose Quake 4 or The Awakening.
- All 13 maps on openQ4's own code. The expansion's monsters, weapons and vehicles are rebuilt in openQ4's engine and game code. openQ4 never loads the alpha's game DLL.
- Finished where the alpha wasn't. Where the alpha's own code or content stopped something working as designed, openQ4 fixes it: the Valkaryne finale, the beach's death-ray turret, the Pain Lord's weak spot and Retches that could never move are the biggest examples.
- New toys. A chaingun turret with heat and its own HUD, and vehicle HUDs in both the new and classic interfaces.
- Two campaigns, kept apart. Each keeps its own saves and settings, and returning to Quake 4, Arena or multiplayer removes the expansion's overrides.
openQ4 ships none of the expansion's content; you supply the files. The Awakening guide shows how.
Looks better, runs faster
- Shadow mapping you can leave on. The Quality and Ultra presets cast soft, stable shadow maps on OpenGL and Vulkan, with sharp contact shadows that soften with distance. They are cached while lights and casters stay still, so they cost less every frame and no longer shimmer as you walk. The flashlight casts shadows on every preset that draws them.
- Vulkan on Windows is now supported and the automatic choice for new installs. It was tested across every stock single-player map and 20 multiplayer maps, picks your GPU in Settings, recovers from driver resets, and falls back to OpenGL in the same launch if it has to.
- Presets re-tiered by frame rate. Ultra now plays smoothly instead of asking for everything at once; the old everything-on Ultra lives on as Maximum. New Low and Enhanced tiers fill the gaps, and Minimum and Low Power suit weak or battery-powered hardware.
- Smarter Auto-Detect reads your active GPU, memory, resolution and CPU, and fresh installs use it automatically.
- No more restarts. V-Sync, MSAA, texture quality, window and fullscreen changes and most presets apply to the running game.
- Quicker loading. Maps load faster, sounds decode in the background, declarations parse a fifth faster and start-up is over a second quicker.
- Cleaner motion. Temporal AA rebuilds full detail below native resolution and holds still when nothing moves, motion blur looks the same at any frame rate, and cutscenes, cameras, ragdolls and spectating stay smooth on high-refresh screens.
A whole new interface
- The Remastered HUD with Minimal, Competitive and Classic layouts, controller button prompts, and Reduced Motion and Reduced Flashing options.
- Rebuilt menus and tabbed settings, including a Display page that keeps or reverts a change on a countdown, plus New Game and Chapters pages for the campaigns.
- Vector weapon wheels and scopes for Marine and Strogg, with live ammo.
- Animated crosshairs. Each weapon's original crosshair assembles, kicks, charges and reloads with the weapon.
- Built for every screen: controllers, touch, portrait and tall layouts, and text up to 200%.
- Arena regains its ceremony: the match-start camera move and "Fight!", opponent introductions and a real pause menu.
Prefer the original look? The classic screens and HUD are one setting away: seta ui_retained 0.
Multiplayer, bots and demos
- Your inputs reach the server on time again, and prediction and lag compensation no longer correct the same delay twice.
- Bots that take orders. They plan safer routes, hear the fight, time item respawns and follow commands such as everyone follow me. They also stop knocking team-mates off walkways, swim to underwater routes and escape napalm and grenades.
- Talk to your team: quick chat on D-pad Down or V, push-to-talk voice chat with per-player mute, and objective markers for flags, assault points and DeadZone zones.
- Demos you can navigate. Multi-view demos gain kill, capture and round markers, Auto-Track and near-instant seeking, and you can record a whole campaign, level transitions included, as one demo.
- Hosting works as set: Create Server options take effect, the Admin page accepts remote-console passwords, dedicated servers change maps faster, and networking is hardened throughout.
Sound and languages
- Room reverb for every map, as the original game had with EAX hardware: closed doors muffle, hangars boom and radio chatter stays dry.
- Truer audio: sound variety, stereo width, radio levels and distance falloff now match the original game, and unplugging your headphones no longer cuts the music.
- Dialogue subtitles with size, backing and speaker names, plus a separate Dialogue Volume.
- Six new languages: German, Brazilian Portuguese, Czech, Hungarian, Turkish and Ukrainian menus and game text.
New frontiers (experimental)
- Android: a signed ARM64 APK with touch controls, portrait play and a touch layout editor, built on Emile Belanger's port and OpenGL ES renderer.
- VR: head-tracked stereo through OpenXR, with motion-controller aiming, room-scale movement and comfort options, now on Vulkan too.
- Level editor: edit brushes, patches and entities in 2D and 3D views, then build and play the map in one click.
- For modders and mappers: physically based materials with reflection probes, recompiled maps that keep their AI cover positions, and clip brushes that block what they were built to block.
Sturdier under the hood
Three code audits and static analysis fixed crash, memory-safety and save-restore defects throughout the engine and game code. Saves are now written in a portable format and reported only once they are safely on disk, and openQ4's own data ships as separate packs so future patches can be small downloads.
Before you install
- You need your own copy of Quake 4 from Steam or GOG. openQ4 finds it on its own.
- Install into a fresh folder. 0.14.1 repacks openQ4's own data, so don't copy it over an older openQ4.
- Your settings, bindings and saves carry over. Reapply your preset in Settings > System to get the re-tiered values. New saves use a format older builds can't read, so back up your saves if you might go back.
- Multiplayer needs matching openQ4 0.14.1 clients and servers. Retail Quake 4 and mods built for its original game DLLs are not compatible.
- Mappers: recompile maps that use vehicle, fly, shot or item clip brushes.
Experimental means experimental: VR has not yet been qualified on headset hardware, Android has been qualified on one phone so far, and The Awakening is built from unfinished alpha content, with full playthrough qualification still in progress. Your reports help.
Download
Grab it from the openQ4 downloads here on ModDB, or get any package from the GitHub release page:
- Windows x64, as an installer or a zip
- Linux x64, as an AppImage or an archive (also used on Steam Deck)
- macOS on Apple Silicon (preview); signed builds are also offered by Mac Source Ports
- Linux ARM64 (preview), Windows ARM64 and Android ARM64 (experimental)
The full release notes list everything new since 0.13.2, with upgrade notes and known limitations.
The road to 1.0
0.14.1 is a deliberate push toward 1.0: fewer new experiments and more polish on what already exists. The same engine work now underpins the rebased openPREY and openD3 projects, which share fixes with openQ4 in both directions.
Thank you to Emile Belanger for the Android port, Justin Marshall for recovering and publishing The Awakening, Schnapple of Mac Source Ports for the signed macOS builds, akacross for tireless playtesting and pull requests, and everyone who reported issues after 0.14.0. Come and tell us how it plays in #openq4 on the DarkMatter Discord.

====================================================================================================

Lego’s BrickLink marketplace has been offline for a week following ‘unusual activity’
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
14:13:58 UTC
23:13:58 KST

Bricklink, a popular online marketplace that Lego bought in 2019 where fans can buy and sell parts and sets, has been down for a week and the company doesn’t know when it will return. The team who runs the site has been sharing updates over the past week including estimates of when it will be back up. It was last expected to return on November 11th, according to Jay’s Brick Blog, but a statement Lego shared to the site yesterday says there’s currently no estimate of when BrickLink will be back online.
Lego’s BrickLink marketplace has been offline for a week following ‘unusual activity’
BrickLink was taken offline on November 5th but Lego doesn’t know when it will return.
Lego hasn’t explained exactly what happened with the marketplace, but says it “took BrickLink offline after identifying unusual activity on the platform. Our immediate priority was to secure the site, protect our members and ensure the integrity of BrickLink.” There are also no specifics on when BrickLink will return, as Lego works to contain what happened. “Before bringing the site back online, we need to fully understand what has happened. That means containing the issue, investigating how it happened and checking for related issues.”
Three years ago, BrickLink experienced a similar issue around the same time of year resulting in the marketplace being offline for several days due to a cybersecurity incident. Lego shut down the site “out of an abundance of caution” after receiving a “threat and ransom demand.” When BrickLink returned, users were prompted to change their passwords with stronger requirements, but were assured their accounts had not been compromised during the incident.

Talking Zen 6 with Mike Clark at AMD In-Depth
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
14:12:37 UTC
23:12:37 KST

Hello you fine Internet folks,
I sat down with AMD’s Mike Clark to talk about Zen 6, the evolution from Zen 5 and what some of the differences are between Zen 6 and Zen 6c and what drove some of those changes.
Hope y’all enjoy!
The transcript below has been edited for readability and conciseness.
George Cozma: Hello, you fine Internet folks. We’re here at AMD In-Depth, where we’re talking about Venice. With me, I have a returning guest, Mike Clark. Would you like to tell the audience who you are?
Mike Clark: Hi, I’m Mike Clark, the original architect of Zen. Some people refer to me as “Zen Daddy.” I’ve been working on cores here at AMD for 33 years, and we’ve got another great one coming, so I’m very proud of the design team I get to represent here today.
George Cozma: I’ve had some hands-on time with Zen 6c. Let’s talk about the change that’s happened with Zen 6 and Zen 6c, what I refer to in my piece as the Zen 6 family. Why is it different from previous generations?
Mike Clark: Right. Starting with Zen 4, we’ve been going through this evolution of having two core families. Before, we were really just using the denser technology as an area play. In this generation, having learned a lot, as we were filling out that denser version of the core, we wanted to leverage it to add some extra IPC within the core. So if you look more in-depth at what we have, what used to be our classic design, we now think of as our high-frequency design. And our dense technology is now our top-of-the-line, no-questions-asked performance core, because we’ve also enabled the full L3 in that implementation, at 4MB per core. So we have 32 of those cores, 128MB of L3, and the highest IPC you can get in that core.
George Cozma: Awesome. So far, one thing I’ve noticed is that the vector scheduler is a bit bigger on Zen 6c. What was the reasoning for increasing that on Zen 6c?
Mike Clark: Well, that wasn’t the only thing we increased. One principle we follow is that we don’t want to create a delta between the two architectures that software has to be optimized for. We want them to have the same fit and feel, so the same optimizations apply to both. But because of its reduced frequency target, Zen 6c can have a little more capacity in the queues, since it doesn’t have to reach the maximum frequency the high-frequency core has to hit. The floating-point scheduler is just one example. There are other queues throughout the core that are larger in Zen 6c, to pull out a little more IPC and get a little more leverage in that SMT environment as well.
George Cozma: Interesting. Both Zen 6 and Zen 6c build on the design you had in Zen 5, and one of the interesting things with Zen 5 was the dual decode setup. Has the front end changed much to let a single thread make better use of both decode clusters?
Mike Clark: Yes. As you said, Zen 5 was a new foundation, with the ability to fetch two basic blocks and get them down the line. But it gets quite complicated with one thread running larger-footprint workloads, where some of it might be hitting the I-cache and some of it might be hitting the op cache, and you’re trying to get everything synced up. So we spent a lot of time optimizing that and making it more efficient from both a delivery and a power perspective: less power wasted, and more efficient delivery from that new front end.
George Cozma: Awesome. Going down into the execution engine, I also noticed that the number of integer multiply units has gone down from three to two. Could you talk a bit about what drove that choice?
Mike Clark: It really came out of the Zen 5 design, where we had a more unified scheduler. To make a scheduler simpler, you want the units it schedules into to be very uniform, and the way we had set up those schedulers, adding a third multiplier to the group it was scheduling into was easy and didn’t add complication. In Zen 6, we realized that to really scale out the architecture, we needed to go back to more distributed schedulers, which now schedule into the ALUs. We hadn’t seen much uptick from that extra multiplier on Zen 5, so to optimize the area and the schedulers, we went back to two multipliers.
George Cozma: Speaking of integer multiply, on the vector side you’ve added two more VNNI units. What was the impetus for adding them, and at what point in the design did you add them?
Mike Clark: As I said in the talk, these are long design programs. We weren’t designing Zen 6 to be an agentic core. When we started, no one was really even using that term. But Zen 5 was a whole new foundation for AI workloads, and we’ve always believed the core has a place in AI: orchestration, data type manipulation, smaller models. So we were always focused on making the core good at AI. It’s not going to beat a GPU, and that’s not the point. We realized we probably should have some more VNNI, given where we were seeing inference go, and the team rallied around that. It was kind of late, since these are long-lead projects and the floor plan was set, but the team rallied, we were able to squeeze a couple more VNNI pipes in there, and it was a big success.
George Cozma: Again on the vector side, one of the oddities of Zen 5 was that although the wires were all there, the load unit could only do two vector loads at a time. What changed within the Zen 6 core to allow four vector loads, assuming they’re either 128 or 256 bits?
Mike Clark: We can do the 256-bit loads. Since we were able to do two 512-bit loads, we already had 1,024 wires headed over there. Obviously, using them piecemeal versus as one contiguous set complicates the design. I think it’s something we always wanted to do on Zen 5, but with all the change and getting it working, we left that for the Zen 6 team, and they were awesome. We’re all really one team at AMD anyway, and sometimes things intersect at different points in the roadmap. This was the time to do it.
George Cozma: Speaking of bandwidth into the caches, the L2 in Zen 5 had some oddities where it couldn’t always hit 64 bytes per cycle on reads. What changed in Zen 6 to allow that? Because I’ve been able to measure it, and it does hit that.
Mike Clark: Honestly, sometimes you just don’t see some of these things. A lot of it was that we would be over-prefetching, and prefetches that hit in the L2 would be robbing L2 bandwidth. So we made sure we could sustain those prefetches alongside demand requests, reduce the conflicts, and deliver the full 64 bytes per cycle that we want.
George Cozma: Awesome. Circling all the way back to the front: the all-important branch predictor. There seems to have been a reduction in branch misprediction latency, so when you miss a branch, the number of cycles it takes to recover has gone down. What I also noticed, which is really neat, is that there wasn’t any reduction in accuracy. So how did you pull off not only a faster branch predictor, but one that’s even slightly more accurate than Zen 5’s already very accurate predictor?
Mike Clark: The branch prediction team, as you know, is very algorithmic. There’s lots of design space and lots of exploration, and AI has even been getting involved in helping us figure out how to optimize it. It’s a great optimization problem: the number of TAGE tables, how much information they hold, accuracy. So we set out to make a more efficient one. That was an area where we didn’t really want to grow; we thought we could be more efficient in the way we lay out the tables for prediction. And we’re always focused on our pipeline length. No matter how good a branch predictor you build, there are always hard-to-predict branches, and you’re always going to mispredict. So each generation, we’re monomaniacally focused on getting going again as fast as we can when we’re wrong. There are lots of unique tricks we pull to get that redirect back into the front end and get it flowing as fast as possible. And you’ve observed it, which is great.
George Cozma: Yeah. I haven’t had time on Zen 6 yet, but I have had time on Zen 6c, and in SPECint 2026 it matches your fastest Zen 5 server CPU. That’s very impressive, especially considering the clock deficit. Wrapping this all up and moving outside the core into the L3, there are obviously topology differences between the 12-core and 32-core CCDs. How much were you able to mitigate those differences inside the core, building up structures to tolerate latency, to compensate for the slightly increased L3 latency?
Mike Clark: As I always talk about, the design has to be balanced. As we grow the capacity of a structure, the new technology helps it shrink, so the physical distance doesn’t grow linearly, but unfortunately the distance does grow. Inside the L3, there are a lot of tricks I can’t talk about...
George Cozma: The secret sauce!
Mike Clark: [In order to] maintain bandwidth within the L3 mesh so we can keep delivering to the cores. And in the cores, we do grow the queues to handle those higher latencies.
George Cozma: Awesome. My penultimate question: Verano is adding LPDDR5X to the EPYC ecosystem. Were there any design considerations between LPDDR5X and DDR5 that you had to think about when designing the core?
Mike Clark: At the CCX level, we focus a lot on bandwidth per core. I think Mahesh talked a lot about the SDP ports, with 32-byte reads per port, so we try to make sure we have enough bandwidth between the CCD and the IOD. But we were already well provisioned for bandwidth with MRDIMMs, so that construction, the winglets and choosing a different memory technology, didn’t really have a major impact on how we built the core.
George Cozma: Awesome. Usually this is where I’d ask what your favorite type of cheese is, but since we already know that: have you tried any new cheeses lately?
Mike Clark: I have. My new favorite is Cotija cheese.
George Cozma: Oh!
Mike Clark: I’ve been eating a lot of tacos.
George Cozma: You’ve got to love Cotija on tacos. It’s absolutely amazing. Well, thank you so much for sitting down with me. If you like videos like this, hit like and subscribe; it does help with the algorithm. There are PayPal and Patreon links in the description, as well as a transcript of this on the Chips and Cheese website. Thank you so much.
Mike Clark: Thanks for having me, George. I always look forward to coming and talking with you about what this awesome AMD CPU design team has created, and you always have so many good questions. I wish I could answer more, since we do have to protect our secret sauce, but I really enjoy helping you understand some of our thought processes, the challenges we face, and how we’ve been able to overcome them.
George Cozma: Well, it’s always fun to sit down with you and talk, not just about what you’re doing, but also what the team is doing and what you’re looking forward to in the future. So thank you so much.
Mike Clark: Thank you.
George Cozma: I’ll see you in the next one. Have a good one, folks!

====================================================================================================

Libreagi – Save OSS Models
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
14:11:58 UTC
23:11:58 KST

Seeding pays by scarcity
Credit grows with size and time, and a model with one copy earns four times the base rate. The rare ones need the most help.
Open weights, members only
Open models should outlive the places that host them. LibreAGI is a private BitTorrent tracker where members keep copies of the models most likely to disappear.
$ mktorrent -p -s libreagi -l 24 ./model
Any format, from safetensors and GGUF to ONNX, under Apache-2.0, MIT or the Qwen Community License. Each file is checked against its source commit, and staff review every model.

Credit grows with size and time, and a model with one copy earns four times the base rate. The rare ones need the most help.

Nvidia’s Jensen Huang resigns, chipmaker names CFO Colette Kress as interim CEO
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
14:10:34 UTC
23:10:34 KST

Jensen Huang has resigned from his position as president and chief executive of Nvidia, the company said on Thursday, just a few days after he admitted to a romantic affair with an alleged Chinese spy, sparking a U.S. national security crisis.
In a regulatory filing, Nvidia said its board accepted Huang’s resignation on Wednesday.
Nvidia said its current chief financial officer and executive vice president, Colette Kress, will take over as interim CEO until the company finds a permanent successor. Kress will continue to serve as the company’s CFO.
Nvidia’s shares were down nearly 12% in early morning trading on Thursday. Since Friday, the company’s stock has fallen by as much as 19%, resulting in a market cap loss of more than $855 billion.
Huang founded Nvidia in 1993 and has led it as CEO ever since. Under his leadership, Nvidia played a crucial role in developing the graphics processing unit (GPU), a specialized chip that does graphics processing for PCs, as we know it today. The chips were not an immediate hit, and the early days of Nvidia were characterized by struggle and layoffs, but eventually, it struck on a GPU design that performed so well, it became the standard for graphics processing.
These chips also turned out to be extremely good at doing large numbers of calculations simultaneously, which has made them popular for computation-intensive tasks like crypto mining, and more recently, training and running neural networks, which are what AI models are at their core.
Nvidia was initially focused on making GPUs aimed at gaming, but it had already moved into general-purpose computing by 2006 with the launch of the CUDA architecture, which lets developers use the chips for other tasks, such as doing calculations and research. After AI researchers began using the company’s GPUs to train neural networks over a decade ago, Huang leaned in toward developing chips for AI and then data centers, and grew it from there into its dominant position.
Under Huang, the company has been by far the biggest benefactor of the AI industry’s skyrocketing growth, which has largely depended on Nvidia’s GPUs for training and running large language models. Before ChatGPT launched and sparked the AI boom, Nvidia shares were trading at about $15. As of last month, they had risen more than 1,450% to an all-time high of $243. Its market cap consequently has also risen by an eye-watering $5.5 trillion since then, reaching as high as $5.88 trillion early in October.
Revenue, of course, has also ballooned, rising more than 1,500% to $96.22 billion as of the company’s second quarter, from $5.93 billion in Q3 2023, right before the ChatGPT moment. Profits increased nearly 88 times in that period to $59.69 billion as of July 2026, from $680 million in October 2023.
That success was predicated in large part by the AI industry’s continued development, which Huang seemingly made his personal mission. He traveled to conferences and events around the world to promote his products and AI, lobbied extensively for looser regulation after the Biden administration placed export controls on the sale of high-power AI chips to China, and cultivated a close relationship with Trump, often to Nvidia’s benefit.
The resignation and controversy come at a crucial moment for Nvidia. China was always a big market for the company, though its impact on the company’s finances has shrunk in recent years due to strict U.S. export controls and an aggressive push by Beijing for domestic alternatives. Chinese customers accounted for revenue of about $7.9 billion in the company’s second quarter, roughly 8% of total revenue.
Repercussions galore
The potential implications of Huang’s departure for the AI and data center industries are vast, given how active Huang, and by extension Nvidia, has been in furthering the AI boom. In particular, it raises questions about the future of Nvidia’s sprawling AI investments, which have helped bankroll everything from startups developing new technologies to the enormous data centers needed to run them.
Under Huang, Nvidia deftly used its financial muscle to support companies buying its chips, effectively helping to finance the market for its own products, as well as lobbying Washington to relax potential rules around AI and data center regulation.
As of the end of its second quarter, Nvidia reported $99 billion worth of investments and another $25 billion of commitments. It also has $36 billion in cloud service commitments, which are arrangements that help AI cloud operators finance the GPUs and data center equipment they buy from Nvidia, with the chipmaker saying it will buy compute from them and potentially earn a share of their operators’ revenue.
Nvidia has also been investing directly in neoclouds, the companies serving a lot of the AI processing needs. In January, the company invested $2 billion in CoreWeave, and another $2 billion in Nebius in March. The chipmaker was said to be considering being a guarantor for Nscale’s planned West Virginia data center project. It’s also signed up to provide up to $105 billion worth of credit guarantees for an OpenAI data center campus in Ohio.
Nvidia’s also a major investor in the labs building the AI models that run on its GPUs, putting $30 billion into OpenAI, and $10 billion into Anthropic. It’s also backed Mira Murati’s Thinking Machines Lab as part of a deal involving at least 1 GW of its Vera Rubin GPU systems. The chipmaker has also invested $3.5 billion in MediaTek to help the Taiwanese company develop custom AI chips that work with Nvidia’s systems.
Many investors have grown uneasy with the enormous amounts of debt companies in the space were raising to sustain the AI infrastructure buildout, and Huang’s exit throws another uncertainty-shaped wrench into the works. CoreWeave’s shares were down about 9% on Thursday, reversing part of a 25% rally on Wednesday, while Nebius is down about 16%. IREN fell 6.6%.
Nvidia’s board has also appointed Harvey C. Jones, a managing partner of Square Wave Ventures and former CEO of Synopsys, as the company’s first-ever chair of the board of directors. Jones has been on Nvidia’s board since 1993. As chair, Jones is likely to play a central role in Nvidia’s search for a permanent CEO to replace Huang.
Huang is the single largest personal shareholder at Nvidia, but he owns only about 3.5% of the company’s shares. Unlike many of Silicon Valley’s tech giants, he does not hold supervoting rights, and the board had the power to vote him out at any time.
Huang famously ran a very flat organization, with around 60 direct reports. There was no heir apparent or succession plan either (although two of his children also worked in executive roles at the company). Huang famously maintained tight control over the company he founded, making it challenging for Nvidia to find a path forward without him.
The industry will get its first glimpse of that next week during the quarterly conference call. Nvidia reports earnings on Tuesday.
“Jensen’s contributions to NVIDIA are profound. NVIDIA is one of the most consequential technology companies in the world. The Board and the company thank Jensen for his immeasurable contributions to the company and the industry,” the company said in a statement.
Note: This story was updated to add details about Nvidia’s growth and Huang’s efforts to promote AI development, as well as its focus on China.

====================================================================================================

Electric Ship Batteries Promise Compact, Cooler Power
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
14:06:03 UTC
23:06:03 KST

Electric ships are a modest business. Most estimates put the global market at under US $5 billion in 2025. But the business is growing, and it is drawing more battery makers. The latest is Xing Mobility, of Taoyuan City, Taiwan. In September, at the SMM Hamburg maritime trade fair, it unveiled its Immersio CTP pack. The pack submerges every cell in a fluid that absorbs heat without conducting electricity but is, notably, flammable.
Xing is not the first to pursue that idea. Kreisel Electric, of Rainbach im Mühlkreis, Austria, won certification for an immersion-cooled marine battery pack in 2023. Its batteries are now powering the P-12 hydrofoil ferry built by Candela, of Stockholm. Wattalps, of Moirans, France, received an approval for its marine batteries in January 2026 from Bureau Veritas, of Neuilly-sur-Seine, France. Xing’s pitch is density. The company says its CTP stores up to twice the energy of conventional marine batteries. It also says its immersion scheme keeps one overheating cell from igniting the rest.
Those claims rest mostly on laboratory data. Only two vessels run on Xing packs, and neither uses the CTP. And the pack has yet to gain approval from DNV, a ship-equipment certifier in Høvik, Norway. Meanwhile, European Union emissions rules are pushing shipowners toward battery power. That pressure makes Xing’s claims worth examining, starting with the one about energy doubling.
Does the Xing CTP Battery Really Double Energy Density?
Most packs hold cells in metal module boxes inside a larger enclosure. The CTP, short for cell-to-pack, omits both layers. A molded composite holder grips the cells, channels the coolant, and forms the structure. “Everything is integrated into one piece,” says Royce Hong, Xing’s founder and CEO. Less structure means less dead weight for each kilowatt-hour stored.
Cedric Lin, senior engineering manager at Xing, says the CTP stores about 200 watt-hours per kilogram in lab tests. He says that pack-level figure includes the coolant and battery-management system. The CTP uses cylindrical 21700 cells, mainly nickel manganese cobalt or nickel cobalt aluminum. Both store more energy per kilogram than the lithium iron phosphate (LFP) packs that Xing used for comparison in its calculations for making its doubling claim.
A more competitive metric is presented by the market leader: Corvus Energy, of Bergen, Norway, which has supplied more than 1,300 maritime projects. Corvus Energy’s data sheets list 168 Wh/kg for its Dolphin NxtGen Energy system. Xing’s CTP beats that, but by only 19 percent (and only according to measurements made in a lab).
Xing Mobility’s batteries are being used in the Porrima P111, the largest solar-powered catamaran in the world.Porrima
The harder test is power. Ships draw heavily, sometimes draining a battery in as little as 20 minutes. Fast discharge, like fast charging, generates a lot of heat. Whether lab results hold at sea depends on how the CTP sheds that heat, and on what happens when a cell fails.
Immersion Cooling Versus Cold Plates
Most marine batteries shed heat through cold plates, metal plates with tiny channels that carry coolant past the cells. But heat must first cross the cell casing, thermal paste, and plate wall. Each layer slows the transfer. Submerging every cell removes those layers. Paul A. Kohl, a chemical and biomolecular engineering professor at Georgia Tech, in Atlanta, backs that reasoning. “Liquid immersion is about as good as heat transfer gets,” he says.
That advantage matters most in thermal runaway, when one damaged cell overheats and ignites its neighbors. Xing’s documentation says immersion can cool a punctured cell from about 1,000 °C to below 100 °C within 10 seconds. Kohl finds that plausible: “It’s well within the realm of feasibility,” he says.
Plausible is not proved, though. Lin says Xing has tested the CTP many times to IEC 62619, an international safety standard for industrial lithium batteries. Those tests ran in laboratories certified by the testing firms TÜV and Intertek, and they tracked cell temperatures over time. Lin did not share the results, nor did he say whether the testers confirmed the 10-second figure.
The tests also leave open a question about the oil itself. Kohl says most immersion systems he has seen use fluorocarbons or silicones, which resist burning. Wattalps, for example, uses a nonflammable fluid. Xing’s nickel-rich cells raise the stakes, because they enter thermal runaway more readily than LFP cells. A failing cell can generate sparks and release oxygen, Kohl explains, and a pack holds a lot of oil. “Using a flammable liquid, of course, you’re throwing away part of the advantage of the immersion battery,” he says.
Longevity is a second question. Lin says the oil lasts as long as the battery, because packs run near room temperature. Kohl is less sure. Over a decade, the fluid heats and cools thousands of times, and oils slowly break down. At sea, water and salt can also contaminate it. “Ten years’ reliability and no degradation is a very demanding requirement,” Kohl says.
What Matters in a Hull Is Space, Not Weight
Xing also touts the compact nature of its CTP system. Aboard a ship, battery volume matters more than mass. Every cubic meter filled by a battery fill is one lost to cargo or passengers. Lin says the packs that Xing now mass-produces store 330 to 350 watt-hours per liter. For comparison, Corvus lists 212.5 Wh/L for its Dolphin NxtGen Energy system.
Xing credits the design. Because the CTP needs no cold plates, cooling pipes, or module boxes, its cells can be packed more tightly. The company also argues that immersion contains failures well enough to place cells and whole packs closer than usual. Kohl says tighter packing makes the doubling claim feasible—if the fluid reliably stops fire from spreading.
Xing has submitted the CTP design to DNV for document review and has begun preparing for physical tests. Hong says DNV approval matters most on large passenger ferries, where many lives are at stake. Smaller vessels and some cargo ships can install the CTP sooner, he says. So, the pack’s first voyages will likely come on small boats carrying few people. Those trips will finally expose Xing’s numbers to salt air, constant vibration, and months of heat cycles. Until then, Kohl’s verdict on the cooling claim fits the whole pitch: plausible, but not yet proved.
Willie Jones is an associate editor at IEEE Spectrum. In addition to editing and planning daily coverage, he manages several of Spectrum's newsletters and contributes regularly to the monthly Big Picture section that appears in the print edition.

====================================================================================================

Jane Street – Our software engineering interviews are changing (a little)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
14:05:28 UTC
23:05:28 KST

Over the last few years, LLMs have changed how software gets built and that’s no different for us at Jane Street. Most of our engineers now use them every day not only for writing code, but also for reading, debugging, and reviewing code, and plenty of things that aren’t code at all.
We’ve talked about this recently in a podcast on how we use AI in our development, and in a post on formal methods that touches on how agentic coding is reshaping the way we work.
Our coding interviews haven’t changed, though. The questions we ask software engineers today are the same style of question we’ve been asking for years: sit down with an interviewer or two, program through a problem together, and talk as you go.
That style has served us well and the key goals remain the same: we want to see how you solve problems, structure your work, communicate, and collaborate in the pursuit of excellent software development. But the shape of the job has evolved, and we think our interviews should evolve, too. As generating code has become arguably cheaper than ever, other parts of the job have come to matter even more: taste about what to build, choosing abstractions that last, and making sure what got built is actually correct, among other things.
So this summer we started trialing some new kinds of interviews in our onsites for campus candidates. Broadly, they involve less code-writing, and lean more higher-level, focusing on software design, on verifying the quality, structure, and intention of code, and on using LLMs to produce code. Most rounds will continue to be traditional coding interviews, conducted without LLM assistance.
We know this sounds vague, but that’s intentional: the formats are still evolving, and we’d rather not describe something in detail today that looks different in six months.
A few things worth knowing:
- Not every onsite includes these new rounds yet, and when you come on-site we’ll give you a heads-up about what to expect.
- The rest of our process is unchanged. If you’ve read our earlier posts about interviewing here, they’re still accurate.
- While we find LLMs incredibly valuable as part of our daily work, we also recognize that not everyone has deep experience working with them. You shouldn’t feel like you need to have a highly worked out methodology for working with agents in order to do well in our process. In the end, we’re looking for people with great instincts for building software, and we don’t feel like we need an LLM to evaluate that.
- Different folks have integrated LLMs into their workflows in different ways and to different degrees, and we don’t expect you to have a particular setup or to use them for everything. What we care about is that you think clearly about the tools you use: what they’re good at, where they fall short, and when to reach for something else.
- As has been the case for some time, our more experienced candidates’ interviews will continue to be more varied, featuring a range of interview styles, from producing software to designing systems and discussing prior work.
As always, we’ll keep iterating, and we’ll write more when there’s more to say.
If you’re interested in working with us, apply on our website!

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigns after reports of extramarital relationships
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
14:00:54 UTC
23:00:54 KST

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has resigned following reports that he had multiple extramarital relationships, including one with a suspected agent of China and another with an executive who reported to him — a stunning turn of events for the head of a company at the heart of the global AI investment boom.
Colette Kress, the company’s chief financial officer, will become interim CEO, Nvidia’s board said in a statement.
Nvidia shares plunged more than 11% after the board’s announcement. A company spokesperson clarified that Huang will not be working with NVIDIA in any capacity going forward.
The company also named its first chairman. That role will be filled by Harvey Jones, who is a former CEO of major chip company Synopsys. The addition of a chairman is a major governance overhaul at the company that for decades relied solely on Huang.
Nvidia’s board met Wednesday night to address Huang’s future at the company, a source familiar with the matter told NBC News, adding that the White House was aware of the meeting.
Huang, 63, co-founded Nvidia in the early 1990s, and led the company’s meteoric rise amid the AI boom of the past several years.
“I believe deeply in this company and in its future,” Kress wrote in her first email to Nvidia employees as interim CEO. “The Board, Estaff and I recognize that this has been an extraordinarily difficult period and I want you to know how grateful we are for your resilience, professionalism and continued dedication. Thank you.”
In a separate note to the company’s workforce, newly-installed chairman Harvey Jones wrote, “The Board takes seriously our duty as stewards of this great company.”
FOR SUBSCRIBERSThe price of baby formula just set a dubious record
FOR SUBSCRIBERSHow measles surged in the U.S.
FOR SUBSCRIBERSWhy Jensen Huang just might survive this scandal
FOR SUBSCRIBERSThe price of baby formula just set a dubious record
FOR SUBSCRIBERSHow measles surged in the U.S.
FOR SUBSCRIBERSWhy Jensen Huang just might survive this scandal
In Collette Kress, Nvidia’s board selected a steady hand to steer the company in its most uncertain period ever.
Kress has been the company’s chief financial officer since 2013. She is a familiar face to Wall Street, regularly appearing at conferences and discussing the intricacies of the company’s sprawling operations. Before Nvidia, Kress worked at Cisco, another major technology company, and Microsoft.
Huang long leaned on Kress during financial conferences and the company’s earnings calls to debrief the investing public about the latest developments with the company’s operations.
Nvidia’s board said that it would search for a permanent CEO and consider internal and external candidates.
Trump weighed in on the search for a permanent CEO shortly after Nvidia’s announcement, saying that Apple executive chair Tim Cook should take the role. “A Legend replaces a Legend!” Trump wrote.
Cook only became Apple’s executive chair on September 1, and did not appear to have plans to leave any time soon.
Kress’ ascension to the CEO suite comes after the second largest public company in the world was hit with multiple scandals involving its founder.
On Wednesday, The New York Times, citing five people who worked at Nvidia, reported that Huang had an extramarital relationship with a married healthcare executive who reported to him, which appears to run afoul of Nvidia’s code of conduct. NBC News has not independently verified the claims.
Nvidia and the executive did not respond to multiple requests for comment Wednesday about the Times’ report.
The report concerning the alleged relationship followed reports that Huang had an affair with Alice Huang, a businesswoman believed to be a spy for China. The two Huangs are not related.
Secretary of State Marco Rubio circulated a memo to officials in the Trump administration this spring outlining how U.S. intelligence showed that Huang was in a romantic relationship with a business executive who was an asset of China’s spy service, according to a person familiar with Nvidia and a former national security official with knowledge of the matter. The New York Times and the Financial Times, citing people familiar with the matter, first reported on the memo last week.
Alice Huang held a senior position at Megaspeed, a Singapore-based company that was buying advanced chips from Nvidia. Megaspeed and other related firms remain under U.S. criminal investigation of allegations they smuggled American-made chips into China.
Alice Huang’s precise role and relationship with China’s spy service is unclear. It is possible she was not a career spy for China’s spy agency, the Ministry of State Security, but was asked to cooperate and share information based on her access to Jensen Huang, according to the former national security official.
The concerns about the Nvidia CEO’s extramarital activity added to worries about Nvidia chips being smuggled or illegally accessed by Chinese AI companies. Nvidia has said that it follows U.S. export laws.
On Sunday, Nvidia’s board announced it was investigating whether Huang had fallen victim to a foreign intelligence plot. It said Tuesday that it had hired a former acting U.S. attorney and his law firm to conduct the investigation.
Huang apologized in a memo to employees and said he supported the independent investigation.
“I was unfaithful to my wife,” he wrote. “My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry.”
Multiple Washington officials have called for greater scrutiny of Nvidia and for Huang to testify about his relationship with the suspected Chinese agent in the wake of the reports.
China has denied the allegations, calling them “purely unsubstantiated and directed at smearing China.”
Even before Wednesday’s Times report, Nvidia shares had plunged 11% this week, wiping out nearly $500 billion in paper value. Huang’s departure leaves a host of unknowns at the company, the valuation of which has soared to more than $1 trillion amid insatiable demand for its GPU chips. Those specialized processors underpin the AI applications that have powered the stock market and economic growth writ large to new heights.
For years, Huang has effectively been the chief ambassador for the artificial intelligence industry, appearing regularly alongside heads of state and other CEOs as the industry spends trillions of dollars investing and building AI models and data centers.
Nvidia acknowledged Huang’s contributions to the sector in its Thursday announcement. “Jensen’s contributions to NVIDIA are profound,” a statement said. “The Board and the company thank Jensen for his immeasurable contributions to the company and the industry.”
After coming up with the idea for Nvidia at a Denny’s restaurant in Silicon Valley with co-founders Chris Malachowsky and Curtis Priem, Huang built the company into a $4 trillion global chip behemoth over the following 30-plus years.
Before Wednesday’s report of a second affair, many high-profile figures across Silicon Valley, Wall Street and Washington had come to Huang’s defense. President Donald Trump told reporters Sunday that Huang had “done an amazing job for our country.”
“I think he’s an honorable guy,” Trump said. “I think he probably has an excuse for it. You have to hear it.”
Shaun Maguire, a partner at the venture capital group Sequoia, said on X that Huang is “one of the greatest entrepreneurs of this generation,” adding that “two mistakes don’t make a right, and I believe Jensen is irreplaceable for America’s AI leadership.”
SpaceX and Tesla CEO Elon Musk responded under Maguire’s post, writing: “I agree.”
Before the decision, Musk said in a response to a post Wednesday night that it “would be an honor” to hire Huang.

Cook only became Apple’s executive chair on September 1, and did not appear to have plans to leave any time soon.
Kress’ ascension to the CEO suite comes after the second largest public company in the world was hit with multiple scandals involving its founder.
On Wednesday, The New York Times, citing five people who worked at Nvidia, reported that Huang had an extramarital relationship with a married healthcare executive who reported to him, which appears to run afoul of Nvidia’s code of conduct. NBC News has not independently verified the claims.
Nvidia and the executive did not respond to multiple requests for comment Wednesday about the Times’ report.
The report concerning the alleged relationship followed reports that Huang had an affair with Alice Huang, a businesswoman believed to be a spy for China. The two Huangs are not related.
Secretary of State Marco Rubio circulated a memo to officials in the Trump administration this spring outlining how U.S. intelligence showed that Huang was in a romantic relationship with a business executive who was an asset of China’s spy service, according to a person familiar with Nvidia and a former national security official with knowledge of the matter. The New York Times and the Financial Times, citing people familiar with the matter, first reported on the memo last week.
Alice Huang held a senior position at Megaspeed, a Singapore-based company that was buying advanced chips from Nvidia. Megaspeed and other related firms remain under U.S. criminal investigation of allegations they smuggled American-made chips into China.
Alice Huang’s precise role and relationship with China’s spy service is unclear. It is possible she was not a career spy for China’s spy agency, the Ministry of State Security, but was asked to cooperate and share information based on her access to Jensen Huang, according to the former national security official.
The concerns about the Nvidia CEO’s extramarital activity added to worries about Nvidia chips being smuggled or illegally accessed by Chinese AI companies. Nvidia has said that it follows U.S. export laws.
On Sunday, Nvidia’s board announced it was investigating whether Huang had fallen victim to a foreign intelligence plot. It said Tuesday that it had hired a former acting U.S. attorney and his law firm to conduct the investigation.
Huang apologized in a memo to employees and said he supported the independent investigation.
“I was unfaithful to my wife,” he wrote. “My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry.”
Multiple Washington officials have called for greater scrutiny of Nvidia and for Huang to testify about his relationship with the suspected Chinese agent in the wake of the reports.
China has denied the allegations, calling them “purely unsubstantiated and directed at smearing China.”
Even before Wednesday’s Times report, Nvidia shares had plunged 11% this week, wiping out nearly $500 billion in paper value. Huang’s departure leaves a host of unknowns at the company, the valuation of which has soared to more than $1 trillion amid insatiable demand for its GPU chips. Those specialized processors underpin the AI applications that have powered the stock market and economic growth writ large to new heights.
For years, Huang has effectively been the chief ambassador for the artificial intelligence industry, appearing regularly alongside heads of state and other CEOs as the industry spends trillions of dollars investing and building AI models and data centers.
Nvidia acknowledged Huang’s contributions to the sector in its Thursday announcement. “Jensen’s contributions to NVIDIA are profound,” a statement said. “The Board and the company thank Jensen for his immeasurable contributions to the company and the industry.”
After coming up with the idea for Nvidia at a Denny’s restaurant in Silicon Valley with co-founders Chris Malachowsky and Curtis Priem, Huang built the company into a $4 trillion global chip behemoth over the following 30-plus years.
Before Wednesday’s report of a second affair, many high-profile figures across Silicon Valley, Wall Street and Washington had come to Huang’s defense. President Donald Trump told reporters Sunday that Huang had “done an amazing job for our country.”
“I think he’s an honorable guy,” Trump said. “I think he probably has an excuse for it. You have to hear it.”
Shaun Maguire, a partner at the venture capital group Sequoia, said on X that Huang is “one of the greatest entrepreneurs of this generation,” adding that “two mistakes don’t make a right, and I believe Jensen is irreplaceable for America’s AI leadership.”
SpaceX and Tesla CEO Elon Musk responded under Maguire’s post, writing: “I agree.”
Before the decision, Musk said in a response to a post Wednesday night that it “would be an honor” to hire Huang.
