Apple’s HomeView is my new favorite smart display — but not my daughter’s
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
14:00:17 UTC
23:00:17 KST

The HomeView nails everything I want from a smart display. It’s superb at controlling my smart home; it’s an excellent hands-free kitchen companion, a good speaker, and a gorgeous digital photo frame. It’s also the first Apple smart speaker with Siri AI, which is an improvement, if still slow at times. But Apple has fallen into the trap that hampers other smart displays I’ve tested: not content with doing its core functions well, it tries to do more, and that’s where the friction creeps in.
Apple’s HomeView is my new favorite smart display — but not my daughter’s
Apple’s first smart display is a gorgeous, speedy smart home hub and a great kitchen companion. It’s when it tries to be the family iPhone that things get awkward.
If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.
Got questions? Ask away.
We’re hosting a subscriber-exclusive AMA all about Apple’s HomeView today at 2PM ET. Drop questions here!
The special Apple sauce here is that HomeView is both a smart display everyone in your home can use and an extension of your iPhone or iPad, providing access to your texts, calendar, and reminders when your phone is at home and on the same Wi-Fi network. This makes it a communal device with personal data, and switching between the two is where HomeView stumbles. Clunky authentication checks and being sent to my iPhone too many times interrupt the hands-free experience. Not to mention that I could easily read my 15-year-old daughter’s text messages, and vice versa.
Her verdict? “I’ll just use my iPhone.” But my largely tech-averse husband fell in love with HomeView, and immediately asked for one for his office.
Apple HomeView
The Good
- Speedy smart home control
- Excellent guided cooking
- Siri AI is helpful in the home
- Gorgeous customizable faces
- Great sound for its size
The Bad
- I could read my daughter’s text messages
- Siri AI can be slow
- The app / widget situation is funky
- Wall mounts cost (a lot) extra
For households deep in the Apple ecosystem, especially those with younger kids who only need access to lights and music and aren’t interested in your text messages, this may be the first smart display your whole family will actually use. But for some, the idea of their texts appearing on a large screen in the middle of the kitchen will send them running.
Apple is late to the game here. Amazon and Google have had smart displays for close to a decade. But the company has debuted with a product that elevates the category in nearly every way — with a few quirks.
Polished and surprisingly small
The HomeView is much smaller than I expected, but it’s a great size for my needs. The star of the device is a 7-inch square touchscreen that displays a gorgeous collection of “faces” that I can customize to fit wherever I put it — the kitchen counter, bedside table, or the wall. It comes in tabletop and wall-mounted versions, and both cost $299, making it Apple’s least expensive screened device after the Apple Watch SE.
The tabletop HomeView fits neatly on my kitchen counter and is light enough to move around for cooking, or when my husband wants to check his schedule over breakfast. The long USB-C cable allows plenty of maneuverability, though after a few days of spinning, it did get a bit twisty.
The screen is clear and bright and looks good in most lighting situations. It’s large enough for reading messages, following a recipe, and video calling while in the kitchen. It’s super responsive, too, showing no lag or stuttering. There’s also a Center Stage camera above the display for taking a FaceTime call while moving around, staying in frame in every corner of my small kitchen. Callers could hear me clearly, although the farther away I was, the more tinny it started to sound.
Sound is very good for the size, with speakers on both the display and in the base of the tabletop model. It’s close to a HomePod Mini 2, not as room-filling, but with a touch more bass. Playing music on the wall-mounted model wasn’t as much of a downgrade as I expected in a device with one less speaker; it was richer and cleaner than on the Echo Show 15 wall-mounted in the same entryway. Siri responded well to voice commands using the device’s microphones, and as long as I remembered to put my phone face down if it was nearby, Siri replied from the correct device.
The one hardware miss is physical buttons: there aren’t any. When I want to adjust the volume quickly or mute Siri, I have to tap the display or pull out my phone.
I have a good spot on my kitchen counter for HomeView, but if you don’t, wall mounting is an option. Apple sent me two mounts for this review, both from Schneider Electric: a USB-C wall mount ($59.99) with a 2-meter / 6.5-foot cable and neat cable management, and a hardwired AC mount ($79.99). There will also be a power-over-Ethernet option for $129.99. The USB-C mount is easy to install: just two screws in the wall, then the display snaps on with magnets so strong that no one will accidentally knock it off the wall.
However, the wall-mounted HomeView ends up costing more than the tabletop version because it’s priced the same but doesn’t include a mount or a power adaptor. You have to buy those separately, and with Apple’s $39 brick, you’re starting at around $400 (until the inevitable third-party options arrive). That’s an extra $100 for a device with fewer speakers. It’s a weird move; Apple should have included a wall mount and adapter or dropped the price.
Superb in the smart home
HomeView nails the smart display essentials: controlling my smart home, monitoring my video doorbell, providing hands-free help in the kitchen, playing music and podcasts, and displaying photos.
Apple put a Thread radio and Matter controller in the HomeView, so it acts as an Apple Home hub, the first with an always-on screen. The Home app is infused throughout the device’s software, giving you multiple ways to control connected smart lights, thermostats, locks, and cameras. This is where HomeView shines. It can connect to any HomeKit or compatible Matter smart device, and it’s impressively fast at controlling them, turning on the lights in my entryway as quickly as a regular light switch. It also offers tons of options for customizing its controls.
I set up the device’s Smart Home face with a large button to turn the lights on and off in the hallway it was in, plus smaller buttons for my Goodnight and Good Morning scenes, along with a live view of the Aqara video doorbell, and controls for my front door lock and Nest thermostat. In the morning, as I walk past, I hit the Good Morning scene and the house wakes up; then I do the reverse at night. Even better, my husband, who has never once opened the Home app, now turns on the lights from the HomeView every morning.
The Camera face, which streams up to nine HomeKit Secure Video cameras at once, is also very fast, and when someone rings the video doorbell, HomeView pulls up a live feed instantly. I can then unlock the door right from the display. I love that I can add multiple Smart Home and Camera faces in different configurations, giving me swipeable access to all the controls I want.
Smart home is my favorite use case, but I also loved cooking with HomeView. You can pull up a recipe on your phone and, using Handoff, send it to the HomeView, which brings up a guided cooking experience. This feature elicited several “Wow, that’s really cool” reactions from visitors (and my editor!).
It was easy to follow the on-screen steps while cooking, as well as use Siri AI for help. While viewing a Creamy Tuscan Chicken recipe, I tapped the cherry tomatoes and said, “Siri, what can I substitute for this?” It suggested some useful options and some not-so-good ones (cucumbers?). I also liked how the HomeView automatically inserted timers into steps like “Cook the chicken for about 8 minutes on each side.” Tapping the timers popped them up into Live Activities at the top of the screen, making it easy to switch back and forth between them and the recipe.
This switching with Live Activities also works for phone calls and music, two things I used HomeView for a lot. Answering a call on the large display while cooking is much easier than reaching for my iPhone, and the audio is loud and clear, so I could keep cooking while chatting from anywhere in the room.
Music and audio playback are great; it’s nice to see album art while blasting Taylor Swift, and the screen makes it easy to pause, skip, or navigate to a new song, album, or playlist. The built-in speaker is good, but I prefer to AirPlay to compatible speakers, and I like that the HomeView remembers which I used last so I don’t have to select them each time.
There are some disappointments. The HomeView has a built-in temperature and humidity sensor, like its HomePod siblings, but it can’t use its onboard 12MP camera as a motion sensor to trigger an automation. HomeView can also only control devices compatible with Apple Home, so that means no Ring doorbells or Nest cameras (without workarounds), or categories like lawnmowers or appliances that Apple Home doesn’t support. I also couldn’t get it to run some of the Siri shortcuts I use to have the voice assistant control unsupported devices. When I say “Hey Siri, feed Smokey” to any of my HomePods, they can trigger my Aqara pet feeder, but HomeView told me it couldn’t do that.
Apple Home still isn’t powerful enough for advanced home setups with complicated automations or a robust home energy management system; if that’s you, you’re probably already using Home Assistant or Homey and could bring your setup into a HomeView. Which you may want to do, because HomeView’s simple, intuitive interface is great for everyone else in your home. For example, my long-suffering spouse, who just wants a way to turn on the smart lights or adjust the thermostat without pulling out his phone, asking a smart speaker, or having me do it for him.
A little too personal
The other big promise of HomeView is to act as a family hub, with everyone’s personal data easily accessible on a communal device in the home while staying separate for privacy. HomeView uses face and voice recognition to show up to six household members their calendars, messages, and calls — in theory, only to them when they’re in the home. If you have an Apple Intelligence-enabled iPhone, it can also answer personal context requests.
In practice, HomeView recognized me instantly, and I could swipe up from the home face to read my texts and access my calendar without any delay. But when it came to switching between profiles, it was too easy to access family members’ personal content. When I tested it with my teenage daughter, HomeView let me stay on her profile and scroll through her texts several times — even though it recognized me and popped up a prompt asking if I wanted to switch.
While this didn’t happen every time, it happened often enough to be a problem, mostly when I used the device right after her. There’s a grace period where the device stays on your content even after you’ve moved away; and if I hopped onto the device within five seconds or so of my daughter leaving, I could sometimes scroll for up to 10 seconds before it checked again and locked me out. (This was purely for testing purposes and with her consent!)
The other time we could reliably read each other’s content was when we were sitting side by side at the kitchen counter near the device. If my daughter was using it, walked away, and sat on the couch behind us, I could scroll through her messages for as long as I wanted because the camera still saw her in the room and didn’t switch to me, even though I was closer to it.
Swiping out of Messages to a face and leaving the room was the most reliable way to protect our stuff — but it wasn’t foolproof. (Watch my video review for more on this.)
I was impressed at how well HomeView switched between us on busy mornings. (Yes, my household does sometimes resemble that HomeView ad, minus the hole in the counter.) My daughter asked when her school project was due, then I jumped in to ask when to leave for the airport. HomeView answered each of us correctly, pulling from our respective calendars and texts — though we had to swivel the screen to face us, since it didn’t always recognize us by just voice. We could have used our phones, but HomeView’s large screen and louder speaker made hands-free use easier.
HomeView was stricter with phone calls than text messages, ringing only when I was directly in front of it. When my phone rang while I was on the other side of the counter, HomeView stayed silent until I spun it around.
This flow scuppers my hopes of using it as a family phone or putting it by my bed so I could banish my iPhone (and TikTok) to the living room at night. This is because it doesn’t show text notifications and won’t ring if it can’t see me, meaning I might miss a late-night call or text from my college-age son or teenage daughter.
Siri AI lands in the home
Tying all of these features together is Siri AI, which can answer general knowledge questions, control smart home devices, play music for you, and tap into personal context. I also didn’t have to repeat the wake word constantly; the Siri bubble animation on the screen clearly indicates when it’s still listening, so I could keep talking. Siri AI on the HomeView is an improvement over the older Siri on the HomePods, especially for controlling my home, and it’s mercifully much less verbose than its competitors. But it can be frustratingly slow to pull up personal context answers, sometimes makes mistakes, and regularly errors out. It is still in beta, so hopefully things will improve.
For smart home requests, Siri is very fast, since those run locally. It now responds to conversational requests like “dim the lights in here,” then “make it a bit dimmer,” making it easier to control devices. After it executes a voice command, it shows the accessory on the screen for additional control, which is handy.
I could also create automations with voice, such as “Hey Siri, open the shades in my office at 8AM every morning” and chain commands like “turn on the lights in here and in the mudroom.” But these only work with one device type at a time, so I couldn’t control lights and the shades with one ask.
I found Siri AI Personal Context handy in several instances, and it’s a feature that makes HomeView stand out from the competition — neither Alexa nor Google offers anything as deep. But here Siri can be slow, because it relies on pulling from your phone, and it struggles with anything taxing, like multiple requests in a row from different people. It frequently exclaims, “Uh oh, something’s wrong,” and gives up.
Then there was the time Siri tried to send my husband in the wrong direction. He asked it about his weekend schedule, and it read out the events in his calendar at the Georgia Bushcraft Fall Gathering he was heading to. When he asked how long it would take to get there, it confidently told him one hour and 47 minutes. He then asked it to show him directions, and it couldn’t because it didn’t actually know the address. Siri had guessed the location based on something in the event that said “Dogwood Stage,” and had made up the travel time. The gathering was actually a five-hour drive away. The event’s address was in his email, but Siri didn’t connect those dots.
When he prompted it to check his email, it found the correct address and calculated the drive time, but couldn’t pull up the route because there’s no Maps app on the HomeView. He asked it to send the route to his phone, and it said: “Done.” But all it sent him was a message with the drive time, no map attached. When he made the same request on his iPhone, it worked great. “It’s like your iPhone, but not as good” isn’t a great pitch here.
Despite these hiccups, I did find myself using Siri AI on HomeView a lot more than I do on my phone, where touch is my first and easiest instinct.
Beyond the basics
Beyond the core functions of a smart display, HomeView starts to struggle.
The built-in apps and widgets, which include Music, Calendar, Weather, Timers, Safari, and Intercom, work well. But if you want to use any other apps or widgets, they have to come from your phone, with some strange restrictions. They only work when your phone is at home, and only audio and video apps that support CarPlay and iPhone widgets that are compatible with the Mac show up. Because widgets point to apps that aren’t on the HomeView, they only work for glanceable info at best — tapping most of them gets you nowhere.
This means the New York Times and Apple News apps that appeared on the device were just podcast feeds, not news apps, and the Ring widget just told me to use my iPhone. A few of the widgets I tested offered some on-screen info, and some could perform one or two pre-programmed actions — there’s potential here, but it’s frustrating on day one. The only video app that works on the HomeView is Apple TV, but the screen is letterboxed to essentially iPhone size, so I wouldn’t recommend anyone buy this for a video-watching experience.
Safari is a workaround, and it’s not a terrible experience. I could browse websites by asking Siri to pull up The Verge or The New York Times in Safari, and I could log in to Netflix and YouTube using the pop-up keyboard. While I won’t be binge-watching the last season of Killing Eve on this, a Gordon Ramsay steak tutorial on YouTube while I cook, works fine but isn’t noticeably better than an iPhone.
There’s also no Maps or Notes app — and I felt their absence. A share sheet and the ability to copy and paste from Safari are also MIA, so sharing a grocery list or texting a link meant going back to my phone. HomeView made me do that a lot, which underscores how many features of this product are an extension of my iPhone rather than a standalone device.
How HomeView compares to the Echo Show and Nest Hub
I use and test all three major smart home platforms — Google Home, Amazon Alexa, and Apple Home. After a week with HomeView and Siri AI, I rank Alexa Plus as the best smart home assistant, Gemini for Home as the smartest assistant, and HomeView as the best smart display hardware and personal assistant.
Apple’s 7-inch square screen fits nicely into my home, and while it has similar dimensions to the Nest Hub, it has a smaller footprint. The Nest Hub Max, which launched in 2019, is showing its age and is significantly slower and laggier than HomeView.
The newer Echo Shows 8 and 11 are closer to HomeView in capabilities, but not as snappy. The just-announced Show 16 has a new UI with similar features to HomeView, but Amazon’s penchant for sticking ads on its smart displays has rightly put off many users. The HomeView has no ads.
When it comes to the AI inside, Alexa Plus is the most capable in the smart home. It can run multi-device, multi-room commands in seconds, and you can create complex automations with voice alone. Gemini can also handle more complex multi-step requests and is better with natural language and inferring intent from vaguer statements, like “I’m cold,” which will have it turn up the thermostat.
Where HomeView wins here is Personal Context. Google and Amazon offer similar profile switching, but neither is connected to your phone the way Siri AI is to your iPhone, so they have far less personal information to draw on. (Gemini for Home could certainly access much of it, but Google hasn’t gone there yet).
If Siri AI were a human personal assistant, I’d fire her for being too slow.
Of course, this is also a drawback: you need an iPhone or iPad to have a profile on HomeView. However, guests or visitors can use the smart home controls, timers, weather, Safari, music, and Siri for general knowledge.
Siri AI is also the only one of the three you don’t have to pay for to get the full voice assistant experience. Alexa Plus costs $20 a month or comes with Prime, and Google’s more advanced voice assistant, Gemini Live, is behind a paywall. Apple has said some Apple Intelligence features “are subject to daily usage limits,” with expanded access “available for a fee” in the future, but I didn’t run into any limits during my testing. However, Alexa Plus and Gemini Live are available on older smart speakers and displays; Siri is currently gated to the newest models.
Alexa and Google also support many more smart home devices; I can’t control my Bosch coffee machine in Apple Home, and my Ring doorbell and Nest floodlight camera don’t natively work with HomeView. Which is a shame, as HomeView is the best smart display for cameras I’ve tested. Ring devices work almost as well on an Echo Show, but Nest cams are slow on a Nest Hub, and third-party cameras struggle on both. The catch is only a handful of brands work with HomeKit Secure Video — including Aqara, Eve, and some Anker Eufy and TP-Link Tapo models. Apple’s own cameras, made with LG, arrive next year.
Apple is late, but pretty great
HomeView is a very good first smart display from Apple: powerful enough to run my smart home but simple enough for anyone in it to use. If you’re an Apple household and want it for any of its core jobs, you’ll be pleased, and at $299 it’s very competitively priced. But it’s not the full-fledged family organizer some households might want; Skylight’s Calendar still owns that niche.
For Apple Home users, the only question is how many do you want?
Where it gets complicated is when it tries to be your iPhone. Siri AI and Personal Context bring something no other smart display currently offers. But potentially seeing each other’s texts will be a deal-breaker for some. Too many tasks send you back to your phone, and if Siri AI were a human personal assistant, I’d probably fire her for being too slow.
This is all fixable: the option to turn off some personal features on the HomeView, a few more functional widgets and apps, and better options for displaying shared personal content like calendars could bring HomeView closer to the family computer I’d like it to be.
Today, HomeView offers enough for its price to be a good choice for anyone with an iPhone or iPad who is intrigued by any of its core features — particularly the smart home. For households already deeply invested in the Apple ecosystem and Apple Home, the only real question is how many are you going to buy?

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigns after reports of second affair deepened scandal

Huang steered Nvidia to enormous success, and in an extraordinary and strangely old-fashioned tale, he was ultimately undone by his love life, our technology and AI editor writes

Nvidia's next CEO will have more oversight as company chair appointedpublished at 17:00 GMT
17:00 GMT
Dearbail Jordan Senior business and economics reporter
Tucked inside the announcement of Jensen Huang’s departure and his temporary replacement, is the news that Nvidia has appointed its first-ever chair.
This is a big deal. Part of a company chair’s responsibility is to have oversight of the chief executive. Nvidia has gone without one for more than 30 years.
Huang often lauded the company’s “flat” management structure. “If you want to empower people, you want to make the organisation as flat as possible so that information travels quickly,” he once said.
Huang had dozens of people reporting to him, which may well have sped up the flow of information and decision-making but also resulted in an extraordinary concentration of power in one man’s hands.
Now, Nvidia’s new boss, whoever that ends up being in the long term, will have someone overseeing them - in this case, Harvey Jones, a tech industry veteran who was the former boss of Synopsys, a chip design software maker.
Nvidia not the only company to suffer amid Huang's resignationpublished at 16:44 GMT
16:44 GMT
Anuk Weerawardana Business reporter
Jensen Huang's resignation as CEO didn't just affect Nvidia's share price today but also that of companies that previously benefitted from its hype.
Neocloud companies like CoreWeave and Nebius - essentially companies that rent out data centres to AI developers - dropped roughly 5% and 16% in share value respectively after the US market opened today.
Both companies had previously received large investments from Nvidia and now rent out its hardware to manage AI workloads.
"Nvidia became such a crucial cog in the machine that it has become an investor, financier and ultimately strategic backer of a lot of companies involved in the supply chain," says Ben Barringer, head of technology research at investment platform Quilter Cheviot.
Nvidia is one of the "key mechanisms" that keep the investment cycle moving so any bump in the road could mean ripples across the wider AI ecosystem.
Yet, Barringer tells me there is still a high probability that the chipmaker will continue to support companies that ultimately buy and use its chips in the long-term.
Huang resignation 'a stress test for the AI boom' - market expertpublished at 16:23 GMT
16:23 GMT
Rachel Clun Business reporter, in New York
Jensen Huang’s resignation and the revelations that led up to it have followed a turbulent period for artificial intelligence and tech firms in general, as investors have in recent weeks reacted to the US midterm elections and massive spending on AI infrastructure.
Stephanie Balaouras, vie president and group research director at market research firm Forrester’s, says Huang’s resignation is "a stress test for the AI boom".
"If Nvidia stabilises quickly under new leadership, the AI infrastructure boom will have demonstrated resilience," she says, adding that if Nvidia’s position remains under pressure it would suggest the AI ecosystem is more fragile.
Eldar Tuvey, is the chief executive of AI procurement platform Vertice. He tells the BBC most of its clients spend money with Nvidia, and one customer has been spending billions.
While he says the turbulence will affect the company and its clients, the bigger picture was that firms would keep spending with Nvidia, Tuvey adds: "I think it will necessarily impact all of us, but I have full confidence in Nvidia to be able to move forward from here."
'One of the most incredible downfalls,' Huang's biographer tells BBCpublished at 16:02 GMT
16:02 GMT
Jensen Huang's biographer, Stephen Witt, has described news of his resignation as "one of the most incredible downfalls of a corporate executive in recent history".
Witt, who wrote The Thinking Machine, a biography of Huang, tells BBC Newsnight it's "almost impossible" to imagine the company without him, as he's been "the only chief executive this company has ever known".
"This is the age of AI," he says, "but this is a human story. This is a very classic human story of hubris, masculine hubris and overconfidence. Some things never change."
Witt has interviewed the 63-year-old six times and describes him as a "demanding" boss, who "really pushes his people to the limit".
While he says Huang is "very brilliant" and a "world-class computer scientist", there's also "a level of arrogance" and "a level of self-deception as well".
Nvidia shares recover some ground - but still down about 6%published at 15:44 GMT
15:44 GMT
Rachel Clun Business reporter, in New York
Nvidia shares have recovered some ground as investors digest the news of Jensen Huang’s resignation.
Shares initially opened down more than 13% after plummeting in after-hours trading, but by mid-morning in the US they were down about 6%.
The tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index had also recovered slightly, trading down about 0.6%, and the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average were also trading in negative territory.

He writes: "NOW THAT Nvidia CEO JENSEN HUANG, who has done a fantastic job, has resigned, there is only one man to take his place - TIM COOK of Apple. A Legend replaces a Legend!"

Nvidia scandal puts pressure on the Trump administrationpublished at 15:24 GMT
15:24 GMT
Bernd Debusmann Jr White House reporter in Washington DC
Image source, AFP via Getty Images
Image caption,
Donald Trump presents the National Medal of Science to Jensen Huang on 8 October
The fallout from Jensen Huang's personal life and subsequent resignation puts the Trump administration in an extremely difficult place politically.
President Trump has repeatedly stood by Huang, and earlier this week suggested that Huang might be able to remain in his position.
But beyond the alleged affairs and Huang's resignation, the wider issues that have come to light this week have the potential to reach far into the Oval Office and the uppermost levels of American political power.
Even before Huang's resignation, Democrats on Capitol Hill were accusing the administration of being "too deferential" to Nvidia, and of endangering US national security to sell advanced Nvidia H200 chips to China at Huang's urging.
At the centre of this debate is Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, who reportedly clashed with other administration officials - including Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent - over the export controls.
Democrats may expect him to come testify before Congress about the matter, which could become a potentially embarrassing moment for the White House.
It's still not clear when exactly Trump was informed of the issue and what he was told, but he is likely to face mounting questions about why Huang was allowed to remain in such close proximity to the administration once he knew.
Jensen Huang resigns - how did we get here?published at 15:11 GMT
15:11 GMT
Image source, EPA
29 October: The New York Times first reports Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang had a "close relationship" with Alice Huang, who it says US officials allege is a Chinese spy.
8 November: Jensen Huang admits to having an affair and apologises for being unfaithful to his wife, although he does not say who the other person was. Nvidia’s board of directors begins an independent investigation.
9 November: US President Donald Trump says the FBI is investigating. Beijing publicly rejects the spying allegations as a smear.
11 November: The New York Times reports that Jensen Huang had been involved in what it describes as another extramarital relationship, this time with an Nvidia executive who reports to him. Nvidia’s board reportedly holds a meeting to discuss the CEO’s future.
12 November: Jensen Huang resigns as CEO. Colette Kress, the company’s chief financial officer, is appointed interim CEO.
Bad news for Nvidia could be good news for its competitorspublished at 14:58 GMT
14:58 GMT
Rachel Clun Business reporter, in New York
Nvidia has been crucial to artificial intelligence development, so the turmoil around its leader has rippled out to affect global AI and tech stocks.
South Korea’s tech and chipmaker-heavy Kospi index closed down 0.6% on Thursday, and the Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company was trading down about 1.7%.
Bad news for Nvidia could be good news for some of its competitors, however.
Shares in Chinese manufacturers of graphics processing units rose, with Moore Threads, MetaX and Cambricon all surging by more than 8%. Shares in American chipmaker AMD rose by 4% in early trading.
A giant on the global economic scene whose spell was broken in a weekpublished at 14:55 GMT
14:55 GMT
Simon Jack Business editor
Image source, Reuters
It's hard to overstate how giant a figure Jensen Huang has cut on the global economic scene. Insatiable appetite for the chips the company he founded makes, huge investments in other AI related companies, and a personal talismanic brand and profile that has rivalled and possibly surpassed that of even Apple's Steve Jobs in his prime.
His trademark all-black outfits, and his mischievous charm at product launches gave him rockstar appeal.
But as an executive at a large financial institution told me, the allegations of an affair with an executive at the Singaporean firm Megaspeed with questions about her links to China, followed by reports of a second affair saw the halo slip and then underlying concerns to emerge.
Prominent among them was governance - in other words how Nvidia was run. He ruled over an unusually flat management structure with dozens of direct reports to him personally - "a ridiculous set up", according to one executive.
The promotion of his children to senior posts may work in a family business but are questionable in a $4 trillion publicly traded company.
Nvidia's role as a cornerstone lender to, and investor in, the rest of the AI industry has raised concerns about the circular financing in the AI industry. It is a fascinating parable to see how quickly the spell he cast over an entire industry was broken in a week.

14:37 GMTBreaking

Nvidia shares opened down more than 13%, wiping more than half a trillion dollars off the firm’s share market value following news that Jensen Huang had resigned as chief executive.
Nvidia shares had been plummeting before the market’s opening bell after news broke on Wednesday evening that Huang had allegedly had an affair with a woman within the company.
The tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index also opened down, as did the S&P 500. Nvidia accounts for about 6% of the value of the S&P 500.
The firm, which is key to the artificial intelligence industry, will report its latest financial results next Tuesday.
Huang, who steered Nvidia to enormous success, ultimately undone by his own love lifepublished at 14:20 GMT
14:20 GMT
Zoe Kleinman Technology and AI editor

If I’d told you this time last week that Jensen Huang was about to leave Nvidia, you would not have believed me.
The man who co-founded this enormously successful AI chip company, working tirelessly to build it from the ground up, steering it through some disastrous times with endless energy and ruthless determination, shaping into the giant it has become, ultimately undone by his own love life.
It’s an extraordinary tale and a strangely old fashioned one for a tech firm with its sights set on the future.
Colette Kress, who takes over for now, will be a familiar face internally and that must feel reassuring.
She’s worked at the firm for 13 years and for those who follow Nvidia’s considerable finances, she is often present on earnings calls. She is a billionaire in her own right.
But she’s not an engineer and will undoubtedly have a very different leadership style to the demanding boss that was Jensen Huang. She will also have to convince investors that its business as usual for Nvidia, and the innovation for which it is renowned will continue at pace.

He's probably not short of offers at the moment - I expect Nvidia's rivals can't believe their luck.
Who is the new interim Nvidia CEO Colette Kress?published at 14:08 GMT
14:08 GMT
Image source, Reuters
Colette Kress, who has just been made the interim CEO of Nvidia, says that she is committed to building on the "the extraordinary foundation" that Jensen Huang has created.
Kress has been the executive vice president and chief financial officer at Nvidia since joining the company in September 2013.
The company's board says that she has led the company's finance organisation and "played a key role in Nvidia's growth and evolution".
Before joining Nvidia, Kress spent nearly 25 years in finance roles at leading technology companies, including Cisco and Microsoft.
After being appointed as interim chief executive officer, Kress says: "Nvidia's strength has always come from its people, and together with our leadership team, I am committed to building on the extraordinary foundation Jensen created over the last 33 years.
"We will continue advancing the work that has made Nvidia a leader in accelerated computing and AI, and execute on the opportunities ahead. Our focus remains on serving our customers, partners, developers and shareholders."

The rolling news of his alleged affairs has affected the firm’s share price for most of the week as investors have fretted over who could run the company should Huang leave.
The board may be hoping that an answer here - that current chief financial officer Colette Kress will fill in as interim chief executive - will allay some of those investor concerns.

Many people own tech stocks, whether they know it or not, expert sayspublished at 13:41 GMT
13:41 GMT
Katie Williams Live reporter
I asked Russ Mould, investment director at AJ Bell, what the latest Nvidia turmoil means for people’s money.
He says "lots of people probably have this stuff in their pension, whether they know it or not".
Worth about $4.2tn, Nvidia is one of the "Magnificent Seven" tech stocks, along with Google’s parent company Alphabet, Meta, Microsoft, Apple, Amazon and Tesla.
Mould says these seven stocks collectively make up more than 30% of America’s S&P 500, which tracks the share prices of the largest publicly traded US companies.
And US equities make up a significant portion of many people’s pensions.
But Matt Britzman, senior equity analyst at Hargreaves Lansdown, tells me that even though Nvidia is an "important" part of the US market, a diversified portfolio is built to "ride out the inevitable ups and downs of individual companies".
Nvidia’s share price has fallen compared with five days ago, while the Nasdaq Composite, which is heavily weighted towards tech companies, has risen by more than 4%, external.
Britzman also expects some "short-term turbulence" for Nvidia, but says "demand for AI isn't going anywhere".

Huang steered Nvidia to enormous success, and in an extraordinary and strangely old-fashioned tale, he was ultimately undone by his love life, our technology and AI editor writes

Live Reporting
Edited by Tinshui Yeung and Ben Hatton, with technology and AI editor Zoe Kleinman and business reporter Rachel Clun
Nvidia's next CEO will have more oversight as company chair appointedpublished at 17:00 GMT
17:00 GMT
Dearbail Jordan Senior business and economics reporter
Tucked inside the announcement of Jensen Huang’s departure and his temporary replacement, is the news that Nvidia has appointed its first-ever chair.
This is a big deal. Part of a company chair’s responsibility is to have oversight of the chief executive. Nvidia has gone without one for more than 30 years.
Huang often lauded the company’s “flat” management structure. “If you want to empower people, you want to make the organisation as flat as possible so that information travels quickly,” he once said.
Huang had dozens of people reporting to him, which may well have sped up the flow of information and decision-making but also resulted in an extraordinary concentration of power in one man’s hands.
Now, Nvidia’s new boss, whoever that ends up being in the long term, will have someone overseeing them - in this case, Harvey Jones, a tech industry veteran who was the former boss of Synopsys, a chip design software maker.
Nvidia not the only company to suffer amid Huang's resignationpublished at 16:44 GMT
16:44 GMT
Anuk Weerawardana Business reporter
Jensen Huang's resignation as CEO didn't just affect Nvidia's share price today but also that of companies that previously benefitted from its hype.
Neocloud companies like CoreWeave and Nebius - essentially companies that rent out data centres to AI developers - dropped roughly 5% and 16% in share value respectively after the US market opened today.
Both companies had previously received large investments from Nvidia and now rent out its hardware to manage AI workloads.
"Nvidia became such a crucial cog in the machine that it has become an investor, financier and ultimately strategic backer of a lot of companies involved in the supply chain," says Ben Barringer, head of technology research at investment platform Quilter Cheviot.
Nvidia is one of the "key mechanisms" that keep the investment cycle moving so any bump in the road could mean ripples across the wider AI ecosystem.
Yet, Barringer tells me there is still a high probability that the chipmaker will continue to support companies that ultimately buy and use its chips in the long-term.
Huang resignation 'a stress test for the AI boom' - market expertpublished at 16:23 GMT
16:23 GMT
Rachel Clun Business reporter, in New York
Jensen Huang’s resignation and the revelations that led up to it have followed a turbulent period for artificial intelligence and tech firms in general, as investors have in recent weeks reacted to the US midterm elections and massive spending on AI infrastructure.
Stephanie Balaouras, vie president and group research director at market research firm Forrester’s, says Huang’s resignation is "a stress test for the AI boom".
"If Nvidia stabilises quickly under new leadership, the AI infrastructure boom will have demonstrated resilience," she says, adding that if Nvidia’s position remains under pressure it would suggest the AI ecosystem is more fragile.
Eldar Tuvey, is the chief executive of AI procurement platform Vertice. He tells the BBC most of its clients spend money with Nvidia, and one customer has been spending billions.
While he says the turbulence will affect the company and its clients, the bigger picture was that firms would keep spending with Nvidia, Tuvey adds: "I think it will necessarily impact all of us, but I have full confidence in Nvidia to be able to move forward from here."
'One of the most incredible downfalls,' Huang's biographer tells BBCpublished at 16:02 GMT
16:02 GMT
Jensen Huang's biographer, Stephen Witt, has described news of his resignation as "one of the most incredible downfalls of a corporate executive in recent history".
Witt, who wrote The Thinking Machine, a biography of Huang, tells BBC Newsnight it's "almost impossible" to imagine the company without him, as he's been "the only chief executive this company has ever known".
"This is the age of AI," he says, "but this is a human story. This is a very classic human story of hubris, masculine hubris and overconfidence. Some things never change."
Witt has interviewed the 63-year-old six times and describes him as a "demanding" boss, who "really pushes his people to the limit".
While he says Huang is "very brilliant" and a "world-class computer scientist", there's also "a level of arrogance" and "a level of self-deception as well".

15:44 GMT

Nvidia shares have recovered some ground as investors digest the news of Jensen Huang’s resignation.
Shares initially opened down more than 13% after plummeting in after-hours trading, but by mid-morning in the US they were down about 6%.
The tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index had also recovered slightly, trading down about 0.6%, and the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average were also trading in negative territory.

Jensen Huang resigns as Nvidia CEO immediately

Jensen Huang resigns as Nvidia CEO immediately
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
13:56:11 UTC
22:56:11 KST

Jensen Huang resigns as Nvidia CEO immediately

====================================================================================================

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigns amid spy, affair scandals
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
13:53:16 UTC
22:53:16 KST

Nvidia’s board announced that Jensen Huang — its longtime co-founder, president, and CEO — has resigned after reports of two extramarital affairs, one with an alleged Chinese spy and one with an Nvidia executive who reported to Huang. Following a late Wednesday night meeting, the board accepted Huang’s letter of resignation as CEO, president, and board member, effective immediately, with current CFO Colette Kress appointed interim CEO. Kress has been with Nvidia since 2013, with previous stops at Cisco and Microsoft.
Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigns amid spy, affair scandals
After reports of Huang’s two extramarital affairs with an alleged spy and an Nvidia executive, he’s out, and Nvidia CFO Colette Kress is in.
Nvidia is also changing its corporate structure in Huang’s absence, appointing former Synopsys CEO Harvey C. Jones as the first board chair in its history. The statement does not include a statement from Huang, while Kress is quoted as saying:
NVIDIA’s strength has always come from its people, and together with our leadership team, I am committed to building on the extraordinary foundation Jensen created over the last 33 years. We will continue advancing the work that has made NVIDIA a leader in accelerated computing and AI, and execute on the opportunities ahead. Our focus remains on serving our customers, partners, developers and shareholders.
Now Nvidia says, “The Board is initiating a search for a permanent Chief Executive Officer and will consider internal and external candidates,” and reports about who that might be have already surfaced, putting Kress, Apple hardware chief Johny Srouji, Nvidia VP Ian Buck, AMD CEO (and cousin of Jensen Huang) Lisa Su, and Arm CEO Rene Haas at the top of the list.
Huang had been at the helm of what’s now known as the most powerful company in AI since its founding in 1993. In the years since, it’s become the main purveyor of the chips fueling AI advancement and deployment in the US, and it has enjoyed near-unbridled power and influence — demand for Nvidia’s chips constantly outpaces supply, and AI CEOs are constantly putting in requests to buy more, sometimes to no avail. Nvidia’s investment portfolio — worth about $99 billion and spanning AI companies from frontier labs to neoclouds — has also granted it a lot of sway in the AI space. And in recent years, Huang himself has enjoyed a close relationship with President Donald Trump.
Over the past week, that influence has been a double-edged sword for Huang, after news broke that he had been involved in a romantic relationship with Alice Huang (no relation), an executive at Megaspeed, a Singapore-based cloud provider and big customer of Nvidia itself. The Megaspeed executive was also allegedly a surveillance operative passing information along to China’s Ministry of State Security. She was focused on gaining information about advanced AI chips in the US and trying to influence the technology being made available to China, The New York Times reported. Huang admitted he had been “unfaithful” to his wife and apologized to his family and shareholders in a memo to Nvidia employees over the weekend.
As The Verge wrote earlier this week, “questions are swirling around how the affair may have influenced Huang’s reported push to sell advanced chips to China — and potentially even influenced President Trump’s own decisions surrounding AI export controls.”
As of Monday, analysts, venture capitalists and even Trump had expected Huang would come out the other side of the scandal with his chief executive status intact. Silicon Valley insiders, including Elon Musk, had backed him despite the news. But on Wednesday, news broke that Huang had had a second extramarital affair — this time with an executive reporting directly to him. The New York Times, citing five people who worked at Nvidia, identified the relationship as being with Nvidia’s VP of healthcare, Kimberly Powell. Soon after the news broke, The Wall Street Journal reported, citing people familiar with the matter, that Nvidia’s board was meeting as soon as Wednesday evening in order to decide Huang’s future at the company and potentially oust him from his role.

====================================================================================================

Jensen Huang resigning effective immediately

NVIDIA Announces CEO Transition
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
13:52:29 UTC
22:52:29 KST

- Jensen Huang Resigns
- Colette Kress, Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer, Appointed Interim CEO
- Harvey C. Jones Appointed First-Ever Chair of the Board
NVIDIA (NASDAQ: NVDA) Board of Directors announced today that Jensen Huang has resigned as President, Chief Executive Officer, and a member of the Board, effective immediately.
Colette Kress, current Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer of NVIDIA, has agreed to serve as Interim Chief Executive Officer. Kress will continue to serve as Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer while serving as Interim Chief Executive Officer until a permanent successor has been named. The Board is initiating a search for a permanent Chief Executive Officer and will consider internal and external candidates.
Kress has served as Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer of NVIDIA since joining the company in September 2013. During her tenure, she has led the company’s finance organization and has played a key role in NVIDIA’s growth and evolution. Prior to joining NVIDIA, Kress spent nearly 25 years in finance roles at leading technology companies, including Cisco and Microsoft.
The Board of Directors appointed Harvey C. Jones as its first-ever Chair of the Board. Mr. Jones has been an entrepreneur, technology executive, and active venture investor for over 30 years, including serving as Chief Executive Officer and Executive Chairman of Synopsys.
“Colette has helped lead NVIDIA through a period of extraordinary growth and has a deep understanding of our business, customers, operations and financial position,” said Harvey Jones, Chair of the NVIDIA Board of Directors. “She is supported by an experienced leadership team that has been instrumental in executing NVIDIA’s strategy and advancing its technology roadmap. The Board has full confidence in Colette and the leadership team, and we expect the company’s operations, customer commitments and product roadmap to continue without interruption.”
“NVIDIA’s strength has always come from its people, and together with our leadership team, I am committed to building on the extraordinary foundation Jensen created over the last 33 years,” said Colette Kress, Interim CEO and CFO, NVIDIA. “We will continue advancing the work that has made NVIDIA a leader in accelerated computing and AI, and execute on the opportunities ahead. Our focus remains on serving our customers, partners, developers and shareholders.”
Jensen’s contributions to NVIDIA are profound. NVIDIA is one of the most consequential technology companies in the world. The Board and the company thank Jensen for his immeasurable contributions to the company and the industry.
NVIDIA will report its third-quarter fiscal 2027 financial results on Tuesday, November 17, at approximately 1:20 p.m. PT, as previously scheduled, followed by its earnings call at 2 p.m. PT.

The pricey new Apple TV 4K is still the best streamer around
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
13:52:05 UTC
22:52:05 KST

I’m sitting in front of my TV, puppy nuzzled up next to me, with four HomePods spread around my living room — two flanking my TV and one at either end of my couch — connected to the brand new Apple TV 4K. Young Anakin Skywalker hits the throttle and flies through the room. There hasn’t been an Apple surround sound system until now, and since it was announced, I was excited to try it out. And let me tell you, watching a pod race with an all-Apple audio setup is a whole lot of fun.
The pricey new Apple TV 4K is still the best streamer around
Apple knows how to make a great streaming box to be the center of your home theater, but there are some misses with the new model.
If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.
Surround sound across four HomePods is one of the flagship features of the fourth-gen Apple TV 4K, which is out tomorrow for $300. It’s an expensive streaming box — double and in some cases quadruple the price of competing options — that tries to earn its place in your living room with premium and convenient new features: a powerful chip, lots of storage, a trackable remote, and lots of AI. If you’re deep in the Apple ecosystem and willing to splurge, the box is a solid choice. But for everyone else, I’m not convinced the added features do enough to justify that steep new price.
The Good
- Easy to use and fast interface
- Supports both Dolby Vision and HDR10+
- Four HomePod system sounds great
The Bad
- Expensive for a streamer
- Siri AI is inconsistent
From the outside, it’s hard to tell what’s new about the fourth-gen Apple TV 4K. Compared to the 2022 model, it’s the same size, in the same finish, with the same number of ports (in the same configuration), showing the same interface, with the same HDR support. Where things are upgraded is largely on the inside: there’s a more powerful chip — the A19, which first showed up in the iPhone 17 — and 128GB of storage by default, which was previously an upgrade. The flip side is that there is no lower-cost 64GB model anymore, which is a shame because I don’t think most people need any more than that.
The included aluminum remote is visually the same as the previous one. It charges via USB-C, has a voice-activation button on its side, and its clickpad is touch-sensitive, which can thankfully be disabled in the menu. There is one small quality of life change, though. The remote now has the Find My feature. It shows up in the Find My app on your phone, which does a great job of pinpointing the remote’s location; it can also make a sound when you can’t spot it. For a busy family like mine, this is great when one of us unintentionally brings the remote into the kitchen or bedroom (or when it’s buried in the cushions or pillows of the couch).
Siri AI is here, too, though it’s also coming to second- and third-gen Apple TV 4Ks with tvOS 27.2. The new Siri can be pretty great, when it works, especially when using personal context to understand your requests. If Apple Intelligence is enabled on your iPhone or iPad and it’s on the same network as the Apple TV 4K, the box can recognize your voice and securely connect to your device to help personalize answers to you. My friend had recently texted me about a show to watch, but I couldn’t remember what it was. When I asked Siri to find the show he had recommended, it searched my messages and opened it up on the TV (it was Pluribus).
But Siri had occasional issues with personalized answers. I asked this request a few times over my days of testing, and it didn’t always give the same answer. While it opened Pluribus on a few occasions, it also once opened The Bondsman which, to be fair, he did recommend to me… just way back in October of 2025. But even when I clarified, “No, the show he messaged about yesterday,” Siri wasn’t able to find it.
The Apple TV 4K also had trouble recognizing my voice from one moment to the next. A few minutes after asking about Pluribus, I put on an episode of Bobby Flay’s The Line. I asked Siri about his competitor Marcus Samuelsson’s restaurant Hav & Mar — where it is, what type of food it serves, if it had any accolades (I found out it was awarded two stars by The New York Times), and when it would be open during an upcoming trip to New York City. But when I changed the topic to a movie my wife and I were texting about, Siri suddenly couldn’t recognize my voice to use personal context, even though it had answered my Pluribus question minutes before. My iPhone was still on the coffee table in front of me and on the Wi-Fi network, so the connection between it and the Apple TV 4K should have been strong enough for personal context to work without issue.
Requests can be misunderstood, too. My days of testing overlapped with the F1 Brazilian Grand Prix, which is broadcast on Apple TV. When live coverage of the race started, I asked Siri, “Play the F1 Brazilian Grand Prix.” Instead of the race, it started the music track “Hymn of the Racers” by the 1995 band F1. “Play the São Paulo F1 Grand Prix” played the song “São Paulo,” a collaboration between The Weeknd and Anitta. I spent more time trying to get Siri to understand what I wanted than it would take to use the remote to start it.
When it hits, Siri AI is great. “Play Empire Strikes Back” started the Star Wars film on Disney Plus in seconds without having to mention the streaming service. Getting Pluribus to open from a question relatively scarce on details was awesome (when it happened). But as it stands right now, Siri AI is still very much a beta project and needs some work and refinement to be consistently useful. That’s not to say it’s much worse than alternatives like Alexa and Gemini, but right now it’s not doing enough to stand apart.
You can talk to Siri using the Apple TV remote, but the new model also has far-field microphones built into the box, so you can talk aloud from anywhere. One of the concerns I had when it was announced that the Apple TV 4K was getting far-field mics was whether it would even be able to hear me while in my home theater stand, but that wasn’t an issue for me. Whenever I said “Hey Siri,” I could see it activated on my TV screen. Now, my credenza has an open back for ventilation, which might have helped, but having a HomePod as part of the system also solves the issue. When one (or more) is part of the TV 4K system, the HomePod mics are listening for the “Hey Siri” requests, communicating through Bluetooth, and determining which device should respond.
Then there’s the new feature I had the most fun with: home theater surround. Apple added the ability to use two second-generation HomePods as a stereo pair (with Atmos) a couple of years ago. But as the hub for a home theater, the Apple TV 4K can now connect with four HomePods for a more immersive experience than what you can get with just your (almost certainly terrible) TV speakers.
This could be four second-gen HomePods, four HomePod mini 2 speakers, or a pair of each, as long as the two up front are the same and the two surrounds are the same. This is the setup I tested, with a pair of second-gen HomePods in front and a pair of HomePod Mini 2s in back.
Calibrating the system is easy. With all of the speakers connected to your Apple Home, each speaker emits a gentle pinging sound and a wash of pink noise that sounds like ocean waves, while a ripple of color moves across the TV screen corresponding to the color on the top of each HomePod to make sure positioning is correct. The process takes only a couple of minutes.
The speakers and the Apple TV 4K need to be a part of the same Apple Home and set up by the same user. My wife’s Apple account is the primary on our Apple Home, and even though we’re all a part of the same Apple family, when I set up the 4K TV with my account, it couldn’t see any of the speakers. This is not a major issue, but it can make setup more difficult.
As a unit, the two second-gen HomePods and two HomePod mini 2 speakers worked together really well. Any of the speakers can be tapped to pause the screen, and the volume controls affect the entire system. The big HomePods have far more bass response than expected for their size. I felt the massive Dreadnought’s cannon fire on the Rebel base in The Last Jedi through the floor. And the car explosions as The Penguin attempts to escape pursuit in The Batman felt deep and impactful. It’s not the same heart-rattling rumble a dedicated subwoofer can provide, but for a smaller space (and one with neighbors) it’s a good amount of bass. (I do hope Apple decides to make a standalone wireless subwoofer for the setup, though.)
The front HomePods create a very convincing phantom center channel, making dialog sound like it’s coming right from the screen. The entire spatial sound placement at the listening plane from the four-speaker system is excellent. The calibration sets the surrounds’ volume at a good level. There’s an option to boost or cut the volume of the surround speaker pair from 8dB to -8dB (in 0.5dB increments), but I didn’t think tweaking was necessary.
The different iconic cars in the opening race of Ready Player One zoomed around the room — as did King Kong as he chased after Parzival and Art3mis. Podracers in The Phantom Menace bumped one another and skittered off in pieces into the desert of Tatooine. The speakers deliver a great surround experience.
Atmos isn’t as convincing. There’s still some height to the sound, thanks to the larger HomePods, but it doesn’t form a full enveloping canopy. As the sandstorm envelops Paul Atreides during the rescue of the spice harvester crew in Dune, there’s an audible shimmer as the spice takes hold and whispers encircle him. As with Ready Player One and The Phantom Menace, the sand swirls around the listener, but doesn’t extend above.
I found the same with Dolby Atmos music, such as “bury a friend” by Billie Eilish. The backup vocals in the surrounds sounds nice and supportive, but there are sonic elements (like a rhythmic drill) that are supposed to fly overhead and instead sound like they move by in front of me. The music mix still sounds great, but it isn’t quite what is intended.
The addition of engaging home theater surround performance and (hopefully) consistent Siri AI integration help to differentiate the new Apple TV 4K from the old. But unfortunately so does its price. At $300, the fourth-gen box is multiple times more expensive than its competitors, many of which are capable streamers, if a bit slower. One of the reasons to pay a premium for the Apple TV 4K in the past is that Apple has one of the best privacy policies on the market. Another is that the Apple TV 4K doesn’t serve up the same level of ads the other streaming devices or built-in TV OSes do (although it still tells me to watch Ted Lasso and Widow’s Bay regularly). Those are compelling reasons to pay more for a streaming box if you can swing it, but this is a major increase in price that doesn’t add features that home theater enthusiasts have been asking for. Adding things like audio passthrough and 120Hz support would set the Apple TV 4K apart from everyone else in capabilities and really validate the bump in cost.
And the expense extends to the home theater system. The entire price of the system I used — two second-gen HomePods, two HomePod mini 2s, and the new Apple TV 4K — costs $1,300. If you already have a couple HomePods in your home and want to repurpose them for your home theater (or you’re already using a stereo pair as your TV speakers), adding in the mini 2 speakers and the new 4K TV is a viable prospect. But if you’re looking to start fresh with a surround system and don’t mind the aesthetics of a soundbar, the Sonos Beam Ultra paired with two Era 100 speakers is an excellent alternative, both for its flexibility of adding more speakers and its superior handling of Atmos content. Plus, you can add a subwoofer later and use a cheaper third-gen Apple TV 4K instead of the new one.
There’s no doubt that Apple still makes one of the best streaming devices available. It’s fast, has a great user experience, and the additions of Find My on the remote, far-field mics, and the home theater surround capability add good value (especially if you already have some newer HomePods).
But the new Apple TV 4K is $300. And Siri AI isn’t there yet to consistently make it worth using. Right now, the Apple TV 4K is an excellent, premium-priced streaming device that doesn’t set itself apart enough from its previous model. And with every other streaming device a fraction of the Apple TV 4K’s price, most people would be well served by a cheaper Google Streamer, Fire TV stick, or Roku stick. Yes, the Apple TV 4K is better than all of them. But as it currently stands, especially with Siri AI still in beta, I don’t think it’s twice as better.

Nine Korean startups vie for a spot in Disrupt 2027’s Startup Battlefield 200 competition
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
13:26:25 UTC
22:26:25 KST

TechCrunch’s yearly Startup Battlefield 200 competition sees hundreds of companies from across the world trying every year to get a chance to pitch in front of VCs and the international tech community at the Disrupt conference.
This week, nine early-stage South Korean startups pitched at DETO 2026, a technology conference in Seoul, to earn a spot in next year’s Startup Battlefield 200.
The startups work across the deep tech sector, including healthcare, fintech, energy, semiconductors, and AI. All nine startups are looking to enter the U.S. market as part of the broader international expansion plans.
Voinosis, which develops AI to detect changes in brain health through voice analysis, won the competition. The other eight startups are listed below in alphabetical order.
Voinosis
Voinosis is developing an AI tool that analyzes speech patterns such as pitch, pauses, rhythm and response times to detect changes linked to brain health. Each voice check takes less than five minutes.
The company says its technology has been tested in Greek, Japanese and English, and its product doesn’t require separate training for each language. In a clinical study involving 146 participants, the startup claims its tech achieved 88% accuracy in screening for potential cognitive issues.
Voinosis targets hospitals, senior living facilities, insurers and individual consumers. It also plans to link voice checks with cognitive training, assessments, and treatment provided by healthcare professionals.
The startup was founded by Grace Shin, who spent more than 25 years as an otologic surgeon. The startup is currently raising a Series A round and has already secured a South Korean pharmaceutical company as a strategic investor.
Awesome Lab
Awesome Lab, based in Seoul, makes ThermoFlux, a heating system that uses water electrolysis and ionization to generate heat. The system can manage heat production for buildings, water supply, and industrial processes. The company says its technology can reduce energy consumption and carbon emissions.
For its international expansion, Awesome Lab is taking a different approach in each market. In the U.S., it plans to supply core OEM modules, and work with boiler, water heater and HVAC companies on product development.
Crosshub
Crosshub helps travelers and international visitors verify their identities and set up payment methods abroad. Its platform, IDBlock, combines identity verification and payment services using Self-Sovereign Identity (SSI) and zero-knowledge proof.
The company says its platform helps users prove their identity across borders without sharing unnecessary personal information. Crosshub is also integrating this technology with payment systems to improve access to financial services for travelers.
DeepFusion AI
DeepFusion AI has developed a 4D radar-based imaging system with additional sensors for autonomous mobility, robotaxi, maritime surveillance, and defense applications. The system combines RF sensors with radar and lidar to detect objects, movement, or distance without a direct line of sight. All sensors are supposedly packed into a single, customizable unit.
EFLOW
EFLOW develops special motors that can produce more torque, or rotational force, while taking up less space for use in robots, drones, satellites, and other mobility applications. The startup also develops actuators that control how robotic joints move.
Otiton Medical
Otition Medical makes two medical devices: one specializes in measuring body temperature, while another can capture ear canal images and analyze the eardrum. The company says its AI was trained on about 55,000 images of eardrums, and the device has received medical certification in South Korea.
Riibotics
Riibotics says it can deploy robots into warehouses within a day. Riibotics wants to reduce deployment costs for warehouses by streamlining setup, which includes infrastructure setup, warehouse mapping, system integration through custom middleware, task programming, and safety checks.
Ternell
Ternell, a semiconductor startup based in Ulsan, is developing a new type of memory to help AI chips use less power.
Unlike conventional two-state memory, Ternell’s T-SRAM system adds a third state for more efficient data storage. Founded in 2019 by KyungRok Kim, a former Samsung Electronics engineer, Ternell reports that tests with 28-nanometer chips showed a 33% to 102% increase in memory capacity per area, and a 55% to 71% reduction in energy needed to access data, compared with conventional SRAM.
Testbank
Testbank is a Korean edtech startup developing Solve, an app that converts printed study materials, such as workbooks and test-prep books, into interactive digital versions.
The startup uses AI to identify question and answer spaces in publishers’ PDF files while preserving the original layout. Solve lets you write answers directly on the page and be graded automatically.

The Most Delicious, Crispiest Potatoes of All Time
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
12:00:00 UTC
21:00:00 KST

The Most Delicious, Crispiest Potatoes of All Time
We independently select these products—if you buy from one of our links, we may earn a commission. All prices were accurate at the time of publishing.
Quick Overview
Why You’ll Love the Most Delicious, Crispiest Potatoes of All Time
- They’re rough around the edges. Par-boiling the potatoes and roughing up the edges creates lots of craggy edges that become shatteringly crisp in the oven.
- Worth the effort. These potatoes are the crispiest and most flavorful we’ve ever tasted, so it’s well-worth the longer cook time.
When I think about my dream roast potato (totally normal to do!), it’s shatteringly crisp on the outside (probably from being roasted in rendered chicken fat aka schmaltz), golden-brown all over with craggy edges, and impossibly tender and fluffy on the inside. It isn’t too salty, but it’s well-seasoned so every bite electrifies my brain with the type of unbridled joy only a potato can bring.

FAQs
- What is the timeline for the IPO? The IPO process begins with a roadshow starting on November 10th (EST). Subject to regulatory clearance, the final IPO share price is expected to be set on November 18th (EST), and shares are expected to begin publicly trading on November 19th (EST).
- What is Anthropic offering in this IPO? Anthropic is offering shares of its Class A common stock to the public, which will trade on Nasdaq under the ticker symbol “ANT”.
- How can I participate in the IPO? You can request shares through a participating brokerage platform. To participate, you must have an active account and submit an indication of interest through their platform.
- Am I eligible to participate in the Anthropic IPO? Your brokerage account or investing app will confirm whether you’re eligible when you submit your request to participate.
- At what price will the shares be offered? The final IPO price is expected to be determined on November 18th (EST), based on regulatory clearance, investor demand and market conditions.
- When will I know if I received shares of Anthropic? Your brokerage account or investing app will confirm your allocation on the first day of trading, which is expected to be November 19th (EST).
- What is the difference between buying in the IPO and buying after the stock begins trading? Shares bought in the IPO will be purchased at the offering price, which is expected to be set on November 18th (EST). Shares bought after Anthropic starts trading will be purchased at the prevailing market price at that time.
- Will I receive all the shares I apply for? There is no guarantee that any investor will receive all or any of the shares they apply for in the Anthropic IPO. However, you should be ready to purchase all the shares for which you apply.
- What is the prospectus? A prospectus is a regulatory filing that provides detailed information about Anthropic’s business, financial statements, risk factors, management team, use of proceeds, offering details, among other items. Investors should review the prospectus closely before investing.

Ring confirms its camera drone won’t have manual controls after crashing on CBS News
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
11:12:32 UTC
20:12:32 KST

When (or if) Ring’s security camera drone flies into our homes in January, you won’t get the option to pilot it yourself. The Ring Drone Cam relies entirely on an “AI Pilot” system to follow pre-programmed routes and navigate autonomously around obstacles, with no manual control option available — and an embarrassing demonstration with CBS News might reveal why.
Ring confirms its camera drone won’t have manual controls after crashing on CBS News
The Ring Drone Cam will entirely rely on AI autopilot to fly around your home.
The video, broadcast on CBS Mornings and published on Threads, shows Ring founder Jamie Siminoff allowing technology reporter Jo Ling Kent to manually fly an early version of the drone via a mobile app with integrated controls.
Ling Kent: “Can I control it?”
Siminoff: “Yeah, so we can load manual controls.”
The demo then shows the drone crashing twice while being manually piloted. In the first instance, Ring reportedly told CBS News that the drone’s collision avoidance technology malfunctioned, and declined to share the video recorded by the drone prior to crashing. CBS News caught the second crash on camera, with the footage showing the drone colliding with a wall after failing to respond to manual controls. According to Siminoff, this occurred because the drone lost internet connectivity — which it also relies on to fly autonomously.
“If it loses connectivity, it’s supposed to stop and land,” Siminoff said in the video. “It did not there.”
Ring declined to show us the app in our own demonstration of the Drone Cam, and said it only works on pre-programmed flight paths. It seems that while manual flight controls were included at some stage of development, Ring won’t be making the feature available to customers when the Drone Cam launches in January.
“We explored a lot of features throughout the development, but autonomous flight to patrol points using the AI Pilot is the best customer benefit and experience,” Ring spokesperson Sam McGee said in a statement to The Verge. Ring didn’t respond to our question regarding whether the decision to not include manual controls was made following the CBS News demo.

Tim Peters, Python User #2 and Prolific Contributor, Dies At 74
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
11:00:00 UTC
20:00:00 KST

Tim Peters, Python User #2 and Prolific Contributor, Dies At 74 (adafruit.com) 7
			
		 	
				Tim Peters, one of Python's earliest and most influential contributors, died on July 15 at age 74. Known as Python's second real user, Peters created the Zen of Python and Timsort sorting algorithm while contributing to numerous core components. He also served as a Python Software Foundation director from 2001 to 2014. Adafruit remembers Peters in a blog post:  He's best known for the Zen of Python, PEP 20, still printed by typing import this. In 2002, he gave Python Timsort, the sorting algorithm he designed for lists, later adopted by Java and Android. He also polished the cyclic garbage collector and the pymalloc allocator, worked on the dict implementation, the float object, the math module and Karatsuba multiplication in longobject, and wrote the first version of doctest, plus difflib, pickletools and tabnanny.
He was a director of the Python Software Foundation from 2001 to 2014. Before Python, he wrote compilers at Cray Research and speech-to-text software at Dragon Systems. The thread is full of recollections from core developers about his patient, detailed answers on the mailing lists, signed with his trademark .  Python creator Guido van Rossum also paid tribute to Peters in a post on discuss.python.org, writing: "It's hard to overestimate Tim's influence on Python. He's probably best known for writing the Zen of Python (PEP 20), but to me he was a mentor and language co-designer long before that..."
Further discussions can be found on Hacker News and Reddit.

Researchers discover rare star system that emits radio signals every 8 minutes
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
10:46:20 UTC
19:46:20 KST

Scientists at the Curtin University node of the International Centre for Radio Astronomy Research (ICRAR) have linked mysterious radio pulses to an unusual pairing of stars — a dead star that spins every eight minutes and a star similar to our own Sun.
The newly discovered system, known as GPM J071507−114153, consists of two stars orbiting each other: a white dwarf — the dense core left behind when a star dies — and a G-type star similar to our own Sun.
It is the first time radio pulses have been detected from a white dwarf orbiting a Sun-like star. Typically, such pulses are observed in systems with smaller and cooler red dwarf stars, meaning the discovery breaks new ground.
These types of stellar pairs belong to a broader class of objects known as long-period transients (LPTs), producing radio signals that switch on and off over intervals ranging from minutes to hours. Around a dozen have been detected since the first was discovered by the same ICRAR team in 2021, and astronomers are still working to understand them.
While several previously discovered LPT systems contain a white dwarf and another star, it has remained unclear which of the two stars was generating the radio pulses.
“This is the first time the pulses are clear enough to see the white dwarf moving in its orbit around its companion, so we know that the pulsations are coming from a region close to the white dwarf, or possibly even from the white dwarf itself,” said lead author and commissioning scientist with the Square Kilometre Array Observatory, Dr Sam McSweeney, who made this discovery while working as a researcher at the Curtin University node of ICRAR. The team processed more than 100 hours of radio data at the Pawsey Supercomputing Research Centre to validate and understand the white dwarf motion.
Associate Professor Natasha Hurley-Walker, co-author on the paper, said that the pulses emitted by the white dwarf allowed the team to detect stellar flares on the Sun-like star, similar to those that create aurorae here on Earth.
“As a bonus, we also saw the white dwarf moving behind a region with a strong magnetic field, most likely a ‘stellar prominence’, which is a large loop of plasma erupting from a star’s surface.”
“It’s amazing that by using radio wavelengths we can detect the storms on a star over 5000 light-years away.”
ICRAR researchers have been at the forefront of discovering these rare systems.
“We detected this system in a large survey using the MWA (Murchison Widefield Array) to scan the entire Milky Way visible from Australia. We then used the MeerKAT radio telescope in South Africa to determine its precise position,” said Associate Professor Hurley-Walker.
After the LPT was located using the radio telescopes, the W.M. Keck Observatory on Maunakea, Hawaiʻi Island, confirmed its association with a G-type star through optical observations.
Both the MWA, led by Curtin University at Inyarrimanha Ilgari Bundara, the CSIRO Murchison Radio-Astronomy Observatory on Wajarri Yamaji Country in Western Australia, and MeerKAT, built and operated by the South African Radio Astronomy Observatory in the country’s North Cape province, serve as precursors for the SKA telescopes, SKA-Low in Australia and the SKA-Mid in South Africa, respectively.
Dr McSweeney said, “If we are already making such discoveries with precursor telescopes, just imagine how many more star systems we will be able to find, and what a beautifully detailed view of their signals we will have, once the SKAO telescopes are completely built.”
This study, ‘A long-period transient with a Sun-like companion’, was published in Nature Astronomy.
