Sunken SS United States Set To Become World's Largest Artificial Reef
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
03:30:00 UTC
12:30:00 KST

Sunken SS United States Set To Become World's Largest Artificial Reef (pbs.org) 41
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from PBS: The historic ocean liner SS United States will be intentionally sunk Wednesday off the coast of the Florida panhandle. Live coverage of the sinking of the SS United States is expected to start at 1 p.m. EST, with the sinking expected at 2 p.m. EST. Watch live in the video player above. In its heyday, the SS United States crushed the trans-Atlantic speed record, which it still holds. The luxury ocean liner took only three days to cross from New York to Europe on its maiden voyage in 1952. Carrying presidents and Hollywood stars, the nearly 1,000-foot vessel was a technical marvel of its time. However, the rise of air travel and high operating costs sunk its economic viability, and the ship was pulled from service in 1969. The ocean liner sat at Philadelphia's Pier 82 for nearly 30 years.
After a contentious back and forth with a pier landlord over rent and docking fees, Okaloosa County in Florida eventually stepped in to purchase the ship and bring it to the Gulf of Mexico to become the world's largest artificial reef. The multi-million dollar project is also aimed at creating an economic development opportunity as a major diving attraction. "It was our goal to make sure that she wasn't lost forever," said David Lawrence, a member of the board for the SS United States Conservancy, the group that sold the vessel to the Florida county.
Like the ship's unconventional life, it will also have an unconventional sinking. Federal environmental and explosives rules prevent the county from using explosives. Instead, tanks inside the ship have been removed and large holes were cut into the decks. Once the valves are opened, water will flood the hull, sinking the ship. Resting at 180 feet below the surface of the water, the SS United States will join more than 500 artificial reefs in Okaloosa County, which the county relies on to support the underwater ecosystem.

====================================================================================================

Bad ideas that aren't bad anymore
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
03:18:13 UTC
12:18:13 KST

I’ve been working in software for about 15 years now, and over that time I’ve made a lot of mistakes and learned from a lot of other people’s mistakes. This experience is a deep well that I can draw from when discussing possible solutions for both technical and non-technical problems. This is how I know what sorts of solutions sound good in theory but run into problems in practice, or what fringe idea is actually a perfect fit for the problem we’re trying to solve here. This knowledge & experience is a big part of what has made me effective as a leader.
Lately, though, a lot of the things my gut tells me are bad ideas are turning out to work really well. In our team’s retro this week that hit me pretty hard, and it was a real learning moment for me. It made me realize that a big chunk of the intuition I’ve been relying on might not be worth as much as I thought anymore, and that I need to question that experience more frequently.
So I wanted to write down a few of the “bad ideas” that we’re doing right now that are actually working really well, and then to try and figure out what that means for people like me.
Nobody reviews anybody else’s PRs
For pretty much my whole career, code review has worked the same way: one person writes the code and opens the PR, and then somebody else on the team reviews it before it gets merged. The second set of eyes catches bugs, spreads knowledge around the team, and keeps any one person from quietly drifting the codebase off in their own direction. Merging your own PR without review was one of those things that was a “break glass in case of emergency” kind of situation.
About a month ago we changed that completely. We had been struggling with code review due to the increased pace of PRs being opened, and so we decided to try something crazy - just not doing it. Now the engineer working on a ticket works with Claude, which then opens the PR, and then the human working on the ticket reviews Claude’s work. Once CI is green & the Greptile review is clean, if the human working on the ticket feels confident that the solution is solid, they merge the PR. That’s it. No waiting on a teammate to find time to context switch into your change, no pinging people in Slack to please take a look.
And it’s been working great! When I actually think about why it works, it’s because the thing that code review was protecting against is still being protected against. There’s still a second set of eyes on every change - it’s just that the author is Claude and the reviewer is the human who owns the ticket. That human understands the problem better than anyone else on the team, because they’re the one who’s been working on it. They’re a much better reviewer for that change than a teammate who has to page in all the context from scratch. But also, Claude & Greptile are just better at finding the kinds of things that are often found in Code Review, like small bugs or inconsistencies. It’s pretty rare (at least on the teams I’ve worked on) for big things like architectural flaws or unintended product impact to be found at this stage.
I’ve written before about how review gates can be a sign of a lack of trust, so maybe I shouldn’t have been so surprised that removing one worked out. But my gut still said “you can’t just merge stuff without somebody else looking at it,” and my gut was wrong. At least so far that’s been true - we’re only one month into the change, but it’s feeling really good so far.
Splitting the team up across separate streams of work
This is the one that came up in our retro. Recently we had a few people out on holiday, and at the same time we had a couple of parallel and fairly separate streams of work going on. So we split it up, and basically each person owned one stream of work and ran with it.
Historically I really haven’t liked doing this. When everybody on a team is working on a different thing, you get knowledge silos. Only one person knows how the thing they built works, and if they leave or get sick or go on holiday, the team is stuck. Even worse, I think it erodes the feeling of actually being a team. You’re not working together to solve the same problem anymore, you’re just a group of people solving different problems at the same time who happen to share a standup. I’ve pushed back on this kind of setup on basically every team I’ve led.
But in the retro, we talked about it, and we’re just not seeing any of the consequences I was worried about - at least not in terms of knowledge silos. And the reason is that it’s become so easy to get up to speed on someone else’s work. If I need to jump into something a colleague has been working on, I can say to Claude “hey, bring me up to speed on this thing they’ve been building - how does it work, where is it at, and what still needs to be done?” A few minutes later I have what I need to start contributing. I don’t need anybody else to onboard me, and I don’t need to spend a day reading through code to figure out what’s going on.
The thing that’s really changed here is that the cost of context switching has dropped dramatically, because we’re operating a level higher than we used to. I don’t really need to context switch into the specific code and how it works (or at least not that much to get started). What I do need to context switch into is the problem. As long as I have a conceptual understanding of the problem that we’re trying to solve, and roughly the shape of the solution we’re going for, I can jump in and be useful.
Will there be some long-term consequences of working this way that we haven’t seen yet? Maybe! I’m honestly not sure. But “maybe there’s a problem we haven’t seen yet” is a very different argument than the one I’d have made a year ago, which was “I’m pretty sure this is a bad idea and we’ll regret it.”
Don’t go deep
This one is less about how my team works and more about the advice I give to the people I manage.
For a long time, my standard advice for engineers on their technical and professional development was the classic “T-shaped” thing. First you build up a solid base of general engineering practices, and then you pick one or two areas and go really deep on them. Become the performance person, or the accessibility person, or the distributed systems person. Or pick a widely used framework and become the expert in it - become a regular contributor to Rails or Phoenix and know all the ins and outs of how it works. That kind of deep expertise used to be one of the most reliable ways to make yourself really valuable.
Now I’m telling people pretty much the opposite. Deep expertise in a specific technology is a lot less valuable when you have a collaborator that has read all of the source code for that framework and can answer any question about it in a few seconds. This is another case (like what I mentioned above) where knowledge is less valuable, but where judgement is still important.
What I’m recommending instead is to still build a very strong foundation in engineering fundamentals, because that’s where I think we as humans offer the most value right now. That’s things like how to architect code, how to design good abstractions, and how to tell when an abstraction isn’t really fitting anymore. It’s being able to look at something and say “this is starting to get a little weak, and we should break it up,” and then come up with what the right abstraction should actually be. It’s noticing that “it seems like we’re missing something here that needs a name,” and being able to find that concept and name it. That’s the stuff that keeps the high-level architecture, the domain modeling and the data modeling at a high level of quality, and that’s where a good engineer really earns their keep these days.
But I’m also telling them that being great at the tech is now only one small part of the job. Being great at the tech is no longer enough. You need to learn more about design. You need to learn about product development and how to be a product manager, at least a little bit. You need to get great at talking to users, doing research and really knowing the business.
I’ve always encouraged that on the teams I’ve led. I’ve believed for a long time that product engineering was the best way to do this job. But I used to think of it as the thing that made someone great, and now I think it’s just table stakes. People who just write code, or who are purely technical, are already much less valuable than they used to be, and I’m not sure how many of those folks will be left in the profession five years from now.
A few more I’m side-eyeing
Once I started looking for these, I started seeing them everywhere. These are a few others where I haven’t fully changed my mind yet, but where I’m a lot less sure of my gut than I used to be:
Rewriting from scratch. “Never do the big rewrite” is about as close to a universal law as we have in this industry. But a lot of the reason rewrites were so dangerous is that they took forever, and the old system kept changing underneath you while you were working on the new one. If a rewrite takes days or weeks instead of years, a lot of that risk goes away.
Building instead of buying. I’ve talked a lot of teams out of building their own internal tools when there’s a SaaS product that does 80% of what they need. But when building the thing that does exactly 100% of what you need takes an afternoon, that math looks pretty different.
Building it three different ways. Writing three different versions of the same feature and throwing two of them away used to be an obviously silly waste of time. Now it’s often the fastest way to figure out which approach is actually right, rather than arguing about it in a design doc. We can skip the “rough mockup” stage of product development and just A-B test with actual users in production if we want, which is a much higher-quality test and leads to things being shipped faster.
Introducing a language or tool nobody on the team knows. “We don’t have anyone who knows Rust, so we shouldn’t use Rust” used to be a pretty much unassailable argument. I’m not so sure it is anymore. Certainly if there are things that require a certain language, like some of the software we use at Beat81 for interfacing with some parts of our system. We don’t have C# knowledge on the team, but it’s also the only driver available for a really good ANT+/BLE receiver we wanted to use.
What I actually got wrong
When I look at these together, I think the pattern is pretty clear. Each of these things was a bad idea because of some cost. I always think about things in terms of cost vs. benefit. Code review by a teammate was worth it because the alternative was nobody else looking at the code at all. Knowledge silos were bad because getting up to speed on someone else’s work was slow and expensive. Deep expertise was valuable because that knowledge was scarce and hard to get.
AI has changed a lot of those costs, and it’s changed them really quickly. The rules that were built on top of those costs don’t automatically update when the costs change, though, and neither does my intuition. My intuition was trained over 15 years in a world where all of those costs were pretty stable, and so it keeps telling me things that were true, with the same confidence it always has.
That’s what really got me in the retro this week. It’s not that I was wrong about one thing (I’m wrong all the time). It’s that the skill I’ve been using to decide whether things are a good idea or not (which has been a big part of my success over my career) is now giving me bad answers a lot more often than it used to.
So I’m trying to change how I use it. When my gut tells me something’s a bad idea, I’m trying to ask the follow-up question: what’s the cost that made this a bad idea, and is that cost still there? Sometimes it is, and the gut feeling is still right. But more and more often, the value proposition may have changed. I’m certainly erring on the side of at least trying more experiments for things I might have shot down in the past.
That’s a weird thing to admit after 15 years of being paid, at least partly, for having good instincts. But I think the leaders who are going to do well over the next few years are the ones who can hold their experience a little more loosely, and who are willing to let their teams prove them wrong. This is a time of massive change for everybody, and so we’ve all got to adapt. Now the thing that I think matters isn’t who has the most experience & knowledge (although that still carries some weight of course), but who can adapt and learn the fastest to acquire new experience & knowledge.

====================================================================================================

Bad ideas that aren't bad anymore
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
03:18:13 UTC
12:18:13 KST

I’ve been working in software for about 15 years now, and over that time I’ve made a lot of mistakes and learned from a lot of other people’s mistakes. This experience is a deep well that I can draw from when discussing possible solutions for both technical and non-technical problems. This is how I know what sorts of solutions sound good in theory but run into problems in practice, or what fringe idea is actually a perfect fit for the problem we’re trying to solve here. This knowledge & experience is a big part of what has made me effective as a leader.
Lately, though, a lot of the things my gut tells me are bad ideas are turning out to work really well. In our team’s retro this week that hit me pretty hard, and it was a real learning moment for me. It made me realize that a big chunk of the intuition I’ve been relying on might not be worth as much as I thought anymore, and that I need to question that experience more frequently.
So I wanted to write down a few of the “bad ideas” that we’re doing right now that are actually working really well, and then to try and figure out what that means for people like me.
Nobody reviews anybody else’s PRs
For pretty much my whole career, code review has worked the same way: one person writes the code and opens the PR, and then somebody else on the team reviews it before it gets merged. The second set of eyes catches bugs, spreads knowledge around the team, and keeps any one person from quietly drifting the codebase off in their own direction. Merging your own PR without review was one of those things that was a “break glass in case of emergency” kind of situation.
About a month ago we changed that completely. We had been struggling with code review due to the increased pace of PRs being opened, and so we decided to try something crazy - just not doing it. Now the engineer working on a ticket works with Claude, which then opens the PR, and then the human working on the ticket reviews Claude’s work. Once CI is green & the Greptile review is clean, if the human working on the ticket feels confident that the solution is solid, they merge the PR. That’s it. No waiting on a teammate to find time to context switch into your change, no pinging people in Slack to please take a look.
And it’s been working great! When I actually think about why it works, it’s because the thing that code review was protecting against is still being protected against. There’s still a second set of eyes on every change - it’s just that the author is Claude and the reviewer is the human who owns the ticket. That human understands the problem better than anyone else on the team, because they’re the one who’s been working on it. They’re a much better reviewer for that change than a teammate who has to page in all the context from scratch. But also, Claude & Greptile are just better at finding the kinds of things that are often found in Code Review, like small bugs or inconsistencies. It’s pretty rare (at least on the teams I’ve worked on) for big things like architectural flaws or unintended product impact to be found at this stage.
I’ve written before about how review gates can be a sign of a lack of trust, so maybe I shouldn’t have been so surprised that removing one worked out. But my gut still said “you can’t just merge stuff without somebody else looking at it,” and my gut was wrong. At least so far that’s been true - we’re only one month into the change, but it’s feeling really good so far.
Splitting the team up across separate streams of work
This is the one that came up in our retro. Recently we had a few people out on holiday, and at the same time we had a couple of parallel and fairly separate streams of work going on. So we split it up, and basically each person owned one stream of work and ran with it.
Historically I really haven’t liked doing this. When everybody on a team is working on a different thing, you get knowledge silos. Only one person knows how the thing they built works, and if they leave or get sick or go on holiday, the team is stuck. Even worse, I think it erodes the feeling of actually being a team. You’re not working together to solve the same problem anymore, you’re just a group of people solving different problems at the same time who happen to share a standup. I’ve pushed back on this kind of setup on basically every team I’ve led.
But in the retro, we talked about it, and we’re just not seeing any of the consequences I was worried about - at least not in terms of knowledge silos. And the reason is that it’s become so easy to get up to speed on someone else’s work. If I need to jump into something a colleague has been working on, I can say to Claude “hey, bring me up to speed on this thing they’ve been building - how does it work, where is it at, and what still needs to be done?” A few minutes later I have what I need to start contributing. I don’t need anybody else to onboard me, and I don’t need to spend a day reading through code to figure out what’s going on.
The thing that’s really changed here is that the cost of context switching has dropped dramatically, because we’re operating a level higher than we used to. I don’t really need to context switch into the specific code and how it works (or at least not that much to get started). What I do need to context switch into is the problem. As long as I have a conceptual understanding of the problem that we’re trying to solve, and roughly the shape of the solution we’re going for, I can jump in and be useful.
Will there be some long-term consequences of working this way that we haven’t seen yet? Maybe! I’m honestly not sure. But “maybe there’s a problem we haven’t seen yet” is a very different argument than the one I’d have made a year ago, which was “I’m pretty sure this is a bad idea and we’ll regret it.”
Don’t go deep
This one is less about how my team works and more about the advice I give to the people I manage.
For a long time, my standard advice for engineers on their technical and professional development was the classic “T-shaped” thing. First you build up a solid base of general engineering practices, and then you pick one or two areas and go really deep on them. Become the performance person, or the accessibility person, or the distributed systems person. Or pick a widely used framework and become the expert in it - become a regular contributor to Rails or Phoenix and know all the ins and outs of how it works. That kind of deep expertise used to be one of the most reliable ways to make yourself really valuable.
Now I’m telling people pretty much the opposite. Deep expertise in a specific technology is a lot less valuable when you have a collaborator that has read all of the source code for that framework and can answer any question about it in a few seconds. This is another case (like what I mentioned above) where knowledge is less valuable, but where judgement is still important.
What I’m recommending instead is to still build a very strong foundation in engineering fundamentals, because that’s where I think we as humans offer the most value right now. That’s things like how to architect code, how to design good abstractions, and how to tell when an abstraction isn’t really fitting anymore. It’s being able to look at something and say “this is starting to get a little weak, and we should break it up,” and then come up with what the right abstraction should actually be. It’s noticing that “it seems like we’re missing something here that needs a name,” and being able to find that concept and name it. That’s the stuff that keeps the high-level architecture, the domain modeling and the data modeling at a high level of quality, and that’s where a good engineer really earns their keep these days.
But I’m also telling them that being great at the tech is now only one small part of the job. Being great at the tech is no longer enough. You need to learn more about design. You need to learn about product development and how to be a product manager, at least a little bit. You need to get great at talking to users, doing research and really knowing the business.
I’ve always encouraged that on the teams I’ve led. I’ve believed for a long time that product engineering was the best way to do this job. But I used to think of it as the thing that made someone great, and now I think it’s just table stakes. People who just write code, or who are purely technical, are already much less valuable than they used to be, and I’m not sure how many of those folks will be left in the profession five years from now.
A few more I’m side-eyeing
Once I started looking for these, I started seeing them everywhere. These are a few others where I haven’t fully changed my mind yet, but where I’m a lot less sure of my gut than I used to be:
Rewriting from scratch. “Never do the big rewrite” is about as close to a universal law as we have in this industry. But a lot of the reason rewrites were so dangerous is that they took forever, and the old system kept changing underneath you while you were working on the new one. If a rewrite takes days or weeks instead of years, a lot of that risk goes away.
Building instead of buying. I’ve talked a lot of teams out of building their own internal tools when there’s a SaaS product that does 80% of what they need. But when building the thing that does exactly 100% of what you need takes an afternoon, that math looks pretty different.
Building it three different ways. Writing three different versions of the same feature and throwing two of them away used to be an obviously silly waste of time. Now it’s often the fastest way to figure out which approach is actually right, rather than arguing about it in a design doc. We can skip the “rough mockup” stage of product development and just A-B test with actual users in production if we want, which is a much higher-quality test and leads to things being shipped faster.
Introducing a language or tool nobody on the team knows. “We don’t have anyone who knows Rust, so we shouldn’t use Rust” used to be a pretty much unassailable argument. I’m not so sure it is anymore. Certainly if there are things that require a certain language, like some of the software we use at Beat81 for interfacing with some parts of our system. We don’t have C# knowledge on the team, but it’s also the only driver available for a really good ANT+/BLE receiver we wanted to use.
What I actually got wrong
When I look at these together, I think the pattern is pretty clear. Each of these things was a bad idea because of some cost. I always think about things in terms of cost vs. benefit. Code review by a teammate was worth it because the alternative was nobody else looking at the code at all. Knowledge silos were bad because getting up to speed on someone else’s work was slow and expensive. Deep expertise was valuable because that knowledge was scarce and hard to get.
AI has changed a lot of those costs, and it’s changed them really quickly. The rules that were built on top of those costs don’t automatically update when the costs change, though, and neither does my intuition. My intuition was trained over 15 years in a world where all of those costs were pretty stable, and so it keeps telling me things that were true, with the same confidence it always has.
That’s what really got me in the retro this week. It’s not that I was wrong about one thing (I’m wrong all the time). It’s that the skill I’ve been using to decide whether things are a good idea or not (which has been a big part of my success over my career) is now giving me bad answers a lot more often than it used to.
So I’m trying to change how I use it. When my gut tells me something’s a bad idea, I’m trying to ask the follow-up question: what’s the cost that made this a bad idea, and is that cost still there? Sometimes it is, and the gut feeling is still right. But more and more often, the value proposition may have changed. I’m certainly erring on the side of at least trying more experiments for things I might have shot down in the past.
That’s a weird thing to admit after 15 years of being paid, at least partly, for having good instincts. But I think the leaders who are going to do well over the next few years are the ones who can hold their experience a little more loosely, and who are willing to let their teams prove them wrong. This is a time of massive change for everybody, and so we’ve all got to adapt. Now the thing that I think matters isn’t who has the most experience & knowledge (although that still carries some weight of course), but who can adapt and learn the fastest to acquire new experience & knowledge.

Who could replace Jensen Huang? A highly unscientific list of possible successors
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
03:13:10 UTC
12:13:10 KST

It’s almost inconceivable that Nvidia would fire its rock star CEO. Yet here we are, on the precipice of this happening. Over at Kalshi, Jensen Huang’s odds of surviving the week are sitting at a lowly 30%. Even though he built one of the world’s most valuable — and, many would argue, dangerously powerful — companies, the tides have completely turned against him. He has confessed to an extramarital affair, but as of Wednesday afternoon it isn’t even clear who he meant: Alice Huang, the Chinese executive suspected by U.S. intelligence of spying for her country, or Kimberly Powell, an Nvidia executive who reports to him and who, five sources told The New York Times in an explosive new report, was also a paramour.
Even if Huang is pushed out as CEO, the board members he has made very rich are unlikely to cut him loose entirely. Barring another shoe dropping, they could keep him on as chairman, a role Nvidia doesn’t currently have. But someone still has to run the company, and there’s no apparent succession plan, which leaves us, like everyone else, to speculate while we wait to see what happens next. While Huang’s 60 direct reports undoubtedly get a closer look, consider this our own parlor game, featuring some obvious and less obvious candidates:
Lisa Su. The AMD CEO runs Nvidia’s most credible rival and is a distant cousin of Huang. The Information floated her name this week, but why would she leave her job for his? Her company stands to benefit from every day that Nvidia spends distracted by an investigation, congressional briefings, and all the mini dramas that are sure to follow. Her best move might be to send a nice note and keep her current corner office.
Eric Schmidt. He was the pick of Jeffrey Sonnenfeld, senior associate dean for leadership studies at the Yale School of Management, who we talked with earlier this week. Schmidt could “parachute in tomorrow and do a very good job,” Sonnenfeld said, and perhaps he could. He ran Google from 2001 to 2011, chaired the National Security Commission on AI from 2018 to 2021, and now runs the rocket company Relativity Space. Chips, software, government, rockets — it’s an impressive range. Then again, Schmidt would bring a lot of baggage when it comes to his own extramarital dalliances. It’s hard to see him clearing the board for that reason.
Lip-Bu Tan. Intel’s CEO knows chips, and right now he might welcome a very soft landing. Last month, Intel reported third-quarter revenue up 21% to $16.5 billion, then warned its fourth-quarter results would fall short of Wall Street’s forecasts. The AI boom has caused a memory-chip shortage, pushing up PC prices and cutting PC sales. That’s hurt Intel (PC chips are a big part of its business), and its stock plunged 20% in after-hours trading on the warning, having already slid more than 30% since September. A move to a company that’s eating everyone’s lunch would be a pretty amazing escape hatch.
Bret Taylor. The former Salesforce co-CEO and OpenAI board chair now runs the AI startup Sierra, and his résumé includes a lot of the biggest brands in Silicon Valley. He co-created Google Maps, served as Facebook’s CTO, and chaired Twitter’s board through its sale to Elon Musk. He then took over as OpenAI’s board chair after the “Blip,” Sam Altman’s firing and fast return. When companies need an unflappable, likable adult in the room, they call Taylor. He probably knows nearly every customer Nvidia has, too.
Ian Buck. If the board feels strongly about having a “builder” at the helm, they could do worse than the Nvidia VP who helped develop CUDA, the chip software that has become central to Nvidia’s dominance in AI. Analysts and former employees have floated him as a possible successor, and Kalshi traders give him a middling shot. He’s no household name, but the Stanford PhD seems more viable if Huang can keep that chairman title and push for a successor he can work with.
Someone else down the hall. Andrew Bell, Nvidia’s senior vice president of hardware engineering, arrived 25 years ago as a fresh Cornell grad and has been on an upward trajectory at the company ever since. Like Buck and dozens of other executives who are presumably being scrutinized, he reports directly to Huang. Nvidia CFO Colette Kress is the insider most often named, but analysts say she isn’t necessarily a natural successor as an innovator and visionary, and Bell has the chip pedigree.
The wild card? Lori Huang. Nobody has floated her, but hear us out: Jensen met his wife as lab partners at Oregon State, so she has been working with him since before Nvidia existed. After graduating, she worked at Silicon Graphics as a microchip engineer (making more money than her husband, he has said), then scaled back to raise the couple’s family, and she now co-leads the family trusts. If the board wants continuity, they could do worse than a humbler Huang who is also a brainiac. There’s a reason they say that behind every great man is a great woman (and vice versa).
Whatever happens next, we’ll know soon.

Lean Grimoire
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
02:36:48 UTC
11:36:48 KST

Back in July, I started experimenting with using modern coding agents to build a number of mathematical visualization applets. With the extremely hectic events of the last few months, I had to set those projects aside; but I recently found a bit of time to work on one such project that I had started back then, which was to modernize a Javascript interactive textbook I had made back in 2018, entitled “QED”, and announced previously on this blog. The purpose of that textbook was to teach propositional and first-order logic in the form of a “crafting game”, where one applied various rules of logic to reach a desired conclusion from a given set of hypotheses.
With a coding agent, I rebuilt the game (which I have given the working title of “Lean Grimoire”) with a number of new features, for instance giving the option to show an in-progress Lean proof of one’s game state. Just for fun, I also added an option to play in a “fantasy mode” in which various game elements are depicted using fantasy-themed flavor text (for instance, mathematical statements become “potions”, mathematical expressions become “scrolls”, and so forth). Here is a typical screenshot, in which one has nearly completed the proof of the assertion given :
I would be interested in having some playtesting of this app to make it more useful as a means to teach logical proofs and/or Lean.

Critical RCE Vulnerability in Apache HTTP Server
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
02:11:11 UTC
11:11:11 KST

Critical RCE Vulnerability in Apache HTTP Server: Everything You Need To Know
Detect and mitigate CVE-2026-119618, a critical RCE vulnerability in Apache HTTP Server. According to Wiz data, 64% of cloud environments have resources vulnerable to CVE-2026-119618. Immediate patching is advised.
A critical synchronization vulnerability (CVE-2026-119618) has been identified in Apache HTTP Server’s HTTP/2 module (mod_http2). The flaw stems from a concurrent execution race condition within the assign_headers() function when handling shared resources. If successfully exploited, a remote, unauthenticated attacker can corrupt heap memory to achieve arbitrary code execution (RCE).
As of the time of this blog’s publication, no exploit is available and there are no reports of exploitation in the wild.
What is CVE-2026-119618?
CVE-2026-119618 is a critical, high-severity vulnerability involving a race condition in the Apache HTTP Server, which was independently discovered by Anthropic and Google Cloud’s Vulnerability Research Team.
Specifically, the flaw exists within the assign_headers() function of the HTTP/2 module (mod_http2). Due to improper synchronization when executing concurrent threads sharing resources, an attacker can trigger heap corruption. This memory corruption can ultimately be leveraged to achieve RCE on the hosting server, allowing unauthorized threat actors to execute arbitrary commands with the privileges of the web server process.
Wiz Research data: what’s the risk to cloud environments?
In cloud environments, a compromised Apache instance running containerized or virtualized workloads can serve as an entry point for lateral movement, potentially exposing adjacent microservices or cloud metadata endpoints.
According to Wiz data 64% of cloud environments have resources vulnerable to CVE-2026-119618, and 51% of cloud environments have publicly exposed instances. Note that this does not take configuration into account.
Which products are affected?
The vulnerability affects deployments utilizing HTTP/2 capabilities on the Apache HTTP Server within the version ranges outlined in the table below:
Which actions should security teams take?
Security operations and engineering teams should execute the following remediation and mitigation steps:
1. Upgrade Deployments
Upgrade Apache HTTP Server: Update affected installations to Apache HTTP Server version 2.4.71 or later. This release addresses the race condition in assign_headers().
Update Standalone Modules: If managing standalone mod_h2 builds from GitHub, upgrade the module to a version newer than 2.0.42.
2. Implement Temporary Mitigations
If immediate patching is not possible due to operational constraints, temporarily disable HTTP/2 support to eliminate the attack vector.
Edit the configuration file (such as httpd.conf) to restrict active protocols to HTTP/1.1 by removing h2 and h2c references:
# Restrict protocols to HTTP/1.1Protocols http/1.1
Wiz customers can use the pre-built query and advisory in the Wiz Threat Center to search for vulnerable instances in their environment.
A threat actor used IAM credentials from Qubole's AWS account to assume integration roles in customer AWS environments. Here's what we observed and how to hunt for it.

I caught Microsoft Defender uploading my unreleased software without asking
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
01:27:10 UTC
10:27:10 KST

Caught Microsoft Red-Handed with Hodometer
Microsoft Defender uploaded programs I’d written and hadn’t released. Its event log recorded 217 sample submissions from my PC over three months. I hadn’t seen a prompt for any of them.
I found them while investigating a lagging connection with Hodometer, a Windows tray tool I built to track traffic by process and destination.
Microsoft’s programs accounted for 4.7 percent of downloads and 4.5 percent of uploads across about 100 hours. Most of the traffic was my browser, development tools and game patches. Defender’s uploads stood out when I looked at the individual processes.
What Defender uploaded
MpCmdRun.exe, Defender’s command-line utility, sent 224 MB to Azure storage in one day. The uploads came in bursts of around 28 MB a minute, with very little coming back.
The traffic was encrypted. To identify the files, I checked Defender’s Operational log. Event 2050 records sample submissions with a file name and SHA-256 hash. The message on my machine read:
  Microsoft Defender Antivirus has uploaded a file for further analysis
Of the 217 entries, 201 were builds of the same Go test program. Most of the other 16 were programs I’d built and hadn’t released. There were 216 different hashes across the submissions; these were mostly fresh binaries, not repeated uploads of an unchanged file. On the busiest day, Defender logged 75 submissions.
Defender’s support log explained the sequence for the uploads it covered. The test binary triggered a low-confidence signature, Defender queried the cloud, and the response supplied an upload address on blob.core.windows.net.
Windows’ per-app data usage records put MpCmdRun.exe at 2.8 GB uploaded over the last 30 days, about 80 percent of this PC’s uploads to Microsoft in that period. That’s the process’s total traffic. I don’t have a byte count for each submitted file.
What Microsoft means by safe samples
Microsoft documents this behaviour. The default is “Send safe samples automatically.” Its examples include .bat, .scr, .dll and .exe files. Safe, in this context, means unlikely to contain personally identifiable information. Files judged likely to contain personal data are supposed to require a prompt.
I object to uploading my unreleased builds under that default. The absence of personal information doesn’t tell Microsoft whether I’m willing to share the program.
New binaries are a normal part of development. In my case, running tests kept producing new builds of one program, and those builds made up almost all the submissions. I only learned about them after checking the traffic and the logs.
Sample submission changed back after I disabled it
On October 28, I ran Set-MpPreference -SubmitSamplesConsent 2 to disable sample submission. At 17:56, Event 5007 recorded the change from value 1, send safe samples automatically, to 2, never send.
At 19:53 that evening, another entry recorded the reverse: 2 back to 1.
I don’t know what changed it back. I couldn’t find anything I’d run that would reset it, and the logs needed to identify the responsible process had already rotated out. A quick scan started under my account nine seconds after the change, so a click in Windows Security is one possible explanation.
There were no sample uploads logged during the nearly two hours it was disabled, and 25 after it was enabled again. I’d turned submission off and hadn’t realised the setting had changed back.
Other files in the logs
The submissions also included a Python script from my Recycle Bin, uploaded in August.
In November, Microsoft’s cloud supplied an upload address for a .tmp data file belonging to a local development database. That attempt failed; it wasn’t a completed upload. I hadn’t seen a consent prompt for either file.
The extensions in Microsoft’s documentation are examples, so a Python script being selected doesn’t by itself contradict the policy. Check scripts and data files as well as compiled programs when you review the logs.
Full command lines in the support log
Over four days, Defender’s support log contained 85 entries marked “command line reported as lowfi.” They included full curl and PowerShell command lines. Seventy-eight contained my user folder and project folder names. An older detection record also held a command line containing text I’d typed into another program.
Microsoft’s cloud-protection documentation says the metadata contains no personal data and that file names are hashed. My local logs contained the full paths. I couldn’t determine whether those strings were hashed or otherwise changed before transmission, so those records don’t establish that Microsoft received the command lines exactly as I saw them.
Defender also made 1,802 cloud requests during those four days. That is a request count, separate from the 217 file submissions across three months.
Copilot and the other connections
The Windows connection audit showed Copilot activity at each of the three sign-ins in a 22-hour period. Each time, it made between 41 and 54 connections to Microsoft. I hadn’t opened the app and couldn’t find an enabled startup entry for it. I haven’t identified what launched it.
The monthly traffic was small: 31 MB down and 6 MB up. I still wanted to know why an app I wasn’t using was making connections when I signed in.
I’d already disabled the main Windows telemetry service on this PC. Its records showed no upload for three months. Other components continued to connect: devicecensus.exe ran twice in one morning, and Defender’s core service made 104 connections in 22 hours to ecs.office.com and mobile.events.data.microsoft.com. Office and OneDrive helpers also connected to the latter host. The queued error-reporting task ran that morning too.
The connection records identify programs and destinations, not the contents of encrypted requests. And this wasn’t a large share of my bandwidth: in a separate 15-minute capture, Microsoft destinations accounted for 0.9 MB out of 343 MB. Disabling that one telemetry service hadn’t stopped the other components from communicating.
How I used Hodometer
An AI agent did most of the digging. I gave it access to Hodometer’s local socket, which requires my approval before a client can connect. It used the process history to identify Defender’s upload, then read the Windows logs to find the files involved.
I built that interface because I wanted an agent to investigate traffic directly. It can inspect connections and handshakes, capture one process’s packets, and issue kill or firewall-block commands. The Sentinel design in the same repository describes using the interface for continuous monitoring and investigation.
Check the logs on your PC
You can check Defender’s submissions without installing Hodometer. This shows Event 2050 entries from the last 90 days:
Get-WinEvent -FilterHashtable @{
    LogName = 'Microsoft-Windows-Windows Defender/Operational'
    Id = 2050
    StartTime = (Get-Date).AddDays(-90)
} | Select-Object TimeCreated, Message | Format-List
Check the file paths in those messages. To look for changes to sample submission in the same period:
Get-WinEvent -FilterHashtable @{
    LogName = 'Microsoft-Windows-Windows Defender/Operational'
    Id = 5007
    StartTime = (Get-Date).AddDays(-90)
} | Where-Object Message -match 'SubmitSamplesConsent' |
    Select-Object TimeCreated, Message | Format-List
Value 1 means send safe samples automatically; 2 means never send. These queries can only show events still retained in the log.
To watch the traffic, download Hodometer.exe from the releases page and run it as administrator. Find MpCmdRun.exe in the process table and double-click it to inspect its connections. You can export that process’s packets as pcapng for Wireshark. A connection to Azure storage alone doesn’t identify a sample upload; check it against the Defender records.
The setting is Automatic sample submission, under Windows Security → Virus & threat protection → Manage settings. Microsoft says disabling it also disables block at first sight based on sample analysis. Detection metadata is still sent. On a development machine, I want control over which builds leave the PC, but changing this setting does give up part of Defender’s protection.
If you build software on Windows, check your build paths in the submission log and the history of that setting.

United Airlines Bans AI Agents From Booking Flights On Behalf of Travelers
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
00:00:00 UTC
09:00:00 KST

United Airlines Bans AI Agents From Booking Flights On Behalf of Travelers 52
			
		 	
				United Airlines is banning unauthorized third-party AI agents from booking flights, checking in passengers or making account changes on travelers' behalf. CBS News reports:  To make an airline booking, an AI agent requires access to a customer's personal information, including credentials like payment information. United has a problem with that, saying that "sharing account credentials with personal, third-party, AI assistants can expose sensitive information, and actions taken by these tools may result in unintended purchases, account changes, or use of MileagePlus miles."
The carrier also said that using an AI agent to, for example, check in for a flight could violate federal regulations, including rules requiring travelers to verify in person that their luggage does not contain banned substances. "As a result, United is taking steps to restrict access -- directly within our digital platforms and indirectly via travel agencies and aggregators -- to prohibit use by unauthorized, third-party personal AI agents or assistants," said the airline, which is updating its booking and ticketing policies to reflect the new rule.  "United is saying they don't want to add yet another, and potentially even more powerful, intermediary than they already face in terms of Google search, price-comparison metasearch sites and online travel agencies," said Henry Harteveldt, an aviation analyst at Atmosphere Research Group. "Flight searches cost airlines money, and undoubtedly United wants to avoid the expense associated with the surge of AI bot flight searches."

Modern Web Guidance 1.0: The web platform expertise your coding agent is missing
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-11
23:22:29 UTC
08:22:29 KST

Published: November 10, 2026
In this new era of agentic web development, developers expect their coding agents to be experts at building for the web. And while the latest frontier coding models are quite capable and can usually produce something that works, if you ask a web development expert to review the AI-generated code, they can almost always find ways to make the code faster, more accessible, or more secure.
Chrome is investing heavily in the future of agentic web development, and to address this challenge, we introduced an early preview of Modern Web Guidance at Google I/O in May. Modern Web Guidance is a set of skills that embed web platform expertise, best practices, and browser compatibility data directly into your coding agents.
Today we're excited to announce the stable release of Modern Web Guidance version 1.0, which includes:
- New modernization workflow that scans your existing codebase to help your agent find opportunities to safely modernize.
- Expanded guide database targeting hundreds of verified AI knowledge gaps with expert-vetted, cross-browser solutions.
- Deeper human expert review for every guide, including sign-off from content area tech leads and a dedicated accessibility review on our user-facing guides.
- Full evaluation coverage across all of our guides with a public eval dashboard showing the measurable improvements compared to agents without Modern Web Guidance.
With version 1.0, we're also excited to share that Modern Web Guidance is becoming a cross-browser effort, with Firefox and Edge joining Chrome to support, improve cross-browser compatibility, and contribute to the project. Building on our shared work with Baseline, our goal is to ensure that anyone coding with an agent can take full advantage of the power and capabilities of the modern web platform.
Regardless of what models or agent harnesses you're using, if you're building for the web, Modern Web Guidance belongs in your agentic workflow.
To install Modern Web Guidance, run this command from your project directory:
npx modern-web-guidance@latest install
Or if you already installed an earlier version, you can update to 1.0 by running:
npx modern-web-guidance@latest update
For additional installation instructions or to learn more, check out the Modern Web Guidance documentation, or watch this video for a brief overview:
Why developers and businesses should use Modern Web Guidance
It's undeniable that coding agents have helped developers write code faster than ever before. But in a world where everyone is using coding agents, speed is no longer a competitive advantage.
These days, it's more important than ever to differentiate on fundamental factors like usability and quality of experience. Here are a few ways Modern Web Guidance can help:
Reinforce best practices that models often miss
While frontier coding models do have a good grasp on many best practices, our evals show that there are still a number of important areas where they fall short, such as:
- Accessibility: Keyboard focus and inert management, reduced-motion support, and accessible form validation.
- User experience: Swipe and touch gestures, dialog and popover transitions, and light/dark theme implementations.
- Performance: Main-thread task scheduling, offscreen rendering isolation, and layout shift prevention.
- Security: Safe HTML sanitization, strict Content Security Policies, and passkey credential management.
Accessibility best practices are particularly important to get right because mistakes like bad ARIA can make a page less accessible than no ARIA at all. For version 1.0, our user-facing guides were reviewed and tested with real assistive technologies by accessibility experts who rely on them every day.
For a deeper look at the specific mistakes models make in each of these areas, read Top 10 things AI gets wrong in web development.
Use the best web feature for the job
AI models are very good at figuring out how to make something work with JavaScript, but in many cases, there are HTML or CSS features that can do the same thing with much less code. And since they're handled by the browser, they usually perform better and come with accessibility affordances. For example:
- Features to animate popovers and dialogs
- Features to enhance scroll- and swipe-based interactions
- Better APIs for smooth and fluid page transitions
New features can also improve the experience of a site in ways that matter to users. For example:
- Passkeys and WebAuthn: For faster and more secure login experiences
- Autofill: To improve checkout flows (especially on mobile) that often fail when using custom JavaScript-based form controls
Modern Web Guidance is designed to recommend the best feature for the job, based on your browser support requirements. Its guides have embedded Baseline and browser compatibility data, so it can tailor its recommendations to your project's needs.
Scan your codebase and find opportunities to modernize
Most web development doesn't happen in brand-new projects. It happens in existing codebases that have accumulated years of outdated workarounds, unnecessary dependencies, and polyfills that are no longer needed.
Version 1.0 adds a new modernization workflow that can analyze your existing HTML, CSS, JavaScript, and package.json to find opportunities to safely upgrade, and then guide your agent through the changes.
/modern-web-guidance Modernize the code in this directory
In this first release, the modernization workflow uses static analysis to check for a limited set of patterns. We chose to start with a narrow scope that yields predictable results, and then expand from there. Over time, we plan to cover many more areas, including agentic audits where your agent reasons about your code directly instead of just matching known patterns.
Stay up to date as the web platform evolves
The web platform is a moving target. New features ship in browsers every milestone, and existing features that you perhaps couldn't use before are becoming Baseline with each new browser release.
Since Modern Web Guidance is built on top of Baseline compatibility data, and its recommendations are maintained by browser engineers, web standards experts, and accessibility specialists, these recommendations evolve along with the platform itself, and now your project can too.
Learn about new web features and best practices
While Modern Web Guidance is primarily built as a tool for agents, one thing we've heard from many developers is that they've actually found the guidance to be quite useful on its own—as a learning resource.
Even in this era of agentic coding, there's still value in building up domain expertise. And Modern Web Guidance can help you do that: either by reading the guides themselves, or using the CLI to search use cases and discover new techniques that may be relevant to your project.
Pair Modern Web Guidance with Chrome DevTools for agents
In agentic web development, authoring code is only one part of the workflow. You also need to run that code to ensure it works as expected. This is why we recommend developers use Modern Web Guidance along with Chrome DevTools for agents.
- Modern Web Guidance gives your agent the up-front expertise needed to write code following modern best practices.
- Chrome DevTools for agents gives your agent runtime visibility so it can verify, debug, and optimize that code across a wide range of device capabilities, user preferences, and network conditions.
Together these help your agent arrive at a better solution in fewer turns. Next are a few examples of how you can use these tools together to maximize effectiveness:
Optimize until you hit your performance target
Modern Web Guidance can teach your agent how to effectively break up long-running JavaScript tasks that block the main thread, but it can't test your code to see how long those JavaScript tasks actually block the main thread for.
With Chrome DevTools for agents, you can run a performance trace on a simulated low-end CPU to see whether or not you have a problem, and then use the performance advice from Modern Web Guidance to iterate until your code never blocks the main thread for more than 50 milliseconds (so it doesn't contribute to poor INP).
Test and refine your animations
Modern Web Guidance can show your agent how to properly and performantly animate features like modal dialogs, tooltips, and popover menus, but even with this guidance you still need to verify that the animations actually work and look good when used in the context of your full application.
Chrome DevTools for agents can run your code, interact with the page, and take a screen recording of the animation in action. It can then study the recording to ensure both that the animation runs and that it fits the style and feel of the rest of the site.
You can also use Chrome DevTools for agents to verify that when prefers-reduced-motion is set, the component honors the user's preferences.
Cache views without memory leaks
Modern Web Guidance can teach your agent how to make SPA transitions feel instant by rendering and caching views in memory, while also properly hiding them from the accessibility tree.
However, when doing this it's important to ensure you properly clean up unused views to not leak memory if the user stays on the page for a long time. Chrome DevTools for agents has memory debugging tools, so your agent can ensure memory use isn't increasing without bound.
For a closer look at how to use Modern Web Guidance and Chrome DevTools for agents together, check out this video:
Success stories
Engineering teams around the world are already using Modern Web Guidance and Chrome DevTools for agents to improve code quality, speed up delivery, and eliminate manual debugging bottlenecks:
Pensionbazaar and Policybazaar
Pensionbazaar, a new retirement planning platform launched by Indian fintech company Paisabazaar, paired Modern Web Guidance with Chrome DevTools for agents to build and verify new pages, shortening turnaround by more than 90% (from about four days to a few hours) and reducing UI bugs on new screens by over 90%. Its sister platform Policybazaar, one of India's leading insurance platforms with more than 97 million registered customers, used Chrome DevTools for agents to automate performance audits across about 30 high-traffic landing pages, cutting manual auditing effort by 92%:
"For Pensionbazaar, our priority was building quality code from day one to avoid technical debt later. Modern Web Guidance steered our coding agent to adopt modern web standards and performance best practices while respecting our specific browser compatibility targets. This made our frontend code reviews substantially easier and faster, ultimately improving our development turnaround time."
—Aman Soni, Engineering Manager, Policybazaar
WeTV
WeTV, Tencent's international video streaming platform, added an AI coding agent to its CI/CD pipeline that uses Chrome DevTools for agents to diagnose performance regressions and Modern Web Guidance to fix them using modern web platform features. The agent now automatically diagnoses and resolves 90% of failed performance checks, cutting debugging and resolution time by 87% (from four hours to 30 minutes, including manual evaluation):
"The results have been very effective in terms of both speed and quality. The diagnostic insights provided by Chrome DevTools for agents and Modern Web Guidance gave us reliable references for fixing complex layout issues. We perform strict verification after every fix, and the current success rate and stability have significantly improved our release confidence."
—Yangjie Zheng, Head of Engineering, WeTV (Tencent)
note inc.
note inc., one of Japan's largest creator and media platforms with over 90 million monthly active users, used Modern Web Guidance alongside its in-house design system as part of an ongoing effort to modernize its Next.js frontend. Across a 180-run evaluation on its production codebase, Modern Web Guidance raised pass rates on modern web checks from 61% to 94% by steering agents to use native HTML and CSS features instead of custom JavaScript for scroll animations, tooltips, and accordions. It also increased proper LCP image prioritization by 28% across 431 merged PRs:
"Our mission at note is 'Start, and Keep on Creating,' so our platform has to stay fast and accessible for every creator and reader. As more of our code is written with AI coding agents, Modern Web Guidance helps them reach for modern, standards-based solutions instead of falling back on older JavaScript patterns. It also points out details that are easy to miss, such as image loading priority and form accessibility. Combined with our design system, it lets us keep improving note while keeping our UI consistent."
—Takehiko Itabashi, VP of Technology, note inc.
MetaMask
MetaMask, a self-custodial financial platform with more than 100 million downloads across approximately 190 countries, runs Modern Web Guidance as an automated review bot on every pull request to its open source browser extension, configured with just a three-line prompt. In its first five weeks, the bot reviewed roughly 480 pull requests and commented on 64 of them, leading engineers to fix 43 findings (mostly accessibility, HTML semantics, and forms issues that linters don't catch) with a median time of 15 minutes from comment to fix:
"The reviews are really helpful. They've helped surface and get our engineers familiar with modern APIs like Interest invokers and the Popover API. It hasn't increased PR review times, if anything it likely reduced them. The issues MWG caught would have otherwise required a human reviewer familiar with these modern web techniques."
—Francis Nepomuceno, UX Engineer, MetaMask
Ecosystem and community support
Modern Web Guidance started as an initiative within Chrome, but reaching this 1.0 milestone was a collaborative effort with numerous experts across the web community.
Thank you to the content area tech leads overseeing each web platform area, our browser partners at Firefox and Edge, the independent web standards and accessibility experts who authored and tested guides, and all of the open source contributors on GitHub who helped shape this release!
If you spot a gap in what coding agents know about the web, or want to help improve an existing guide, see the contribution guide to get involved.
Get started with Modern Web Guidance
To add Modern Web Guidance to your project, run the CLI installer from your project directory:
npx modern-web-guidance@latest install
Or if you already installed an earlier version, you can update to 1.0 by running:
npx modern-web-guidance@latest update
In addition to the CLI, you can install and update Modern Web Guidance directly through most popular coding agent plugin marketplaces, including Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, Antigravity, Copilot, WebStorm, Kimi Code, and Grok Build.

Rands Fall Housecleaning
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-11
23:13:05 UTC
08:13:05 KST

Riffing on the work started with Ask Rands, I’ve been spending the days before the holiday seasons polishing existing features while also building some new ones for the blog.
Briefs
If you’ve been watching the RSS feed for the past week, you’ve seen an increase in the number of posts. During the last major redesign of the site, Alex and I created a post type called Excerpts — the idea was a new post type for links and small bits of content. I posted many of these, but then stopped… I don’t know why. Essay-length pieces seemed to be more my jam?
With my robot-friends, I’ve built a new post type called Briefs, which include photos, text, songs, and links. Yes, I know… hello 2005… but I wanted a path to post more frequently on smaller topics, so now we have Briefs.
Tags
Building Briefs revealed another feature that was popular, I don’t know, two decades ago? Tags. Yeah, Web 2.0.
In my head at redesign time, WordPress Categories were tags, but over the years Categories became more defined themes (Management, Product, Tech Life, etc.) than tags. Thanks again to the robots, I’ve added tags to Briefs, as well as all existing posts. There’s a very tight vocabulary to prevent tag-bloat, and you can find a very well-organized list of tags here.
Tags now appear at the bottom of posts. I’ve done some behind-the-scenes wizardry to map Categories to Tags such that I hope you never notice. Holler if you do, and it’s annoying.
How Ask Rands Works
One of the unexpected benefits of the Ask Rands feature is the questions Grumbles has no idea how to answer. He logs these questions and tells me. Every morning. Thanks, Grumbles.
Many readers have asked Grumbles how Ask Rands works. There are two parts:
On WP Engine:
- On randsinrepose.com, the /ask/ page and the Ask button are part of the site’s theme.
- A search runs in your browser. Yes, you are downloading a snapshot of every article, then searching. This costs nothing and requires no server. I’m hoping you have not noticed this.
On Railway:
- A small answer server lives here. This is Grumbles. You send a question to this server, which calls Claude and streams the answer back. Many of you have asked about the Grumbles “voice”. This is the prompt that I provide to Claude as a template for Grumbles’ personality1.
- Repeated questions are stored and echoed back to reduce cost.
- All questions, anonymized, are sitting in a small database along with answers which I review daily.
- The server also enforces spending caps, per-reader limits, and timeouts.
Finally, Cloudflare throws up those “are you a person” checks and does rate limiting.
Since launch, I’ve added the following features to Ask Rands:
- Contextually aware follow-ups.
- A smarter “I don’t know” — Grumbles was claiming I covered topics that I didn’t.
- Reversal detection. If I changed my mind about something, you see both sides of the argument.
- A bunch of better matches for specific questions.
- Made it clear that Grumbles doesn’t search my books — just the blog posts where most of the books started.
I’ve been fascinated by the questions being asked. Grumbles highlights gaps in my current writing on preventing gaslighting, dealing with underperformers, absent leadership, and other specific bad cases.
And finally…
Search 2.0
At the top of the least-visited top-level page lives a search box. This is a Web 1.0 dumb as dirt keyword search that I have confirmed you never use. This page has been untouched for years, primarily because I believe most folks look for Rands stuff on Google.
Google AI Overviews decimate traffic — a 50%+ decrease in clicks to the first link. That’s good for Google, but bad for content producers. Here’s the thing: thanks to the robots, you are no longer as dependent on Google for search. Ask Rands and Tags are both features that have been vastly easier to build with the help of the robots, but I don’t think that’s what is going to drive humans to human-produced content.
The voice of Google AI Overviews is the same soulless sound as many of the default robots. You have the same reaction to AI-generated art: this is slop, and I want heart.
My hope is that, because many of the services and tools have been democratized with the help of the robots, content producers can provide you, dear reader, a more compelling experience and more reasons to hang out.
We’ll see.
- Here’s an example of that prompt: At most one joke per answer about being a robot, and skip the worn ones: nobody thanking you or asking you, never being invited, unpaid, the Mac Studio, rebooting. Most asides should come from the reader’s situation, not yours. ↩︎
