Why are we still working this hard?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-11
20:05:29 UTC
05:05:29 KST

In 1973, what a forty-hour week of work produced takes sixteen hours to produce today.
That’s according to Bureau of Economic Analysis (BEA) input output tables. An hour of work in American business now makes two and a half times what it made in 1973. For every forty hours that used to be needed, twenty-four have been freed.
You have probably not noticed them. The forty-hour week was written into American law by an act of 1938, and hasn’t changed. Counted for each head of population, the hours worked in American business in 2024 were 10 percent higher than in 1973. As a country, the US took the whole of fifty years’ progress as more output and none of it as time.
It was supposed to go differently. In 1930 the economist John Maynard Keynes wrote a short essay about the world his grandchildren would live in. He predicted that “the standard of life in progressive countries one hundred years hence will be between four and eight times as high as it is to-day.” And with so much plenty, he thought, we would hardly need to work at all: “Three-hour shifts or a fifteen-hour week” would be enough. He is usually said to have been right about the wealth and wrong about the leisure. Output per person in the United States in 2024 was 7.8 times its level in 1930. But look again at the sixteen hours. He was right about both. The fifteen-hour week arrived in terms of output, but not in the workweek.
I have spent a long time trying to find out where those twenty-four hours went, with a co-author, a small economy built on paper, and more national accounts than I’d like to admit. This essay is what I found. Eighteen of the hours never reached ordinary paychecks. The other six reached them in a form that couldn’t be taken as time. Both happened for the same reason, which has to do with what a wage is for. The same reason explains why people are frightened of machines that ought to be good news. And it points to a way out that is simpler, and nearer, than I expected when I started.
A note: The numbers are American, because the United States publishes the accounts this needs. I live in Sweden but it’s probably quite a similar story. The formal paper underpinning this work is here:
https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=7226858
What is a wage for?
Three things:
A wage is a price. It tells a business what an hour of someone’s time costs, so that the hour gets used where it is worth most, and it tells people which work is wanted. A wage is good at this.
A wage is also a “ticket”. It is how nearly everyone gets a share of what is made. Bread reaches me because I have money, and I have money because I sold some hours. That isn’t a law of nature. It is an arrangement, and it has worked so well for so long that we’ve stopped seeing it as one.
And a wage is a place: a shape to the day, people to be among, a sign that somebody needs you.
One thing can do three jobs only while the jobs pull the same way. For two centuries they did, because of the kind of machine we had. The machines of the industrial age were strong and stupid. They could lift, spin, stamp and haul, and they couldn’t see, judge or decide. Nearly everything worth making took both kinds of ability, so every new machine needed people standing next to it, and the owners of machines had to bid for those people. The price of an hour rose with everything else, and a ticket made of that price was a good ticket.
The machines being built now can see, read, write and decide. I write software for a living and use them every day, and I find it harder each year to name a kind of work that is safely out of their reach.
My claim is that the price and the ticket have been pulling apart for fifty years, and that each is damaging the other, and that fundamentally this is driven by changes in technology.
How important is this?
Probably a lot. It surprises me just as much as you that some random 26 year old trans girl might work this out, but the maths has been checked, the paper is going through review, and I’ve done everything I can to try and break it. If you can see what’s really wrong with it: wilson@wilsoniumite.com
Follow a dollar you spend in a shop. Some of it pays the people who worked to make the thing. Some of it pays whoever owns what was used to make it: the oven, the truck, the building, the ground underneath. America’s national accounts are detailed enough to chase a dollar all the way back through every supplier, and when you do it for 2023, of what is paid to Americans for making the things households buy, 66 percent is pay for work and 34 percent goes to owners.
A third of the economy, in other words, already pays people for owning things, with no work asked of them. That would be a curiosity if we all held a bit of both. We don’t.
The poorest tenth of American households lives mostly on government benefits. From the second tenth to the ninth, about four-fifths of what a household earns in the market is pay for work. Then, in the richest tenth, it changes all at once, and pay and ownership are about half and half. That one tenth receives two-thirds of all the income from ownership.
Benefits look like a second source for everyone else. But 68 cents of every tax dollar in America is raised from pay for work, so a benefit is mostly a claim on somebody else’s wage. Follow the money far enough and nine households in ten are living on each other’s wages. We live off the earth and off the machines we’ve built on it. Our share of what they produce reaches us through one narrow channel, which is being paid for a job.
By the way at this point you may notice that “machines” sounds like capital, and yes, that would be the technical term. But to try and escape what we usually feel when we hear those terms, I will use work instead of labor, machines instead of capital, and land instead of non-produced inputs (the term used in our paper).
Where did the eighteen hours go?
Until 1973, what an hour of work produced and what an hour of ordinary pay could buy rose together. Between 1950 and 1973 the first rose by 81 percent and the second by 66 percent. (By ordinary pay I mean the hourly pay of everyone on a private payroll who isn’t a manager or a supervisor, measured against the prices of everything except housing.) Then they parted. Since 1973 the output of an hour has risen two and a half times, and an hour of ordinary pay buys 19 percent more. You might have heard of this as “the great stagnation” and yes, I claim to in large part be able to explain what happened, that technology is what changed. Consider that moors law is usually said to start around that period, and you begin to see what I mean.
So to buy what forty hours of ordinary pay bought in 1973 now takes thirty-four hours. Making it takes sixteen. The eighteen in between went somewhere else.
I would like to tell you they went to the owners of the machines. About a fifth of the gap did. About two-fifths of it is pay spreading out among workers, with the top pulling away from the ordinary worker. The rest I can’t separate cleanly. It is a tangle of prices, benefits and measures that don’t line up.
But look at what the largest piece is. Daron Acemoglu, who shared the Nobel prize in economics in 2024, and Pascual Restrepo estimate that between 50 and 70 percent of the change in America’s wage structure over the four decades to 2016 came from “relative wage declines of worker groups specialized in routine tasks in industries experiencing rapid automation.” Machines learned to do what ordinary workers did before they learned to do what the best-paid did.
So both pieces are the same thing happening. A wage is a price, and a price follows scarcity. It doesn’t follow effort, and it doesn’t follow need. The day a machine learns your job, you are working exactly as hard as you did the day before, and your hour is worth less. That is how a price ought to behave, and it is a fine way to decide who should do what. It is a strange way to decide who gets to live well.
That is the price shrinking the ticket. So far it has been slow.
And the other six hours?
Ordinary pay did rise, by a fifth. That could have been six hours off the week at the same standard of living. Instead the hours went up, and I don’t think it was because nobody wanted the time.
Think about what a price tells you. When I pay three dollars for a loaf of bread, the baker learns something true about me: the bread was worth at least three dollars to me, because I could have walked away. A wage is supposed to carry the same kind of information the other way. If I take a job at fifteen dollars an hour, the hour must have been worth less than that to me, or I would have kept it. A great deal of economics leans on this. It is why a job that somebody is willing to do is assumed to be a job worth doing.
It works only if I could have said no. If selling hours is the only way I eat, I will sell them for whatever they fetch, and the wage records how badly I needed the money. It says very little about what the hour was worth to me. It might have been an afternoon with my child, or a visit to my mother, or the Tuesday I would have spent running the club. Those lost a contest they were never allowed to enter. An auction in which one side can’t walk away tells you very little about what the thing was worth.
This is the ticket spoiling the price. Because the wage is the ticket, most people can’t refuse it, and a price that can’t be refused has stopped being a price. When we argue over “does money represent value”, this is one of the main reasons that isn’t true.
Two things then happen. The first is that some work gets done that isn’t worth doing. Take any job that produces a little more than the wage it pays and less than the hour is worth to the person doing it. The job covers its costs. The employer is doing nothing wrong. And every hour of it uses up something worth more than what it makes, with the whole of the loss falling on the worker. Nobody can see this, because all the prices look fine. Think uber or doordash. Would you ask your friend to pick you up because you are drunk and pay them a few dollars for it, knowing they’d rather be asleep? Maybe, just maybe, that job shouldn’t exist, at least not done by a person.
The second is that our economy has no way of saying “enough.” Other markets can. When there is more of something than people want, its price falls and less of it gets made. The market for work can’t, because for the person selling, a price that falls far enough means not eating. So we treat every job as a good in itself and every hour sold as proof that it was wanted. I once asked a friend why we assume that everyone ought to be working, and she said of course they should, she’d like to be able to buy more stuff. It’s a fair answer, and it contains an assumption, which is that every extra hour worked makes more of what we want. Labor is a resource, like water, and we don’t drain the oceans looking for a use for every drop.
I can’t put a number on what this costs, and the reason I can’t is part of the argument. Everything we measure about work is a record of what people did when they could not refuse. It is like trying to learn how much people like a food by watching what they buy when it is the only thing on the shelf. So let me ask you instead.
Do the people you know have more free time than their parents had at the same age? In a country that has made itself two and a half times as productive within one working lifetime, do they seem less pressed? When they do take time, for a new child or a dying parent, does it feel like something they are free to choose, or like something they will pay for later? And of the things that seem to have thinned out around you, the visits, the clubs, the neighbors who knew each other, how many were things that anyone was ever paid to do?
The harder evidence points the same way. Where ordinary people did gain free time over the last century, most of it came at the two ends of life, as longer schooling and longer retirement. Those are the two stretches that come with a ticket that isn’t a wage: a parent or a loan at one end, a pension at the other. And when researchers in the United States gave 1,000 low-income people $1,000 a month for three years, with no conditions, those people took roughly a sixth of the money as time. They received $11,400 a year more than a comparison group, and their other income ended up about $1,900 a year lower. The authors call that “a moderate labor supply effect.” People who could refuse a little did refuse a little.
What happens if machines get much better?
So far both failures have been slow. Nobody knows whether machines are about to get much better, or how fast. What I can do is show you what happens in an economy where they do.
An economic model is a small economy on paper, simple enough to solve and fitted to the real one wherever the two can be compared. In mine, businesses give each task to whoever does it more cheaply, a person or a machine. People are far better than machines at some tasks and only a little better at others. Machines have to be built, out of other machines, some human work and some land. Whoever pays for a machine wants a return of 10 percent a year. And there is only so much land. Fitted to the United States of today, this economy pays 60 percent of its income for work, 31 percent to the owners of machines and 9 percent to the owners of land.
Now turn one dial. Let machines get steadily better at everything, until they are a hundred times better relative to us than they are now, and let them learn to build one another.
For a long while very little happens. When machines are ten times better, work is still paid 52 percent of all income, because people move to what they are best at. Then, at about 35 times, the machines that could replace even the hardest hour of human work take less than an hour of human work to build, and from there no wage can stay above what the replacement costs. By the hundredfold point work is paid 17 percent of all income. At ten thousand times it is paid less than 1 percent.
Here is something fun though about this economic model. Technically, measured in “stuff”, nobody in this economy gets poorer. But, what stuff you can buy has changed:
At the hundredfold point total income is 32 times what it was, and an hour of work buys 14 times as many goods as it does today. What an hour stops buying is land: an hour’s pay rents about a twelfth of the land it does now. And the income of those who own machines and land is 66 times what it was.
Look at what has happened to the two jobs. As a price, the wage is working perfectly. It is telling everyone, accurately, that human hours are no longer what production is short of. As a ticket it has all but failed. It delivers a sixth of the income, in the middle of the largest boom there has ever been.
Who would that fall on? If ownership stayed as it is today and work were paid 17 percent of income, the richest tenth of American households would receive 62 percent of all market income, up from 46. The poorer half would go from 14 and a half percent to 10. People sometimes hope that an economy like that would seize up for want of customers. It wouldn’t. The owners are the customers, and the economy would turn to face them. Nothing in economics says an economy has to be for everybody.
Keep three cautions in mind. The dial measures technology and not time: a hundredfold improvement takes 117 years at 4 percent a year, the pace at which machinery improved for its price after the Second World War, and 25 years at 20 percent, and nobody knows the pace. If people turn out to have abilities that no machine can match at any price, the same dial stops with work at 48 percent. And it is a model, with one kind of worker and no politics.
So what would fix it?
There are two problems here with one cause. We ask the wage to be a price and a ticket. Being the ticket, it can’t be refused, so it has stopped pricing time. Being a price, it follows scarcity, so it is a ticket that shrinks whenever a machine learns something. The repair is to stop asking. Let the wage be a price, which it is good at, and give people a second ticket.
The question is what a second ticket can be made of.
It can’t be made of wages. A benefit paid out of today’s taxes is, for 68 cents in the dollar, wage earners paying each other. It moves the first ticket around.
It can’t be made by taxing what machines earn, which was my own first idea. In the model, a 20 percent tax on what machines earn their owners raises 6.3 percent of national income, and wages fall by 6.1 percent of national income. The reason is that investors build machines until the last one just pays the return they want. Tax the return and they build fewer, until the ones that remain earn the old return after tax. The owners keep their rate of return on a smaller pile. The people who work alongside the machines have fewer of them to work with, and are paid less. The tax goes straight through the owners and lands on the wage.
What works is owning the machines, and there is a way to do it that disturbs nothing. Let a company deduct the full cost of every investment from its taxable profit on the day it makes it. Then on every new machine the government is, in effect, putting up a fifth of the cost and collecting a fifth of what the machine earns. That is a partnership. The investor’s return on their own four-fifths is untouched, so just as many machines get built. The public gets its fifth, which is 6.2 percent of national income in the model, and wages don’t change. The honest catch is the machines that already exist, whose owners lose about a fifth, once.
Land is different, for one reason. Nobody makes it. Tax machines and there will be fewer machines. Tax land and there is as much land as before. Thomas Paine, the pamphleteer of the American Revolution, argued in 1797 that “Man did not make the earth,” and that every landowner therefore owed the public a ground-rent, to be paid out to everyone. “It is not charity but a right,” he wrote. I reached that idea on my own before I’d heard of him, which I’ve decided to find encouraging, and for a while I thought it was most of the answer. The numbers talked me out of it. The rent of the ground under America’s homes came in 2024 to between 4.3 and 7.1 percent of households’ disposable income, or between $2,776 and $4,627 a year for every person in the country. That is real money, and it isn’t what a country can live on. It grows, though. In the model, land’s share of income more than triples. And it can be collected by taxing only the growth from here on, which leaves today’s owners the rent they have now, though not the rise they were counting on.
So the second ticket has to be made of the other third of the dollar. It has to be ownership.
What would that take?
Less than you’d think. For the United States it comes to three things.
Hold a share in common, and pay everyone its income. A fifth of all the machines and land in the country would have paid $4,493 a person in 2023, or $374 a month, for every man, woman and child. Most of that fifth is within reach without buying anything: the tax America already levies on company profits, turned into the partnership I described, and the growth in land rent together come to about a seventh of all ownership income, and a public fund could buy the rest. It isn’t free money. The profits tax is already being spent, so paying it out means finding $608 billion a year somewhere else, and whatever a fund buys, somebody pays for.
Let people buy the rest out of wages. A common share of about a seventh would be enough to keep the poorer half of households whole, even if wages went to nothing. It can’t do that for the comfortable middle, who earn nearly half of all the pay in the country. They have to buy their ticket, and with the common fifth in place, saving 5 percent of pay and reinvesting what it earns at 5 percent a year would do it in 34 years, which is a working life.
And move taxes off the wage, toward spending, so that the safety net stops depending on the same thing as the incomes it is there to protect.
None of this is untried. Since 1982 the state of Alaska has paid every resident a yearly dividend out of a fund built from its oil revenues. Alaska’s dividend paid $1,312 a person in 2023 and $1,702 in 2024, to more than 600,000 people each year, and the fund behind it is worth $88 billion. Norway’s national fund owns, in its own words, “almost 1.5 percent of all shares in the world’s listed companies.” And Sweden puts 2.5 of the 18.5 percent of pay that goes toward the public pension into invested accounts that belong to the worker.
It starts small. A dividend of $374 a month is 29 percent of the poverty line for a person living alone. But it is made of the thing that is growing. In the model, the income of a common fifth is 13 percent of all wages today and 96 percent of all wages at the hundredfold point. And it answers the question I left hanging. A floor paid out of something everyone owns is mostly people paying themselves. As it grows, refusing becomes possible, a little and then more, and the wage goes back to being a price that means something.
There is a deadline. Today, everything that pays ownership income in the model economy, every machine and every acre, is worth about 6.7 years of the whole country’s wages. That is the price of a second ticket in the only currency most households have. At the hundredfold point it is 48 years. The saving that takes a household in the comfortable middle 34 years today takes 76 years there. The households that most need a second ticket are the ones who would have to buy it out of wages, and wages are the thing losing its value.
Is it fair?
The standard most often offered in defense of things as they are is that people are entitled to what they contribute, and that what you are paid measures what you add. I think that is a reasonable standard. My complaint is that its only reasonable if it’s wholly true.
Hold the kinds of income up to it. A wage is paid for work. The return on a machine is paid to someone who went without something in order to build it. The rent of land is paid for something that nobody made. Now look at what America taxes. It raises 68 percent of its revenue from pay for work, the most plainly earned income there is, and its tax on what machines earn lands, in the model, largely on wages again. By its own favorite standard, the country taxes most heavily what is most clearly earned. What I’ve described is that standard applied all the way through.
Now push it. Suppose machines can do nine-tenths of all work. The standard doesn’t break. It still says that those who work should keep their pay and those who saved should keep their return. It has less and less to say, because less and less of all income is anyone’s contribution. In the model with the program in place, pay for work goes from 60 percent of all income today to 17 at the hundredfold point and to less than 1 in the end. What is held in common, the public’s fifth of the machines plus the growth in land rent, goes from 6 percent of all income today to 40 at the hundredfold point and 48 in the end. That is about half, and nobody chose the number. It is where the contribution standard arrives by itself when work becomes scarce.
Is there any hope in this?
I think there is
The first reason is the one I started with. The hard problem, the one every earlier generation faced, was making enough. That problem is very nearly solved. We make in sixteen hours what took forty within living memory.
In 1955, paying every American a poverty-line income would have cost between 27 and 52 percent of everything the country produced. In 2024 it would cost between 9 and 18 percent. What is left is a problem of arrangement, of who holds which ticket. Arrangements are made by people, and they can be remade.
The second reason is that most of this can still be done as a trade, in which nobody is made worse off. Today, a household gets a second ticket by putting aside some of its pay and buying ownership from someone willing to sell it, and each side is paid in full for what it gives up. After a large shift, the same move is a transfer from those who gained to those who lost, which is a much harder thing to ask. We are still in the before. None of us knows for certain which side of the line our own work will fall on, and I can’t think of a better condition for agreeing on a rule.
The third is that every piece of it already exists somewhere, and works.
The fourth is what the machines themselves are offering. In the small economy on paper, the same hundredfold improvement produces two different worlds, and the only difference between them is who holds the tickets. In one, a tenth of the households receive three-fifths of the income, and everyone else lives on a wage that has stopped meaning much. In the other, people own a share of what makes everything, the wage is one of two incomes, and work is something you choose. That world is 32 times richer than ours, and your time in it is your own. It is the world Keynes was describing. He assumed that a society eight times as rich would simply work less, and it turns out that working less is something you can do only if you hold something besides your work. The wealth came to us as a country. The time has to come one household at a time, and most households have had no way to receive it.
I would be glad to be wrong about where the machines are heading. Very little of this depends on that. The twenty-four hours are already there. They were freed by people who are still working forty. What stands between those people and their own time is a second ticket, and it will not get cheaper by waiting.
I also made a video going over the maths, if you’d like to hear more:
For the careful: how sure am I?
Taken from public data, with every number and figure reproducible: that an hour of work makes two and a half times what it did in 1973, that an hour of ordinary pay buys a fifth more, and that Americans work more hours per head than they did then; that two-thirds of what Americans buy is paid for by work; that nine households in ten live mostly on pay and one in ten receives two-thirds of the ownership income; that 68 percent of taxes rest on pay; the size of land rent; and the falling cost of keeping everyone out of poverty.
From a model, and true to the extent that the model is correct: what happens when machines improve a hundredfold; that a tax on what machines earn lands on wages; that ownership costs more in years of wages the further the shift goes; and that the common part of income rises to about half. The model has one kind of worker, no risk and no politics. The result on taxing machines is a long-run result. In the short run the machines already exist, and their owners pay.
From argument, with modest evidence behind it: that a wage which cannot be refused says little about the worth of an hour, that some work is done which isn’t worth doing, and that our economy has no way of saying “enough.”
From nothing firmer than my own judgment: that machines will keep taking over tasks, and faster than they have; that a good deal of today’s work would not be done if the people doing it could afford to refuse; and that most of us would take more of our wealth as time if we could.
I have left things out. Every household is given the same average shopping basket, and renters are worse placed than that makes them look. The richest tenth is lumped together, though ownership is concentrated within it. Everything here is American.
How this was made. I wrote this with the help of Claude Fable 5.2, Anthropic’s AI model. If you don’t trust AI, just know that these are the same models that are currently wiping the floor with every other field, and I really didn’t try and force fable to write something I already agreed with. Actually, this whole experience has been me realising just how wrong I am about some things. Anyways, Claude did the measurements with me, drew the figures and drafted most of the text from my earlier essays. The model economy is joint work with Johan Båge. Every number comes from code in a public repository, wilsoniumite/labor
Sources

Output per hour, hours worked and the wage series are from the Bureau of Labor Statistics. The poverty line is that of Mollie Orshansky, “Children of the Poor,” *Social Security Bulletin* 26(7), 1963, updated for prices.

Why are we still working this hard?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-11
20:05:29 UTC
05:05:29 KST

In 1973, what a forty-hour week of work produced takes sixteen hours to produce today.
That’s according to Bureau of Economic Analysis (BEA) input output tables. An hour of work in American business now makes two and a half times what it made in 1973. For every forty hours that used to be needed, twenty-four have been freed.
You have probably not noticed them. The forty-hour week was written into American law by an act of 1938, and hasn’t changed. Counted for each head of population, the hours worked in American business in 2024 were 10 percent higher than in 1973. As a country, the US took the whole of fifty years’ progress as more output and none of it as time.
It was supposed to go differently. In 1930 the economist John Maynard Keynes wrote a short essay about the world his grandchildren would live in. He predicted that “the standard of life in progressive countries one hundred years hence will be between four and eight times as high as it is to-day.” And with so much plenty, he thought, we would hardly need to work at all: “Three-hour shifts or a fifteen-hour week” would be enough. He is usually said to have been right about the wealth and wrong about the leisure. Output per person in the United States in 2024 was 7.8 times its level in 1930. But look again at the sixteen hours. He was right about both. The fifteen-hour week arrived in terms of output, but not in the workweek.
I have spent a long time trying to find out where those twenty-four hours went, with a co-author, a small economy built on paper, and more national accounts than I’d like to admit. This essay is what I found. Eighteen of the hours never reached ordinary paychecks. The other six reached them in a form that couldn’t be taken as time. Both happened for the same reason, which has to do with what a wage is for. The same reason explains why people are frightened of machines that ought to be good news. And it points to a way out that is simpler, and nearer, than I expected when I started.
A note: The numbers are American, because the United States publishes the accounts this needs. I live in Sweden but it’s probably quite a similar story. The formal paper underpinning this work is here:
https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=7226858
What is a wage for?
Three things:
A wage is a price. It tells a business what an hour of someone’s time costs, so that the hour gets used where it is worth most, and it tells people which work is wanted. A wage is good at this.
A wage is also a “ticket”. It is how nearly everyone gets a share of what is made. Bread reaches me because I have money, and I have money because I sold some hours. That isn’t a law of nature. It is an arrangement, and it has worked so well for so long that we’ve stopped seeing it as one.
And a wage is a place: a shape to the day, people to be among, a sign that somebody needs you.
One thing can do three jobs only while the jobs pull the same way. For two centuries they did, because of the kind of machine we had. The machines of the industrial age were strong and stupid. They could lift, spin, stamp and haul, and they couldn’t see, judge or decide. Nearly everything worth making took both kinds of ability, so every new machine needed people standing next to it, and the owners of machines had to bid for those people. The price of an hour rose with everything else, and a ticket made of that price was a good ticket.
The machines being built now can see, read, write and decide. I write software for a living and use them every day, and I find it harder each year to name a kind of work that is safely out of their reach.
My claim is that the price and the ticket have been pulling apart for fifty years, and that each is damaging the other, and that fundamentally this is driven by changes in technology.
How important is this?
Probably a lot. It surprises me just as much as you that some random 26 year old trans girl might work this out, but the maths has been checked, the paper is going through review, and I’ve done everything I can to try and break it. If you can see what’s really wrong with it: wilson@wilsoniumite.com
Follow a dollar you spend in a shop. Some of it pays the people who worked to make the thing. Some of it pays whoever owns what was used to make it: the oven, the truck, the building, the ground underneath. America’s national accounts are detailed enough to chase a dollar all the way back through every supplier, and when you do it for 2023, of what is paid to Americans for making the things households buy, 66 percent is pay for work and 34 percent goes to owners.
A third of the economy, in other words, already pays people for owning things, with no work asked of them. That would be a curiosity if we all held a bit of both. We don’t.
The poorest tenth of American households lives mostly on government benefits. From the second tenth to the ninth, about four-fifths of what a household earns in the market is pay for work. Then, in the richest tenth, it changes all at once, and pay and ownership are about half and half. That one tenth receives two-thirds of all the income from ownership.
Benefits look like a second source for everyone else. But 68 cents of every tax dollar in America is raised from pay for work, so a benefit is mostly a claim on somebody else’s wage. Follow the money far enough and nine households in ten are living on each other’s wages. We live off the earth and off the machines we’ve built on it. Our share of what they produce reaches us through one narrow channel, which is being paid for a job.
By the way at this point you may notice that “machines” sounds like capital, and yes, that would be the technical term. But to try and escape what we usually feel when we hear those terms, I will use work instead of labor, machines instead of capital, and land instead of non-produced inputs (the term used in our paper).
Where did the eighteen hours go?
Until 1973, what an hour of work produced and what an hour of ordinary pay could buy rose together. Between 1950 and 1973 the first rose by 81 percent and the second by 66 percent. (By ordinary pay I mean the hourly pay of everyone on a private payroll who isn’t a manager or a supervisor, measured against the prices of everything except housing.) Then they parted. Since 1973 the output of an hour has risen two and a half times, and an hour of ordinary pay buys 19 percent more. You might have heard of this as “the great stagnation” and yes, I claim to in large part be able to explain what happened, that technology is what changed. Consider that moors law is usually said to start around that period, and you begin to see what I mean.
So to buy what forty hours of ordinary pay bought in 1973 now takes thirty-four hours. Making it takes sixteen. The eighteen in between went somewhere else.
I would like to tell you they went to the owners of the machines. About a fifth of the gap did. About two-fifths of it is pay spreading out among workers, with the top pulling away from the ordinary worker. The rest I can’t separate cleanly. It is a tangle of prices, benefits and measures that don’t line up.
But look at what the largest piece is. Daron Acemoglu, who shared the Nobel prize in economics in 2024, and Pascual Restrepo estimate that between 50 and 70 percent of the change in America’s wage structure over the four decades to 2016 came from “relative wage declines of worker groups specialized in routine tasks in industries experiencing rapid automation.” Machines learned to do what ordinary workers did before they learned to do what the best-paid did.
So both pieces are the same thing happening. A wage is a price, and a price follows scarcity. It doesn’t follow effort, and it doesn’t follow need. The day a machine learns your job, you are working exactly as hard as you did the day before, and your hour is worth less. That is how a price ought to behave, and it is a fine way to decide who should do what. It is a strange way to decide who gets to live well.
That is the price shrinking the ticket. So far it has been slow.
And the other six hours?
Ordinary pay did rise, by a fifth. That could have been six hours off the week at the same standard of living. Instead the hours went up, and I don’t think it was because nobody wanted the time.
Think about what a price tells you. When I pay three dollars for a loaf of bread, the baker learns something true about me: the bread was worth at least three dollars to me, because I could have walked away. A wage is supposed to carry the same kind of information the other way. If I take a job at fifteen dollars an hour, the hour must have been worth less than that to me, or I would have kept it. A great deal of economics leans on this. It is why a job that somebody is willing to do is assumed to be a job worth doing.
It works only if I could have said no. If selling hours is the only way I eat, I will sell them for whatever they fetch, and the wage records how badly I needed the money. It says very little about what the hour was worth to me. It might have been an afternoon with my child, or a visit to my mother, or the Tuesday I would have spent running the club. Those lost a contest they were never allowed to enter. An auction in which one side can’t walk away tells you very little about what the thing was worth.
This is the ticket spoiling the price. Because the wage is the ticket, most people can’t refuse it, and a price that can’t be refused has stopped being a price. When we argue over “does money represent value”, this is one of the main reasons that isn’t true.
Two things then happen. The first is that some work gets done that isn’t worth doing. Take any job that produces a little more than the wage it pays and less than the hour is worth to the person doing it. The job covers its costs. The employer is doing nothing wrong. And every hour of it uses up something worth more than what it makes, with the whole of the loss falling on the worker. Nobody can see this, because all the prices look fine. Think uber or doordash. Would you ask your friend to pick you up because you are drunk and pay them a few dollars for it, knowing they’d rather be asleep? Maybe, just maybe, that job shouldn’t exist, at least not done by a person.
The second is that our economy has no way of saying “enough.” Other markets can. When there is more of something than people want, its price falls and less of it gets made. The market for work can’t, because for the person selling, a price that falls far enough means not eating. So we treat every job as a good in itself and every hour sold as proof that it was wanted. I once asked a friend why we assume that everyone ought to be working, and she said of course they should, she’d like to be able to buy more stuff. It’s a fair answer, and it contains an assumption, which is that every extra hour worked makes more of what we want. Labor is a resource, like water, and we don’t drain the oceans looking for a use for every drop.
I can’t put a number on what this costs, and the reason I can’t is part of the argument. Everything we measure about work is a record of what people did when they could not refuse. It is like trying to learn how much people like a food by watching what they buy when it is the only thing on the shelf. So let me ask you instead.
Do the people you know have more free time than their parents had at the same age? In a country that has made itself two and a half times as productive within one working lifetime, do they seem less pressed? When they do take time, for a new child or a dying parent, does it feel like something they are free to choose, or like something they will pay for later? And of the things that seem to have thinned out around you, the visits, the clubs, the neighbors who knew each other, how many were things that anyone was ever paid to do?
The harder evidence points the same way. Where ordinary people did gain free time over the last century, most of it came at the two ends of life, as longer schooling and longer retirement. Those are the two stretches that come with a ticket that isn’t a wage: a parent or a loan at one end, a pension at the other. And when researchers in the United States gave 1,000 low-income people $1,000 a month for three years, with no conditions, those people took roughly a sixth of the money as time. They received $11,400 a year more than a comparison group, and their other income ended up about $1,900 a year lower. The authors call that “a moderate labor supply effect.” People who could refuse a little did refuse a little.
What happens if machines get much better?
So far both failures have been slow. Nobody knows whether machines are about to get much better, or how fast. What I can do is show you what happens in an economy where they do.
An economic model is a small economy on paper, simple enough to solve and fitted to the real one wherever the two can be compared. In mine, businesses give each task to whoever does it more cheaply, a person or a machine. People are far better than machines at some tasks and only a little better at others. Machines have to be built, out of other machines, some human work and some land. Whoever pays for a machine wants a return of 10 percent a year. And there is only so much land. Fitted to the United States of today, this economy pays 60 percent of its income for work, 31 percent to the owners of machines and 9 percent to the owners of land.
Now turn one dial. Let machines get steadily better at everything, until they are a hundred times better relative to us than they are now, and let them learn to build one another.
For a long while very little happens. When machines are ten times better, work is still paid 52 percent of all income, because people move to what they are best at. Then, at about 35 times, the machines that could replace even the hardest hour of human work take less than an hour of human work to build, and from there no wage can stay above what the replacement costs. By the hundredfold point work is paid 17 percent of all income. At ten thousand times it is paid less than 1 percent.
Here is something fun though about this economic model. Technically, measured in “stuff”, nobody in this economy gets poorer. But, what stuff you can buy has changed:
At the hundredfold point total income is 32 times what it was, and an hour of work buys 14 times as many goods as it does today. What an hour stops buying is land: an hour’s pay rents about a twelfth of the land it does now. And the income of those who own machines and land is 66 times what it was.
Look at what has happened to the two jobs. As a price, the wage is working perfectly. It is telling everyone, accurately, that human hours are no longer what production is short of. As a ticket it has all but failed. It delivers a sixth of the income, in the middle of the largest boom there has ever been.
Who would that fall on? If ownership stayed as it is today and work were paid 17 percent of income, the richest tenth of American households would receive 62 percent of all market income, up from 46. The poorer half would go from 14 and a half percent to 10. People sometimes hope that an economy like that would seize up for want of customers. It wouldn’t. The owners are the customers, and the economy would turn to face them. Nothing in economics says an economy has to be for everybody.
Keep three cautions in mind. The dial measures technology and not time: a hundredfold improvement takes 117 years at 4 percent a year, the pace at which machinery improved for its price after the Second World War, and 25 years at 20 percent, and nobody knows the pace. If people turn out to have abilities that no machine can match at any price, the same dial stops with work at 48 percent. And it is a model, with one kind of worker and no politics.
So what would fix it?
There are two problems here with one cause. We ask the wage to be a price and a ticket. Being the ticket, it can’t be refused, so it has stopped pricing time. Being a price, it follows scarcity, so it is a ticket that shrinks whenever a machine learns something. The repair is to stop asking. Let the wage be a price, which it is good at, and give people a second ticket.
The question is what a second ticket can be made of.
It can’t be made of wages. A benefit paid out of today’s taxes is, for 68 cents in the dollar, wage earners paying each other. It moves the first ticket around.
It can’t be made by taxing what machines earn, which was my own first idea. In the model, a 20 percent tax on what machines earn their owners raises 6.3 percent of national income, and wages fall by 6.1 percent of national income. The reason is that investors build machines until the last one just pays the return they want. Tax the return and they build fewer, until the ones that remain earn the old return after tax. The owners keep their rate of return on a smaller pile. The people who work alongside the machines have fewer of them to work with, and are paid less. The tax goes straight through the owners and lands on the wage.
What works is owning the machines, and there is a way to do it that disturbs nothing. Let a company deduct the full cost of every investment from its taxable profit on the day it makes it. Then on every new machine the government is, in effect, putting up a fifth of the cost and collecting a fifth of what the machine earns. That is a partnership. The investor’s return on their own four-fifths is untouched, so just as many machines get built. The public gets its fifth, which is 6.2 percent of national income in the model, and wages don’t change. The honest catch is the machines that already exist, whose owners lose about a fifth, once.
Land is different, for one reason. Nobody makes it. Tax machines and there will be fewer machines. Tax land and there is as much land as before. Thomas Paine, the pamphleteer of the American Revolution, argued in 1797 that “Man did not make the earth,” and that every landowner therefore owed the public a ground-rent, to be paid out to everyone. “It is not charity but a right,” he wrote. I reached that idea on my own before I’d heard of him, which I’ve decided to find encouraging, and for a while I thought it was most of the answer. The numbers talked me out of it. The rent of the ground under America’s homes came in 2024 to between 4.3 and 7.1 percent of households’ disposable income, or between $2,776 and $4,627 a year for every person in the country. That is real money, and it isn’t what a country can live on. It grows, though. In the model, land’s share of income more than triples. And it can be collected by taxing only the growth from here on, which leaves today’s owners the rent they have now, though not the rise they were counting on.
So the second ticket has to be made of the other third of the dollar. It has to be ownership.
What would that take?
Less than you’d think. For the United States it comes to three things.
Hold a share in common, and pay everyone its income. A fifth of all the machines and land in the country would have paid $4,493 a person in 2023, or $374 a month, for every man, woman and child. Most of that fifth is within reach without buying anything: the tax America already levies on company profits, turned into the partnership I described, and the growth in land rent together come to about a seventh of all ownership income, and a public fund could buy the rest. It isn’t free money. The profits tax is already being spent, so paying it out means finding $608 billion a year somewhere else, and whatever a fund buys, somebody pays for.
Let people buy the rest out of wages. A common share of about a seventh would be enough to keep the poorer half of households whole, even if wages went to nothing. It can’t do that for the comfortable middle, who earn nearly half of all the pay in the country. They have to buy their ticket, and with the common fifth in place, saving 5 percent of pay and reinvesting what it earns at 5 percent a year would do it in 34 years, which is a working life.
And move taxes off the wage, toward spending, so that the safety net stops depending on the same thing as the incomes it is there to protect.
None of this is untried. Since 1982 the state of Alaska has paid every resident a yearly dividend out of a fund built from its oil revenues. Alaska’s dividend paid $1,312 a person in 2023 and $1,702 in 2024, to more than 600,000 people each year, and the fund behind it is worth $88 billion. Norway’s national fund owns, in its own words, “almost 1.5 percent of all shares in the world’s listed companies.” And Sweden puts 2.5 of the 18.5 percent of pay that goes toward the public pension into invested accounts that belong to the worker.
It starts small. A dividend of $374 a month is 29 percent of the poverty line for a person living alone. But it is made of the thing that is growing. In the model, the income of a common fifth is 13 percent of all wages today and 96 percent of all wages at the hundredfold point. And it answers the question I left hanging. A floor paid out of something everyone owns is mostly people paying themselves. As it grows, refusing becomes possible, a little and then more, and the wage goes back to being a price that means something.
There is a deadline. Today, everything that pays ownership income in the model economy, every machine and every acre, is worth about 6.7 years of the whole country’s wages. That is the price of a second ticket in the only currency most households have. At the hundredfold point it is 48 years. The saving that takes a household in the comfortable middle 34 years today takes 76 years there. The households that most need a second ticket are the ones who would have to buy it out of wages, and wages are the thing losing its value.
Is it fair?
The standard most often offered in defense of things as they are is that people are entitled to what they contribute, and that what you are paid measures what you add. I think that is a reasonable standard. My complaint is that its only reasonable if it’s wholly true.
Hold the kinds of income up to it. A wage is paid for work. The return on a machine is paid to someone who went without something in order to build it. The rent of land is paid for something that nobody made. Now look at what America taxes. It raises 68 percent of its revenue from pay for work, the most plainly earned income there is, and its tax on what machines earn lands, in the model, largely on wages again. By its own favorite standard, the country taxes most heavily what is most clearly earned. What I’ve described is that standard applied all the way through.
Now push it. Suppose machines can do nine-tenths of all work. The standard doesn’t break. It still says that those who work should keep their pay and those who saved should keep their return. It has less and less to say, because less and less of all income is anyone’s contribution. In the model with the program in place, pay for work goes from 60 percent of all income today to 17 at the hundredfold point and to less than 1 in the end. What is held in common, the public’s fifth of the machines plus the growth in land rent, goes from 6 percent of all income today to 40 at the hundredfold point and 48 in the end. That is about half, and nobody chose the number. It is where the contribution standard arrives by itself when work becomes scarce.
Is there any hope in this?
I think there is
The first reason is the one I started with. The hard problem, the one every earlier generation faced, was making enough. That problem is very nearly solved. We make in sixteen hours what took forty within living memory.
In 1955, paying every American a poverty-line income would have cost between 27 and 52 percent of everything the country produced. In 2024 it would cost between 9 and 18 percent. What is left is a problem of arrangement, of who holds which ticket. Arrangements are made by people, and they can be remade.
The second reason is that most of this can still be done as a trade, in which nobody is made worse off. Today, a household gets a second ticket by putting aside some of its pay and buying ownership from someone willing to sell it, and each side is paid in full for what it gives up. After a large shift, the same move is a transfer from those who gained to those who lost, which is a much harder thing to ask. We are still in the before. None of us knows for certain which side of the line our own work will fall on, and I can’t think of a better condition for agreeing on a rule.
The third is that every piece of it already exists somewhere, and works.
The fourth is what the machines themselves are offering. In the small economy on paper, the same hundredfold improvement produces two different worlds, and the only difference between them is who holds the tickets. In one, a tenth of the households receive three-fifths of the income, and everyone else lives on a wage that has stopped meaning much. In the other, people own a share of what makes everything, the wage is one of two incomes, and work is something you choose. That world is 32 times richer than ours, and your time in it is your own. It is the world Keynes was describing. He assumed that a society eight times as rich would simply work less, and it turns out that working less is something you can do only if you hold something besides your work. The wealth came to us as a country. The time has to come one household at a time, and most households have had no way to receive it.
I would be glad to be wrong about where the machines are heading. Very little of this depends on that. The twenty-four hours are already there. They were freed by people who are still working forty. What stands between those people and their own time is a second ticket, and it will not get cheaper by waiting.
I also made a video going over the maths, if you’d like to hear more:
For the careful: how sure am I?
Taken from public data, with every number and figure reproducible: that an hour of work makes two and a half times what it did in 1973, that an hour of ordinary pay buys a fifth more, and that Americans work more hours per head than they did then; that two-thirds of what Americans buy is paid for by work; that nine households in ten live mostly on pay and one in ten receives two-thirds of the ownership income; that 68 percent of taxes rest on pay; the size of land rent; and the falling cost of keeping everyone out of poverty.
From a model, and true to the extent that the model is correct: what happens when machines improve a hundredfold; that a tax on what machines earn lands on wages; that ownership costs more in years of wages the further the shift goes; and that the common part of income rises to about half. The model has one kind of worker, no risk and no politics. The result on taxing machines is a long-run result. In the short run the machines already exist, and their owners pay.
From argument, with modest evidence behind it: that a wage which cannot be refused says little about the worth of an hour, that some work is done which isn’t worth doing, and that our economy has no way of saying “enough.”
From nothing firmer than my own judgment: that machines will keep taking over tasks, and faster than they have; that a good deal of today’s work would not be done if the people doing it could afford to refuse; and that most of us would take more of our wealth as time if we could.
I have left things out. Every household is given the same average shopping basket, and renters are worse placed than that makes them look. The richest tenth is lumped together, though ownership is concentrated within it. Everything here is American.
How this was made. I wrote this with the help of Claude Fable 5.2, Anthropic’s AI model. If you don’t trust AI, just know that these are the same models that are currently wiping the floor with every other field, and I really didn’t try and force fable to write something I already agreed with. Actually, this whole experience has been me realising just how wrong I am about some things. Anyways, Claude did the measurements with me, drew the figures and drafted most of the text from my earlier essays. The model economy is joint work with Johan Båge. Every number comes from code in a public repository, wilsoniumite/labor
Sources
The measurements are my own calculations from the national accounts of the U.S. Bureau of Economic Analysis: the input–output accounts, the distribution of personal income by tenth of households, and the accounts of government receipts. Output per hour, hours worked and the wage series are from the Bureau of Labor Statistics. The poverty line is that of Mollie Orshansky, “Children of the Poor,” *Social Security Bulletin* 26(7), 1963, updated for prices.

Olaf Sakkers is betting  he can beat the emerging-manager squeeze
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-11
20:04:28 UTC
05:04:28 KST

Entering into the world of venture capital has never been easy, especially as a first-time fund manager. But it’s especially daunting right now.
According to PitchBook data, firms raising their fourth fund “or later” captured a whopping 88.2% of U.S. VC fundraising dollars through the first three quarters of this year, while first-time funds captured a measly 4.5%. Just 81 first-time funds closed in the first nine months of 2026. That puts the year on track to be the worst for first-time managers in at least a decade, according to PitchBook’s estimate.
Olaf Sakkers has spent the past five years proving that difficult doesn’t mean impossible. He founded his firm RedBlue Capital in 2021 and closed its first fund with roughly $5 million in capital commitments in 2023. He has now held a first close on a second fund that he says will total at least $50 million.
Raised in South Africa, he studied anthropology at Princeton, focusing on global health and health policy, and wrote his thesis on HIV in his home country. He moved to Israel after graduating and was drafted into the Israel Defense Forces, where he says he had a desk job liaising with the U.S. military and where he became interested in autonomous vehicles.
An introduction led to Michael Granoff, founder of the venture firm Maniv Mobility and an early backer of electric-vehicle startup Better Place. Granoff, who had money to invest, hired him on the spot, Sakkers says. Neither had been a venture investor at that point, so they built a fund “almost by accident,” learning as they went. Sakkers says he ran the investment process day to day, backing Israeli founders who were bringing cybersecurity skills into the automotive world, and later expanding to the U.S. and other markets.
By 2021, Sakkers says, he was an equal partner in Maniv’s economics, but he wanted something different — more formal and scalable — so struck out on his own. Today, RedBlue backs what he calls the “real economy,” including robotics, logistics, manufacturing, and energy.
Getting to this point has been a test of endurance as much as salesmanship, seemingly. Sakkers says that institutional limited partners, outfits like endowments and pension funds, have been the toughest to win over because his checks are small (and theirs are comparatively huge) and because their risk model is built to “avoid getting a zero,” which is why they tend to gravitate toward established brands.
Says Sakkers, it’s akin to founders who come to be seen as “inevitable,” after they’ve previously founded another company. Meanwhile first-time founders often battle against the “gravity of perception,” meaning a more dominant brand can bend how people perceive a newer one.
His answer has been to let the results speak, but it takes time. “It’s a long-term game of building trust, demonstrating performance over time,” Sakkers says.
The first fund was a proof of concept, backed by LPs he had known for a while, including family offices. The second draws on family offices and institutions.
RedBlue writes small checks, $50,000 to $100,000, mostly joining deals led by other investors rather than leading them. That lets it do more deals and be “the last investor that they take” on by promising practical help, including introductions to lead investors like Andreessen Horowitz and Thrive Capital, and to customers. Founders expect little from VCs, in Sakker’s view, so RedBlue tries to overdeliver on the support part.
By his account, the results are strong. Of 28 investments, he says that 15 have been marked up, and he cites a net internal rate of return of “significantly above 50%.” There has been just one exit — an acqui-hire — so most gains from the tiny fund remain on paper and unrealized.
Public records support part of Sakker’s story. HappyRobot, which RedBlue backed early, raised a $150 million Series C at a $1.2 billion valuation in August. Westmag, which makes motors for drones and robots and which Sakkers backed at pre-seed alongside Nat Friedman, emerged in June with an $11 million seed led by Andreessen Horowitz. Flai, an AI startup for car dealerships, followed its 2025 seed with a $27 million Series A. And Humble Robotics, a driverless-truck startup in whose $24 million seed round RedBlue participated, announced a $51 million Series A led by Obvious Ventures earlier this month.
Sakkers argues that discipline is his edge. RedBlue publishes thesis essays, such as one on robotics, and runs on a custom database that he built with Claude Code to log relationships, meeting notes, and introductions.
That lets him and his lone full-time principal, based in New York, work asynchronously. AI agents, he says, are “useless if they just have to guess,” so everything gets written down. He contrasts that with Benchmark’s more artisanal model, which he says suits a firm with a record he lacks. Long exit timelines for physical-world companies don’t worry him, either: Even a $50 million to $100 million fund, he says, can sell stakes on the secondary market.
The data isn’t uniformly grim. Smaller venture capital funds between $1 million and $10 million consistently outperform larger funds of $100 million or more at the top end of net TVPI (total value to paid-in capital, which compares a fund’s total value to the money invested in it) across recent vintages tracked by the software outfit Carta.
In the meantime, Sakkers is out there knocking on doors, making introductions between disparate parties, and calling up reporters like this one to tell his story. It’s not easy to make a mark in a world where the spoils accrue to the few, but he says he never seriously considered a safer job. “Life is short, and regrets last forever,” he says.

Ukraine Strikes Cause Permanent Data Loss at Russia's Yandex Cloud
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-11
20:00:00 UTC
05:00:00 KST

Ukraine Strikes Cause Permanent Data Loss at Russia's Yandex Cloud (united24media.com) 62
			
		 	
				Russia's Yandex says 2.84% of customer data has been permanently lost following Ukrainian strikes on data centers in the country. "According to the company, recovery is impossible because of critical damage to equipment and the lack of backup copies on the platform," reports UNITED24 Media. From the report:  Yandex clarified that customers who had previously enabled the platform's backup tools did not experience any data loss, as their information remained fully preserved. The incident has prompted the company to revise its approach to data protection and disaster recovery, particularly the default settings for cloud infrastructure. Under the updated policy, automatic backups will be enabled by default whenever customers create new virtual machines on Yandex Cloud. Users will retain the option to disable the feature manually through the management console. In addition to changing its backup policy, Yandex Cloud announced the launch of a new availability zone, designated ru-central1-f. The new availability zone operates from a separate location, improving the resilience and reliability of the services.  The report says Ukrainian drones targeted three Yandex data centers in Russia over four days, beginning October 8 with a strike on a facility in the Ryazan region that houses two of the company's three supercomputers. A second attack hit Yandex's largest data center in the Kaluga region on October 9, followed by a third strike on October 11 targeting a facility in the Vladimir region that houses the company's remaining supercomputer.

CoreWeave turns to customer prepayments, shorter contracts to fund AI growth
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-11
19:54:00 UTC
04:54:00 KST

CoreWeave is rewriting how it pays for the AI boom, according to the company’s third-quarter earnings, reported on Tuesday. CoreWeave, a neocloud that rents out the computing power behind AI, said it’s increasingly asking customers to pay up front, borrowing against specific contracts, and even pursuing short-term deals that could lead to higher prices.
The new strategies could help CoreWeave finance its growth more efficiently while squeezing more cash from existing infrastructure. But these bets only hold if today’s demand and pricing power continue.
CoreWeave reported $3.66 billion in revenue, up 168% from the year prior, but it also posted a $709 million net loss and $846 million in quarterly interest expense.
“We will not use the unsecured debt market to fund our business at current yields, nor do we need to,” CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator said during Tuesday’s earnings call.
Instead, CoreWeave’s plan is to rely on loans backed by specific customer contracts, drawing on funds as needed to finance most of the infrastructure it still has to build to deliver on its significant backlog of $132.5 billion.
CoreWeave also said it expects about $9 billion in customer prepayments and deposits over the next three months, including more than $3 billion tied to signing new customer contracts for capacity it has already built, which requires no additional spending on hardware.
Rivals Nebius and IREN have also relied on hefty Microsoft prepayments to pay for computing capacity, signaling that the industry is moving toward asking Big Tech to pay up for their buildouts.
Upfront payments could alleviate CoreWeave’s immediate borrowing needs, but they also commit the company to providing services at a price that might be less attractive down the road if compute demand continues to surge.
CoreWeave is also looking to capitalize on that growing demand by signing more shorter-term contracts that can command much higher prices.
The company raised prices across its offerings by about 25% in July and by another 10% later in the quarter. Prices are still climbing this quarter, in a way that “more than offset[s] cost increases,” said Intrator.
“We are also allocating more capacity to short-term opportunities, where the pricing justifies it, selling capacity at more than $40 million per megawatt annually, while pricing for new infrastructure on four- and five-year contracts is moving to $20 million to $25 million per megawatt,” the chief executive said.
CoreWeave’s financing strategy now revolves around those opportunities. In August, the company closed a $2.6 billion loan, its first designed specifically for shorter-term customer contracts. Known as DDTL 5.5, it lets CoreWeave borrow in installments as it needs the money.
While short-term deals let CoreWeave charge higher prices and change them more frequently, it’s a trade-off. Lenders give more weight to longer-term contracts, even if they’re priced lower, because they value predictable cash flow and consistency to repay the loan above all.
Even though Wall Street reacted positively to CoreWeave’s earnings, bond investors are much less sanguine. Last month, yields on some of the company’s bonds hit above 15%, highlighting that investors are concerned about the debt needed to fund data center buildouts before those investments see a return.
In short, CoreWeave is finding new ways to expand without relying on unsecured debt. The strategy it has chosen could boost profits, but it also carries greater risk around contract renewals and pricing — and it just goes to show how dependent the AI infrastructure boom is on sustained, and as yet unproven, demand.
TechCrunch has reached out to CoreWeave for more information about its loan facility.

IBM Quantum Nighthawk Demonstrates New Levels of Speed and Quality
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-11
19:47:59 UTC
04:47:59 KST

All press releases
IBM Quantum Nighthawk Demonstrates New Levels of Speed and Quality, with Plans to Scale Beyond 1,000 Qubits in 2027
YORKTOWN HEIGHTS, NY, November 11, 2026 — IBM (NYSE: IBM) today announced IBM Quantum Nighthawk r3, its next-generation processor, will be built to accurately execute 10,000 quantum operations across 520 qubits to tackle larger, more complex problems while maintaining the speed and quality demonstrated by the current generation Nighthawk available today.
In 2027, IBM plans to link two Nighthawk r3 processors into a single system with more than 1,000 qubits.
To be at the frontier of quantum computing, industry-relevant applications demand both computational quality and speed. Speed matters because a quantum computer delivers its answer in samples: each run of a quantum circuit returns one result. Applications increasingly require circuits executing thousands of quantum operations on approximately 100 qubits or more, generating millions to billions of samples. Scale and quality enable increasingly complex computations, but speed determines whether these demanding workloads can be completed in practical timeframes and at a reasonable cost.
“Useful quantum computing hinges on whether results can be delivered with accuracy and speed, both are fundamental,” said Jay Gambetta, Director of IBM Research and IBM Fellow. “Nighthawk is already showing us what that looks like today. As we scale this architecture to larger and more powerful quantum computers, the capabilities of quantum computation will reach new levels as we integrate more closely with classical resources and march ahead to fault tolerance.”
Nighthawk r2: Delivering Speed for Real-World Workloads
Introduced in September, IBM Quantum Nighthawk r2 delivers up to 100,000 samples per second while supporting accurate computations with more than 7,500 quantum operations on 120 qubits. Its high-speed qubit reset technology dramatically reduces the time between circuit executions, enabling applications to collect the millions or billions of samples needed for complex computations far more efficiently.
Clients and partners including E.ON, Algorithmiq, and Q-CTRL have executed 34 billion circuits on Nighthawk r2 to date, reporting performance improvements of up to 19x while maintaining the quality needed for scientific computation.
The results are already demonstrating value across industries. E.ON reported a 15x speedup on Nighthawk r2 in quantum machine learning for energy grid optimization. IBM used Nighthawk r2 for a 12x speedup of an experiment ran earlier this year with Oak Ridge National Laboratory, which simulated real magnetic materials and produced results consistent with neutron scattering experiments. Both of these speedups were achieved over previous work on IBM Quantum Heron. Quantum Elements and ParityQC are also among the partners reporting significant performance improvements on their own Nighthawk r2 workloads.
The quantum advantage experiments IBM and its partners published in July were first run on IBM Quantum Heron, with circuits and results openly available for independent testing through the community-led Quantum Advantage Tracker. An experiment with Algorithmiq to simulate a heterogeneous quantum material has now been extended to Nighthawk r2, leveraging its speed and connectivity to execute circuits 8 times faster than the original experiment on Heron. At the same time, the new circuits are significantly more challenging for classical computers to simulate, requiring an estimated 240 times more memory to achieve comparable accuracy when using leading classical tensor-network methods.
Nighthawk r3: Scaling to More Than 1,000 Qubits
Building on the performance demonstrated by Nighthawk r2, Nighthawk r3 will extend the same high-speed architecture to larger quantum computers. The new processor is designed to support 10,000 quantum operations on 520 programmable qubits, with 2,034 total physical qubits. It retains r2's square-lattice connectivity and fast operations while introducing 10 chip-to-chip interconnects, called l-couplers, designed to connect processors into larger systems.
IBM has already demonstrated two critical technologies that enable this modular approach. This year, IBM connected Nighthawk r1 processors using four l-couplers, achieving 99.3 percent quantum state transfer fidelity and progressing toward its 99.9 percent target. IBM also connected two cryogenic modules into a single system for the first time, reaching operating temperature in under five days and cooling below 15 millikelvin.
Together, these advances demonstrate critical progress in modularity and provide confidence in IBM's ability to scale Nighthawk to larger quantum computers. In 2027, IBM plans to connect two Nighthawk r3 processors into a single 1,040-qubit system, extending its high-performance architecture to larger and more complex computations.
Quantum Meets Classical on the IBM Quantum Platform
Advancing quantum computers requires continued progress in how they work alongside classical compute to handle errors. Industry-relevant workloads will demand layers of quantum error correction and error mitigation techniques alongside noise learning and post-processing. As quantum workloads scale, they will call for more classical resources, and the challenge now is how to best scale this integration.
To unite these paradigms, IBM Quantum Platform is bringing high-performance (HPC) classical and quantum computing together into a single managed environment. Users can now integrate quantum workflows directly with a range of classical compute – whether from their laptop or a powerful supercomputer – or with the HPC and classical resources that are newly available on the platform.
Users are seeing promising results from these integrations. For example, using the new environment, IBM and RIKEN* scientists executed their original sample-based quantum diagonalization (SQD) workflow roughly 5 times faster while performing 100 times more computations than in the original study. Cleveland Clinic and RIKEN also leveraged the platform to automatically process 608 million samples for modeling biologically relevant structures, 33 times faster than without workflow automation.
Enhanced capabilities will also fuel breakthroughs in quantum error correction. IBM Quantum Platform offers pulse-level capabilities to give researchers precise control over how they can detect, understand, and better correct errors within quantum and classical architectures. This helps them better time operations, reset qubits faster, and extract more information from quantum measurements – giving researchers the visibility to improve error correction at its most fundamental levels.
IBM recently launched a new co-design platform for quantum error correction with Oak Ridge National Laboratory and AMD that physically co-locates Nighthawk with ORNL’s Umbra High-Performance Computing (HPC) system, powered by AMD Instinct™ MI355X GPUs and 5th Gen AMD EPYC™ CPUs.
About IBM
IBM is a leading global hybrid cloud and AI, and business services provider, helping clients in more than 175 countries capitalize on insights from their data, streamline business processes, reduce costs and gain the competitive edge in their industries. Thousands of governments and corporate entities in critical infrastructure areas such as financial services, telecommunications and healthcare rely on IBM's hybrid cloud platform and Red Hat OpenShift to affect their digital transformations quickly, efficiently and securely. IBM's breakthrough innovations in AI, quantum computing, industry-specific cloud solutions and business services deliver open and flexible options to our clients. All of this is backed by IBM's long-standing commitment to trust, transparency, responsibility, inclusivity and service.
For more information, visit https://research.ibm.com.
Media contacts:
Brittany Forgione
IBM
Brittany.forgione@ibm.com
Erin Angelini
IBM
edlehr@us.ibm.com
* This project is supported by NEDO (New Energy and Industrial Technology Development Organization), an organization under the jurisdiction of Japan's Ministry of Economy, Trade and Industry (METI)'s "Research and Development of Quantum-Supercomputers Hybrid Platform for Exploration of Uncharted Computable Capabilities" (Project Leader: Mitsuhisa Sato) as part of the "Project for Research and Development of Enhanced Infrastructures for Post 5G Information and Communications Systems (JPNP20017)."

IBM also connected two cryogenic modules into a single system for the first time, reaching operating temperature in under five days and cooling below 15 millikelvin.
