In 2027, IBM plans to connect two Nighthawk r3 processors into a single 1,040-qubit system, extending its high-performance architecture to larger and more complex computations.
Quantum Meets Classical on the IBM Quantum Platform
Advancing quantum computers requires continued progress in how they work alongside classical compute to handle errors. Industry-relevant workloads will demand layers of quantum error correction and error mitigation techniques alongside noise learning and post-processing. As quantum workloads scale, they will call for more classical resources, and the challenge now is how to best scale this integration.
To unite these paradigms, IBM Quantum Platform is bringing high-performance (HPC) classical and quantum computing together into a single managed environment. Users can now integrate quantum workflows directly with a range of classical compute – whether from their laptop or a powerful supercomputer – or with the HPC and classical resources that are newly available on the platform.
Users are seeing promising results from these integrations. For example, using the new environment, IBM and RIKEN* scientists executed their original sample-based quantum diagonalization (SQD) workflow roughly 5 times faster while performing 100 times more computations than in the original study. Cleveland Clinic and RIKEN also leveraged the platform to automatically process 608 million samples for modeling biologically relevant structures, 33 times faster than without workflow automation.
Enhanced capabilities will also fuel breakthroughs in quantum error correction. IBM Quantum Platform offers pulse-level capabilities to give researchers precise control over how they can detect, understand, and better correct errors within quantum and classical architectures. This helps them better time operations, reset qubits faster, and extract more information from quantum measurements – giving researchers the visibility to improve error correction at its most fundamental levels.
IBM recently launched a new co-design platform for quantum error correction with Oak Ridge National Laboratory and AMD that physically co-locates Nighthawk with ORNL’s Umbra High-Performance Computing (HPC) system, powered by AMD Instinct™ MI355X GPUs and 5th Gen AMD EPYC™ CPUs.
About IBM
IBM is a leading global hybrid cloud and AI, and business services provider, helping clients in more than 175 countries capitalize on insights from their data, streamline business processes, reduce costs and gain the competitive edge in their industries. Thousands of governments and corporate entities in critical infrastructure areas such as financial services, telecommunications and healthcare rely on IBM's hybrid cloud platform and Red Hat OpenShift to affect their digital transformations quickly, efficiently and securely. IBM's breakthrough innovations in AI, quantum computing, industry-specific cloud solutions and business services deliver open and flexible options to our clients. All of this is backed by IBM's long-standing commitment to trust, transparency, responsibility, inclusivity and service.
For more information, visit https://research.ibm.com.

The Utah Teapot
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-11
19:45:10 UTC
04:45:10 KST

The Utah Teapot has been the symbol of computer graphics. It was one of the first computer graphics models created using Bézier patches. This is the official Utah Teapot page, where you can you can visualize and download any of these historical versions or customize it, using the settings above.
Terms of Use and Attribution: The Utah Teapot model files are freely available for any use, including commercial use. To preserve the historical identity and provenance of the model, users are requested to identify the model, and any substantially derived version of it, as a Utah Teapot (or Modified Utah Teapot) and to acknowledge its origin as the historic Utah Teapot developed at the University of Utah. This request is intended solely to preserve the historical identity of the Utah Teapot and is not intended to restrict use of the model. The model files are provided “as is,” without warranty of any kind. The provider of these files shall not be liable for any claims, damages, or other liability arising from their use.
The Utah Teapot was originally created by Martin Newell and went through a number of modifications over the years. It quickly became famous within the graphics community after making its first appearance at SIGGRAPH in 1976. Its extensive use in graphics prompted Arvo and Kirk [1987] to name it the “Teapotahedron” and jokingly call it “the sixth Platonic solid”. It remains one of the standard models for evaluating new computer graphics algorithms and frequently appears in technical papers presented at SIGGRAPH and other graphics conferences. Utah Teapot has also appears in art installations, movies, video games, and a wide range of other graphics applications. Versions of the Utah Teapot model are included in numerous rendering and modeling programs and graphics APIs, among them 3ds Max, Direct3D, Houdini, Modo, OpenGL, POV-Ray, Quartz Composer, and RenderMan. See the Wikipedia page on the Utah Teapot.
Martin Newell, Jim Blinn, and Cem Yuksel tell the story of the Utah Teapot at SIGGRAPH 2026, commemorating the 50th anniversary of the Utah Teapot's first appearance at SIGGRAPH in 1976. The extended abstract of this talk includes additional information: “The Return of the Utah Teapot” (PDF) by Cem Yuksel, Jim Blinn, and Martin Newell.
The Utah Teapot has a number of versions that were developed and released over the years by different people from the University of Utah, as summarized below. Version 1975 is how Martin Newell modeled it. Version 1976 is the scaled down version by Jim Blinn. Version 1987 by Frank Crow adds the round bottom. Version 1992 is the first attempt by Hank Driskill for turning the Teapot into a solid object. Version 2006 by Russ Fish improves the interior shape. Finally, Version 2026 by Cem Yuksel further improves the interior, adds chamfers where the handle and the spout connect to the body, and makes the shape of the Teapot curvature-continuous to achieve reflection continuity.
This is the original version created by Martin Newell, while he was a PhD student at the University of Utah, by approximating the proportions of a physical teapot (Melitta by Friesland Porzellan). It is formed by 28 cubic Bézier patches, which he first drew the control points on an engineering paper. It is one of the first computer graphics object modeled using a set of nonlinear surfaces, rather than a collection of polygons. Notably, Martin Newell's version is taller than the commonly known shape of the famous Utah Teapot, and it has no bottom. This is the model that appeared in his dissertation, titled “The utilization of procedure models in digital image synthesis” in 1975, along with the rest of the tea set he modeled, though he prepared these models earlier, sometime in 1974.
Martin Newell's drawing of the Teapot and the tea set image from his dissertation.
Here is a YouTube video, where Martin Newell, at an event celebrating the 50th anniversary of Computer Science department at the University of Utah, talking about the Teapot model.
Jim Blinn, while he was a PhD student at the University of Utah, scaled Martin Newell's teapot model by 3/4 during a demo. He liked its new shape and kept it that way. In fact, everyone liked this shorter version, including Martin Newell. Since 1976, this version of the teapot appeared in numerous publications, turning it into a symbol of computer graphics.
Utah Teapot was first introduced to the computer graphics community at SIGGRAPH 1976 in an article by Jim Blinn and Martin Newell, titled “Texture and Reflection in Computer Generated Images” (PDF). It was also published in the Communications of the ACM journal and an image from this article, featuring the Utah Teapot with a texture mapped to its patches, was used as the cover of the October 1976 issue.
Jim Blinn explains the components of the teapot and why it was squished in an article titled “What, Teapots Again?” (PDF), published in IEEE Computer Graphics & Applications in September 1987. There, he also suggests adding a flat bottom cap.
The cover of the CACM journal in October 1976, featuring one of the images from Blinn and Newell's paper at SIGGRAPH 1976, and two other images from the same paper with the original pixels computed on PDP-11 (after tone mapping) by Jim Blinn.
Here is a YouTube video, where Jim Blinn, at a reception honoring Ivan Sutherland at SIGGRAPH 2018, explaining why the Utah Teapot model is squished.
The most common version of Utah Teapot that appears in numerous graphics applications is the one released by Frank Crow, who was another PhD graduate of the University of Utah, in an article titled “The Origins of the Teapot” (PDF), published in IEEE Computer Graphics & Applications in January 1987. This article discusses the important role that the Utah Teapot model played in the early days of computer graphics and includes the control points of the scaled model by Jim Blinn. Except that all control points are shifted up just a bit to make room for a round bottom surface, defined by 4 additional Bézier patches. This brings the total number of patches to 32 and the total number of control points to 512.
Crow's figure showing the patch boundaries and the control polygon of the Teapot without its body, but with the bottom that he added.
The handle and the spout patches of the original Utah Teapot model intersect with its body. This was necessary, as it was modeled using cubic Bézier patches. This was also a useful feature for testing various hidden surface removal algorithms that were being developed at the time, and their abilities in rendering intersecting surfaces. Also, because cubic Bézier patches cannot form perfectly circular shapes, the teapot's body was not perfectly round. Perhaps more importantly, it did not contain an interior and a rather visible gap around the lid.
In 1992, Hank Driskill, another PhD student at the University of Utah, addressed all these problems. He used the Alpha_1 software, which was being developed by Professors Elaine Cohen and Rich Riesenfeld and their students/collaborators, and he prepared a trimmed NURBS model of the Utah Teapot, connecting its perfectly round body to its handle and spout, and a new interior surface. Its bottom was flat, as suggested by Jim Blinn, instead of the round bottom that was previously added by Frank Crow. Nonetheless, Hank Driskill's design, titled “Utah, the Next Generation,” featuring this new Utah Teapot (with warp nacelles), won the SIGGRAPH 1992 T-Shirt Contest.
*Hank Driskill's version used a thick curve to cut a path through the spout. Here, we use the spout interior designed by Russ Fish instead.
The interior of this version was defined by revolving B-spline curves, resulting in 86 additional patches, so this version of the Utah Teapot has 118 patches in total.
Driskill's Teapot images with his new model and warp nacelles, and the award-winning t-shirt at SIGGRAPH 1992.
The version by Hank Driskill was later improved by Russ Fish, a co-founder and technical lead of the Alpha_1 project at the University of Utah. Russ Fish modeled a new interior with a hollow knob for the lid and presented his “third-generation” teapot at SIGGRAPH 2006 with the title “Teapot Subdivision.” He also released and documented this new and earlier versions of the Utah Teapot. In particular, his solution for generating the interior of the spout is elegant and closely follows the exterior contours prepared by Martin Newell. Similar to Hank Driskill's version, this one also has a flat bottom, rather than the round bottom of Frank Crow's version.
The "Teapot Subdivision" image generated by Russ Fish.
This version forms even more patches to model the interior, resulting in 134 patches in total.
The latest version of the Utah Teapot is prepared by Prof. Cem Yuksel of the University of Utah. This version uses much fewer patches for modeling the Teapot's interior and more closely follows the contours of its exterior. It contains a solid knob for the lid. The spout interior is also slightly modified to ensure that the surface normals are continuous where the tip of the spout meets the interior surface.
The new interior design works with both the round bottom of Frank Crow's model and the flat bottom used by Hank Driskill and Russ Fish. The official version has a round bottom, so that it matches the most commonly used form of the Utah Teapot today.
In addition, this latest version also includes two types of modifications to the exterior surface of the Utah Teapot:
Its original spline models are modified using a new continuity enhancement method to ensure curvature continuity. This ensures that the reflections on the teapot appear smooth, without the sharp changes that appear in the earlier versions. See the SIGGRAPH 2026 paper on this topic by Cem Yuksel, titled “Continuity-Enhancing Degree Elevation and Splits” for more information.
It also includes small chamfers where the spout and the handle meet the Teapot's body, ensuring that the teapot's surface normal is continuous over the exterior surface. The actual physical teapot that Martin Newell used as a reference has much larger chamfers, but adding such larger chamfers would deviate too much from the historical shape of the digital model.
The lightweight interior model of this version only needs 30 additional patches, so the resulting teapot has 62 patches in total.
Cem Yuksel also added texture mapping layouts that can be applied all versions of the Utah Teapot to achieve a bijective or a lower-distortion injective mapping.

A Song To Get You Through To The Weekend
Posted on November 11, 2026 Posted by Athena Scalzi 5 Comments
I thought for sure that I had previously done a post over League of Legends fictional K-Pop girl group, KDA, but it turns out I have never expressed my adoration for them here beyond sharing one of Madison Beer’s songs that I love (she’s one of the real artists behind the fictional popstars).
So, as the title of the post suggests, I was only going to share their newest song with you, but since I haven’t posted about KDA on here before, I’m going to do just a tad more than show you their new video.
I have been enamored with KDA since their debut eight years ago when they released “POP/STARS.” Not only did I love the immensely catchy song, but I also really liked the characters and the visual style of the music video, even though I had no idea who these characters from the game were since I don’t play League.
So definitely check out the video for “POP/STARS” first so you can get a feel for the overall vibe and art style.
Pretty cool, right? Well buckle up because the animators spent these past eight years well, and it shows very clearly in their newest release, “FIRESTARTER.”
WAS THAT NOT THE MOST AMAZING THING YOU’VE EVER SEEN? I am so beyond blown away. IT LOOKS SO FUCKING GOOOOD. When I watched it for the first time last week I genuinely thought to myself this was the greatest music video I’ve ever seen and I stand by that statement.
The fire? When she’s on the skateboard? The inverted colors dance? Lord, the animators have BLESSED US.
If you like either of these songs I highly recommend checking out their entire discography, all of them are fucking incredible and have been on repeat in my car for years. This one will certainly be added to that list.
Hope this music brightened your day! Do you like KDA, too? Do you play League? Let me know in the comments, and have a great day!
-AMS

====================================================================================================

With $720M round, Britain’s Fractile gets a leg up in the race for AI chips
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-11
19:34:55 UTC
04:34:55 KST

Fractile, a British startup developing AI chips, has raised a $720 million Series C backed by Britain’s Sovereign AI Fund with hopes of “ensuring the U.K. has a greater stake in the hardware that will underpin its future economy,” its government stated.
Alongside the likes of Axelera, Etched, and OLIX, this makes Fractile one of the top-funded startup players in the race for purpose-built AI chips. Such chips are one of several avenues being explored to reduce the cost of inference — aka using increasingly demanding AI models in production.
However, Fractile seeks to go beyond hardware with a full-stack approach that also includes a machine learning compiler to maximize the speed at which LLMs will get executed on its chips.
As a result, the company frames itself as a frontier AI company, and its latest round led by Thrive Capital, Founders Fund, Lightspeed Venture Partners, and Redpoint was priced accordingly. According to the British government, the valuation was “over £5 billion” — quite possibly $7 billion, a figure reported both by the Financial Times and a Reuters source.
Still, Fractile’s main differentiator will arguably be its hardware design. After a pivot, its approach now revolves on DRAM, the most common type of RAM, but stacked on top of each chip in such a way that it is hoping will bypass the usual trade-off between memory and capacity.
This trade-off has become more problematic with the rise of agentic AI, as companies seek to perform longer chains of tasks with large models without compromising on speed. As compute struggles to keep up, hopes are high for AI chips, despite their limited availability to date.
In a LinkedIn post, Fractile CEO Walter Goodwin wrote that “there is still a lot of work ahead” for the company, and that the new funding will enable the startup to “ship [its] first chips and systems next year.”

Congress to call Jensen Huang in to answer for alleged Chinese spy affair
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-11
19:22:22 UTC
04:22:22 KST

Congress is gearing up to subpoena Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang to answer their questions about his affair with an alleged Chinese spy, Bloomberg reports, citing two House Republicans.
The report comes a week after House Republicans Brian Mast, John Moolenaar, and Rick Crawford sent a letter to Huang asking him to appear before their committees for a joint, closed-door briefing in regards to his relations with Alice Huang, the Megaspeed executive and suspected Chinese spy.
House China committee Chairman Moolenaar said Wednesday on Fox Business that Huang has until the end of the day to respond to the request. If he doesn’t agree to the briefing, he’ll likely be subpoenaed, said Moolenaar.
Mast, who chairs the House Foreign Affairs Committee, on Wednesday said his committee is preparing a subpoena for Huang to send if he doesn’t respond.
Nvidia did not immediately respond to TechCrunch’s request for comment.

Pulling the Lever in a Subway Problem
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-11
19:22:09 UTC
04:22:09 KST

Hi! I'm the person who pulled the brake. What I saw:
The train starts pulling out of the station. Someone begins shouting that their arm is caught in the door. I look over, see that the door is closed somewhere between their elbow and wrist, but it's not clear exactly where with their jacket on. People, including me, start shouting to pull the brake, but no one does. The train keeps accelerating, and it looks to me like once we get to the end of the station they're going to lose their hand. I get to the back of the car and pull the brake.
After the train stops, the man manages to pull his arm into the train, with his bag still on the outside. If he'd let go of his bag I think he would have been able to do this sooner, but he was panicking as I suspect most people would with their arm half out of the train like that.
I was pretty shaken up, and didn't end up blogging about it, but it came up again at one of our effective altruism dinners. This got me thinking about ways that philosophical problems do and don't translate into real life.
In this situation the stakes were lower than in the traditional trolley problem, and the situation is closer to Scanlon's Transmitter Room (What We Owe to Each Other, quoted jokingly here). The worst case was someone's arm being badly injured, maybe permanently. On the other side was delaying the whole line for an hour, perhaps losing ~4 years of waking life in total (~25k people * 1hr / 16 / 365 = 4.3y). But were these the real stakes?
I now think his arm probably wasn't caught in the door. That's what it looked like to me, and I was very sure at the time, but people who were closer disagree:
 I was in the car too -- I could tell that it was just his bag in the
 door, but in a split second, I was having a hard time telling how big
 his bag was, and I wasn't about to guess how wide the tunnels were
 since he wasn't letting go of his bag when the train started
 moving. I'm glad u/cbr ran to pull the brake -- I was one of the
 folks yelling to passengers to hit the call button to get the T to
 stop, and everyone at that end of the train just seemed to freeze.
 —zapdot
And:
I think what the above poster is saying is that there was a lot of
miscommunication inside the car. I watched it happen from the outside
and I never saw the dude's hand in the door. I saw him pulling the bag
with both hands then I saw the brake come on.
—dizzyrags
This illustrates a major way philosophical problems can fail to translate to real life: we're always acting under uncertainty, without knowing the consequences of our options. People understand this, and often "fight the hypothetical", but needing to make decisions when you really don't know whether you've read the situation correctly is a key part of real life morality. We need to make decisions that are robust to us having misinterpreted the situation.
(When Julia found herself in "drowning child" situation it was similar: the hard part was assessing the world, not whether to act.)
The other big way this differs from a textbook thought experiment is that it's not clear that the "inaction" case actually would have avoided a train delay. If I'd been right about his arm, I expect once the accident happened he would have been screaming even more, and I or someone else would have pulled the brake anyway. Which might well mean a worse delay, since now there's an injury involved too.
Which illustrates another way that intuitions built from philosophical problems can lead us the wrong way: this didn't occur to me at the time. It's hard to think very far ahead in an emergency, but morality problems often suggest a frame of mind where we have time to fully reason out the likely effects of each of our options. If I had thought this through it would have been an additional point in favor of pulling the brake. Or maybe I would have thought even further ahead: "if I do not pull the brake, I will become a famous example of a calculating (derogatory) utilitarian. I MUST NOT DO THAT."
Another thing I learned is that the suggestion Cialdini makes in Influence (quoted in Yudkowsky's Bystander Apathy) to point to one single person and ask them for help doesn't reliably work. When this happened I wasn't near the brake and I pointed to the person next to it and shouted something like "you, the brake!" They didn't, so I ran down the train and did it myself.
One philosophical idea that did transfer to real life is the Unilateralist's Curse. Perhaps the reason no one else on the train pulled the lever is that they all correctly perceived that the person was only in danger of losing their bag and not their arm. The one person (hi!) who came to a different conclusion still delayed the train for everyone. I don't think that was fully what happened here, since others were also shouting to pull the brake (though they were not independent observers). But since the "it's ok" position was harder to communicate, I think part of what happened was what we could call "expressivity bias", where an idea that was harder to express lost out, combined with it just being very hard to integrate diverse viewpoints under time pressure in a stressful situation.
As someone who had often speculated about philosophical problems, the experience of being in one was very stressful, and it's not one I hope to repeat.
Comment via: facebook, lesswrong, the EA Forum, mastodon, bluesky

Retirement Savings Are Fueling the AI Bubble
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-11
19:06:43 UTC
04:06:43 KST

Are we on the brink of the next financial crisis? Last Thursday, there was a “bloodbath in tech stocks,” as the stock market lost $3.2 trillion in a single day. The media is blaming the “blue wave,” but anxiety about the AI boom was already rising before the election. The week prior, on October 29, the “Magnificent Seven”—the tech companies that have driven much of the stock market’s recent growth—lost nearly a trillion dollars in value. The immediate catalyst for that sell-off was the realization that Google, Amazon, and Meta have all become “cash incinerators,” as their latest earnings reports showed astronomical spending on AI and a growing reliance on borrowing. The question that skeptics have long raised about the data center buildout has suddenly reached a fever pitch: will there actually be enough demand for AI for the companies to cover these massive investments and pay back their loans?
What has yet to enter public consciousness, however, is the extent to which working people’s financial assets have been used to fuel this bubble. Individuals protesting data centers often have no idea that their retirement accounts are being used to finance the companies responsible for these projects. Nor are they aware that they are at risk of being left holding the bag.
As “private” credit and equity funds have become a new center of gravity in financial markets, our public pension fund systems have increasingly allocated the retirement savings of public sector workers to these opaque, unregulated institutions. Similarly, thanks to recent efforts by the Trump administration, the $15 trillion held in private sector workers’ retirement accounts is now, for the first time in history, available to be invested in private funds. And it is to these funds that the companies building out data centers (and the gas plants necessary to power them) have turned for financing. According to a new report from Americans for Financial Reform, for instance, private equity firms are behind 25-30% of data center financing. By putting our retirement chips in their basket, we are bound up in the risks that these companies are taking in the AI boom—even though they do not have to disclose those risks to us.
What are those risks? While the AI bubble is not, thankfully, built on an edifice of predatory mortgages, the complexity and scale of the loans made to data center operators and Big Tech is giving many people painful flashbacks to 2008. As the credit ratings agency S&P stated plainly in September, “the credit quality of hyperscalers is weakening,” due to the projected $7 trillion in spending by 2030, much of which has to be financed by debt. That deterioration is already appearing in credit markets. Sales of the infamous credit default swaps (CDS) are quickly increasing: more than $9 billion of CDS linked to Big Tech bonds was traded weekly in September, and they’re getting more expensive as there is more demand for the protection they offer. All of the financial transactions involved in building data centers are also increasingly correlated due to the dense web of financing and customer relationships in the sector, as well as the complexity of financing relationships in many data center projects. As S&P states, “if demand disappoints, chip values fall, or data center utilization declines, do the losses sit with the operator, the sponsor or the guarantor?”
These risks are complicated by last week’s election. Voters overwhelmingly rejected the construction of data centers—electing governors in Texas, Georgia, and New Mexico who have committed to some form of data center slowdown or moratorium, while approving nearly every ballot measure that called for data center moratoria or slowdowns. According to Axios, Texas was projected to account for nearly half of the data center buildout in 2028. Gina Hinojosa’s victory over Greg Abbott just changed the whole trajectory of the industry. Elon Musk, as the results came in, observed that “data centers in space looking pretty appealing right now.” This is an excellent outcome—not least because it may prompt some companies to revise their projected data center buildout—yet it does not wipe away the mountain of debt that has already accumulated, nor the risk that these debts will become unpayable.
The risk of financial collapse is not, at the end of the day, the most damaging part of the AI buildout. Yet it is the aspect over which pension fund trustees and people saving for retirement have the most control—we can demand that our pensions refrain from holding risky, unregulated financial assets linked to the data center boom, as some public pension fund trustees have started to do. Employees can demand that their employer find an asset manager that will not move their funds into unregulated financial assets. Note, however, that this will require more than shifting our portfolios “back” towards holding index funds that buy the tech-heavy stock market. As SpaceX becomes part of the S&P 500, and Anthropic and OpenAI become publicly traded, the stock markets will only get more AI-pilled.
In the longer term, with enough political will, we can redirect our retirement savings towards productive investment, such as building out affordable housing and funding the renewable energy transition. For example, NYC Comptroller Mark Levine announced a $4 billion affordable housing initiative in April. Pension fund trustees could follow his lead rather than direct money towards Blackstone, one of the country’s largest landlords and a major fund manager for public pensions. More ambitiously, as I’ve written elsewhere, the government should establish public asset managers and a public credit fund to change the structure of how public pension funds are deployed. We do not need to act as if pension funds and retirement accounts are being directed into the AI bubble by the invisible hand of the market: portfolio allocation choices are, in the context of this complicated disaster, some of the easiest places to apply pressure.
Recently, Michael Burry, the hedge fund manager made famous by Michael Lewis’ The Big Short, said “all the capital being spent and lent by the richest companies on Earth won’t be enough to keep the bubble from eventually bursting.” One of the most galling claims after the Great Financial Crisis was that no one could have seen it coming. This time, at least, it is staring us right in the face.

====================================================================================================

Court Says Trump Can't Use Leaks To Justify Spying On Journalists' LinkedIn DMs
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-11
19:00:00 UTC
04:00:00 KST

Court Says Trump Can't Use Leaks To Justify Spying On Journalists' LinkedIn DMs (arstechnica.com) 27
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from Ars Technica: The Trump administration's fight to stop suspected leaks of top-secret military information on LinkedIn can't sweep in "a vast number of confidential sources" of random journalists "who have no conceivable relationship" to the Department of Justice's investigation, an appeals court ruled (PDF) Tuesday. The order came after the DOJ asked LinkedIn to turn over 18 months of communications from targeted journalists who collectively published more than 1,000 articles on many different topics over that time period. The request was not limited to journalists involved in a controversial report from a news outlet that Trump said exposed confidential military information in a foreign country. (The outlet is not being named to protect national security secrets.) Instead, the DOJ cast a wide net, requesting extensive LinkedIn data on journalists who had any possible link to the journalists around the time the report was drafted.
The majority of journalists have LinkedIn accounts and publish their contact information to encourage sources to connect on the platform, LinkedIn argued. So allowing that government overreach went beyond harming targeted journalists and threatened to undermine a critical platform where journalists frequently connect with confidential sources, judges ruled. Even more concerning, the Trump administration secured a secrecy order barring LinkedIn from telling anyone about the sweeping request for a year. That left journalists unaware for far too long that their First Amendment rights had been compromised, judges ruled. "A request by the government to scoop up reporters' records wholesale and ascertain the identity of their sources would tip the balance against informed discourse in a manner inimical to democratic health," Wilkinson wrote. He criticized the lower court for approving the request simply because the records were technically feasible to collect, emphasizing that "serious security and speech claims are in tension in this case. There is space under law for both."
While declining to quash the order entirely, Wilkinson also directed the lower court to reconsider a limited exception to the government's secrecy order that would allow LinkedIn to notify an attorney representing the news outlet under investigation.
Bruce D. Brown, President of The Reporters Committee for Freedom of the Press (RCFP), celebrated the ruling, saying: "This ruling is a big deal and will quickly become one of the leading opinions in the area -- one we will be quoting from it for a long time. The court recognized that confidential sources are the 'lifeblood' of investigative reporting and that dragnet demands from technology companies to reveal those sources violate the law."

Cancer vaccine for Yash, he's diagnosed with stage IV pancreatic cancer
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-11
18:57:01 UTC
03:57:01 KST

rnafoundry.com/yash, manufactured at the RNA Foundry.
In January 2025, 32-year-old Yashdeep Bindal was diagnosed with stage IV pancreatic cancer. He had just returned to work after the birth of his daughter, Maya.
At the RNA Foundry, we are developing a personalized cancer vaccine for Yash. We’re sharing his tumour DNA, tumour RNA, matched-normal DNA and vaccine designs so that other researchers can help refine the work.
Get the data · See the lead design · Run the code · Contribute
The data guide describes each dataset, sample and reference scope. The file catalog lists locations, sizes and SHA-256 checksums.
Clinical timeline — diagnosis, treatments, scan findings and specimen collection.
- Stanford pathology and IHC — January 2025.
- Stanford STAMP — 31 January 2025.
- Caris tissue profiling — 22 February 2025.
- Guardant360 liquid biopsy — 21 October 2025.
Design #2 is the lead design, generated using matched-normal white-blood-cell DNA alongside tumour DNA and RNA. It contains 24 antigen regions in a 724-aa protein: 12 mutation, 5 splice, 5 CTA/self and 2 fusion regions.
- Selected antigens · protein · antigen order.
- RNA segment · coding DNA · annotated DNA segment.
- Methods · antigen-by-antigen review.
PolyA and any downstream extension are unspecified; nucleotide files contain the known segments. Design #1, generated without matched-normal DNA, is retained for comparison.
With Python 3.11 or 3.12, run from the repository root:
python3.11 -m venv .venv
source .venv/bin/activate
python -m pip install -e '.[mrna]'
yash-vaccine verify
yash-vaccine reproduce --output work/design-2-replay
These commands check and replay the lead design using bundled evidence and recorded predictions. They need no sequencing downloads or cloud account. The usage guide explains replay and alternative designs; the pipeline guide covers new raw-read processing and inference.
Fork the repository, add your work under designs/your-design/, and submit a pull request. Include your rationale, data sources, code, commands and useful results. Annotated protein or RNA sequences are welcome; label unspecified components explicitly. An analysis or antigen list can be contributed before a complete construct is assembled.
See CONTRIBUTING.md. Questions and ideas are welcome as GitHub issues or at yash@rnafoundry.com.
This is one approach to vaccine design. Alternatives could examine MHC class II targets, immunogenicity, antigen trafficking, or linker optimization for processing and presentation.
Shared data are in data/, designs in designs/, maintained helpers in src/, and upstream jobs in workflow/. See the agent guide, reference acquisition guide, provenance records, required credits and retained-source notices for details.

Gemini Decisions API
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-11
18:32:42 UTC
03:32:42 KST

Gemini Decisions API: Fast, calibrated multimodal decisions
Today, we’re introducing the Gemini Decisions API, a new endpoint for fast, closed-set decisions powered by our new Gemini Decision 1 model (gemini-decision-1), available through the Gemini API in Google AI Studio.
The Decisions API gives you purpose-built primitives for bounded choices like tool selection, policy checks, model routing, and context grading. You can pass one multimodal input across text, images, audio, video, and PDFs (up to 256K tokens) along with up to 1,000 independent questions. Rather than generating answer text token by token, Gemini Decision 1 scores your candidate options in a single forward pass, returning probabilities and confidence scores in 150-250 ms end-to-end latency at $0.10 per 1M input tokens with zero output tokens billed.
Strong performance and calibration
On benchmarks spanning agent routing, reasoning, ranking, and multimodal understanding, Gemini Decision 1 leads across the suite, with the lowest calibration error.
Gemini Decision 1 scores options without token-by-token decoding, keeping median latency 50-100 ms of model compute (150-250 ms end-to-end), across multimodal inputs and contexts up to 16K tokens, remaining nearly flat from 1 to 100 questions per request. With zero output tokens billed, Gemini Decision 1 with the Decisions API is nearly 5x cheaper across the shared benchmark suite than Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite with the Interactions API.
Native multimodal evaluation across three decision types in one pass
Built on Gemini’s native multimodal architecture, Gemini Decision 1 can process text, images, audio, video, and PDFs directly in a single forward pass. You can combine modalities in one shared input, such as pairing a call recording with account metadata or a scanned PDF claim with damage photos, and tune resolution on image and video inputs to control visual detail and token cost.
You can evaluate multiple questions against that shared input in a single request, mixing yes/no checks, multiple choice, and rubric scores. Every answer includes a confidence score (from 0 to 1) so your code can route ambiguous cases to a fallback or human reviewer:
- noul (Yes/No): Estimates the probability that a yes/no condition holds, with optional true and false description criteria.
- choice (Multiple Choice): Selects from 2 to 255 choices, returning the winner and normalized probabilities for every candidate.
- score (Ordered Rubric): Grades inputs across an ordered rubric (starting at 0), returning a continuous weighted score and per-level probabilities.
The input field uses the same Content block format as the Interactions API. Here is how to check a photo of a breakroom counter, together with a text note, against all three question types in one request:
from google import genai
client = genai.Client(http_options={"api_version": "v1beta"})
result = client.decisions.create(
    model="gemini-decision-1",
    input=[{
            "type": "image",
            "uri": "https://storage.googleapis.com/generativeai-downloads/images/spill.jpg",
            "mime_type": "image/jpeg",
        },
        {
            "type": "text",
            "text": "Breakroom counter check before the 9 AM client meeting.",
        },
    ],
    questions={
        "has_spill": {
            "type": "noul",
            "instructions": "Is there an uncleaned liquid spill on the counter?",
        },
        "next_action": {
            "type": "choice",
            "instructions": "Which cleanup action fits the scene?",
            "criteria": {
                "wipe_with_cloth": "Small counter spill with a cloth or towel already nearby.",
                "dispatch_janitorial": "Large floor hazard, broken glass, or active leak.",
                "ready_for_meeting": "Counter is clean and ready.",
            },
        },
        "severity": {
            "type": "score",
            "instructions": "Rate the severity of the counter mess.",
            "criteria": [
                "Clean and ready.",
                "Minor localized spill.",
                "Major spill or safety hazard.",
            ],
        },
    },
)
How teams are building with the Decisions API
Teams across Google are already using the Decisions API as a low-latency control plane, shipping features simply by defining options rather than training custom classifiers or waiting on generative calls:
- Dynamic model routing in Gemini Notebook: Gemini Notebook uses the Decisions API as a real-time router to instantly direct incoming queries to the optimal model tier and thinking level for each request. The Decisions API classifies intent in milliseconds to serve everyday queries with up to 40% lower latency while reserving deep reasoning models for complex tasks.
- Rapid design iteration in Stitch: The new rapid canvas experiment in Stitch uses the Decisions API to generate playable wireframes of a product flow in seconds and to apply edits made by voice or tap almost instantly. When the flow feels right, one click hands the screens off to the full Stitch experience for deeper design polish.
- Emoji selection in CC: CC from Google Labs uses the Decisions API to pick the right emoji reaction from a palette of more than 200 options in a fraction of a second, so reactions appear instantly without adding latency.
- Safety layer for MCP tools in Search: Google Search uses the Decision API to detect adversarial behavior and content against adversarial evals with high efficacy within the latency envelope.
Stitch Rapid uses the Decisions API to design an entire product flow in seconds (1x speed).
Start building today
Gemini Decision 1 is priced at $0.10 per 1M input tokens and $0 for output tokens, with an API no-cost tier you can use in Google AI Studio for prototyping. Your shared input is billed once per request no matter how many questions you evaluate (each question adds only its own instruction and criteria tokens). There are no output-token charges, variable thinking-token bills, or separate audio transcription and OCR costs.
You can start building today by grabbing an API key in Google AI Studio, trying our Doorstep Parcels, Speed Sketch, and Find the Zoo Keeper demo apps, and following the Python, JavaScript, and REST guides in the Decisions API documentation.
Ecosystem integrations are rolling out across OpenRouter, LangChain, Vercel, LiteLLM, Pydantic AI, Pipecat, LiveKit, and OpenCode alongside the google-genai SDKs, with support coming soon to Gemini in Google Cloud.

====================================================================================================

AWS Emulator and the engineer's job when agents write the code
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-11

AWS Emulator and the engineer's job when agents write the code
In 2004, barely a month into my new job at Amazon, I got the opportunity to pick an intern to join my team. I had an advantage over my colleagues: I had already heard about a brilliant young man working toward his PhD at the Vrije Universiteit in Amsterdam (where I happened to have received mine as well :-)). I immediately picked Swaminathan Sivasubramanian, whom you all now know as Swami. It was probably one of the best decisions I have made in my 22 years as Amazon’s CTO.
After a disastrous Christmas in 2004, when commercial relational databases could not scale to Amazon’s needs, I asked Swami to do a deep dive into how relational databases were actually used at Amazon. It turned out that 70% of database operations were not relational at all. They were key-value. That insight led to Dynamo, which Swami helped architect. When his internship was over, I hired him full-time right away, even before he had finished his PhD.
Bringing in another opinionated academic led to many fantastic discussions with Amazon’s practical engineers, who had become scale experts by actually building at scale. Eventual vs. strong consistency, CAP vs. Paxos, and more. Over time, Swami became one of the principal architects of AWS’s data strategy. And as we have seen in recent years, he has also been one of the intellectual drivers behind AI innovation at Amazon.
I am honoured that he wanted to write a guest post about the fundamental distributed systems work that sits behind many of AWS’s services, and about how the engineer’s work changes when agents write the code. AWS Emulator, launched today, gives agents the kind of test environment that work relied on. Swami’s story is another great example of invisible engineering.
Now, go build.
-W
AWS Emulator and the engineer's job when agents write the code
Today we release AWS Emulator, which gives coding agents a local copy of AWS to test against, and it connects to a project from early in my career. In many ways, this emulator launch reminds me of the effort we put into reliable software engineering to improve the quality of the distributed systems we build. To that end, we wanted to share a story:
About twenty years ago, Werner asked me to work with one of Amazon’s earliest engineers, Al Vermeulen, on a distributed lock service we called “Alf”. Alf was built on Paxos, a protocol based on Leslie Lamport’s work that lets a group of servers agree on one value, and a replication engine in it kept copies of the data consistent across servers. Alf, to date, is the service that powers many of our complex distributed systems like S3, DynamoDB and many more (although it has evolved quite a bit with various changes on top – will save it for another blog!).
Distributed systems are hard to test
Paxos is the gold standard among distributed systems consensus protocols and was well understood in academia. However, building it at the scale Amazon and AWS were growing into was not easy. We had to deal with not just replication across servers and data centers but also tens of thousands of clients asking for locks at the same time, renewing their leases during network partitions all the while with the core requirement: Always be correct! At that time, the best ideas for testing distributed systems were large-scale system testing and chaos-based testing. Both worked, but they were expensive, and they could not reproduce many kinds of conditions on demand. That mattered for Alf, which was meant to be the truth store, giving every client a strongly consistent view of the truth, and which had to keep working through server failures and packet loss as well. Several industry papers had written that Paxos-based locking services were hard to get right, and the difficulty of testing them was part of the reason.
We addressed that by simulating Alf’s environment with as much fidelity as we could manage. We ran the servers inside a JVM and simulated the network and the rest of the infrastructure they depended on, so the whole system lived in one program that we controlled. We could drop packets and inject other faults whenever we chose, and we built tools that poked at the simulation for bugs and scalability problems. Concurrency bugs appear only when events happen in a particular order, and in the simulation we could set up many of those orderings on purpose and inspect how Alf handled each one. We also used formal reasoning, through theoretical techniques such as TLA+, and it found many issues as well. By testing this way we ironed out most of Alf’s concurrency bugs before it ran on real servers. Testing in a real environment then turned up different problems, because the real world exposes things a simulation cannot.
Outgrowing hand-built test environments
Simulation and formal reasoning both improved the software quality of the distributed systems we applied them to. The simulation worked because a small team could build a copy of one system’s environment by hand, and at the time AWS had only a small handful of services. But today AWS has over 200 services, and an application that calls AWS needs a test environment for each one it uses. Building all of those by hand would take years of engineering, and agents now write code far faster than any team can check it in production. So, we wanted to know whether agents could build and test those environments themselves.
The idea came from Matt Goldberg, a Senior Principal Engineer on the AWS Developer Tools team. His hypothesis was that, given a written description of how a service should behave and the real service to check against, an agent could build a working version of that service on its own. For AWS, the written description is the set of public API models, written in Smithy, AWS’s format for describing a service’s operations and their inputs, outputs, and errors. The real service to check against is the AWS cloud itself, the same system customers use.
The agents work from fidelity tests, which are end-to-end customer scenarios devised from the public API models. A test might check, for example, that in Amazon Simple Queue Service a received message stays hidden from other readers until its visibility timeout runs out. The agents correct each test until it passes against real AWS, so the test records how AWS behaves. The agents then build the emulator until it passes that same test, and a test that still fails points at a gap in the emulator. Matt began with SQS alone, and when he gave Kiro one prompt describing this approach, it returned a passable first version of an SQS emulator in about 15 minutes. Matt Goldberg’s launch post tells how that prototype grew into AWS Emulator. All of AWS Emulator’s code was written and tested by agents, and it is built from more than 400 API models covering more than 200 AWS services. It runs free and offline on your own machine. Teams of agents test it against real AWS and fix the gaps they find. Every change goes through a merge gate, a deep bench of automated checks that every change must pass before it enters the codebase, including several security agents doing adversarial reviews.
Infrastructure in the coding loop
A coding agent works in a loop: it writes code, runs it, and reads the result before deciding what to change next. In distributed systems that loop runs inside infrastructure, so the services it calls have to behave like the real ones. When they do, an agent can check its own work as it writes and produce correct code on its first attempt far more often, which improves software quality. AWS Emulator puts that infrastructure inside the loop on your own machine.
The engineer’s job when agents write the code
When agents write the code and check their own work, the engineer’s job is to give them room to work on their own and to build the specs, tests, and merge gates that decide when that work is correct. It was a lesson I learned the hard way when I used Kiro to rebuild the replication engine that Al and I had built for Alf, which took us about four months the first time. I supervised the agent for four nights before I saw that it had no way to check its results. Once it had a spec and a test environment, the rebuild took about two hours.
The agents building AWS Emulator had both from the start, in the public API models and in real AWS. An engineer built the scaffolding that runs them. An internal version came together over a weekend and started gaining adoption within a month. Turning that into a robust solution took much more work.
Software quality in the AI era depends on the guardrails that engineers design around agents. In AWS Emulator, those guardrails are the fidelity tests verified against real AWS, a merge gate with multiple security agents that each review a change for different ways to attack it alongside a general code reviewer, and deterministic checks that stop agents from touching code they are not supposed to. We expect agents to build and check most software, with engineers deciding what gets built and when the work is correct. We are early in learning how engineers drive teams of agents to build reliable, high-quality software, and the way AWS Emulator came to life is a good example of how that works in practice. We expect to keep adding guardrails and refining how the agents build it.
What to know at launch
The emulator code is released under CC0, a Creative Commons dedication to the public domain.
We will share more about this work at re:Invent. The full commit history and every issue the agents have filed are public in the GitHub repository, and its “things-that-do-not-work” folder holds the issues describing behaviors the agents found that do not yet match real AWS.

====================================================================================================

AWS Finally Built Its Own AWS Emulator
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-11
18:21:38 UTC
03:21:38 KST

AWS Emulator is a local emulator of the AWS APIs. You use it to test your software without calling AWS, on your own machine, on a development server, or in continuous integration (CI). You don’t need an AWS account. Your requests go to the emulator, not to AWS. So you work with emulated resources instead of AWS resources. AWS Emulator has no additional service fee. You pay only for AWS resources when you deploy on AWS.
The emulator is a local program that serves an AWS API endpoint. It has no graphical interface. After you start the emulator, you set environment variables to point the AWS CLI or an AWS SDK at its endpoint. Then you run your code as usual. In embedded mode, you pass the emulator’s HTTP client to your SDK instead.
AWS Emulator is for building and testing. Don’t use it for production workloads or to test security. See Security in AWS Emulator.
Documentation: AWS Emulator User Guide
Install the aws-emulator skill, then ask your agent to set up the emulator. This needs the AWS CLI version 2.35.0 or later. See Getting started with an AI coding agent.
aws agent-toolkit add-skill --skill-name aws-emulator --region us-east-1
- Linux: install the RPM or Debian package and run aws-emulator. See Getting started on Linux.
- Windows / macOS: run the emulator in a container built from a short Dockerfile. See Getting started with Docker.
Point your SDK at a running emulator by setting environment variables. See Pointing your AWS SDK at AWS Emulator.
Add the emulator as a dev-dependency and run it in-process, one isolated instance per test. There is no endpoint URL, certificate, or environment variable to set. Your test wires the emulator's HTTP client straight into the SDK client. See Getting started with embedded mode, and Building from source below for its build cost.
To see how closely each service matches AWS, check the fidelity scores published with each release on the releases page. To check whether your own project is expected to work, see Checking whether your project works with AWS Emulator.
Individual gaps are tracked in the repository's issues/ directory. APIs and behaviors that don't work yet are under issues/things-that-do-not-work/, and ones that have since been fixed move to issues/things-that-now-work/.
You need this if you are modifying the emulator, running it from a checkout, or using embedded mode. Embedded mode compiles the emulator into your test binary, so the prerequisites and build-cost guidance below apply to it too. Most other users install a prebuilt package instead (see Quick start). Building from source is supported on Linux (x86_64 and aarch64); on Windows and macOS, use the Docker path above.
- A Rust toolchain with Cargo (install via rustup).
- A C compiler, such as GCC or clang. Some dependencies build C code.
- At least 100 GB of free disk for target/. The workspace has one crate per AWS service, so a debug build of everything is large, and it grows as services are added.
- A multi-core machine. A cold full build compiles every service crate, so the first build takes a while; fewer cores take proportionally longer. After that, rebuilding after a change to one service is much faster.
cargo build -p aws-emulator-router
cargo run   -p aws-emulator-router
The emulator serves HTTPS on 127.0.0.1:9297 (self-signed certificate).
Embedded mode compiles the emulator into your test binary, so your project's first cargo test builds the emulator's service crates alongside your own code. Budget a first build and target/ on the same order as building from source above, then fast incremental builds. Recommendation: reuse one CARGO_TARGET_DIR across projects, cache target/ (or use sccache) in CI, and pin the emulator to a release commit so it isn't rebuilt on every change.
.
├── Cargo.toml              # workspace: one member per service, plus the core crates
├── services/
│   └── service-<name>/     # ONE CRATE PER SERVICE
│       ├── smithy-model/   # <name>.json — the Smithy model that drives the wire protocol
│       ├── build.rs        # generates the service declaration from the model
│       ├── src/
│       │   ├── service.rs  # request routing and refusal overrides
│       │   ├── state.rs    # this service's in-memory state
│       │   └── ops/        # one file per operation
│       └── tests/
├── core/
│   ├── router/             # the emulator binary: mounts every service on one endpoint
│   └── embedded/           # the in-process (embedded mode) facade
├── vendor/                 # framework crates — do not edit (replaced on update)
└── issues/                 # tracked fidelity issues
AWS Emulator is configured to collect and report usage data to AWS, to gain insight into how AWS Emulator is used. Telemetry is on by default. AWS Emulator collects telemetry data with strict data minimization in mind, preventing identification or attribution to specific users, accounts, or organizations. Its records never include what’s in your requests or responses, your code, your credentials, or personal data. We don’t share telemetry with third parties.
For details on what is collected and how the data is handled, see Telemetry in AWS Emulator. To turn telemetry off, use one of the following options.
Start the emulator with the --telemetry option (short form -t) set to off. This setting applies only to that emulator process.
aws-emulator --telemetry off
In a container, add --telemetry off after the image name in the docker run command.
Set EMULATOR_TELEMETRY to off before you start the emulator. This setting applies to every emulator that you start from that shell.
EMULATOR_TELEMETRY accepts on, true, or 1 to turn telemetry on, and off, false, or 0 to turn it off. Values aren’t case sensitive. Any other value turns telemetry off, and the startup notice shows a warning.
export EMULATOR_TELEMETRY=off
aws-emulator
To turn telemetry off for every run on a machine, add these lines to the emulator settings file, config.toml. The file is ~/.aws/emulator/config.toml on Linux and macOS, and %USERPROFILE%\.aws\emulator\config.toml on Windows. To use a different file, set the EMULATOR_CONFIG environment variable to its path.
[telemetry]
enabled = false
In embedded mode, turn off telemetry in the options that you pass when you create the emulator.
use aws_emulator_embedded::{AwsEmulatorEmbedded, EmbeddedOptions, Telemetry};
fn main() {
    let emulator = AwsEmulatorEmbedded::new(EmbeddedOptions::default().telemetry(Telemetry::Off));
    let _http_client = emulator.http_client();
}
You might build a tool that includes the emulator, such as a test framework that other people run. If you do, tell your users about telemetry and give them a way to turn it off. You can also ship your tool with telemetry off.
Report bugs and fidelity gaps in GitHub Issues. The most useful report names the service, the operation, your SDK and version, the request you sent, and how the response differed from real AWS. This repository does not take PRs.
This software is licensed under the CC0 1.0 Universal (CC0-1.0) License, except the code in the vendor/ directory, which is under the Apache License 2.0. See LICENSE for details, and vendor/README.md for the license of the code in vendor/.

====================================================================================================

}
You might build a tool that includes the emulator, such as a test framework that other people run. If you do, tell your users about telemetry and give them a way to turn it off. You can also ship your tool with telemetry off.
Report bugs and fidelity gaps in GitHub Issues. The most useful report names the service, the operation, your SDK and version, the request you sent, and how the response differed from real AWS. This repository does not take PRs.
This software is licensed under the CC0 1.0 Universal (CC0-1.0) License, except the code in the vendor/ directory, which is under the Apache License 2.0. See LICENSE for details, and vendor/README.md for the license of the code in vendor/.

Loot boxes, influencers, and addictive features face an EU crackdown
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-11
18:13:52 UTC
03:13:52 KST

Gaming, social media, and e-commerce platforms are facing a fresh wave of sweeping European Union rules that aim to make “your online experience fairer.” The newly proposed Digital Fairness Act (DFA) published by the EU Commission on Wednesday is designed to tackle practices that exploit consumers into spending more of their time and money online, including misleading prices or availability, addictive design features like gaming loot boxes, undisclosed influencer marketing, and more.
Loot boxes, influencers, and addictive features face an EU crackdown
The proposed Digital Fairness Act seeks to bolster consumer protections for online users.
“The Act will protect consumers of all ages shopping and browsing for goods or services online,” the EU Commission said in a news bulletin. “It will also mean fairer competition for businesses, which are expecting to save EU consumers up to €13.7 billion ($15.1 billion) a year.”
Here’s an overview of what the DFA proposal includes:
- A ban on manipulative marketing practices and so-called “dark patterns” that push consumers into making decisions they might not otherwise make, such as fake claims about a product almost being sold out.
- A requirement to give consumers the choice to turn off addictive features, such as persistent prompts, endless scrolling, and autoplay of videos. Restrictions around addictive design also apply to “AI companions and general conversational chatbots.”
- Restrictions on unfair practices that encourage overspending, such as banning gambling-like features (pay-to-win and pay-to-progress, for example) for minors.
- Requiring influencers to clearly disclose advertising content, and restrictions to prevent them from exploiting the trust of children.
- Restrictions on unfair personalised ads and prices that target minors or take advantage of sensitive data.
- Making subscriptions and digital contracts easier to cancel, with prompts to remind customers about upcoming automatic renewals and free trial periods coming to an end.
- Requiring clear and transparent pricing, especially around dynamic pricing and purchases using virtual in-app currency. When consumers wait in virtual queues for cultural and sports event tickets, companies will have to show the current price in real time and at the highest possible price.
- Giving consumers the right to communicate with a human customer service representative, and clear rights using an AI agent to make purchases.
The scope of the restrictions being proposed is broad, and could have a significant impact on major parts of the online digital landscape, from the “rabbit hole” algorithms across YouTube, TikTok, and Meta platforms, to AI chatbots and apps like DuoLingo that relentlessly nudge users to keep interacting with them. Some gaming organizations have already slammed the DFA proposal, describing its restrictions around virtual currencies, loot boxes, and offering micro-transactions to minors as being “unworkable, unjustified and disproportionate.”
Given this is a draft proposal, the DFA will undergo various approvals through the European Parliament and the Council of the European Union before being passed into law, and its current measures could be changed or removed entirely as part of that process. When (or if) it does come into force, the EU Commission says companies who violate the regulations could see fines of up to six percent of their worldwide annual turnover.
“Our consumer protection rules will help ensure that businesses compete on the merits of their products and services - not on their ability to exploit consumers or evade the rules. We are not making it harder to do business. We are making it harder to do business unfairly,” EU Commissioner Michael McGrath said in remarks following the DFA draft publication. “We cannot have 21st-century consumers protected by rules that have not kept pace with the 21st-century economy.”

I Never Make Stuffing in the Oven — This Easy Recipe Is a Lifesaver for Thanksgiving
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-11
18:00:00 UTC
03:00:00 KST

I Never Make Stuffing in the Oven — This Easy Recipe Is a Lifesaver for Thanksgiving
Thanksgiving is one of my favorite holidays. And as a vegetarian, it’s really all about the sides for me — my plate simply isn’t complete without a hefty scoop of stuffing. If you’re the one in charge of preparing the meal (hats off to you), you know that oven space comes at a premium on the big day. Well, thankfully this recipe might just give you a little of it back. With a bit of prep, your stuffing will be ready in a few hours of fully hands-off cooking — and then you can get on to making the other elements of the meal. Here’s why you should totally give it a try this Thanksgiving.
Get the recipe: Slow Cooker Stuffing
What Makes Slow Cooker Stuffing So Good
The hardest part of this recipe is making the croutons — seriously. To start, you’ll toast cubes of crusty bread for about an hour-and-a-half, checking and tossing them periodically until they’re crisp. But after that, it’s smooth sailing. Simply sauté the rest of the ingredients and seasonings, toss them with croutons and an eggy broth mixture, and let the slow cooker do its thing. In about 3 to 4 hours, you’ll have a delicious stuffing with incredible texture — all without even turning on the oven!
The pieces that are closer to the center are softer and more pillowy, while the outer chunks of bread have that crispy, chewy texture that I can’t get enough of. Plus, it’s soaked up all the delicious flavors of the broth, onion, celery, garlic, thyme, and sage (anything with those two herbs practically screams fall to me). It’s much more flavorful than the bagged stuff, and doesn’t require too much extra effort. Plus, you’ll be so thankful for the extra oven space for the candied yams, mac and cheese, and turkey on the big day.
How to Make Slow Cooker Stuffing
- Make the croutons. Toast the cubed bread until crispy.
- Sauté the vegetables. Cook the onion, celery, and garlic in melted butter, stirring in the thyme and sage at the end.
- Mix the stuffing. Whisk eggs, broth, salt, and pepper together. Add in the toasted bread and sautéed vegetables, and mix until combined.
- Slow-cook the stuffing. Add the mixture to a slow cooker and cook on low for about 3 to 4 hours.
Tips for Making Slow Cooker Stuffing
- Use stale bread: Stuffing is one of those recipes that’s pretty forgiving, and stale bread actually works perfectly here because it’s already dried-out, so you can skip the toasting step.
- Tweak to your liking: This recipe is pretty classic, but feel free to add your own spin with dried fruit, sausage, chestnuts, or mushrooms.
Get the recipe: Slow Cooker Stuffing
